Front matter · Why read this book

When Common Sense Stops Working

The intuitions that serve us in the physical world, and why they fail between machines

You have spent your whole life in a world with some very reliable rules. Things happen in an order that everyone around you agrees on. "Now" is the same moment for you and for the person next to you. When you hand someone a cup, they either have it or they do not. If you ask a question and hear nothing, the other person did not answer. When something breaks, it usually looks broken. And if you write something down twice, you have two identical copies.

Programming on a single computer mostly preserves these rules. A function call either returns or throws. Memory holds one value at a time, and every thread eventually agrees on it. The clock on the machine is the clock. When the computer fails, it fails completely, and everything stops together.

Distributed systems break every one of these rules. Not occasionally, and not only in exotic systems: in every web application talking to a database, every mobile app syncing with a server, every service calling another service, every message queue, every cluster in the cloud. Most software developers today work on distributed systems, whether or not anyone calls them that. And most of the confusing, expensive incidents they will ever be involved in come from one of these familiar rules quietly failing to hold.

The rules that stop working

What common sense says What happens between machines Where this book deals with it
There is one "now". Everyone agrees what time it is and what happened first. Every machine has its own clock, and the clocks disagree, drift and jump. Two events on different machines may have no meaningful order at all. Chapter 4
An action either happened or it did not. A request that timed out may have succeeded, failed, or still be running. You can be left not knowing, permanently. Chapter 2
An error means it failed. A connection reset, a gateway timeout or a 500 error can arrive after the work was done. The error is real; the failure may not be. Chapters 2 and 11
Silence means nothing is happening. Silence may mean a crash, a lost message, a slow machine, a paused process or a broken network. They all look the same from outside. Chapters 1 and 3
Broken things look broken. The most damaging failures are partial: a machine that is slow but alive, a network that loses 1 % of packets, a service that answers health checks but returns wrong results. Chapters 1, 3 and 12
A copy is a copy. Replicas lag, diverge and conflict. Two users can read different "current" values at the same moment, and both are right from where they stand. Chapters 5 and 6
Doing it again is harmless. A retry can charge a card twice, book two seats, or turn a brief slowdown into an outage lasting hours. Chapters 2 and 11
If everyone agrees, we can proceed. If messages can be delayed without limit, agreement between machines that may fail is provably impossible to guarantee. Practical systems get there by choosing what to give up. Chapters 3 and 7
More machines means more reliable. Only if their failures are independent, and they rarely are. More components in a chain make a system less available, not more. Chapter 1
A change happens at one moment. A deployment takes minutes or hours, during which old and new versions run side by side, and a rollback sends part of the system back in time. Chapters 14 and 15

None of this is a flaw in particular products. It follows from physics and from how networks are built. A message takes time to travel, and while it travels, the world at the other end keeps changing. Nobody can see the state of a remote machine directly; you can only see messages it sent in the past. As chapter 4 explains, the situation is closely related to Einstein's relativity: there is no single, global "now" that every observer shares, only the order that information could have travelled.

Note · A useful way to think about it

Imagine running a company where everyone works in a different city, communicates only by post, and the post sometimes loses, delays or duplicates letters. Nobody can see anyone else's desk. When a reply does not come, you cannot tell whether your letter was lost, the reply was lost, or your colleague is simply busy. Every technique in this book has an equivalent in that company: numbering letters, writing "reply even if you have already done this", agreeing that a decision needs a majority, sending a copy of the ledger every week, and asking each office to say clearly when it is too busy to take more work.

The common thread gives this book its title. On a single computer, things mostly either work or crash. In a distributed system, some parts fail while others carry on, and the parts that carry on often cannot tell what has failed, or whether a change has reached everyone yet. Almost every technique in the following chapters, from timeouts and idempotency to consensus, circuit breakers and compatible deployments, is a response to this partial failure.

Why it matters to you

You could treat all of this as theory and rely on frameworks, databases and cloud services to handle it. They do handle a great deal. But they cannot make the rules above true again. They can only choose which ones to approximate, and at what cost, and they document those choices in terms you need to understand: "eventually consistent", "at-least-once", "read committed", "multi-AZ", "exactly-once semantics". Without the concepts behind those words, it is easy to build something that works perfectly in testing and fails, quietly or spectacularly, the first time the network has a bad day.

The consequences land on real people. On passengers who were charged twice, or who stood at the wrong platform. On customers whose changes disappeared. On colleagues woken at three in the morning by an outage that kept itself going long after its cause had gone. On teams who cannot release their work because every change must be coordinated with ten other teams.

This book is for developers who want to stop being surprised by these things. It will give you:

  • a vocabulary precise enough to describe what can go wrong, so that design discussions and incident reviews become concrete instead of speculative;
  • mental models that let you predict how a design will behave under partial failure, overload and change, before production teaches you;
  • patterns and techniques that are used in real systems to cope: idempotency, quorums, consensus, CRDTs, backpressure, compatible versioning, progressive deployment;
  • judgement about trade-offs, because almost nothing in this field is free: every guarantee costs latency, availability, money or simplicity somewhere;
  • a critical eye, so that you can read a design document, a database's consistency claims or a vendor's postmortem and see what is being promised and what is being quietly left out;
  • the habits of change: organising services around teams, changing interfaces without breaking their consumers, and deploying safely and often;
  • practice, through labs that make you watch your own code fail under simulated partitions, retries and overload.

What you will not find here

This is not a guide to a particular database, cloud provider or framework. Products change every few years; the problems in this book have stayed the same for decades. Where the book names products, it uses them as examples of an idea. Nor is it a mathematics textbook: there are a few small calculations, and optional deep dives for readers who want proofs and research, but the main text is about understanding what happens to your software once it leaves a single machine.

Essential question

Which of the rules in the table above does the system you work on today silently rely on?

Keep your answer in mind. By the end of the book, you should be able to say how each of those assumptions can fail, what it would cost, and what you would do about it. The preface that follows explains how the book is organised and how to read it.

Front matter

Preface

Who this book is for, how it is organised, and how to read it

This preface is practical: who the book is written for, what it assumes, how it is organised and how to read it. Why the subject deserves your time is the topic of the previous section, "When Common Sense Stops Working".

Who it is for, and what it assumes

The book is written for working software developers: people who build services, integrations, data pipelines and platforms, and who either keep running into the behaviour of distributed systems or are now responsible for designing one. The examples come from systems such developers actually meet: booking and timetable services, message queues, databases with replicas, Kubernetes deployments, monitoring stacks, and the incidents that happen when these meet the real world.

It assumes that you write code for a living, or have equivalent experience. It does not assume a computer science degree. Readers who have one, including advanced students, will find extra depth in the optional deep dives and the labs. Chapter 0 lists the background knowledge the rest of the book relies on, so you can check for gaps before you start.

How the book is organised

The order of the chapters is deliberate. Leading courses (Martin Kleppmann's Cambridge lectures, MIT 6.5840, KTH ID2203) share one principle: describe what can go wrong before describing the solutions, and introduce time and ordering before replication and consensus. This book follows the same path.

  • Part I – Foundations (chapters 0–4). What a distributed system is, how its parts talk, what can fail, and why "what time is it?" is such a hard question.
  • Part II – Data and agreement (chapters 5–9). How we copy and split data, what "consistent" can mean, how nodes agree despite failures, how transactions span machines, and how some systems avoid coordination altogether.
  • Part III – Building and running systems (chapters 10–12). Architecture patterns, state, resilience under overload, and observability and testing.
  • Part IV – Architecture and change (chapters 13–16). A distributed system is also distributed between teams and customers, and it never stops changing. This part is about being a good neighbour: drawing service boundaries around teams, changing interfaces without breaking anyone, deploying safely, and finally real outages analysed with everything you have learned.

Each part opens with a short introduction that explains why it matters when you build and run real systems for real users, and how its chapters fit together.

At the end of the book, Appendix A is a complete lab guide, with step-by-step instructions for every lab and hidden hints that explain how to approach each solution. The Recommended reading guide is more than a bibliography. It explains why each book, course and paper is worth your time, who it suits, and in which order to read them.

Three layers in every chapter

Readers arrive with different backgrounds, so each chapter is written in three layers:

Layer Who it is for What it looks like
Core Everyone Plain explanations, analogies, definitions. Read all of it.
Application Most readers Worked examples, "Test yourself" boxes, labs. Do as many as you can.
Deep dive The curious Collapsible "Deep dive" panels with proofs, research results and open debates. Skip them on a first reading without losing the thread.

The boxes you will see

Definition

A precise statement of a term. Definitions appear the first time a term is used and are collected in the glossary.

Example

A worked scenario with a concrete setup, a walkthrough and the key insight it demonstrates.

Common mistake

A misconception or pitfall that catches experienced engineers too.

Test yourself

A retrieval exercise. Try to answer from memory before opening the answer. Struggling to recall is what makes the knowledge stick.

Show answer

Exactly. The answer hides behind a click so that you think first.

Lab

A hands-on exercise, usually with Fly.io's Gossip Glomers challenges (built on Jepsen's Maelstrom) or MIT 6.5840's Raft labs. Chapter 0 explains how to set up your environment, and Appendix A describes every lab in detail, with hidden hints and solution approaches.

A note on accuracy and dates

Distributed systems is a field in which folklore spreads faster than facts. Where this book states a fact about a specific system, paper or incident, it tries to name the source. Dates are given as ISO 8601 (2026-10-01). Incident timelines come from the companies' own postmortems, so read the originals before quoting figures in your own work.

Let us begin with what you should bring along.

Part I · Foundations
Part I · Introduction

Expect the Network to Lie

Why the foundations matter when real people depend on your system

The table at the start of this book listed the everyday rules that stop working between machines. Part I takes the most basic of them apart, one at a time, and puts something you can reason with in their place. This matters because most production incidents do not come from exotic algorithms. They come from those ordinary rules, applied where they no longer hold, and the result is a passenger charged twice, a lost update, or a departure board still showing a train that left an hour ago.

Part I exists to replace those assumptions with an accurate picture. It is the vocabulary you need for everything that follows, and also the vocabulary you need on an ordinary working day: in a design review when someone proposes "just retry it", in an incident channel when nobody knows whether a write went through, or when a vendor promises "exactly once".

The chapters build the picture step by step:

  • Chapter 0, Before you begin, sets out the background the book assumes, and shows why even a perfectly reliable network cannot tell you whether your request was processed.
  • Chapter 1, Why distribute, and why it is hard, names the defining problem of the field, partial failure, and the arithmetic of availability that decides whether redundancy actually helps.
  • Chapter 2, Communication, looks at what really happens to a remote call: timeouts that turn synchronous calls asynchronous, retries, idempotency, and how much slower a network call is than a function call.
  • Chapter 3, System models and failure, makes "what can go wrong" precise: crashes, partitions, pauses, slow hardware, and the limits on what any algorithm can guarantee.
  • Chapter 4, Time and order, explains why clocks cannot be trusted to order events, and what to use instead.
Essential question

What are you silently assuming about the network, the clock and the other machines, and what will it cost the people using your system on the day the assumption is wrong?

None of this is theory for its own sake. Every later part of the book, from database replication to safe deployments, is a set of answers to the questions Part I raises.

Chapter 00

Before You Begin

The toolkit you bring, and the one you will build

Here is a puzzle to start with. You open a TCP connection to a server, send the bytes of an HTTP request, and the server's TCP stack acknowledges every byte. Then the connection drops. Did the server process your request?

Most people answer "yes, because TCP acknowledged it". That answer is wrong. TCP's acknowledgement means the bytes reached the server's kernel buffer. It says nothing about whether the application read them, whether it crashed halfway through updating the database, or whether it committed the change and died before replying. This small gap between "delivered" and "done" is where the whole subject of this book begins.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • List the background knowledge the rest of the book assumes and identify your own gaps (Remember)
  • Explain what TCP does and does not guarantee to an application (Understand)
  • Describe the end-to-end argument and apply it to a request/response exchange (Apply)
  • Set up the Maelstrom lab environment and run a first test (Apply)
Essential question

If every layer of the network stack is "reliable", why can a distributed application still lose or duplicate work?

Before you start

You probably already know how a single program works: it has memory, threads and a call stack, and a function call either returns or throws. Keep that picture in mind. This book is largely about what happens when that picture stops being true.

The chapter in brief: a self-assessment of prerequisites, a short refresher on networking (including where the OSI model fits), the end-to-end argument, and setting up your lab.

What you should already know

At a glance
  • Four areas of background knowledge: concurrency, networking, databases, one programming language
  • A quick self-assessment to find your gaps
  • Where to patch them quickly

This book assumes you already write software professionally, or have the equivalent of a few years of computer science study. You do not need to be an expert in any of the four areas below, but you should be comfortable with the ideas. If one of them feels rusty, a few evenings with the suggested material will be enough.

Area You should be able to… If you are rusty
Concurrency Explain a race condition; use a mutex; describe a deadlock Operating Systems: Three Easy Pieces (free online), the concurrency part
Networking Explain the difference between TCP and UDP; describe what an IP address and a port are; read an HTTP request Any introductory networking text; the refresher below
Databases Explain what a transaction is; describe what an index does; write basic SQL The first chapters of Designing Data-Intensive Applications
Programming Write a small networked program in one language Go is recommended for the labs because both Gossip Glomers and MIT 6.5840 use it
Test yourself

Two threads each execute counter = counter + 1 on a shared variable that starts at 0, without a lock. What final values are possible, and why?

Show answer

Both 1 and 2 are possible. The statement is really three steps: read, add, write. If both threads read 0 before either writes, both write 1 and one increment is lost. Keep this "lost update" in mind; it reappears across machines in chapter 8, and no mutex is available there to save you.

A networking refresher for distributed systems

At a glance
  • The layered model of the network and why the OSI model is mostly a teaching aid
  • What TCP guarantees, precisely
  • What TCP cannot guarantee, no matter how well it works

Layers, briefly

Networks are built in layers, where each layer offers a service to the one above it. The OSI reference model describes seven layers: physical, data link, network, transport, session, presentation and application. The Internet actually runs on the simpler TCP/IP model, which merges these into four: link, internet (IP), transport (TCP, UDP, QUIC) and application (HTTP, gRPC, DNS).

For this book you only need to keep three layers in mind:

  1. IP moves individual packets between machines on a best-effort basis. Packets can be lost, duplicated, delayed or reordered.
  2. TCP builds a reliable, ordered byte stream between two endpoints on top of IP. It uses sequence numbers, acknowledgements and retransmission.
  3. The application (your service) interprets those bytes as requests and responses.
Note

The OSI model is valuable as a vocabulary ("is this a layer-4 or a layer-7 load balancer?"), but nobody implements its session and presentation layers as separate pieces. When this book says "layer 4" it means transport (TCP/UDP), and "layer 7" means application (HTTP).

What TCP promises

Within one connection, TCP guarantees that the bytes you write are delivered to the peer's kernel in order, without gaps and without duplicates, or else the connection reports an error. It also performs flow control (the receiver advertises how much buffer space it has, so a fast sender cannot overwhelm a slow receiver) and congestion control (the sender backs off when the network is overloaded). Flow control is your first example of backpressure, an idea we will return to in chapter 11.

What TCP cannot promise

TCP's guarantees end at the kernel buffer on the other side. Look at what can still happen:

  • The guarantees hold per connection. If the connection breaks and the client reconnects, TCP has no memory of what was in flight. Any retry is a new conversation.
  • TCP knows nothing about application-level success. A byte being acknowledged does not mean a request was processed.
  • TCP cannot tell you why a connection failed: a crashed peer, a network partition and a very slow peer can look the same.

The end-to-end argument

At a glance
  • A classic design principle from 1984
  • Why reliability must ultimately be checked by the application endpoints
  • How it shapes everything from file transfer to payments

In 1984, Jerome Saltzer, David Reed and David Clark published End-to-End Arguments in System Design. Their example was a file transfer. Even if every network hop checks its packets carefully, the file can still be corrupted on the sender's disk, in a buggy buffer copy or on the receiver's disk. The only way to be sure the file arrived intact is for the receiving application to compute a checksum over the final file and compare it with the sender's.

Definition · The end-to-end argument

A function such as reliable delivery, duplicate suppression or integrity checking can be implemented completely and correctly only with the knowledge of the application at the endpoints. Lower layers may provide partial versions of these functions as a performance optimisation, but never as a complete guarantee.

Apply this to the payment example above. TCP removes duplicate packets, but if the client retries a payment after a timeout, the server sees two different requests. Only the application can recognise that they represent the same intended payment, for instance by attaching a unique idempotency key to the request. You will meet this technique properly in chapter 2.

Example · Turning the end-to-end argument into design

Scenario: A mobile app submits orders over HTTPS. Users on trains often lose connectivity mid-request, and the app retries automatically.

Walkthrough:

  1. The app generates a random order ID (for example a UUID) before the first attempt and sends it with every retry.
  2. The server stores the order ID together with the order inside the same database transaction.
  3. If a retry arrives with an ID that already exists, the server returns the stored result instead of creating a new order.

Key insight: The network layers were reliable the whole time. The duplicates came from the application's own retries, so only the application could remove them.

Test yourself

A colleague says: "We use a message broker with guaranteed delivery, so we don't need to worry about duplicate processing." Using the end-to-end argument, what would you ask them?

Show answer

Ask what happens if the consumer processes a message and crashes before acknowledging it to the broker. The broker will redeliver it, so the consumer processes it twice. "Guaranteed delivery" is a lower-layer guarantee. Exactly-once effects still require the consumer itself to detect duplicates, for example with a message ID stored transactionally.

Setting up your lab

At a glance
  • Maelstrom, a workbench for distributed systems
  • Your first "echo" node
  • How labs are organised in this book

You learn distributed systems by watching your own code fail. The labs use two freely available toolkits:

  1. Gossip Glomers (fly.io/dist-sys): a series of challenges built by Fly.io together with Kyle Kingsbury, author of Jepsen. Each challenge runs your program as several nodes on Maelstrom, a simulated network that can delay and drop messages, partition nodes, and afterwards check whether the history of operations satisfied a consistency model.
  2. MIT 6.5840 labs (pdos.csail.mit.edu/6.824): a more demanding series in Go, including a complete Raft implementation. These are used in chapters 5 and 7 as optional extensions.
Lab · Set up Maelstrom and run "echo"
  1. Install a JDK (Maelstrom runs on the JVM), plus Graphviz and gnuplot, which Maelstrom uses for its reports.
  2. Download the latest Maelstrom release from its GitHub repository (jepsen-io/maelstrom) and unpack it.
  3. Install Go and create a module that imports the Go client library github.com/jepsen-io/maelstrom/demo/go.
  4. Write a node that handles a message of type echo by replying with type echo_ok and the same echo field. The Gossip Glomers challenge 1 page shows the complete code.
  5. Build it and run:
./maelstrom test -w echo --bin ~/go/bin/maelstrom-echo --node-count 1 --time-limit 10
  1. Look for Everything looks good! ヽ('ー)ノat the end of the output. Then run./maelstrom serve` and open the web view to see the message diagram of your run.

What to notice: Maelstrom records every message as a timestamped event. These diagrams are the same kind of space-time diagrams used throughout this book.

In Appendix A: A.1 Maelstrom setup and Echo (hints)

Tip

Keep a lab notebook. For every lab, write down what you expected to happen, what actually happened, and what the difference taught you. Research on learning consistently shows that predicting before observing improves retention, and the notebook will be useful when you reach the case studies in chapter 16.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Go back to the opening puzzle. The question "did the server process my request?" cannot be answered by the network, however reliable it is, because the network only knows about bytes, not about intentions. That is the first lesson of distributed systems: guarantees are layered, and each layer's guarantee ends exactly where the next layer's uncertainty begins. Throughout the book you will see this pattern again. A database guarantees durability, but only once a write is acknowledged. A consensus algorithm guarantees agreement, but only among a majority. The skill you are building is to ask, for every guarantee, where does it end?

Key terms

OSI model, TCP/IP model, flow control, congestion control, end-to-end argument, idempotency key, Maelstrom.

Review questions

  1. (Understand) Explain in your own words what a TCP acknowledgement does and does not tell the sender.
  2. (Understand) Why is the OSI model still useful even though the Internet does not implement it directly?
  3. (Apply) A file sync tool copies files over TLS (which includes integrity checks). Why might it still compute a SHA-256 hash of each file after writing it to disk?
  4. (Analyze) Compare the guarantees of "TCP is reliable" and "my message broker guarantees delivery". In what way are they the same kind of guarantee?
  5. (Evaluate) A team proposes to make retries safe by using HTTP PUT instead of POST everywhere. Assess this proposal. What does it get right, and what does it leave unsolved?

Reflection

Think of a system you have worked on. Where does it rely on a lower layer's guarantee as if it were an end-to-end guarantee? What would happen if that assumption broke?

Looking ahead

You now know that the network can leave you in doubt. Chapter 1 asks a bigger question: if distribution brings this much uncertainty, why do we distribute systems at all?

Further reading

  • Saltzer, Reed and Clark, End-to-End Arguments in System Design (1984). Six pages that shaped Internet architecture.
  • Fly.io, Gossip Glomers challenge pages. The lab sequence for this book.
  • See the Recommended reading guide at the end for a full annotated list.
Chapter 01

What Is a Distributed System, and Why Is It Hard?

The price and the payoff of spreading work across machines

In May 1987, Leslie Lamport sent an email to colleagues at the DEC Systems Research Center containing a sentence that has been quoted ever since:

"A distributed system is one in which the failure of a computer you didn't even know existed can render your own computer unusable."

It is a joke, but a precise one. It captures three properties that make distributed systems different from everything else you have programmed: there are many computers, they depend on each other, and you cannot see all of them. This chapter unpacks those three properties and asks why anyone would accept them willingly.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Define a distributed system and identify examples in everyday software (Remember)
  • Explain the concept of partial failure and why it changes how we write programs (Understand)
  • List the eight fallacies of distributed computing and relate each to a concrete failure (Understand/Apply)
  • Calculate the availability of serial and redundant components and evaluate the independence assumption behind the calculation (Apply/Evaluate)
Essential question

When is distributing a system the right engineering decision, and when is it an expensive way to create new problems?

Before you start

In chapter 0 you saw that a single request across a network can leave you uncertain about its outcome. Now multiply that by thousands of requests per second, dozens of services and hundreds of machines.

The chapter in brief: a working definition; four reasons to distribute; partial failure as the defining challenge; the eight fallacies; and the arithmetic of availability.

A working definition

At a glance
  • A definition you can use in practice
  • Examples you use every day
  • What distinguishes distributed from merely "networked"

Start with something concrete. When you check a train departure board on your phone, your request might pass through a CDN edge node, a load balancer, an API gateway, a timetable service, a real-time position service fed by trackside sensors, a cache and a database replicated across two data centres. You see one answer. Behind it, perhaps fifteen machines cooperated, and any one of them could have been slow or broken.

Definition · Distributed system

A collection of independent computers that communicate over a network and cooperate to provide a service, where the failure of one part does not necessarily mean the failure of the whole, and no part has a complete, instantaneous view of the others.

Two properties in this definition matter more than the rest:

  1. Independent failure. Each computer, link and process can fail on its own, at any time.
  2. No global view. No node can observe the current state of all other nodes. It only knows what messages have told it, and those messages describe the past.

By this definition, many systems you would not call "distributed" qualify: a mobile app with an offline cache that syncs to a server; a CI pipeline coordinating build agents; a monitoring stack where agents ship metrics to a central store. If a part can fail independently and others must cope with not knowing, you are in distributed territory.

Test yourself

A web server and its database run on two separate virtual machines in the same rack. Is this a distributed system by the definition above? Why or why not?

Show answer

Yes. The two machines can fail independently: the database VM can crash while the web server keeps running. The web server can only learn about the database's state through messages that may be delayed or lost. Being close together lowers the probability of some failures but does not change their nature.

Four reasons to distribute

At a glance
  • Scale: more work than one machine can do
  • Fault tolerance: surviving the loss of a machine
  • Latency: being close to users
  • Isolation: separating teams, tenants or failure domains

If distribution is so troublesome, why do it? Because some requirements cannot be met any other way.

1. Scale

A single machine has finite CPU, memory, disk and network bandwidth. Vertical scaling (scaling up) means buying a bigger machine. It is simple, and you should usually try it first, but it hits a ceiling and costs grow faster than capacity at the high end. Horizontal scaling (scaling out) means adding more machines of ordinary size. It can grow much further, but only if the work can be split among them. Splitting is easy for pure computation. For everything else, the real question is where the state lives: a "stateless" service that scales out effortlessly has usually moved its state into a database, and must fetch it again on every request. Chapter 10 examines that trade in detail.

2. Fault tolerance

Every machine eventually fails. Disks wear out, power supplies die, kernels panic, and operators make mistakes. If your service must keep running when a machine fails, you need more than one machine and a way to move work between them. This is often the primary reason to distribute, even for workloads that would fit on one machine.

3. Latency and geography

Light in optical fibre travels at roughly 200,000 km/s. A round trip between Stockholm and San Francisco (about 8,600 km each way in a straight line, more along real cable routes) therefore takes at least 86 ms, and in practice closer to 150–170 ms. No amount of engineering removes this. If users are spread across the world and need fast responses, data and computation must be close to them.

4. Isolation

Sometimes we split systems to contain damage or to let teams work independently: one tenant's traffic spike should not slow down another tenant, and one team's deployment should not require another team's release. This organisational motive is behind much of the move towards microservices, for better and for worse.

Common mistake · Distribution as a default

Distribution is a cost you pay to get one of the four benefits above. A system split into twenty services without a reason inherits all the problems of this book and gains none of the benefits. Before you distribute, name which of the four reasons applies, and check whether a bigger machine or a better single-node design would do.

Partial failure: the defining challenge

At a glance
  • What partial failure is
  • Why a single computer hides it from you
  • Why "slow" and "dead" look the same over a network

On a single computer, hardware designers go to great lengths to make failures total. When the hardware detects a fault it cannot correct, such as an uncorrectable memory error, the machine typically halts or crashes rather than quietly carrying on with half-wrong data. Programmers rely on this: a function call either returns a result or throws, and if the machine dies, everything dies together, so nothing is left in an inconsistent half-state that someone else must clean up.

A distributed system cannot offer this. Some nodes crash while others keep going. Some network links drop packets while others work. A node may be running, but so slowly that it is effectively useless.

Definition · Partial failure

A state in which some components of a system have failed while others continue to operate, and the operating components may not know which ones have failed.

The second half of that definition is what hurts. Suppose node A sends a request to node B and hears nothing back for two seconds. The possibilities include:

What actually happened What A observes
The request was lost in the network No reply
B crashed before processing it No reply
B processed it and crashed before replying No reply
B is overloaded and will reply in 30 seconds No reply (for now)
B replied, but the reply was lost No reply
B is paused by a long garbage collection No reply (for now)

From A's point of view, all six rows are identical. This is the central epistemological problem of the field: you cannot distinguish a slow node from a dead one, or a lost request from a lost reply, using only the messages you receive. Every technique in this book is a way of acting sensibly under that uncertainty.

Example · A slow node is worse than a dead one

Scenario: A service calls three backends in parallel and waits for all of them. One backend's disk starts failing. It does not crash; it just takes 20 seconds to answer each request.

Walkthrough: Requests pile up, each holding a thread and a connection while waiting for the slow backend. The thread pool fills. Requests that do not even need the slow backend now queue behind those that do. Within minutes the whole service stops responding, although only one disk on one machine is faulty.

Key insight: A crashed process is often detected quickly, because its host refuses the connection at once. A slow node gives no such signal, and consumes your resources while you wait. This is why chapters 11 and 12 spend so much time on timeouts, bulkheads and detecting "gray" failures.

The eight fallacies of distributed computing

At a glance
  • Eight assumptions that newcomers make, and that break real systems
  • Each fallacy points to a chapter in this book

In the 1990s, engineers at Sun Microsystems collected the false assumptions that developers new to networked applications tend to make. The list is usually attributed to L Peter Deutsch, who wrote down seven of them in 1994; James Gosling added the eighth a few years later. More than thirty years on, every one of them still causes outages.

# Fallacy Reality Where we deal with it
1 The network is reliable Packets and connections are lost; switches fail; cables get cut Ch. 2, 3
2 Latency is zero Every remote call costs time, and the tail is much worse than the average Ch. 2, 10, 13
3 Bandwidth is infinite Large payloads and fan-out saturate links Ch. 10
4 The network is secure Every hop can be observed or tampered with (outside the scope of this book)
5 Topology doesn't change Nodes come and go; IPs change; routes shift Ch. 5, 9
6 There is one administrator Many teams and providers own parts of the path Ch. 12–16
7 Transport cost is zero Serialisation, egress fees and CPU cost real money Ch. 10, 13
8 The network is homogeneous Different hardware, protocols and versions coexist Ch. 2, 14

To get a feel for fallacy 2, it helps to know how large the differences are: a call to another service inside the same data centre costs roughly a million times more than a function call in the same process. Chapter 2 has a table of typical latencies, from a CPU cache hit to an intercontinental round trip.

Test yourself

A developer writes a loop that fetches 200 product details one at a time from a remote service, each taking about 5 ms. Which fallacy is at work, and what is the minimum time the loop takes?

Show answer

Fallacy 2: latency is zero. The loop takes at least 200 × 5 ms = 1 second, and probably longer because some calls will hit the latency tail. Batching the requests or fetching them in parallel would reduce this considerably, though parallel fan-out brings its own tail-latency problem (chapter 10).

The arithmetic of availability

At a glance
  • What "nines" mean in hours and minutes
  • How availability combines in series and in parallel
  • The independence assumption, and why it is usually false

Nines

Availability is the fraction of time a service is working correctly. It is usually expressed in "nines":

Availability Downtime per year Downtime per 30 days
99 % ("two nines") ≈ 3.65 days ≈ 7.2 hours
99.9 % ("three nines") ≈ 8.77 hours ≈ 43 minutes
99.99 % ("four nines") ≈ 52.6 minutes ≈ 4.3 minutes
99.999 % ("five nines") ≈ 5.26 minutes ≈ 26 seconds

Components in series

If a request needs all of several components, the availabilities multiply. A service that depends on five components, each 99.9 % available, has an availability of at most 0.999⁵ ≈ 0.995, or about 99.5 %. Every dependency you add in series lowers your availability.

Components in parallel

If a request needs any one of several redundant replicas, the unavailabilities multiply instead. Two replicas, each 99.9 % available (0.1 % unavailable), give 1 − 0.001² = 0.999999, six nines, if their failures are independent.

The independence trap

That six-nines figure is almost never achieved in practice, because failures are correlated. Replicas share power feeds, network switches, deployment pipelines, configuration files, software versions and the same bug. When a bad configuration is pushed to all replicas at once, redundancy offers no protection at all.

Cloud providers address this with failure domains. In AWS, for instance, a Region contains several Availability Zones: groups of data centres with separate power, cooling and networking, located far enough apart to avoid sharing most physical risks. Spreading replicas across zones removes many correlated physical failures. It does not remove correlated logical failures such as shared control planes, global configuration or a common software bug. Chapter 16 shows a 2025 incident in which exactly such a shared dependency took down services across many zones.

Example · Computing realistic availability

Scenario: A booking API runs on three instances across three zones (each instance 99.5 % available) and depends on a database cluster (99.95 %) and an authentication service (99.9 %).

Walkthrough:

  • Instance tier, assuming independence: 1 − 0.005³ ≈ 0.99999988
  • In series with the database and authentication: 0.99999988 × 0.9995 × 0.999 ≈ 0.9985

Result: about 99.85 %, or roughly 13 hours of downtime a year. The redundant instance tier contributes almost nothing to the downtime; the single-instance dependencies dominate.

Key insight: Redundancy is most valuable at the weakest link in the series chain. Adding a fourth instance changes nothing; making authentication more available changes a lot.

Deep dive Gray failure and the limits of availability numbers

Availability calculations assume a component is either up or down. Real components are often partially broken: a network card dropping 2 % of packets, a disk that is slow on one sector, a service that fails only for one customer's requests. Huang and colleagues at Microsoft named this gray failure in a 2017 paper (Gray Failure: The Achilles' Heel of Cloud-Scale Systems, HotOS). They observed that gray failures often show differential observability: the system's own health checks say "healthy" while the applications using it experience failure. A health check that pings a process cannot see that its disk is slow. This is one reason why chapter 12 recommends measuring what users experience, not only whether processes are alive.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Lamport's joke turns out to contain the whole chapter. "Many computers" is the reason we distribute: for scale, fault tolerance, proximity and isolation. "Depend on each other" is why availability arithmetic matters: every dependency in series lowers your ceiling. "Cannot see all of them" is partial failure: a world where silence is ambiguous and slow is indistinguishable from dead.

The tension in the essential question resolves into a design principle: distribute for a named reason, and then design explicitly for partial failure. A system that is distributed by accident inherits the costs without having planned for them.

Key terms

Distributed system, partial failure, vertical / horizontal scaling, availability, nines, failure domain, Availability Zone, correlated failure, gray failure, fallacies of distributed computing.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) List the four reasons to distribute a system.
  2. (Understand) Explain why a slow node can be more harmful than a crashed one.
  3. (Understand) Why is it impossible for node A to tell whether node B crashed or is merely slow, using only the messages it receives?
  4. (Apply) A request passes through four services in series, each with 99.95 % availability. What is the end-to-end availability, and how much downtime per year does that represent?
  5. (Analyze) Two database replicas are placed in two Availability Zones. Name three ways in which their failures could still be correlated.
  6. (Evaluate) A team wants to split a monolith that serves 50 requests per second into twelve microservices "for scalability". Using this chapter, evaluate the proposal.

Reflection

Pick a service you know well. Draw its dependencies and mark which ones are in series. Which single dependency, if it became twice as reliable, would improve the whole service most?

Looking ahead

We have established that messages are the only way nodes learn about each other. Chapter 2 looks closely at those messages: how requests and replies travel, what a timeout really means, and why "exactly once" is harder than it sounds.

Further reading

  • Roberto Vitillo, Understanding Distributed Systems, chapter 1. A short, practical framing of the same ideas.
  • Arnon Rotem-Gal-Oz, Fallacies of Distributed Computing Explained. A readable walk through each fallacy.
  • Huang et al., Gray Failure (HotOS 2017). Four pages that change how you think about health checks.
Chapter 02

Communication Across an Unreliable Network

Requests, replies, timeouts, and the myth of exactly once

Two armies are camped on hills on either side of a valley. In the valley sits an enemy city. If both armies attack at the same time, they win. If only one attacks, it is destroyed. The generals can only communicate by sending messengers through the valley, and any messenger might be captured.

General A sends a messenger: "Attack at dawn." Will A attack? Only if A knows B received the message. So B sends a messenger back: "Agreed." But B does not know whether that confirmation arrived, and B will not attack unless A is certain to attack too. So A must confirm the confirmation…

This is the Two Generals Problem. It has no solution. This chapter shows why, and what engineers do instead, because every RPC you have ever written is a smaller version of it.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Describe the failure modes of a remote procedure call and explain why a timeout leaves the outcome unknown (Understand)
  • Compare the cost of a network call with memory and disk access, in orders of magnitude (Understand)
  • Explain why the Two Generals Problem has no solution with a finite number of messages (Understand)
  • Distinguish at-most-once, at-least-once and effectively-once processing (Analyze)
  • Classify errors as definite failures, safe to retry, or ambiguous, and explain why a connection reset can arrive after the work was done (Analyze)
  • Design an idempotent API using idempotency keys and version numbers (Apply/Create)
  • Use sequence numbers to detect reordering and duplication (Apply)
Essential question

If you can never be sure a message arrived, how is it possible to build systems that move money correctly?

Before you start

Chapter 1 showed that silence is ambiguous. In this chapter we move from that observation to its consequences: what a program should actually do when a call does not return.

The chapter in brief: the RPC illusion; how much slower a remote call is; timeouts and what they mean; the Two Generals; delivery semantics; idempotency; ordering and sequence numbers; synchronous versus asynchronous communication.

The RPC illusion

At a glance
  • Remote procedure calls make network messages look like function calls
  • Four ways a remote call differs from a local one
  • Why the illusion is useful but dangerous

A remote procedure call (RPC) lets a program invoke a function on another machine as though it were local. Frameworks such as gRPC, Thrift, Java RMI and REST client libraries generate code that serialises the arguments, sends them, waits for a reply, and deserialises the result. The idea dates back to Birrell and Nelson's 1984 paper Implementing Remote Procedure Calls.

The illusion is convenient, and it is also misleading. A remote call differs from a local one in at least four ways:

  1. It can fail in ways a local call cannot. The request or reply can be lost; the remote machine can crash; the network can partition.
  2. It can take an unbounded and unpredictable time. A local call takes nanoseconds; a remote call takes milliseconds on a good day and seconds on a bad one.
  3. It can have an unknown outcome. A local call that throws did not complete. A remote call that times out may or may not have completed.
  4. Its arguments are copied. You cannot pass a pointer to shared memory; everything is serialised.
Common mistake · Hiding the network

Early distributed object systems (CORBA, DCOM) tried to make remote objects indistinguishable from local ones. Waldo, Wyant, Wollrath and Kendall argued in A Note on Distributed Computing (1994) that this is a mistake: latency, partial failure and concurrency cannot be hidden behind an interface, and code that pretends otherwise breaks in production. Modern RPC frameworks make the remoteness explicit, with deadlines, status codes and retry policies. Use them.

How much slower is a remote call?

At a glance
  • Latency numbers every developer should know
  • Orders of magnitude, from CPU cache to another continent
  • Why a remote call is not "a slightly slower function call"

The RPC illusion is most dangerous when it comes to time. A remote call looks like a function call in the code, but its cost is in a different universe. It helps to know the rough cost of common operations. The table below is in the spirit of the "latency numbers every programmer should know" popularised by Jeff Dean and Peter Norvig, updated loosely for current hardware. The figures are orders of magnitude, not measurements: they vary with hardware, network and software, but the ratios between them are what matter.

Operation Typical time Times slower than L1 If an L1 cache hit took 1 second
L1 cache hit ≈ 1 ns 1 (10⁰) 1 second
L2 cache hit ≈ 4 ns ≈ 4 (10⁰) 4 seconds
Main memory access ≈ 100 ns ≈ 100 (10²) about 1.7 minutes
Read 1 MB sequentially from memory ≈ 50 µs ≈ 50,000 (10⁴–10⁵) about 14 hours
Random 4 KB read from an NVMe SSD ≈ 20–100 µs ≈ 20,000–100,000 (10⁴–10⁵) about 6 hours to a day
Network round trip within one data centre ≈ 100–500 µs ≈ 100,000–500,000 (10⁵) about 1–6 days
Service-to-service call in one data centre, with serialisation, TLS, proxies and load balancer ≈ 0.5–5 ms (and a long tail) ≈ 1 million (10⁶) about 6–60 days
Round trip between Availability Zones in one region ≈ 0.5–2 ms ≈ 1 million (10⁶) about 6–23 days
Spinning-disk seek ≈ 5–10 ms ≈ 10 million (10⁷) about 2–4 months
Round trip Stockholm–Frankfurt ≈ 20–30 ms ≈ 25 million (10⁷) about 8–11 months
Round trip Europe–US west coast ≈ 150 ms ≈ 150 million (10⁸) about 5 years

The two right-hand columns make the point that numbers in nanoseconds and milliseconds hide: moving from the CPU's caches to main memory costs two orders of magnitude, a network round trip inside the data centre about five, a typical service call about six, and a call across a continent about eight. A function call inside a process costs a few nanoseconds; a call to another service in the same data centre costs around a million times more, before counting the time the other service spends doing anything.

The consequences run through the rest of this book. Every network hop you add to a request multiplies its latency (chapter 10's tail-latency arithmetic), its exposure to failure (chapter 1's availability arithmetic) and its sensitivity to changes in the network (chapter 3's slow networks). Chapter 13 returns to this table when it asks how finely a system should be divided into services.

Test yourself

A function looks up a customer's discount 40 times while rendering a page. Today the lookups are in-process map reads. A colleague proposes moving discounts into their own service, called once per lookup. Using the table, estimate the added time per page.

Show answer

In-process, 40 map lookups cost a few microseconds at most, even if each one misses the CPU caches. As service calls at roughly 1 ms each (0.5–5 ms in the table), the same 40 sequential lookups cost about 40 ms, and more in the tail: around ten thousand times slower. If the change goes ahead, the calls should at least be batched into one request for all 40 discounts, or the data kept as a local copy.

Timeouts: the most important number in your system

At a glance
  • A timeout converts "maybe forever" into "unknown"
  • Why a timeout is not a failure signal
  • How to choose a value

Without a timeout, a caller waiting for a crashed server would wait forever. So every remote call needs a timeout: a maximum time the caller is willing to wait. But consider carefully what a timeout tells you.

Definition · Timeout

A local decision by the caller to stop waiting for a response. It does not mean the operation failed. It means the caller no longer knows, and has chosen to act under uncertainty.

When a call times out, the operation may have:

  • never reached the server;
  • reached the server and failed;
  • reached the server and succeeded, with only the reply lost or delayed;
  • still be in progress, and succeed or fail later.

A useful way to see it: a timeout turns a synchronous call into an asynchronous one with an unknown result. The work may continue in the background after you have given up on it. Your program must be prepared for the effect to appear later.

This is worth dwelling on, because almost every service is written as if its calls were synchronous: call, wait, get an answer, continue. That model holds only as long as calls finish within their timeout. The moment one does not, the program is suddenly running an asynchronous protocol it was never designed for, with work still in flight on the other side and no way to hear how it ended.

Example · The double booking

Scenario: A seat reservation service calls reserveSeat(14C) with a 500 ms timeout. The server is under load and takes 800 ms. At 500 ms the client gives up, reports "reservation failed" to the user, and the user picks seat 15C instead.

What happens next: At 800 ms the server completes the reservation of 14C. The user now holds two seats, one of which they do not know about.

Key insight: The client treated "timed out" as "failed". The correct interpretation is "unknown". The fix is either to make the operation safe to retry and retry it (so the client learns the true outcome), or to query the outcome later before reporting anything to the user.

Choosing a timeout

Too short, and you give up on requests that would have succeeded, causing unnecessary retries and load. Too long, and callers tie up threads and connections waiting for a dead server. A common starting point is to measure the latency distribution of the dependency and set the timeout slightly above a high percentile, such as the 99.9th, accepting that about 1 in 1,000 requests will be timed out unnecessarily. Chapter 11 returns to this with retries and budgets.

Common mistake · Retrying every timeout

The most common reaction to a timeout is to retry it, immediately and automatically. Resist it. A timeout usually means the server is slow because it is overloaded, and the original request is probably still waiting in its queue. An immediate retry adds a second copy of the same work to a server that has no capacity for the first. Done routinely, by many clients or by one busy client, this is one of the most reliable ways to turn a brief slowdown into a long outage. Chapter 11 explains the mechanism and gives a safe policy: retry only idempotent operations, with jittered backoff, inside a retry budget and the original deadline.

Test yourself

Your dependency's median latency is 20 ms, its 99th percentile is 120 ms and its 99.9th percentile is 400 ms. A colleague proposes a 50 ms timeout "because most requests take 20 ms". What will happen?

Show answer

Somewhere between 1 % and 50 % of requests will time out (anything slower than 50 ms), even though the dependency is healthy. Each of those may be retried, adding load to a dependency that is already slow at that moment. A timeout should be based on the tail of the distribution, not the median.

Errors that might still mean success

Timeouts are not the only ambiguous outcome. Many errors that look like failures can arrive after the work was already done. The error is real; the failure may not be. Before you handle or retry an error, ask one question: could the operation have happened anyway?

What the client sees Did the operation happen? Safe to retry blindly?
Connection refused, DNS failure, TLS handshake failure (before anything was sent) No. The request never reached the application. Yes
400-range client errors such as validation failures, 401, 403, 404 No (by the server's own account) No: retrying will not help
429 Too Many Requests or 503 with Retry-After Usually no, but a server cannot always promise it Only after the indicated delay, and only if idempotent
Connection reset or broken pipe after the request was sent Unknown. The server may have processed it and failed while replying. Only if idempotent
Timeout (client or gateway, such as 504 Gateway Timeout or gRPC DEADLINE_EXCEEDED) Unknown Only if idempotent, and with care (chapter 11)
502 Bad Gateway, 500 Internal Server Error Unknown. A proxy or the server failed somewhere; the work may be partly or fully done. Only if idempotent
gRPC UNAVAILABLE Usually not processed, but gRPC's own documentation warns that retrying non-idempotent operations is not always safe Only if idempotent

The dangerous rows are the ones in bold, and they are exactly the ones developers are most tempted to retry. A connection reset in the middle of a payment request feels like "the network failed, so the payment failed". It may equally well mean "the payment succeeded, and the network failed while telling you".

Example · The reset that came after the commit

Scenario: A ticket service sends POST /payments to a payment provider through a corporate proxy. The provider debits the card and commits the transaction. While the response is on its way back, the proxy is restarted during a routine update, and the client sees connection reset by peer.

The naive handler treats the reset as a failure and immediately retries the request. The provider receives a second, identical payment request and, without an idempotency key, charges the card a second time.

The correct handler knows that a reset after sending is ambiguous. Because every payment request carries an idempotency key (generated once, before the first attempt), the retry is safe: the provider recognises the key and returns the result of the first payment. Where no such key is available, the handler must instead look up the outcome (for example "is there a payment with my order ID?") before deciding whether to try again.

Key insight: the decision to retry depends on whether the operation is idempotent, not on how alarming the error message looks.

Two further traps are easy to miss:

  • Somebody else may already be retrying for you. HTTP client libraries, service meshes, load balancers and API gateways often retry automatically on connection errors and certain status codes. Most are careful to retry only idempotent methods by default, but configurations vary, and a retry policy added to a proxy "to improve reliability" can quietly start duplicating POST requests. Know which layers in your call path retry, and on what.
  • Errors can be partial. A request that writes to several places (a database and a message broker, say) can fail after some of the writes. Retrying the whole operation must then be safe for the parts that already happened: the idempotency and outbox techniques of this chapter and chapter 8.
Tip · A rule of thumb for error handling

Sort every error into one of three groups. Definite failure (nothing happened): fix the request or give up; retrying the same request is pointless. Safe to retry (nothing could have happened, such as a refused connection): retry with backoff. Ambiguous (timeouts, resets, 5xx from gateways and servers): retry only an idempotent operation, within a retry budget, or find out what happened before acting. When in doubt, treat an error as ambiguous.

The Two Generals Problem

At a glance
  • A formal version of "you can never be sure"
  • The impossibility argument in four sentences
  • What engineers do instead

The Two Generals Problem was first described by Akkoyunlu, Ekanadham and Huber in 1975 and given its name by Jim Gray in 1978. Here is the core of the impossibility argument.

  1. Suppose there is a protocol that guarantees both generals attack together, using some finite number of messages.
  2. Take the shortest such protocol, and consider its last message. The protocol must work even if that message is lost.
  3. If the protocol works without the last message, the last message was unnecessary, so a shorter protocol exists. That contradicts our choice of the shortest one.
  4. Therefore no finite protocol guarantees agreement over a channel that can lose messages.

What do real systems do? They give up on certainty and settle for probability or for eventual knowledge:

  • Accept a small risk. A bank's card network sends a request and an acknowledgement, and handles the rare ambiguous cases afterwards through reconciliation.
  • Make the action safe to repeat. If attacking twice is harmless, the generals can keep sending "attack" until they hear a reply. In software, this is idempotency.
  • Make the action reversible. If a payment can be refunded, an uncertain payment can be checked and compensated later. This is the idea behind sagas (chapter 8).

Delivery semantics

At a glance
  • At-most-once, at-least-once and "exactly-once"
  • Why exactly-once delivery is impossible
  • How effectively-once processing is achieved

When a caller times out, it must choose: retry, or not?

Semantics Strategy Risk Typical use
At-most-once Send once, never retry The operation may be lost Metrics samples, UDP telemetry
At-least-once Retry until acknowledged The operation may happen more than once Most message queues, webhooks
Exactly-once — Impossible as a delivery guarantee over an unreliable network —

The Two Generals argument shows why exactly-once delivery cannot exist: the sender can never be certain whether its last message arrived, so it must either risk loss (stop) or risk duplication (retry).

What can exist is exactly-once processing, sometimes called effectively-once: at-least-once delivery combined with a receiver that detects and discards duplicates, so that the effect of each operation is applied once.

Definition · Effectively-once processing

At-least-once delivery plus deduplication or idempotent application at the receiver, such that repeated deliveries of the same message have the same effect as one delivery.

Note

When a product advertises "exactly-once semantics", read the fine print. Apache Kafka's exactly-once support, for instance, combines an idempotent producer (the broker discards duplicate writes using producer IDs and sequence numbers) with transactions that atomically commit output and consumer offsets. It is real and useful, but it applies within Kafka. If your consumer also writes to an external database or calls an API, you need your own deduplication there too.

Idempotency: making retries safe

At a glance
  • What idempotency means
  • Naturally idempotent and non-idempotent operations
  • Idempotency keys and conditional writes
Definition · Idempotent operation

An operation that has the same effect whether it is applied once or many times. Formally, f(f(x)) = f(x).

Example: "Set the balance to 100" is idempotent. "Add 10 to the balance" is not.

Some operations are naturally idempotent: setting a value, deleting a record by ID, or HTTP PUT and DELETE when used as the HTTP specification intends. Others are not: appending, incrementing, creating with a server-generated ID, or sending an email.

You can make a non-idempotent operation idempotent with two common techniques.

Technique 1: idempotency keys

The client generates a unique key (for example a UUID) for each logical operation and sends it with every attempt. The server records processed keys, together with their results, in the same transaction as the operation's effect.

POST /payments
Idempotency-Key: 9f1c2b7e-4a35-4d6e-9b0a-3c8e5f21d7aa
{ "from": "acc-17", "to": "acc-42", "amount": 250 }

Server logic (inside one database transaction):
  if key exists in processed_requests:
      return stored response
  perform transfer
  insert (key, response) into processed_requests
  commit

Payment providers such as Stripe popularised this pattern with an Idempotency-Key HTTP header, and the IETF has worked on standardising the header.

Common mistake · Checking the key outside the transaction

If you check "have I seen this key?" in one step and perform the operation in another, two retries arriving at the same moment can both pass the check. The key must be recorded atomically with the effect, for example with a unique constraint on the key column in the same transaction.

Technique 2: version numbers and conditional writes

The client reads a record together with its version number, and writes back only "if the version is still v". If someone else changed the record in between, the write is rejected and the client must re-read. This is optimistic concurrency control, discussed further in chapter 8. A retry of an already-applied conditional write also fails harmlessly, because the version has moved on.

Test yourself

A service sends a welcome email when a user signs up. The signup handler is retried after a timeout. How can you make the email idempotent, given that you cannot "unsend" an email?

Show answer

Record the fact "welcome email for user U" in a durable store before or atomically with asking the email provider to send it, keyed by user ID. On retry, check the record and skip sending. Many email APIs also accept an idempotency key. A small risk remains: if you crash after the provider sends the email but before you record it, you may send twice. You can only reduce this window, not remove it, which is the Two Generals Problem again.

Ordering: when messages overtake each other

At a glance
  • Why messages arrive out of order
  • Sequence numbers for per-sender ordering
  • Version numbers on the client for monotonic reads

Even a single sender's messages can arrive out of order when they travel on different connections, through different load-balancer paths, or are retried. Two updates sent in the order "set status = shipped", then "set status = delivered" can arrive in the reverse order, leaving the record permanently showing "shipped".

The standard tool is the sequence number. Each sender numbers its messages 1, 2, 3, … The receiver:

  • applies a message only if its number is the next one expected;
  • buffers messages that arrive early, until the gap is filled;
  • discards messages with a number it has already seen, which also handles duplicates.

This is exactly what TCP does inside a connection. When your messages cross connections, retries or queues, you must do it again at the application level, the end-to-end argument once more.

Client-side version tracking

A client can protect itself from going backwards in time even when the servers it talks to are not perfectly synchronised. If every response includes a version number for the data, the client remembers the highest version it has seen for each item, and treats any response with a lower version as stale: either it ignores the response or it retries against another replica.

Example · A dashboard that never goes back in time

Scenario: A monitoring dashboard polls a replicated store for the latest alert state. Replicas lag behind each other by up to a few seconds, and the load balancer sends each poll to a random replica.

Problem: The user sees an alert appear, then disappear, then reappear, because successive polls hit replicas at different points in the replication stream.

Solution: Each replica returns the alert state with the version (log position) it reflects. The dashboard stores the highest version seen and rejects older responses, re-polling instead.

Key insight: The client has built monotonic reads for itself, a consistency guarantee you will meet formally in chapter 6.

Synchronous and asynchronous communication

At a glance
  • Request/response versus messaging
  • What a queue buys you, and what it costs

In synchronous communication, the caller sends a request and waits for the response before continuing. HTTP and gRPC calls are typical. The caller's availability depends on the callee's: if the callee is down, the caller fails too.

In asynchronous communication, the sender hands a message to an intermediary (a queue or log such as RabbitMQ, Amazon SQS or Kafka) and carries on. A consumer processes it later.

Synchronous Asynchronous (queue)
Coupling in time Both must be up at once Producer and consumer can be up at different times
Latency Response immediately available Result arrives later, if at all
Load spikes Passed straight to the callee Absorbed by the queue
Failure visibility Immediate error Errors appear later, often in a dead-letter queue
Complexity Lower Higher: ordering, duplicates, poison messages

Asynchronous messaging does not remove the problems of this chapter. It moves them: the producer must still know whether the queue received the message, and the consumer must still cope with duplicates and reordering.

Lab · Gossip Glomers challenges 1 and 2
  1. Complete challenge 1 (Echo) if you have not already.
  2. Complete challenge 2 (Unique ID Generation). Each node must generate globally unique IDs while the network may be partitioned, so the nodes cannot coordinate.
  3. Before you write code, write down in your notebook why simply using the current time in milliseconds does not work. Then design an ID scheme that does.

Hint: What information does each node have that no other node has?

In Appendix A: A.2 Unique ID generation (hints)

Deep dive Why "exactly once" keeps being claimed

The phrase "exactly once" survives because it is true at one layer and false at another. Within a closed system that controls both the log and the consumer's state (for example a stream processor that commits its state and its input offsets atomically), effects can be made exactly-once. The guarantee breaks at the boundary to any system that does not participate in that atomic commit. The precise question to ask of any "exactly-once" claim is: exactly once with respect to which observer, and inside which boundary? The end-to-end argument predicts that the answer is always "inside some boundary".

Synthesis

Synthesis

The Two Generals cannot reach certainty, and neither can your services. Engineers do not solve this problem; they design around it. The three escape routes from the opening (accept risk, make actions repeatable, make actions reversible) map onto at-most-once delivery, idempotent at-least-once processing, and compensation. Money moves correctly not because messages are reliable, but because payment systems are built from idempotent operations, unique identifiers and reconciliation.

Notice too that the dashboard example used a version number to build a guarantee on the client side. That idea, ordering information carried inside messages, is the seed of chapter 4.

Key terms

RPC, timeout, Two Generals Problem, at-most-once, at-least-once, exactly-once delivery, effectively-once processing, idempotency, idempotency key, sequence number, optimistic concurrency control, synchronous / asynchronous communication.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) List four possible states of an operation after its caller timed out.
  2. (Understand) Explain in your own words why the Two Generals Problem has no finite solution.
  3. (Understand) Why is "exactly-once delivery" impossible, while "exactly-once processing" is achievable?
  4. (Apply) Design an idempotent version of POST /orders. Specify what the client sends, what the server stores, and in what transaction.
  5. (Analyze) A consumer reads from a queue, writes to a database, then acknowledges the message. Identify the failure window that causes duplicates and propose how to close it.
  6. (Evaluate) A team argues that switching from HTTP calls to a message queue will "eliminate failures". Assess this claim.

Reflection

Find an API you have written or used that is not idempotent. What would a retry do? What would it take to make it safe?

Looking ahead

We have been talking about "crashes", "lost messages" and "slow nodes" informally. Chapter 3 makes these precise with system models, the assumptions every distributed algorithm is built on, and meets a famous impossibility result that explains why no algorithm can escape timeouts altogether.

Further reading

  • Birrell and Nelson, Implementing Remote Procedure Calls (1984). Where RPC began.
  • Waldo et al., A Note on Distributed Computing (1994). The case against hiding the network.
  • Malcolm Featonby, Making retries safe with idempotent APIs (Amazon Builders' Library). How AWS designs idempotent APIs.
Chapter 03

System Models, Faults and the Limits of Knowledge

Saying precisely what can go wrong, and what follows from it

Here is a myth worth breaking: "With a good enough algorithm, a cluster can always decide, in bounded time, even if a node crashes."

In 1985, Michael Fischer, Nancy Lynch and Michael Paterson proved that this is impossible. In a fully asynchronous system, no deterministic algorithm can guarantee that a set of processes reaches agreement if even a single one of them may crash. Not a clever algorithm, not a well-tuned one: none at all.

And yet etcd, ZooKeeper, Kafka and every cloud database reach agreement millions of times a day. Both statements are true. The way they fit together is the subject of this chapter, and it requires us to be precise about something we have so far left vague: what exactly are we assuming about the world?

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Define the main node failure models (crash-stop, crash-recovery, omission, Byzantine) and network models (reliable, fair-loss, arbitrary) (Remember)
  • Distinguish synchronous, partially synchronous and asynchronous timing models (Understand)
  • Explain why slow, degraded components (limpware, fail-slow hardware, lossy networks) can do more damage than crashed ones, and how slowness exposes latent race conditions (Understand/Analyze)
  • Explain safety and liveness and classify a property as one or the other (Understand/Analyze)
  • State the FLP result and explain why practical consensus still works (Understand)
  • Evaluate failure detectors in terms of completeness and accuracy (Evaluate)
Essential question

How can we prove anything about systems whose behaviour depends on failures we cannot predict?

Before you start

In chapter 2 you met the problem of not knowing whether a message arrived. Now we step back and ask: what kinds of misbehaviour should an algorithm be designed to tolerate? A bridge engineer specifies a design load before calculating; we must specify a fault model before designing.

The chapter in brief: why we need models; three dimensions of a system model; slowness as a failure mode; safety and liveness; Byzantine faults; failure detectors; the FLP impossibility result.

Why we need models

At a glance
  • Algorithms are only correct relative to assumptions
  • A system model is the contract between the algorithm and reality
  • Choosing a model is an engineering trade-off

Every distributed algorithm is correct with respect to some assumptions. Raft is correct if nodes crash and recover but never lie. A Dynamo-style store behaves as described if nodes eventually recover. A Bitcoin-style protocol is designed to tolerate participants who actively lie, at a large cost in performance.

Definition · System model

A precise description of what nodes, the network and time are allowed to do. An algorithm is proved correct for a system model; if reality violates the model, the proof no longer applies.

Choosing a model is an engineering trade-off. A model that assumes too little (for example "nodes may behave arbitrarily, messages may take any time") makes algorithms expensive or impossible. A model that assumes too much ("clocks are synchronised to within 1 ms") makes algorithms simple, but they break the day the assumption does.

A system model has three dimensions: how nodes fail, how the network behaves, and what we assume about timing.

Dimension 1: how nodes fail

At a glance
  • Four failure models, from benign to malicious
  • Why crash-recovery is the realistic default
Model What a faulty node can do Example
Crash-stop (fail-stop) Stops executing and never comes back A server is decommissioned after a hardware fault
Crash-recovery Stops, possibly loses its in-memory state, and later restarts with whatever it saved to stable storage A process is killed and restarted by Kubernetes
Omission Fails to send or receive some messages, but otherwise behaves correctly A node with an overflowing network buffer
Byzantine (arbitrary) Does anything at all: sends wrong or contradictory data, lies, colludes A compromised node, or memory corruption producing garbage

The models nest: a Byzantine node can do everything a crash-stop node can and much more. An algorithm tolerating Byzantine faults also tolerates crashes, but at a higher cost.

Crash-recovery is the realistic default for most data-centre systems. It brings an important subtlety: a recovering node must know what it promised before it crashed. If a Raft node voted for candidate X in term 5 and then crashed, it must not vote for candidate Y in term 5 after restarting. Raft therefore requires each node to write its current term and its vote to stable storage before replying. Forgetting a promise is a correctness bug.

Common mistake · "The process restarted, so all is well"

In the crash-recovery model, a node that restarts must recover its durable promises before talking to anyone. A bug where a restarted node forgets what it acknowledged (for instance because a write was cached but never flushed with fsync) silently converts a crash-recovery failure into a much worse one, where the node effectively lies about its past.

Dimension 2: how the network behaves

At a glance
  • Reliable, fair-loss and arbitrary links
  • Why "the network is reliable" is a model you build, not one you get
Model Assumption
Reliable link A message sent is delivered exactly once, eventually. It may be delayed or reordered.
Fair-loss link Messages may be lost, duplicated or reordered, but if you keep resending a message it is eventually delivered.
Arbitrary link An adversary may drop, duplicate, modify or inject messages.

Real networks behave like fair-loss links. A fair-loss link can be turned into a reliable one with retries and deduplication, which is exactly what TCP does within a connection and what idempotency keys do at the application level. An arbitrary link can be made fair-loss with cryptography: TLS or message authentication codes detect tampering and injection.

A network partition is a period during which some nodes cannot communicate with others, even though all are running. Partitions are not exotic. Bailis and Kingsbury's 2014 survey The Network is Reliable (ACM Queue) collected many documented partitions in production systems, caused by switch failures, misconfigured firewalls, saturated links and software bugs in network devices.

Dimension 3: timing

At a glance
  • Synchronous, asynchronous and partially synchronous systems
  • Why the partially synchronous model describes real data centres
  • Process pauses: the silent timing failure
Model Assumption Realistic?
Synchronous Known upper bounds on message delay, processing time and clock drift Rarely: only in some embedded and real-time systems
Asynchronous No bounds at all: messages and computation can take arbitrarily long Safe to assume, but makes some problems unsolvable
