Partially synchronous The system behaves asynchronously for unknown periods, but eventually there are periods in which bounds hold A good description of most real systems

The partially synchronous model was introduced by Dwork, Lynch and Stockmeyer in 1988. It captures what operators experience: most of the time the network and machines are fast and predictable, but sometimes, unpredictably and for an unknown duration, delays become extreme.

Process pauses

Timing violations do not only come from the network. A running process can be paused for a long time without knowing it:

  • a stop-the-world garbage collection pause (seconds in a badly tuned JVM);
  • the hypervisor suspending a virtual machine, or live-migrating it to another host;
  • the operating system swapping the process's memory to disk;
  • a laptop lid being closed;
  • an operator sending SIGSTOP.

After such a pause, the process resumes executing as if no time had passed. If it held a lock with an expiry time, it may now be acting on a lock that expired long ago. Chapter 7 shows how fencing tokens defend against this.

Test yourself

A service uses a 10-second lock lease. A node acquires the lock, checks that it has 9 seconds left, and starts writing. Name two events that could make that check worthless.

Show answer

Any pause between the check and the write: a long GC pause, VM suspension or live migration, heavy swapping. Also a clock jump on the node (for example an NTP correction) could make its view of "9 seconds left" wrong. The check-then-act sequence is not atomic with respect to time.

Slow is a failure mode too

At a glance
  • Degraded hardware and networks: "limpware" and "fail-slow"
  • Why a little packet loss or lost bandwidth hurts out of all proportion
  • How slowness uncovers race conditions nobody has seen before
  • Designing and testing for slowness

The failure models above are about components that stop: crash, omit messages or lie. Some of the most damaging failures in practice are components that keep working, just slowly. A network card that drops from 1 Gbit/s to a trickle, a switch port with a faulty optic that loses 1 % of packets, a router whose buffers are full, a link saturated by a backup job or by a storage system re-replicating data. Nothing is down. Every health check passes. And the whole system misbehaves in ways nobody has seen before.

Researchers have studied this systematically. In Limplock: Understanding the Impact of Limpware on Scale-Out Cloud Systems (SoCC 2013), Thanh Do and colleagues described limpware, hardware whose performance has degraded far below its specification while it keeps running. They cite a real incident in which a 1 Gbit/s network card suddenly transmitted at around 1 kbit/s and set off a chain reaction that made a cluster unusable. In their experiments with Hadoop, HDFS, ZooKeeper, Cassandra and HBase, a single limping component could degrade a whole cluster; in one Hadoop experiment, throughput fell from about 172 jobs per hour to about 1. The systems had redundancy, but they could not make the slow component "fail in place": their timeouts were too coarse to notice it, and their retries kept choosing it. Gunawi and colleagues followed up in Fail-Slow at Scale (FAST 2018) with 101 reports of fail-slow hardware from 12 institutions, and found that such problems often took hours, days or even months to diagnose.

Why a little slowness hurts so much

Small degradations are amplified by the layers above them:

  • TCP punishes loss hard. When a packet is lost, TCP waits for a retransmission timeout or duplicate acknowledgements before resending; on Linux the minimum retransmission timeout is 200 ms. A request that normally takes 1 ms can suddenly take more than 200 ms whenever one of its packets is lost. A well-known model of TCP (Mathis et al., 1997) shows that a connection's maximum throughput falls roughly with the square root of the loss rate, so a rise from 0.01 % to 1 % packet loss can cut a single connection's throughput by about a factor of ten.
  • Timeouts start firing. Requests that used to finish in milliseconds now cross their timeouts. Clients retry, adding traffic to a network that is already degraded (chapter 11).
  • Failure detectors make false suspicions. Heartbeats arrive late; leases expire; nodes are declared dead although they are alive. Failovers, leader elections and re-replication begin.
  • Recovery traffic competes with real traffic. Re-replication, rebalancing, backups and log shipping are bulk transfers. Started by the false suspicions, they fill the same links that are already struggling, which delays heartbeats further. Amazon's 2011 EBS outage (chapter 16) began this way, as a re-mirroring storm after a network change shifted traffic onto a network that could not carry it.

Slowness reveals new race conditions

There is a subtler effect. Most code is tested under conditions where messages arrive in a fairly predictable order and within a fairly narrow time window. Many races are therefore latent: they exist in the code, but the interleaving that triggers them has never happened. Slowness, especially uneven slowness that affects some links and not others, changes the interleavings:

  • a reply arrives after the caller has timed out and retried, so the operation is applied twice;
  • a heartbeat arrives after a lease has expired and a new owner has taken over, so two nodes act as owner;
  • a "delete" overtakes the "create" it refers to, because they travelled different paths;
  • a node that was declared dead finishes its work after its replacement has started.

These are exactly the hazards of chapters 2, 4 and 7, but they appear only when the network is slow enough to change who wins the race. The EBS incident involved precisely such a case: a race condition in the storage nodes, triggered under the unusual load of the re-mirroring storm, crashed more nodes and made the storm worse.

Tip · Designing and testing for slowness
  • Treat slow as failed, explicitly. Track latency per peer and per link, and compare peers with each other: a replica that is ten times slower than its siblings should be avoided, even if it answers health checks.
  • Use fine-grained timeouts on progress, not only on completion: a transfer that has moved 1 KB in 30 seconds is not "in progress", it is stuck.
  • Separate and limit bulk traffic. Rate-limit re-replication, rebalancing and backups, give them lower network priority (QoS) or separate links, and make them back off when foreground latency rises.
  • Make retries remember. Do not keep retrying against the same slow replica; Limplock called this "memoryless" retry.
  • Test with degraded networks, not only broken ones. Linux tc netem can add latency, jitter, 1 % packet loss or bandwidth limits to a single link; run your test suite and your Jepsen or Maelstrom tests under those conditions, and you will find races that never appear on a clean network.
Test yourself

A storage cluster runs fine at 0 % packet loss. A faulty switch port starts dropping 2 % of packets on the link to one rack. Describe, step by step, how this could turn into a cluster-wide problem, even though no machine has failed.

Show answer

TCP connections to that rack suffer retransmission timeouts, so requests to its nodes become slow and throughput drops. Clients time out and retry, adding load. Heartbeats from the rack arrive late, so the failure detector suspects its nodes. The cluster starts re-replicating their data to other racks, a large bulk transfer that adds traffic across the network, including the degraded link. Requests elsewhere slow down, more timeouts fire, and more nodes may be suspected. Under the changed timing, latent race conditions (for example, a node that was declared dead completing a write) may surface. Nothing crashed, but the system's own reactions to slowness turned a 2 % loss on one link into a cluster-wide incident.

Safety and liveness

At a glance
  • Two kinds of properties every protocol must state
  • How to tell them apart
  • Why trade-offs usually sacrifice liveness, not safety

When we say an algorithm is "correct", we usually mean two kinds of property.

Definition · Safety and liveness
  • A safety property says something bad never happens. If it is violated, you can point to the moment it happened. Example: "Two nodes never both believe they are leader in the same term."
  • A liveness property says something good eventually happens. It can never be shown violated at any finite moment, since it might still happen later. Example: "Every request eventually receives a response."

The distinction (formalised by Alpern and Schneider in 1985) matters because, under bad conditions, well-designed systems give up liveness to preserve safety. During a network partition, a consensus system stops accepting writes on the minority side (a liveness loss) rather than let two sides accept conflicting writes (a safety violation). Liveness returns when the network heals; a safety violation such as corrupted data may never be undone.

Test yourself

Classify each property as safety or liveness: (a) "A committed write is never lost." (b) "If a leader crashes, a new leader is eventually elected." (c) "A read never returns a value that was never written." (d) "Every message sent is eventually delivered."

Show answer

(a) safety, (b) liveness, (c) safety, (d) liveness. A quick test: if the property can be violated at a specific moment you could point to, it is safety. If it is about something that should happen "eventually", it is liveness.

Byzantine faults

At a glance
  • The Byzantine Generals Problem
  • Why tolerating f liars requires 3f + 1 nodes
  • When you actually need Byzantine fault tolerance

In 1982, Lamport, Shostak and Pease published The Byzantine Generals Problem. It extends the Two Generals: now the messengers arrive reliably, but some generals are traitors who send different messages to different loyal generals to stop them from agreeing.

Their key result: with only unsigned (oral) messages, agreement is possible only if fewer than one third of the generals are traitors. To tolerate f Byzantine nodes you need at least 3f + 1 nodes in total.

Where the numbers come from

Compare the two quorum rules you will meet again in chapter 7:

  • Crash faults, n = 2f + 1. A quorum is a majority, f + 1 nodes. Any two majorities overlap in at least one node, and since crashed nodes do not lie, that overlapping node carries the truth from one quorum to the other.
  • Byzantine faults, n = 3f + 1. A quorum is 2f + 1 nodes. Any two such quorums overlap in at least f + 1 nodes. Even if f of those are liars, at least one honest node is in the overlap.

Do you need it?

Most systems inside a single organisation's data centres assume crash faults only: all nodes run the same trusted code, and data corruption is handled with checksums. Byzantine fault tolerance is used where participants do not trust each other (blockchains), in some safety-critical aircraft and spacecraft systems, and in research systems such as PBFT (Castro and Liskov, 1999).

Common mistake · "2f + 1 nodes tolerate f failures"

This is true only for crash faults. If your threat model includes nodes that may send corrupt or malicious data, a majority quorum is not enough. Be explicit about which kind of failure your design tolerates.

Failure detectors

At a glance
  • Detecting crashes in practice: heartbeats and timeouts
  • Completeness and accuracy
  • Adaptive detectors such as phi accrual

Since nodes cannot observe each other directly, systems use failure detectors: components that output a list of nodes suspected to have crashed. In practice a failure detector is usually a heartbeat with a timeout: if no message has arrived from node X for T seconds, suspect X.

Chandra and Toueg (1996) characterised failure detectors by two properties:

  • Completeness: every node that actually crashes is eventually suspected.
  • Accuracy: correct nodes are not suspected (or, in weaker forms, eventually stop being suspected).

In an asynchronous system, no failure detector can be both complete and perfectly accurate, because slow and dead are indistinguishable. A short timeout gives quick detection but many false suspicions; a long one gives few false suspicions but slow detection.

Adaptive detectors improve this trade-off. The phi accrual failure detector (Hayashibara et al., 2004), used in Apache Cassandra and Akka, does not output yes/no. It outputs a suspicion level φ based on the observed distribution of heartbeat inter-arrival times. A node whose heartbeats normally arrive every 1.0 ± 0.1 s becomes suspicious after 3 s of silence; a node whose heartbeats are normally irregular is given more slack.

Example · A false suspicion in practice

Scenario: A three-node cluster suspects a node dead after 2 s without a heartbeat. A JVM on node B performs a 4-second garbage collection pause.

What happens: Nodes A and C suspect B, elect a new leader and continue. B resumes, unaware that time has passed, and still believes it is leader. For a brief window it may try to act as leader.

Key insight: The failure detector was inaccurate: B was alive. A correct algorithm must stay safe despite this. Raft does so with terms: B's messages carry an old term number and are rejected. You will see the details in chapter 7.

The FLP impossibility result

At a glance
  • What FLP says, precisely
  • Why it does not stop consensus in practice
  • How safety and liveness are separated to escape it

We can now state the result from the chapter opening precisely.

Definition · FLP impossibility (Fischer, Lynch and Paterson, 1985)

In an asynchronous system where at least one process may crash, there is no deterministic algorithm that always solves consensus, that is, that guarantees agreement, validity and termination.

The intuition: in an asynchronous system, an algorithm can never tell a crashed process from a slow one. FLP shows that an adversarial scheduler, by delaying the right messages at the right time, can keep any deterministic algorithm forever undecided, without ever actually crashing anyone.

So how do etcd and ZooKeeper work? They change the model and separate safety from liveness:

  1. Safety is guaranteed always, even in a fully asynchronous system. Paxos and Raft never let two nodes decide different values, however slow the network is.
  2. Liveness is guaranteed only under partial synchrony. When the network is well-behaved for long enough, timeouts work, a stable leader emerges, and decisions are made.
  3. Some protocols use randomisation to make non-termination happen with probability zero.

In practice, the FLP scenario looks like a cluster that keeps holding elections and never settles, typically during severe network instability. That is exactly what you observe in production when a consensus cluster is unhealthy: it stops making progress but does not corrupt data.

Deep dive Why separating safety from liveness is the deepest idea in this chapter

FLP tells us that in an asynchronous world we must choose between always deciding and always deciding correctly. Almost every practical protocol makes the same choice: never be wrong, and accept occasional periods of not deciding. This pattern reaches far beyond consensus. The CAP theorem (chapter 6) is the same trade-off, applied to reads and writes during partitions: a CP system gives up availability (liveness) to keep linearizability (safety). When you design your own systems, ask of every mechanism: if things go badly, what do we give up first? For data, the answer should almost always be "progress, not correctness".

Lab · Thought experiment: design your own failure model

Write a one-page "system model" for a system you know: for instance, a set of monitoring agents shipping data to a central store.

  1. Which node failure model applies? Can agents restart? Can they send corrupted data?
  2. Which network model? Can messages be duplicated?
  3. Which timing model? What is the longest pause a process could experience?
  4. For each assumption, describe one real event that would violate it.

Keep this page. In chapter 12 you will compare it against what your monitoring actually observes.

In Appendix A: A.3 Write a system model (hints)

Synthesis

Synthesis

The opening seemed to present a contradiction: consensus is impossible, yet everyone does it. The resolution is that impossibility results are statements about models, not about practice. FLP closes one door, the one marked "deterministic, asynchronous, guaranteed termination", and practical systems walk through the one next to it: always safe, live when the network behaves. Making this choice explicitly, and writing down your system model, is what separates a design that degrades gracefully from one that fails in ways nobody predicted.

Key terms

System model, crash-stop, crash-recovery, omission fault, Byzantine fault, fair-loss link, network partition, synchronous / asynchronous / partially synchronous, process pause, limpware / fail-slow, latent race condition, safety, liveness, failure detector, completeness, accuracy, phi accrual, FLP impossibility.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the three dimensions of a system model and give the options for each.
  2. (Understand) Explain why a crash-recovery node must persist its vote before replying.
  3. (Understand) Why can a failure detector in an asynchronous system not be both complete and accurate?
  4. (Apply) You must tolerate two simultaneous crash failures. How many nodes do you need? How many if the failures could be Byzantine?
  5. (Analyze) Classify "every committed transaction is eventually visible to all readers" as safety or liveness. Justify your answer.
  6. (Evaluate) A vendor claims their database "guarantees consensus in bounded time even under network partitions". Using FLP, evaluate the claim and identify what must be missing.
  7. (Analyze) Why can a network card that runs at 1 % of its rated speed do more damage to a cluster than one that fails completely? Name two mechanisms.

Reflection

Think of an incident you have experienced or read about. Which assumption of the system's (often unwritten) model was violated?

Looking ahead

Timeouts appeared everywhere in this chapter, and timeouts depend on clocks. Chapter 4 shows why clocks in distributed systems cannot be trusted, and how we order events without them.

Further reading

  • Fischer, Lynch and Paterson, Impossibility of Distributed Consensus with One Faulty Process (JACM, 1985). Short, and readable after this chapter.
  • Lamport, Shostak and Pease, The Byzantine Generals Problem (1982).
  • Bailis and Kingsbury, The Network is Reliable (ACM Queue, 2014). Evidence that partitions happen.
  • Do et al., Limplock: Understanding the Impact of Limpware on Scale-Out Cloud Systems (SoCC 2013), and Gunawi et al., Fail-Slow at Scale (FAST 2018). Evidence that slow hardware is a failure mode of its own.
  • Kleppmann's Cambridge lecture 2 covers system models with clear diagrams.
Chapter 04

Time, Clocks and the Ordering of Events

Why "what happened first?" has no simple answer

On 30 June 2012, a leap second was added to Coordinated Universal Time: the clock read 23:59:60 before rolling over to midnight. Within hours, servers around the world started burning 100 % CPU. Sites including Reddit, LinkedIn, Yelp and Foursquare reported problems, and the airline Qantas had trouble with its check-in systems. The cause was a bug in how the Linux kernel's timer handling interacted with the leap second, which left applications spinning on timers that fired constantly.

Nothing in those systems had "failed" in the usual sense. The network was up, the disks were fine. One second, inserted deliberately and announced months in advance, was enough. If time is that fragile, how can we decide which of two events in different machines happened first?

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Explain why physical clocks drift, jump and disagree, and distinguish time-of-day clocks from monotonic clocks (Understand)
  • Define the happens-before relation and identify concurrent events in a space-time diagram (Understand/Analyze)
  • Compute Lamport timestamps and vector clocks for a given execution (Apply)
  • Compare Lamport clocks, vector clocks, hybrid logical clocks and TrueTime and select one for a given requirement (Analyze/Evaluate)
  • Describe FIFO, causal and total order broadcast (Understand)
Essential question

If there is no global clock, is there still a meaningful sense in which one event happened "before" another?

Before you start

Chapter 3 showed that timeouts are how we detect failure, and that process pauses can make a node's sense of time wrong. You also met version numbers in chapter 2 as a way to order updates. This chapter generalises that idea: we can order events by causality instead of by clock time.

The chapter in brief: physical clocks and their failures; the happens-before relation; Lamport clocks; vector clocks; hybrid logical clocks and TrueTime; ordering guarantees for broadcast.

Physical clocks and why they lie

At a glance
  • How computer clocks work and why they drift
  • NTP and its limits
  • Time-of-day clocks versus monotonic clocks

Quartz and drift

Most computers keep time with a quartz crystal oscillator. Quartz is cheap and fairly stable, but each crystal runs slightly fast or slow, and its rate changes with temperature. Typical drift is in the order of tens of parts per million (ppm). At 50 ppm, a clock gains or loses about 4.3 seconds per day (50 × 10⁻⁶ × 86,400 s). Two servers left alone will disagree by seconds within a day.

NTP

The Network Time Protocol (NTP) corrects drift by asking time servers what time it is and adjusting the local clock. NTP compensates for network delay by measuring round-trip times, but it can only estimate the one-way delay, and over the public Internet accuracy is typically in the range of milliseconds to tens of milliseconds. In a well-run data centre with local time servers, accuracy can be much better, and the Precision Time Protocol (PTP) with hardware timestamping can achieve sub-microsecond accuracy.

NTP adjusts the clock in two ways: by slewing (running it slightly faster or slower until it converges) or, if the error is large, by stepping it, jumping directly to the right time. A step can move the clock backwards.

Two kinds of clock

Operating systems therefore provide two different clocks, and confusing them is a classic bug:

Clock What it tells you Can it jump backwards? Use it for
Time-of-day (wall clock), e.g. clock_gettime(CLOCK_REALTIME), System.currentTimeMillis() The current date and time Yes, after NTP steps or manual changes Timestamps shown to humans; coarse comparisons across machines with known error
Monotonic, e.g. CLOCK_MONOTONIC, System.nanoTime() Time elapsed since some arbitrary point No Measuring durations and timeouts on one machine
Common mistake · Measuring duration with the wall clock

elapsed = now() - start using the wall clock can return a negative number, or a huge one, if NTP steps the clock in between. Always use a monotonic clock for timeouts and latency measurement. And never compare monotonic readings from two different machines: they have no common reference point.

Test yourself

Two servers each record the wall-clock time of a write: server A records 10:00:00.120 for write W1, server B records 10:00:00.100 for write W2. Their clocks are known to be within 50 ms of each other. Can you conclude that W2 happened before W1?

Show answer

No. The timestamps differ by 20 ms, but the clocks may differ by up to 50 ms. W1 could have happened before W2, after it, or at the same instant. With a known error bound ε, you can only order two events if their timestamps differ by more than 2ε (or ε, depending on how the bound is defined). This idea is exactly what Google's TrueTime makes explicit, later in this chapter.

Last-writer-wins and the danger of physical timestamps

At a glance
  • How databases use timestamps to resolve conflicts
  • How clock skew silently loses data

Many replicated databases resolve conflicting writes with last-writer-wins (LWW): each write carries a timestamp, and when two replicas hold different values, the one with the higher timestamp wins.

Example · Losing a write to clock skew

Scenario: A client writes x = 1 via node A, whose clock is 100 ms fast; the write is stamped 12:00:00.200. Shortly afterwards, another client writes x = 2 via node B, whose clock is correct; the write is stamped 12:00:00.150, although it truly happened 50 ms later.

Result: When the replicas sync, LWW keeps x = 1 because 0.200 > 0.150. The later write, x = 2, is silently discarded. No error is reported anywhere.

Key insight: LWW with physical timestamps is not "last writer wins". It is "writer with the fastest clock wins". If you use LWW, you are choosing to lose some concurrent writes, and clock skew decides which.

Physical clocks are useful for many things, but they cannot reliably tell us the order of events on different machines. Leslie Lamport's answer, published in 1978, was to stop asking what time events happened and ask instead which events could have influenced which.

The happens-before relation

At a glance
  • Causality as the real meaning of "before"
  • Three rules that define happens-before
  • Concurrent events

Lamport's paper Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System (1978) is one of the most cited papers in computer science. Its central insight: in a distributed system, what matters is not when something happened by the clock, but whether information could have flowed from one event to another.

Definition · Happens-before (→)

For events a and b, a → b ("a happens before b") if:

  1. a and b occur on the same process, and a occurs before b in that process's execution; or
  2. a is the sending of a message and b is the receipt of that same message; or
  3. there is an event c such that a → c and c → b (transitivity).

If neither a → b nor b → a, the events are concurrent, written a ∥ b.

"Concurrent" here does not mean "at the same instant". It means neither event could have known about the other. Two events a minute apart on different machines that never communicated are concurrent.

Happens-before is a partial order: some pairs of events are ordered, others (the concurrent ones) are not. Many consistency problems come down to the question: are these two updates causally ordered, or concurrent? If causally ordered, the later one should win. If concurrent, there is a genuine conflict, and the system must decide what to do.

Test yourself

In the diagram above, is a → e? Is e → d?

Show answer

a and e are concurrent: no chain of messages leads from a to e or from e to a. e → d holds, because e and d happen on the same process P3 with e first. (Note that in the diagram, e occurs on P3 before P3 receives m2.)

Lamport clocks

At a glance
  • A simple counter that respects causality
  • The algorithm in three rules
  • What Lamport timestamps can and cannot tell you

Lamport also gave a mechanism to assign numbers to events that respect happens-before.

Definition · Lamport clock algorithm

Each process keeps an integer counter t, initially 0.

  1. Before each local event, increment: t = t + 1.
  2. When sending a message, increment t and attach the value to the message.
  3. When receiving a message with timestamp t', set t = max(t, t') + 1, and stamp the receive event with that value.

The resulting Lamport timestamps L satisfy the clock condition:

If a → b, then L(a) < L(b).

To get a total order on all events (useful for breaking ties deterministically), compare (L, process ID) pairs: order by timestamp, and break ties by process ID.

Example · Lamport timestamps step by step

Three processes start with t = 0.

  1. P1 performs local event a: P1's t = 1. L(a) = 1.
  2. P1 sends m1 to P2: P1's t = 2; m1 carries 2.
  3. P3 performs local event e: P3's t = 1. L(e) = 1.
  4. P2 receives m1: t = max(0, 2) + 1 = 3. L(b) = 3.
  5. P2 sends m2 to P3: P2's t = 4; m2 carries 4.
  6. P3 receives m2: t = max(1, 4) + 1 = 5. L(c) = 5.
  7. P3 performs local event d: t = 6. L(d) = 6.

Key insight: L(e) = 1 < L(b) = 3, yet e and b are concurrent. Lamport timestamps never contradict causality, but a smaller timestamp does not imply that one event happened before another.

Common mistake · Reading causality from Lamport timestamps

The clock condition works in one direction only: a → b implies L(a) < L(b), but L(a) < L(b) does not imply a → b. Lamport timestamps cannot detect concurrency. If you need to know whether two updates conflict, you need vector clocks.

Vector clocks

At a glance
  • One counter per process instead of one in total
  • The algorithm and comparison rule
  • Detecting concurrent updates

Vector clocks, developed independently by Colin Fidge and Friedemann Mattern in 1988, capture causality exactly. Each process keeps a vector of counters, one entry per process.

Definition · Vector clock algorithm

For n processes, each process i keeps a vector V of n integers, initially all 0.

  1. Before each local event, increment its own entry: V[i] = V[i] + 1.
  2. When sending, increment V[i] and attach a copy of V.
  3. When receiving a vector V', set each entry to V[j] = max(V[j], V'[j]), then increment V[i].

Comparison: V(a) ≤ V(b) if every entry of V(a) is ≤ the corresponding entry of V(b). Then:

  • a → b if and only if V(a) ≤ V(b) and V(a) ≠ V(b).
  • a ∥ b if neither V(a) ≤ V(b) nor V(b) ≤ V(a).

Applying this to the same execution as before (vector order [P1, P2, P3]):

Event Process Vector Reason
a P1 [1, 0, 0] local event
send m1 P1 [2, 0, 0] send
e P3 [0, 0, 1] local event
b (recv m1) P2 [2, 1, 0] max([0,0,0],[2,0,0]), then increment P2
send m2 P2 [2, 2, 0] send
c (recv m2) P3 [2, 2, 2] max([0,0,1],[2,2,0]) = [2,2,1], then increment P3
d P3 [2, 2, 3] local event

Now compare e = [0, 0, 1] and b = [2, 1, 0]: neither is ≤ the other, so the vector clocks correctly report them as concurrent. And a = [1, 0, 0] ≤ d = [2, 2, 3], so a → d.

The cost: the vector has one entry per participant. With thousands of clients this becomes large, which is why real systems use variants. Amazon's Dynamo attached vector clocks to object versions, keyed by the server that coordinated each write, and truncated old entries. Riak later moved to dotted version vectors to address problems with that approach.

Test yourself

Two replicas of a shopping cart hold versions with vector clocks [3, 1] and [2, 2]. What should the system do when it sees both?

Show answer

Neither vector dominates the other (3 > 2 in the first entry, 1 < 2 in the second), so the versions are concurrent: a genuine conflict. The system must keep both ("siblings") and merge them, either automatically (for a cart, take the union of items) or by asking the application. Discarding either would lose an update. Chapter 9 shows data types designed to merge automatically.

Hybrid logical clocks and TrueTime

At a glance
  • Getting causality and wall-clock closeness together
  • Google Spanner's approach: measure the uncertainty and wait it out

Logical clocks capture causality but have no relation to real time: you cannot ask a Lamport clock for "all writes before 14:00". Physical clocks relate to real time but get causality wrong. Two approaches combine them.

Hybrid logical clocks (HLC)

Kulkarni and colleagues proposed hybrid logical clocks in 2014. An HLC timestamp has two parts: a physical component l, which is the largest physical time the node has heard of, and a logical counter c, used to order events that share the same l. HLC timestamps:

  • always respect happens-before, like Lamport clocks;
  • stay close to physical time, within the clock synchronisation error;
  • fit into a 64-bit integer, like an ordinary timestamp.

HLCs are used by databases such as CockroachDB and YugabyteDB to order transactions and to support reads "as of" a point in time.

TrueTime

Google's Spanner (Corbett et al., 2012) took a different path. Each data centre has GPS receivers and atomic clocks, and the TrueTime API does not return a single time. It returns an interval, TT.now() = [earliest, latest], which is guaranteed to contain the true absolute time. The width of the interval, the uncertainty ε, is typically a few milliseconds.

To commit a transaction, Spanner picks a timestamp s and then performs a commit wait: it waits until TT.now().earliest > s before making the transaction visible. After the wait, the true time is guaranteed to be past s, so any transaction that starts later, on any node, gets a larger timestamp. This gives external consistency: if transaction T1 commits before T2 starts (in real time), T1's timestamp is smaller.

Key insight: TrueTime does not remove clock uncertainty. It measures it and pays for it with latency. The better the clocks, the shorter the wait, which is why Google invested in atomic clocks.

Ordering guarantees for broadcast

At a glance
  • FIFO, causal and total order broadcast
  • Why total order broadcast leads to consensus

Many distributed algorithms send each message to all nodes (broadcast). The order in which nodes deliver those messages determines what they agree on.

Guarantee Promise Implemented with
FIFO broadcast Messages from the same sender are delivered in the order sent Per-sender sequence numbers
Causal broadcast If sending m1 happens-before sending m2, every node delivers m1 before m2 Vector clocks
Total order broadcast All nodes deliver all messages in the same order A leader that assigns sequence numbers, or consensus

Causal broadcast is strictly stronger than FIFO broadcast, because messages from one sender are always causally ordered. Total order broadcast is a different kind of promise: it makes every node deliver the same sequence, but on its own it does not guarantee that the sequence respects FIFO or causal order. Protocols used for replication usually provide both, sometimes called FIFO-total order broadcast. Total order broadcast (also called atomic broadcast) is the one that matters most for replication: if every replica receives the same commands in the same order and applies them deterministically, all replicas end up in the same state. Achieving it fault-tolerantly turns out to be equivalent to consensus, which is why chapter 7 exists.

Lab · Compute it, then simulate it
  1. Draw a space-time diagram with three processes and at least six messages. Compute Lamport timestamps and vector clocks for every event. Mark all pairs of concurrent events.
  2. Write a small simulation in your language of choice: three goroutines or threads exchanging messages with random delays, each maintaining a vector clock. Log every event with its vector.
  3. Write a checker that reads the log and verifies that for every message, the send's vector is strictly less than the receive's vector.
  4. Extension: implement causal broadcast: each node buffers incoming messages until all their causal predecessors have been delivered.

In Appendix A: A.4 Clocks: compute, simulate, causal broadcast (hints)

Deep dive Logical time and Einstein

Lamport has said that the 1978 paper was inspired by special relativity. In relativity, there is no absolute simultaneity: two events far apart may appear in different orders to different observers, but if one event can causally influence another (a light signal can travel between them), all observers agree on their order. Replace "light signal" with "message", and you have happens-before. The set of events that could have influenced an event e is its "past light cone". A vector clock records exactly that cone. The analogy is not just poetic: it explains why no algorithm can give you a global "now", since such a thing does not physically exist.

Synthesis

Synthesis

The 2012 leap second showed that physical time is a fragile foundation. Lamport's answer was to stop depending on it: what matters is not when something happened by a clock, but whether it could have influenced something else. From that single idea come Lamport clocks (consistent with causality), vector clocks (exactly equal to causality), and practical hybrids (causality plus approximate wall-clock time). Spanner shows the opposite strategy: keep physical time, but measure its uncertainty and wait it out.

Notice the recurring theme: concurrency is not a bug in the system; it is a fact about information flow. When two updates are concurrent, no clock can tell you which was "really" first, because there is no such thing. The system must instead decide how to resolve the conflict, which is the subject of Part II.

Key terms

Clock drift, NTP, slewing / stepping, time-of-day clock, monotonic clock, last-writer-wins (LWW), happens-before, concurrent events, Lamport clock, vector clock, hybrid logical clock (HLC), TrueTime, commit wait, external consistency, FIFO / causal / total order broadcast.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) State the three rules that define happens-before.
  2. (Understand) Why should timeouts be measured with a monotonic clock rather than the wall clock?
  3. (Understand) Explain why L(a) < L(b) does not imply a → b.
  4. (Apply) Compute the vector clocks for this execution with processes P1 and P2: P1 local event; P1 sends to P2; P2 local event; P2 receives P1's message; P2 sends to P1; P1 receives.
  5. (Analyze) A database uses LWW with NTP-synchronised timestamps. Describe a concrete scenario in which a user's update is lost, and explain how vector clocks would have changed the outcome.
  6. (Evaluate) You are designing an audit log for a system spread across three data centres. Auditors want to see events "in the order they happened". Which clock mechanism would you choose, and what would you tell the auditors about the limits of the ordering?

Reflection

Look for the word timestamp in a codebase you work on. Which clock produces it? What would happen if that clock jumped back ten seconds?

Looking ahead

You now have the tools to order events. Part II puts them to work. Chapter 5 copies data to several machines and splits it across many, and immediately faces the question: when copies disagree, which one is right?

Further reading

  • Lamport, Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System (CACM, 1978). Essential reading.
  • Kulkarni et al., Logical Physical Clocks (OPODIS 2014). Hybrid logical clocks.
  • Corbett et al., Spanner: Google's Globally-Distributed Database (OSDI 2012). Section 3 on TrueTime.
  • Kleppmann's Cambridge lectures 3 and 4 cover this chapter's material with excellent diagrams.
Part II · Data and agreement
Part II · Introduction

When Copies Disagree

Why data and agreement are where correctness lives

Users rarely care how many servers you run. They care whether the seat they booked is really theirs, whether their payment happened exactly once, whether the timetable they see is the current one, and whether the change they just made is still there when they reload the page. Those are questions about data: where it is stored, how many copies exist, and what happens when the copies disagree.

In a distributed system, they always disagree at some point. Replicas lag behind each other, partitions split them, nodes crash in the middle of writes, and concurrent users change the same thing at the same moment. Whether that turns into a bug that someone has to explain to an angry customer depends on choices that are usually made early and quietly: which database, which replication mode, which consistency level, which isolation level, which "default" nobody questioned.

Part II gives you the means to make those choices deliberately, and to read the promises of databases and cloud services critically:

  • Chapter 5, Replication and partitioning, shows how data is copied for safety and split for scale, and what failover really does to your last few seconds of writes.
  • Chapter 6, Consistency models, gives precise names to what a system promises about the values you read, and states CAP and PACELC correctly.
  • Chapter 7, Consensus, explains how machines that may fail agree on anything at all, and how to use that safely through leases, fencing tokens and coordination services.
  • Chapter 8, Transactions across machines, covers isolation anomalies, two-phase commit, sagas and the outbox pattern: keeping invariants true when one operation spans several systems.
  • Chapter 9, Gossip and CRDTs, shows when you can avoid coordination altogether, and how to tell whether a problem allows it.
Essential question

For each piece of data your users depend on, which anomalies are you willing to let them see, and which would be a real failure?

This is the most demanding part of the book. It is also the part where the cost of getting it wrong is highest, because a data bug is often silent: it does not page anyone, it simply leaves the wrong answer in place until a customer finds it.

Chapter 05

Replication and Partitioning

Copying data for safety, splitting it for scale

Suppose you keep your only copy of a database on one server, and that server's disk dies. Whatever was written since the last backup is gone. So you keep a second copy on another server, and now you have a new problem: every write must reach both copies, and if it reaches only one before something fails, the copies disagree. Which one is right?

Then your data grows beyond what one server can hold, so you split it across ten servers. Now a query about "all customers in Dalarna" must ask all ten, and if one is down, the answer is incomplete.

These are the two fundamental operations on data in a distributed system: replication (keeping copies of the same data on several nodes) and partitioning (splitting different data across nodes, also called sharding). Almost every distributed database combines both. This chapter is about how they work and what they cost.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Describe leader-based, multi-leader and leaderless replication (Understand)
  • Explain the anomalies caused by replication lag and the hazards of failover (Understand)
  • Apply quorum arithmetic (R + W > N) and analyze why it does not guarantee strong consistency on its own (Apply/Analyze)
  • Compare range partitioning, hash partitioning and consistent hashing (Analyze)
  • Design a replication and partitioning scheme for a given workload and justify it (Create/Evaluate)
Essential question

When copies of the same data disagree, who gets to decide which version is true?

Before you start

Chapter 4 ended with total order broadcast: if every replica applies the same writes in the same order, they stay identical. Replication schemes differ in how hard they try to achieve that order, and what they do when they cannot.

The chapter in brief: why replicate; leader-based replication and its lag; failover; multi-leader and leaderless replication; quorums; partitioning strategies; consistent hashing; request routing.

Why replicate?

At a glance
  • Three reasons to keep copies
  • The core difficulty: handling changes

Replication serves three goals:

  1. Durability and availability. If one node fails, the data survives and can still be served.
  2. Read scaling. More copies can serve more read requests.
  3. Latency. Copies placed near users answer faster.

If the data never changed, replication would be trivial: copy it once. All the difficulty lies in propagating changes. There are three broad families of algorithms for this, distinguished by who accepts writes.

Family Who accepts writes Examples
Single-leader One designated node PostgreSQL and MySQL streaming replication, MongoDB replica sets, Kafka partitions
Multi-leader Several nodes, typically one per data centre Multi-site MySQL setups, CouchDB, offline-capable apps
Leaderless Any replica Amazon's Dynamo, Apache Cassandra, Riak

Single-leader replication

At a glance
  • One leader accepts writes and streams them to followers
  • Synchronous, asynchronous and semi-synchronous replication
  • What replication lag looks like to users

In single-leader (also called primary-replica, and historically master-slave) replication, one node is the leader. All writes go to the leader, which applies them locally and then sends them to followers as a replication log or change stream. Followers apply the changes in the same order. Reads can go to the leader or to any follower.

Synchronous or asynchronous?

Mode The leader confirms a write when… Durability Latency and availability
Synchronous all (or a specified set of) followers have it No acknowledged write is lost if the leader dies Slow; a single slow follower blocks all writes
Asynchronous it has written locally Recent acknowledged writes may be lost if the leader dies Fast; followers can lag without affecting writes
Semi-synchronous at least one follower has it Survives the loss of the leader Moderate; one slow follower is tolerated

Most production setups are asynchronous or semi-synchronous. That means followers are usually a little behind the leader: milliseconds normally, but seconds or minutes under load or after a network problem. This delay is replication lag.

Anomalies caused by replication lag

Replication lag produces anomalies that users notice:

  • Reading your own writes fails. A user updates their profile and reloads the page. The read goes to a follower that has not yet received the update, so the change appears to have been lost.
  • Time goes backwards. A user refreshes twice. The first request hits an up-to-date follower, the second a lagging one. A comment that was just visible disappears.
  • Effects before causes. A reply to a question is replicated faster than the question itself. An observer sees the answer before the question was asked.

Each of these has a name and a formal definition in chapter 6 (read-your-writes, monotonic reads and causal consistency), and each has standard fixes, such as routing a user's reads to the leader for a short time after they write, or the client-side version tracking from chapter 2.

Test yourself

A user posts a comment and is redirected to the comment page, which is served by a follower. How could you make sure the user sees their own comment, without sending all reads to the leader?

Show answer

Several options: remember the time or log position of the user's last write (for example in a cookie or session) and route that user's reads to the leader, or to a follower that has caught up to at least that position, for a short while; or return the new comment from the write response and render it on the client directly. This provides read-your-writes for that user without loading the leader with everyone's reads.

Failover: when the leader dies

At a glance
  • Detecting a dead leader and promoting a follower
  • Three ways failover goes wrong

If a follower fails, it simply catches up from the leader's log when it returns. If the leader fails, a follower must be promoted, and clients and other followers must be redirected. This is failover. It can be manual or automatic, and automatic failover is full of traps:

  1. Lost writes. With asynchronous replication, the new leader may not have the old leader's most recent writes. If the old leader returns, its extra writes conflict with what the new leader has accepted since. They are often simply discarded, and acknowledged writes are lost.
  2. Split brain. If the old leader is not actually dead, only partitioned or paused, two nodes may both believe they are the leader and both accept writes. Data diverges, sometimes irreparably.
  3. Wrong timeout. A timeout that is too short causes unnecessary failovers during load spikes, which add even more load. One that is too long leaves the system without a leader for longer.
Example · A failover that lost data

Scenario: A MySQL database with asynchronous replication fails over to a follower that is slightly behind. The database assigns primary keys with an auto-increment counter, and the new leader's counter is lower than the old leader's was.

Consequence: New rows reuse primary keys that had already been handed out by the old leader for rows the new leader never received. A separate Redis store still holds data under those old keys, so it is now attached to the wrong rows, and some users see data that belongs to other users.

Key insight: Failover is not a local event. It affects every system that held assumptions about the old leader's state. This scenario is a real 2012 GitHub incident, described in Designing Data-Intensive Applications. Chapter 16 analyses a larger GitHub failover incident from 2018.

The safe way to elect a leader and to fence off an old one requires consensus, which is why chapter 7 exists.

Multi-leader replication

At a glance
  • One leader per data centre, or per device
  • Write conflicts and how to resolve them

In multi-leader replication, several nodes accept writes and replicate them to each other asynchronously. The typical case is one leader per data centre: each data centre keeps working even if the link between them fails, and users write to a nearby leader with low latency.

The price is write conflicts: two leaders may accept conflicting changes to the same record at roughly the same time. Conflict resolution strategies include:

  • Last-writer-wins by timestamp: simple, but silently loses writes (chapter 4).
  • Merge: combine the values (for example the union of two shopping carts), using techniques from chapter 9.
  • Keep both: store all conflicting versions and ask the application or user to resolve them later.
  • Avoid conflicts: route all writes for a given record to the same leader, so conflicts only arise when that leader fails over.

Offline-capable applications are an extreme case of multi-leader replication: every phone or laptop is a "leader" that accepts writes while disconnected and syncs later. Collaborative editors such as shared documents work the same way.

Leaderless replication and quorums

At a glance
  • Any replica accepts reads and writes
  • Quorum conditions: R + W > N
  • Read repair, anti-entropy, sloppy quorums and hinted handoff
  • Why quorums alone do not give strong consistency

Amazon's Dynamo paper (DeCandia et al., 2007) popularised leaderless replication. There is no leader. The client (or a coordinator node acting for it) sends each write to several replicas in parallel and each read to several replicas in parallel.

The quorum condition

Let N be the number of replicas for a key, W the number that must acknowledge a write for it to succeed, and R the number that must respond to a read.

Definition · Quorum condition

If R + W > N, every set of R replicas overlaps every set of W replicas in at least one node. A read will therefore reach at least one replica that received the most recent successful write.

Example: N = 3, W = 2, R = 2. Any 2 read replicas and any 2 write replicas share at least one node.

The reader receives several values (here 7 from B and 3 from C) and picks the newest, using version numbers or vector clocks. It can also write the newest value back to the stale replica: this is read repair. A background process can additionally compare replicas and copy missing data (anti-entropy). Dynamo and Cassandra use Merkle trees, hash trees over key ranges, so that two replicas can find their differences by exchanging only a few hashes.

Common configurations:

Setting Effect
N = 3, W = 2, R = 2 Balanced. Tolerates one unavailable replica for both reads and writes.
N = 3, W = 3, R = 1 Fast reads, but a single unavailable replica blocks all writes.
N = 3, W = 1, R = 1 Maximum availability and speed; R + W ≤ N, so reads can be stale.

Sloppy quorums and hinted handoff

What if a network problem makes some of a key's N "home" replicas unreachable? Dynamo's answer is a sloppy quorum: write to any W reachable nodes, even ones that are not among the key's home replicas. The temporary holder keeps the write with a "hint" saying where it belongs and passes it on when the home node returns (hinted handoff). This raises write availability considerably, but now a later read from the R home replicas may not overlap the W nodes that actually hold the write.

Why R + W > N is not enough

The quorum condition is often presented as a recipe for strong consistency. It is not. Even with R + W > N, a reader can observe stale or surprising results:

  1. Sloppy quorums break the overlap, as just described.
  2. Concurrent writes must be resolved somehow. If the resolution is last-writer-wins by timestamp, clock skew can drop writes (chapter 4).
  3. Partially failed writes are not rolled back. A write that reached one replica but not W replicas is reported as failed, yet it remains on that replica and may be returned by later reads.
  4. Read-then-read anomalies. If a write is in progress, one reader may see the new value and a later reader the old one, unless readers perform read repair synchronously before returning.
  5. Restored replicas. If a replica holding a new value fails and is restored from an older copy, the number of replicas holding the new value can drop below W.

These effects are why chapter 6 needs a precise definition of what "consistent" means, and why systems that need it use consensus (chapter 7) rather than quorums alone.

Common mistake · "We use quorum reads and writes, so we're strongly consistent"

R + W > N guarantees only that read and write quorums overlap. It does not guarantee linearizability, the strong consistency model defined in chapter 6. Kleppmann's Designing Data-Intensive Applications and Jepsen's analyses of Dynamo-style databases document the gap in detail.

Test yourself

A Cassandra cluster uses N = 3, W = 2, R = 2. A write succeeds on replicas A and B. Later, B's disk fails and B is rebuilt from a backup taken before the write. Can a quorum read now return the old value?

Show answer

Yes. Only A holds the new value. A read that contacts B and C gets two old values, and the overlap argument no longer holds, because the number of replicas holding the write has dropped below W. Anti-entropy will eventually copy the value from A, but until then quorum reads can be stale.

Partitioning

At a glance
  • Splitting data so that each node holds a part
  • Range partitioning and hash partitioning
  • Hot spots and skew

Replication puts the same data on several nodes. Partitioning (sharding) puts different data on different nodes, so that the dataset and the load can exceed what one node can handle. Each record belongs to exactly one partition, and each partition is usually replicated using one of the schemes above.

Range partitioning

Assign each partition a contiguous range of keys, like volumes of a printed encyclopedia: A–C, D–F, and so on. Range queries ("all orders between 1 and 31 January") touch few partitions. The risk is hot spots: if keys are timestamps, all of today's writes go to one partition while the others sit idle. Systems that use range partitioning include HBase, Bigtable and Spanner, typically splitting ranges automatically when they grow.

Hash partitioning

Apply a hash function to the key and assign ranges of the hash to partitions. A good hash spreads keys evenly, removing most hot spots, but destroys key ordering: a range query must ask every partition.

Skew remains

Even perfect hashing cannot spread load for a single hot key: a celebrity's account, a viral product, a popular timetable. Common mitigations are caching, splitting the hot key into several sub-keys (appending a random suffix and combining results on read), or giving the key a dedicated replica set.

Range partitioning Hash partitioning
Range queries Efficient Must query all partitions
Even load Only with good key design Usually, except for single hot keys
Typical use Time series, ordered scans Key-value lookups

Consistent hashing and rebalancing

At a glance
  • Why hash(key) mod N is a trap
  • Consistent hashing on a ring
  • Virtual nodes for even load

The naive way to assign keys is partition = hash(key) mod N, where N is the number of nodes. When N changes from 10 to 11, almost every key maps to a different node, and the whole dataset must move.

Consistent hashing (Karger et al., 1997) solves this. Picture the hash space as a ring. Each node is placed on the ring at the position of its own hash. Each key is stored on the first node found by walking clockwise from the key's position.

When a node is added, it takes over only the keys between its position and its predecessor's: on average about 1/N of all keys. When a node is removed, only its keys move, to its successor.

With few nodes, positions on the ring are uneven, so some nodes get far more keys than others. The standard fix is virtual nodes: each physical node is placed on the ring many times (for example 100–256 positions), so that its share averages out. Virtual nodes also let a more powerful machine take a larger share simply by having more positions.

Note

Not every system uses a hash ring. Many choose a fixed number of partitions much larger than the number of nodes (for example 1,024 partitions on 10 nodes) and move whole partitions between nodes when the cluster changes. This is simpler to reason about and is common in systems such as Elasticsearch, Riak and Kafka (whose partitions are fixed per topic).

Request routing

How does a client find the node that owns a key? Three common approaches:

  1. Any node: the client talks to any node, which forwards the request if needed (Cassandra's gossip-based approach).
  2. Routing tier: a proxy layer knows the partition map and forwards requests.
  3. Partition-aware client: the client knows the map and connects directly.

All three need an authoritative, up-to-date partition map. Many systems store it in a coordination service such as ZooKeeper or etcd, which is built on consensus (chapter 7).

Lab · Build and measure a consistent hashing ring
  1. Implement a ring with configurable virtual nodes per physical node. Use a sorted array of positions and binary search for lookups.
  2. Insert 100,000 random keys across 5 nodes. Measure the distribution of keys per node with 1, 10 and 200 virtual nodes each. Plot or tabulate the standard deviation.
  3. Add a sixth node. Count how many keys changed owner. Compare with hash mod N.
  4. Optional, MIT 6.5840: lab 5 (sharded key/value service) implements partitioning with reconfiguration on top of replicated groups. Attempt it after chapter 7.

What to notice: With one virtual node, distribution is very uneven. With many, it approaches the ideal, and roughly 1/6 of keys move when the sixth node joins.

In Appendix A: A.5 Consistent hashing ring (hints)

Deep dive Placement, failure domains and rack awareness

Where replicas live matters as much as how many there are. Three replicas on three machines in the same rack share a top-of-rack switch and power feed; three replicas in three Availability Zones do not. Production systems therefore use rack-aware or zone-aware placement: the placement algorithm ensures that replicas of each partition span failure domains. Cassandra's NetworkTopologyStrategy, Kafka's rack-aware replica assignment and Kubernetes' topology spread constraints all serve this purpose. The availability arithmetic of chapter 1 depends on it: replicas only multiply your nines if their failures are independent, and placement is how you make them more independent.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Return to the opening question: when copies disagree, who decides which version is true? The three replication families give three different answers. Single-leader: the leader decides, and the hard part is agreeing who the leader is. Multi-leader: nobody decides up front, so conflicts must be resolved after the fact. Leaderless: the reader decides by comparing versions, and quorums make it likely, but not certain, that the newest version is among those compared.

Partitioning adds a second dimension but the same question arises one level up: who decides which node owns which key? In every case, a truly authoritative answer requires nodes to agree. That is the subject of chapter 7. But first we need precise language for what "agree" and "consistent" mean, which is chapter 6.

Key terms

Replication, partitioning / sharding, leader / follower, synchronous / asynchronous / semi-synchronous replication, replication lag, failover, split brain, multi-leader replication, write conflict, leaderless replication, quorum (N, W, R), read repair, anti-entropy, Merkle tree, sloppy quorum, hinted handoff, range / hash partitioning, hot spot, consistent hashing, virtual node, request routing.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the three families of replication and one example system for each.
  2. (Understand) Explain why asynchronous replication can lose acknowledged writes during failover.
  3. (Apply) For N = 5, list all (W, R) combinations that satisfy the quorum condition with 2 ≤ W ≤ 4 and 2 ≤ R ≤ 4. Which tolerates the most unavailable replicas for writes, and what does it cost on reads?
  4. (Apply) A ring has 4 nodes with 1 virtual node each and 1 million keys. Approximately how many keys move when a fifth node joins? How many would move with hash mod N?
  5. (Analyze) List three reasons why a leaderless store with R + W > N can still return a stale value.
  6. (Evaluate) A team stores sensor readings keyed by timestamp in a range-partitioned database and complains that one node is overloaded. Diagnose the cause and propose two alternative key designs.

Reflection

Consider a database you use. Is its replication synchronous or asynchronous? What happens to the last second of writes if its leader's machine catches fire?

Looking ahead

We have used words like "stale", "newest" and "consistent" informally. Chapter 6 turns them into precise models, from linearizability down to eventual consistency, and finally states the CAP theorem correctly.

Further reading

  • Kleppmann and Riccomini, Designing Data-Intensive Applications (2nd ed., 2026), chapters on replication and sharding. The best extended treatment.
  • DeCandia et al., Dynamo: Amazon's Highly Available Key-value Store (SOSP 2007). Quorums, sloppy quorums, hinted handoff and vector clocks in one paper.
  • Karger et al., Consistent Hashing and Random Trees (STOC 1997). The origin of consistent hashing.
Chapter 06

Consistency Models, CAP and PACELC

What a system promises about what you will read

Alice and Bob are waiting at a train station. Alice refreshes the departure app on her phone and sees: "Platform changed: departs from platform 4." She turns to Bob and says, "It moved to platform 4." Bob refreshes his own phone, and his app still says platform 2.

Bob's phone is not broken. It simply asked a different replica, one a few hundred milliseconds behind. Each replica is "correct" in the sense that it holds a value that was true recently. Yet from where Alice and Bob stand, the system has told them two contradictory things, and Bob refreshed after Alice told him. Something has been violated. But what, exactly?

This chapter gives that "something" a name. More precisely, it gives a whole family of names, because "consistent" can mean many different things, and confusing them is one of the most common sources of bugs and misleading marketing in this field.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Define linearizability, sequential consistency, causal consistency, PRAM and eventual consistency (Remember/Understand)
  • Explain the four session guarantees and apply them to concrete user-facing anomalies (Understand/Apply)
  • Place consistency models in a hierarchy and determine which are achievable during a network partition (Analyze)
  • State the CAP theorem precisely and correct common misstatements of it (Understand/Evaluate)
  • Use PACELC to compare real databases' trade-offs (Analyze/Evaluate)
Essential question

How strong does consistency need to be for a given application, and how would you know if you had chosen too weak a model?

Before you start

Chapter 5 showed how replication lag causes anomalies: reads that miss your own writes, time going backwards, effects before causes. Here we turn each anomaly into a formal guarantee you can ask for, or deliberately give up.

The chapter in brief: what a consistency model is; linearizability; sequential consistency; causal consistency; PRAM and session guarantees; eventual consistency; the hierarchy; CAP done properly; PACELC.

What is a consistency model?

At a glance
  • A contract between a data store and its clients
  • Models are defined by the histories they allow
  • "Stronger" means fewer allowed histories
Definition · Consistency model

A contract that specifies which results reads are allowed to return, given the writes that have occurred. Formally, it is the set of histories (sequences of operations with their invocation times, response times and results) that the system is allowed to produce.

A stronger model allows fewer histories, so it is easier to program against and harder to implement. A weaker model allows more histories, which permits more availability and lower latency but leaves more anomalies for the application to handle.

Two families of models are discussed in this chapter, and they come from different traditions:

  • Data-centric models (linearizability, sequential, causal, PRAM) describe what all processes observe of a shared object.
  • Client-centric models (the session guarantees) describe what a single client observes during its session.

Transactional isolation levels (serializability, snapshot isolation) are a third family, covering groups of operations; they appear in chapter 8.

Linearizability: the illusion of one copy

At a glance
  • The strongest single-object model
  • Real-time ordering as the defining property
  • Where you need it and what it costs
Definition · Linearizability (Herlihy and Wing, 1990)

Every operation appears to take effect atomically at some instant between its invocation and its response, and all operations appear in an order consistent with those instants. In practice: the system behaves as if there were a single copy of the data, and once a write completes, every later read (in real time) sees it or a newer value.

Return to the station. Alice's read returned "platform 4" and completed. Bob's read started after Alice's completed (Alice told him, then he refreshed). Under linearizability, Bob's read must return "platform 4" or something newer. It returned "platform 2", so the system is not linearizable.

Notice that Alice told Bob outside the system, by speaking. Linearizability matters exactly when there are such side channels: one part of the system acts on a value and then signals another part through a different path. Examples:

  • Locks and leader election. Every node must agree on who holds the lock right now.
  • Uniqueness constraints. Two users must not both successfully claim the username "dala_horse".
  • Cross-channel timing. A web server stores an uploaded image, then sends a message to a resizing service through a queue. If the resizer reads from a non-linearizable replica, it may find no image yet.

The cost

Linearizability requires coordination: in a replicated system it is usually implemented with a single leader that replicates synchronously to a majority, or with consensus (chapter 7). Every write, and every read that must be guaranteed fresh, needs at least one round trip to a majority of replicas. Across regions, that means tens to hundreds of milliseconds. And, as we will see, it cannot be provided during a network partition by the nodes that are cut off from the majority.

Test yourself

A key-value store has a single leader and asynchronously replicated followers. Reads from the leader are linearizable. Are reads from followers linearizable? What about reads from the leader if the leader might not know it has been replaced?

Show answer

Follower reads are not linearizable, because a follower can be behind. Leader reads are linearizable only if the leader really is the current leader. A leader that has been partitioned away and replaced, but has not noticed, may serve stale reads. This is why Raft-based systems confirm leadership with a quorum (a "read index" round) or use time-based leases before serving linearizable reads.

Sequential consistency

At a glance
  • One global order, but not necessarily real-time order
  • Each process's operations stay in program order
Definition · Sequential consistency (Lamport, 1979)

The result of any execution is the same as if the operations of all processes were executed in some single sequential order, and the operations of each individual process appear in that order in the order the process issued them.

Compared with linearizability, sequential consistency drops the real-time requirement. Everyone sees the same order of operations, but that order may differ from the order in which operations actually happened in time. In the station example, a sequentially consistent system could legally show Bob "platform 2", as long as every observer agrees on one consistent ordering of all reads and writes and Bob never afterwards sees platform 2 after having seen platform 4.

Sequential consistency originated in multiprocessor memory models, where it describes what programmers intuitively expect from shared memory. It is rarely the explicit target of distributed databases, but it is a useful stepping stone between linearizability and the weaker models.

Causal consistency

At a glance
  • Cause before effect, everywhere
  • Concurrent writes may be seen in different orders
  • The strongest model that remains available during partitions
Definition · Causal consistency

Writes that are causally related (by the happens-before relation of chapter 4) must be seen by every process in the same order. Writes that are concurrent may be seen in different orders by different processes.

In a discussion forum: Ana posts a question, Björn reads it and posts an answer. The answer is causally dependent on the question. A causally consistent system guarantees that nobody sees Björn's answer without Ana's question. But if Cecilia and David post unrelated comments at the same time, different readers may see those two comments in different orders.

Causal consistency is attractive because it rules out the most confusing anomalies (effects before causes) while still allowing replicas to accept reads and writes during a partition. Its precise availability properties are subtle: Jepsen's consistency map classifies causal consistency as sticky available, meaning that a client remains served during a partition as long as it keeps talking to the same server or replica group. Implementations track dependencies with vector clocks or similar metadata, which costs storage and network overhead.

Common mistake · Mistaking "causal" for "chaotic"

"Causal" means related by cause and effect. A causally consistent system is not a weak or unpredictable one: it is the strongest consistency model that can remain available during a partition (in the sticky sense above), and research systems such as COPS (Lloyd et al., 2011) have shown it can scale across data centres.

PRAM and the session guarantees

At a glance
  • PRAM: each writer's writes are seen in order
  • Four session guarantees from the Bayou project
  • How they combine

PRAM consistency

Definition · PRAM consistency (Lipton and Sandberg, 1988)

Also called FIFO consistency. All processes see the writes of each single process in the order that process issued them. Writes from different processes may be seen in different orders by different observers.

PRAM stands for Pipelined RAM. It is weaker than causal consistency because it ignores dependencies between processes: if Björn writes an answer after reading Ana's question, PRAM does not require anyone to see the question first, because they come from different writers.

The four session guarantees

In 1994, Terry and colleagues at Xerox PARC, working on the Bayou system for mobile computing, defined four guarantees that a client can ask for within its own session (the sequence of operations by one client):

Guarantee Promise to the client Anomaly it prevents
Read your writes (RYW) A read reflects all writes previously made in the same session You update your profile and the page shows the old version
Monotonic reads (MR) Successive reads never return older data than a previous read did A comment appears, then disappears on refresh
Monotonic writes (MW) Writes from a session are applied everywhere in the order they were issued Your "set address", then "ship order" are applied in reverse
Writes follow reads (WFR) A write made after reading a value is ordered after the write that produced that value Your reply to a post is visible somewhere the post is not

The session guarantees combine neatly. Brzeziński, Sobaniec and Wawrzyniak showed in 2003 that PRAM is exactly equivalent to RYW + MR + MW, and in 2004 that adding WFR gives causal consistency. In other words, the four session guarantees are the building blocks of causal consistency.

Example · Implementing monotonic reads and read-your-writes on the client

Scenario: A train-crew scheduling app talks to an eventually consistent store through a load balancer that picks a random replica for each request. Every response includes the replica's log position for the data returned.

Walkthrough:

  1. The client keeps seen = highest log position observed so far and wrote = log position of its own last write.
  2. With every read it sends min_position = max(seen, wrote).
  3. A replica that has not reached min_position either waits until it has, or rejects the request so that the client retries elsewhere.

Result: The client gets monotonic reads (it never goes backwards) and read your writes (it always sees its own updates), even though the store itself only promises eventual consistency.

Key insight: Session guarantees can be layered on top of weaker systems with a small amount of client-side metadata, the same idea as the dashboard example in chapter 2. MongoDB's causally consistent sessions and many other systems work this way.

Test yourself

A user writes a comment (write W1) and then edits it (write W2). Another user sees the edit, but later sees the original comment again. Which session guarantee or guarantees are violated, and for which user?

Show answer

The second user experiences a violation of monotonic reads: after seeing the state that includes W2, a later read returned older data. If the system also applied W2 before W1 on some replica, the first user's monotonic writes guarantee is violated too.

Eventual consistency

At a glance
  • The weakest common guarantee
  • What it promises, and what it does not
  • Strong eventual consistency
Definition · Eventual consistency

If no new updates are made to an item, eventually all replicas will return the same value for it.

Werner Vogels, CTO of Amazon, popularised the term in 2008. It is a liveness property only: it says nothing about what you read before convergence, how long "eventually" is, or which value the replicas converge to. A system that always returns "42" until someone looks away for a year would technically qualify.

In practice, eventual consistency is useful when convergence is fast and the application tolerates temporary anomalies: social media like counts, DNS, product reviews, monitoring dashboards.

A more useful variant is strong eventual consistency (Shapiro et al., 2011): any two replicas that have received the same set of updates are in the same state, regardless of the order in which they received them. This adds a safety property to eventual consistency and is what the CRDTs of chapter 9 provide.

The hierarchy of models

At a glance
  • How the models relate to each other
  • Which ones can stay available during partitions
Model Ordering promise Available during a partition?
Linearizable Single copy; real-time order No
Sequential One global order; per-process order No
Causal Causal order everywhere Sticky available
PRAM Per-writer order Sticky available
RYW Your own writes visible to you Sticky available
Monotonic reads / writes, WFR Per-session ordering Yes (totally available)
Eventual Convergence, eventually Yes

Jepsen's interactive consistency map at jepsen.io/consistency is the best single reference for these relationships, including the transactional models.

The CAP theorem, stated properly

At a glance
  • What Brewer conjectured and Gilbert and Lynch proved
  • What C, A and P mean precisely
  • Five common misconceptions

Eric Brewer presented the CAP conjecture in a keynote at the PODC symposium in 2000. Seth Gilbert and Nancy Lynch proved a formal version in 2002.

Definition · CAP theorem (Gilbert and Lynch, 2002)

In an asynchronous network where messages can be lost, it is impossible for a read/write register to guarantee both:

  • Consistency (C): linearizability, as defined above; and
  • Availability (A): every request received by a non-failing node must eventually receive a response;

in all executions, including those where the network partitions (P).

The proof is short. Partition the nodes into two groups that cannot communicate. A client writes a new value on side 1. A client then reads on side 2. If side 2 responds (availability), it cannot know about the write, so the read is stale (not linearizable). If side 2 waits for the partition to heal (consistency), it does not respond (not available).

So, during a partition, a system must choose for each operation: stay linearizable and refuse some requests (a "CP" behaviour), or keep responding and give up linearizability ("AP").

Five misconceptions

  1. "Pick two of three." Partitions are not optional, so you cannot "choose CA". The real choice is between C and A during a partition. Brewer himself wrote in 2012 that the "2 of 3" formulation was always misleading.
  2. "C in CAP is the C in ACID." It is not. CAP's C is linearizability. ACID's C means that a transaction preserves application invariants. Chapter 8 covers ACID.
  3. "A in CAP means high availability." CAP's A is extremely strict: every non-failing node must respond. A CP system that serves all clients on the majority side of a partition is not "available" in the CAP sense, but it may well meet a 99.99 % availability target.
  4. "NoSQL is AP and SQL is CP." These labels describe configurations, not technologies. A replicated SQL database with asynchronous replicas serves stale reads (not C); a Cassandra cluster with appropriate settings can behave closer to CP for some operations. Choices are often per operation.
  5. "CAP explains latency trade-offs." It says nothing about the normal case without partitions, which is where most of the trade-off actually lives.
Test yourself

A three-node etcd cluster is partitioned into {A} and {B, C}. A client connected to A tries to write. What happens, and which side of CAP is etcd choosing?

Show answer

A cannot reach a majority, so it cannot commit the write; the client's request fails or times out. B and C form a majority and continue to serve requests. etcd chooses consistency (linearizability) over CAP availability: node A is non-failing but does not serve writes. Note that from the perspective of clients that can reach B or C, the service is still up.

PACELC: the rest of the story

At a glance
  • Latency versus consistency when there is no partition
  • Classifying real systems
