Daniel Abadi pointed out, first in a 2010 blog post and then in a 2012 article, that CAP describes a rare situation and ignores a constant one. Even when the network is healthy, replicating data with strong consistency means waiting for other replicas, which costs latency. He proposed PACELC:

If there is a Partition, choose between Availability and Consistency; Else, choose between Latency and Consistency.

System (typical configuration) During partition (PA / PC) Else (EL / EC)
Dynamo-style stores (Cassandra, Riak with defaults) PA EL
Google Spanner, etcd (with its default linearizable reads) PC EC
MongoDB with majority write concern and linearizable reads PC EC
ZooKeeper PC EC for writes, EL for reads

Configurations matter more than products: the same database can land in different cells depending on its settings, and sometimes in different cells for different operations. ZooKeeper is a good example. Its writes go through the leader and are linearizable, but by default a client reads from whichever server it is connected to, which may be slightly behind. Reads are fast and possibly stale (EL) unless the client first calls sync.

Lab · Gossip Glomers challenge 3: broadcast
  1. Complete challenges 3a–3c (Single-Node, Multi-Node and Fault Tolerant Broadcast). Every node must eventually learn every message, even when Maelstrom partitions the network.
  2. When your solution passes, rerun it with --nemesis partition and open the Maelstrom web view. Look at a read that returned an incomplete set during a partition.
  3. In your notebook, answer: which consistency model does your broadcast provide? Is it linearizable? Eventually consistent? Why does the checker accept it?
  4. Continue with 3d and 3e (Efficient Broadcast), which add latency and message-count targets. Note how the PACELC "else" trade-off appears: fewer messages usually means slower convergence.

In Appendix A: A.6 Broadcast (Gossip Glomers 3a–3e) (hints)

Deep dive How Jepsen checks consistency

Jepsen tests a database by running many concurrent clients against it while injecting faults (partitions, crashes, clock skew), recording every operation's invocation, completion and result. Afterwards, a checker asks: does a legal order of these operations exist under the claimed consistency model? For linearizability, the Knossos checker searches for such an order; the problem is NP-complete in general, so Knossos uses pruning heuristics. For transactional models, the Elle checker (Kingsbury and Alvaro, VLDB 2020) infers dependency graphs from carefully designed workloads and looks for cycles that would be forbidden by models such as serializability or snapshot isolation. Jepsen's published analyses have found consistency violations in many widely used databases, often where documentation claimed stronger guarantees than the implementation provided. Reading a few of these analyses is one of the best ways to learn what consistency models mean in practice.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Back at the station, we can now name what went wrong: Bob's read began after Alice's completed but returned an older value, a linearizability violation caused by a side channel (Alice's voice). Had the app promised only causal consistency, the history would have been legal, because Bob's refresh did not causally depend on Alice's read within the system.

That is the deeper lesson. Consistency models are not a ranking from "good" to "bad". They are contracts, each with a price. The skill is to find the weakest model that your application's invariants and side channels can tolerate, because every step up the hierarchy costs latency, and the top steps cost availability during partitions. CAP tells you this trade-off exists during a partition; PACELC reminds you that you pay for consistency with latency every day.

Key terms

Consistency model, history, linearizability, side channel, sequential consistency, causal consistency, PRAM / FIFO consistency, session guarantees (RYW, MR, MW, WFR), eventual consistency, strong eventual consistency, sticky availability, CAP theorem, PACELC.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) State the definition of linearizability in one sentence.
  2. (Understand) Explain the difference between linearizability and sequential consistency using an example.
  3. (Understand) Which combination of session guarantees equals PRAM? Which equals causal consistency?
  4. (Apply) A user changes their email address and immediately receives a "verification sent to old address" message. Which guarantee was violated? Propose a fix.
  5. (Analyze) Explain why "choose CA" is not a meaningful option for a distributed database.
  6. (Analyze) A system serves reads from the nearest replica and writes through a global leader. Classify it with PACELC.
  7. (Evaluate) You are designing a system for allocating unique ticket numbers to passengers. Which consistency model do you need, and why? Which could you use for displaying seat availability?

Reflection

Pick one feature of a product you work on. Write down the weakest consistency model it could tolerate. Then think of a side channel (an email, a push notification, a person speaking) that could reveal an inconsistency to users.

Looking ahead

Linearizability and total order broadcast both need nodes to agree despite failures. Chapter 7 shows how Paxos and Raft achieve that agreement, the hardest and most important algorithm in this book.

Further reading

  • Jepsen, Consistency Models (jepsen.io/consistency). The interactive map and clear definitions of every model in this chapter.
  • Herlihy and Wing, Linearizability: A Correctness Condition for Concurrent Objects (1990).
  • Terry et al., Session Guarantees for Weakly Consistent Replicated Data (1994).
  • Brewer, CAP Twelve Years Later: How the "Rules" Have Changed (IEEE Computer, 2012).
  • Abadi, Consistency Tradeoffs in Modern Distributed Database System Design (IEEE Computer, 2012).
Chapter 07

Consensus, Leader Election and Coordination

How machines that may fail agree on anything at all

Leslie Lamport submitted the first paper describing the Paxos algorithm in 1990. It told the story of a fictional Greek island whose part-time parliament passed laws although legislators kept wandering in and out of the chamber. Reviewers found the allegory confusing, and the paper, The Part-Time Parliament, was not published until 1998. In 2001 Lamport wrote a shorter version, Paxos Made Simple, whose abstract reads in full: "The Paxos algorithm, when presented in plain English, is very simple."

Many engineers disagreed. In 2014 Diego Ongaro and John Ousterhout at Stanford published Raft, a consensus algorithm whose explicit design goal was understandability. They tested it on students: in a user study, students who learned both algorithms scored better on a Raft quiz than on a comparable Paxos quiz.

Consensus is the hardest algorithm in this book, and also the one on which most of the others depend. This chapter teaches it through Raft, with Paxos as the foundation underneath.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Define the consensus problem and explain its relationship to total order broadcast and state machine replication (Understand)
  • Describe single-decree Paxos and explain why its two phases guarantee safety (Understand)
  • Trace Raft's leader election and log replication, including the commit rule and the election restriction (Apply/Analyze)
  • Calculate the fault tolerance of a cluster and justify odd cluster sizes (Apply)
  • Design safe use of distributed locks with leases and fencing tokens (Create)
Essential question

Why is agreeing on a single value so hard that it took decades to find understandable algorithms for it?

Before you start

You already have all the pieces. From chapter 3: crash-recovery nodes, partial synchrony and the FLP result (safety always, liveness when the network behaves). From chapter 4: total order broadcast. From chapter 5: leaders, followers and the dangers of failover. From chapter 6: linearizability. Consensus is the machinery that ties them together.

The chapter in brief: the consensus problem; state machine replication; quorums; Paxos; Raft (elections, replication, safety); practical concerns; leases and fencing; coordination services.

The consensus problem

At a glance
  • Three properties that define consensus
  • Why it is equivalent to total order broadcast
  • State machine replication: the main application
Definition · Consensus

Several processes each propose a value, and they must decide on one value, such that:

  • Agreement: no two correct processes decide different values. (safety)
  • Validity: the decided value was proposed by some process. (safety)
  • Termination: every correct process eventually decides. (liveness)

Deciding a single value sounds modest. Its power comes from repetition. If nodes run consensus once for "what is the 1st command?", again for "what is the 2nd?", and so on, they obtain a replicated log: an identical sequence of commands on every node. That is exactly total order broadcast from chapter 4, and the two problems are equivalent: either can be built from the other.

State machine replication

A replicated log lets us replicate any deterministic service. Each replica starts in the same state, applies the same commands in the same order, and therefore ends in the same state. This is state machine replication, described by Lamport in 1978 and surveyed by Fred Schneider in 1990.

Almost every strongly consistent system in production is built this way: etcd and Consul (Raft), ZooKeeper (Zab, a Paxos-like protocol), Google's Chubby and Spanner (Paxos), Kafka's KRaft controller (Raft), CockroachDB and TiKV (Raft per data range).

Quorums and fault tolerance

At a glance
  • Majorities overlap, and that is the whole trick
  • How many failures a cluster survives
  • Why clusters have an odd number of nodes

Consensus algorithms for crash faults rely on majority quorums. A cluster of n nodes makes progress as long as a majority, ⌊n/2⌋ + 1 nodes, can communicate. Because any two majorities share at least one node, information from one decision is always visible to the next.

Cluster size Majority Crash failures tolerated
1 1 0
2 2 0
3 2 1
4 3 1
5 3 2
7 4 3

A 4-node cluster tolerates no more failures than a 3-node cluster, but needs one more vote for each decision and has one more machine to fail. That is why consensus clusters almost always have 3, 5 or 7 members. Larger clusters are rare, because every decision needs a majority of replies and becomes slower as the cluster grows.

Common mistake · Spreading two nodes per zone

Five consensus nodes placed as 2 + 2 + 1 across three Availability Zones survive the loss of any single zone, because at least three nodes remain. Six nodes placed as 3 + 3 across two zones survive no zone failure at all: losing either zone leaves only three of six, which is not a majority. Think in terms of which failure domains can fail, not only how many nodes.

Paxos

At a glance
  • Proposers, acceptors and learners
  • Two phases: prepare/promise and accept/accepted
  • Why the value of the highest-numbered accepted proposal must be reused

Paxos defines three roles. A single process often plays all three.

  • Proposers propose values.
  • Acceptors vote on proposals; a majority of acceptors constitutes a quorum.
  • Learners find out which value has been chosen.

Each proposal carries a proposal number that is unique and totally ordered, for instance a (counter, node ID) pair.

The two phases of single-decree Paxos

Phase 1: prepare and promise

  1. A proposer chooses a number n and sends Prepare(n) to a majority of acceptors.
  2. An acceptor that has not already responded to a Prepare with a number greater than n replies with a Promise: it will not accept any proposal numbered less than n. In the promise, it includes the highest-numbered proposal it has already accepted, if any.

Phase 2: accept and accepted 3. If the proposer receives promises from a majority, it sends Accept(n, v), where v is the value of the highest-numbered accepted proposal reported in the promises, or, only if none was reported, its own value. 4. An acceptor accepts the proposal unless it has since promised a higher number, and tells the learners. 5. A value is chosen once a majority of acceptors have accepted the same proposal.

Why it is safe

Step 3 is the heart of Paxos. Suppose value v was already chosen by proposal m: a majority of acceptors accepted (m, v). Any later proposer with a higher number must collect promises from a majority, and that majority overlaps the one that accepted v. So at least one promise reports an accepted proposal, and the proposer is obliged to propose the value of the highest-numbered one it hears about. One can show by induction that this value is v. Once a value is chosen, every later proposal carries that same value. Agreement holds regardless of message delays, crashes or how many proposers compete.

Why it might not terminate

Two proposers can duel forever: A prepares with 5, B prepares with 6 (invalidating A's), A retries with 7 (invalidating B's), and so on. This is the FLP result made concrete. The practical fix is to elect a distinguished proposer, a leader, and have others defer to it, which works whenever the network is stable enough for leader election to settle.

Multi-Paxos

Running both phases for every log entry is expensive. Multi-Paxos keeps a stable leader that runs phase 1 once for all future log slots, and then needs only phase 2, a single round trip to a majority, per command. This is the form of Paxos used in practice, and it looks a lot like Raft.

Test yourself

In phase 2, why may a proposer not simply propose its own value when some acceptor reported an already accepted value?

Show answer

Because that value may already have been chosen by a majority that the proposer cannot fully see. If the proposer substituted its own value, a second, different value could become chosen, violating agreement. Reusing the value of the highest-numbered accepted proposal guarantees that a chosen value is never replaced.

Raft: consensus designed for understanding

At a glance
  • Three roles and the concept of terms
  • Leader election with randomised timeouts
  • Log replication and the consistency check
  • The commit rule and the election restriction

Raft decomposes consensus into three relatively independent subproblems: leader election, log replication and safety. It is equivalent to (Multi-)Paxos in fault tolerance and performance, but its structure is easier to follow and to implement.

Roles and terms

Each node is in one of three states:

  • Follower: passive; responds to requests from leaders and candidates.
  • Candidate: trying to become leader.
  • Leader: handles all client requests and replicates the log.

Time is divided into terms, numbered with consecutive integers. Each term starts with an election. At most one leader can be elected per term. Every message carries its sender's term. A node that sees a higher term immediately updates its own term and, if it was a leader or candidate, steps down to follower. Messages with a lower term are rejected. Terms act as a logical clock that exposes stale leaders.

Leader election

The leader sends periodic heartbeats (empty AppendEntries messages) to all followers. If a follower hears nothing for an election timeout, it assumes the leader has failed and starts an election:

  1. It increments its current term, becomes a candidate and votes for itself.
  2. It sends RequestVote to all other nodes.
  3. Each node grants at most one vote per term, to the first candidate that asks, provided the candidate's log is at least as up to date as its own (the election restriction, explained below).
  4. A candidate that receives votes from a majority becomes leader and immediately sends heartbeats.

If two candidates split the vote, nobody wins; both time out and try again in a new term. To make split votes rare, each node picks its election timeout at random from an interval, for example 150–300 ms as suggested in the Raft paper. Usually one node times out first, wins, and suppresses the others with heartbeats before they time out.

Log replication

Once elected, the leader accepts client commands, appends each to its log as an entry tagged with the current term, and sends AppendEntries to followers. Each AppendEntries includes the index and term of the entry immediately before the new ones. A follower accepts only if its own log contains an entry with that index and term. Otherwise it rejects, and the leader retries with an earlier entry until it finds the point where the logs agree, then overwrites the follower's conflicting entries from there on.

This consistency check guarantees the Log Matching Property: if two logs contain an entry with the same index and term, the logs are identical up to and including that entry.

Commitment

An entry is committed when the leader that created it has replicated it on a majority of nodes. The leader then applies it to its state machine, replies to the client, and tells followers the new commit index in subsequent messages, so they apply it too. Committing an entry also commits all entries before it.

There is one subtle exception, discussed at length in the Raft paper: a leader counts replicas only for entries from its own current term. Entries from earlier terms become committed indirectly, once an entry of the current term after them is committed. Without this rule, an entry replicated on a majority could still be overwritten by a future leader in a specific sequence of crashes (figure 8 of the Raft paper).

The election restriction

How can a newly elected leader be sure to have every committed entry? Through the voting rule. A node refuses to vote for a candidate whose log is less up to date than its own, comparing first the term of the last entry and then, if equal, the log length. A committed entry is stored on a majority; a winning candidate needs votes from a majority; the two majorities overlap; and the overlapping node only votes for a candidate whose log is at least as complete. Therefore every leader holds all committed entries. This is Raft's Leader Completeness Property.

Example · A leader crash, step by step

Scenario: A five-node cluster S1–S5. S1 is leader in term 2 and appends entry 7 ("set seat 14C = Eva"). It reaches S2 and S3, so entry 7 is committed. Then S1 crashes.

Walkthrough:

  1. S4 times out first, starts term 3, and asks for votes. S4's log ends at entry 6.
  2. S2 and S3 refuse: their logs end with entry 7, which is more up to date. S5, whose log also ends at entry 6, votes for S4. S4 has 2 of 5 votes, not enough.
  3. S2 times out, starts term 4. S3, S4 and S5 grant their votes, since S2's log is at least as up to date as each of theirs. S2 becomes leader with entry 7.
  4. S2 replicates entry 7 to S4 and S5, along with new entries.

Key insight: The voting rule makes it impossible for a node lacking a committed entry to become leader. Safety comes from quorum overlap, not from timing.

What a node must persist

Before responding to any RPC, each Raft node must write to stable storage: its current term, the candidate it voted for in that term (if any), and its log entries. Forgetting any of these after a crash could let a node vote twice in a term or forget an entry it acknowledged, either of which breaks safety.

Test yourself

A three-node Raft cluster has nodes A (leader, term 5), B and C. The network partitions A from B and C. Describe what happens on both sides over the next few seconds, and what happens when the partition heals.

Show answer

B and C stop receiving heartbeats. One of them times out, starts term 6 and, with two of three votes, becomes leader. It commits new client writes. A still believes it is leader of term 5 and accepts client requests, but cannot get a majority to acknowledge them, so none of them commit; clients time out. When the partition heals, A receives a message with term 6, steps down to follower, and its uncommitted entries are overwritten by the new leader's log.

Practical concerns

At a glance
  • Membership changes and log compaction
  • Linearizable reads
  • Client retries and duplicate commands

A production consensus implementation needs more than the core algorithm.

  • Membership changes. Adding or removing nodes is dangerous: during the switch, an old majority and a new majority might each elect a leader. Raft's paper describes joint consensus, a transitional configuration requiring majorities of both; Ongaro's dissertation describes a simpler scheme that changes one server at a time.
  • Log compaction. The log cannot grow forever. Nodes periodically take a snapshot of their state machine and discard the log entries it covers. A leader sends its snapshot to followers that have fallen too far behind.
  • Linearizable reads. A leader that has been partitioned away may not know it was replaced, so a read from it may be stale. Options include sending the read through the log like a write; confirming leadership with a heartbeat round before answering (Raft's ReadIndex); or using time-based leader leases, which are faster but depend on bounded clock drift.
  • Duplicate commands. If a client's request times out and is retried, the command might be committed twice. Clients attach a unique ID and a sequence number to each request, and the state machine ignores duplicates (chapter 2's idempotency, again).

Leases, fencing and split brain

At a glance
  • Distributed locks are leases
  • How a paused process breaks a lock
  • Fencing tokens as the fix

Consensus systems are often used to implement distributed locks, for instance to ensure only one worker processes a given job. In a distributed setting, a lock must have an expiry; otherwise a crashed holder would keep it forever. A lock with an expiry is a lease (a concept introduced by Gray and Cheriton in 1989).

Leases are dangerous in combination with the process pauses from chapter 3.

Example · The lock that expired during a pause

Scenario: Client 1 acquires a lease on file F with a 10-second expiry. It begins writing and is paused by a 15-second garbage collection. The lease expires, and client 2 acquires it and writes to F. Client 1 resumes and, believing it still holds the lease, finishes its write, overwriting client 2's changes.

The fix: fencing tokens. Each time the lock service grants the lease, it returns a number that increases with every grant. Client 1 receives token 33, client 2 receives token 34. The storage service records the highest token it has seen and rejects writes with a lower token. When client 1 resumes and writes with token 33, the storage rejects it because it has already seen 34.

Key insight: The protected resource itself must check the tokens. A client cannot reliably determine whether its own lease is still valid, because it cannot detect its own pauses.

This example comes from Martin Kleppmann's 2016 analysis How to do distributed locking, which argued that the Redlock algorithm for Redis is unsafe for correctness purposes because it depends on timing assumptions and provides no fencing token. ZooKeeper's transaction IDs (zxid) and etcd's revision numbers can serve as fencing tokens.

Split brain, two nodes simultaneously acting as leader, is the same problem at the level of a whole cluster. Raft's terms are fencing tokens built into the protocol: an old leader's messages carry an old term and are rejected by any node that has seen the new one.

Coordination services

At a glance
  • Don't build consensus yourself
  • What ZooKeeper, etcd and Consul provide

Implementing consensus correctly is very hard. Most teams should use a coordination service rather than build one:

Service Protocol Typical uses
etcd Raft Kubernetes' cluster state; configuration; leader election
Apache ZooKeeper Zab Configuration and coordination for Hadoop, HBase, older Kafka versions
HashiCorp Consul Raft Service discovery, configuration, locks
Google Chubby Paxos Locks and small files inside Google (Burrows, 2006)

These services provide a small, strongly consistent key-value store with features designed for coordination: watches (notify me when this key changes), ephemeral keys (deleted when the session that created them ends, useful for membership and leader election), compare-and-swap operations and monotonically increasing revision numbers. They are optimised for small amounts of slowly changing data, not as general-purpose databases.

Note

If you operate Kubernetes or OpenShift, you already run a Raft cluster: every object you create is stored in etcd. Its health, its disk latency (every committed write is fsync'd to disk) and its quorum are critical to the whole platform. Monitoring etcd's leader changes and commit latency is a direct application of this chapter.

Lab · Implement Raft

Main lab (MIT 6.5840, labs 3A–3D): Implement Raft in Go following figure 2 of the extended Raft paper: leader election (3A), log replication (3B), persistence (3C) and log compaction (3D). The lab's test harness drops, delays and reorders messages and crashes nodes. Then build a fault-tolerant key/value service on top (lab 4).

Lighter alternative (Gossip Glomers challenge 5): Build a Kafka-style replicated log. Challenges 5b and 5c give you access to Maelstrom's linearizable key/value service (lin-kv), so you use consensus instead of implementing it.

Notebook prompts: Which test failed most often? Which property in figure 2 did the failing code violate? How long did an election take on average, and how did that depend on your timeout range?

In Appendix A: A.7 Raft (MIT 6.5840 labs 3A–3D and 4) (hints) · A.8 Kafka-style log (Gossip Glomers 5a–5c) (hints)

Deep dive Why Raft and Multi-Paxos are almost the same algorithm

Howard and Mortier's paper Paxos vs Raft: Have we reached consensus on distributed consensus? (2020) compared the two in detail and concluded that they differ mainly in how they elect leaders. In Multi-Paxos as usually described, any node can become leader and must then learn and re-propose possibly accepted values from other nodes during phase 1. Raft avoids this by allowing only candidates with up-to-date logs to win, so a new leader never needs to fetch entries from others. Raft's understandability comes largely from this design choice and from specifying the full system (membership, compaction, client interaction) in one place. For learning, this makes Raft the better first algorithm; for research, Paxos remains the more general framework, with many variants such as Fast Paxos, Flexible Paxos (which shows that phase 1 and phase 2 quorums only need to intersect each other) and EPaxos.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Why did understandable consensus take so long? Because consensus must hold up against every possible interleaving of crashes, restarts, delays and competing leaders, and intuition is poor at enumerating interleavings. Both Paxos and Raft rest on one simple fact: any two majorities overlap. Paxos uses that overlap to carry a possibly chosen value forward to the next proposal; Raft uses it to ensure that only a node holding every committed entry can win an election. Timing (heartbeats, election timeouts) affects only when progress happens, never whether the result is correct, which is exactly the separation of safety and liveness that FLP forced on us in chapter 3.

Leases and fencing tokens extend the same idea to the outside world: authority must carry a monotonically increasing number that the protected resource checks, because no process can be sure its own authority has not silently expired.

Key terms

Consensus, agreement / validity / termination, replicated log, state machine replication, majority quorum, proposer / acceptor / learner, proposal number, Multi-Paxos, term, follower / candidate / leader, heartbeat, election timeout, AppendEntries / RequestVote, Log Matching Property, commit index, election restriction, Leader Completeness, joint consensus, snapshot, ReadIndex, lease, fencing token, split brain, coordination service.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) State the three properties of consensus and classify each as safety or liveness.
  2. (Understand) Explain why consensus and total order broadcast are equivalent.
  3. (Understand) Why does Raft randomise election timeouts?
  4. (Apply) How many simultaneous node failures can a 7-node Raft cluster tolerate? What if the 7 nodes are spread 3 + 3 + 1 across three zones and the zone holding 3 nodes fails?
  5. (Analyze) Explain how the election restriction and quorum overlap together guarantee that a new leader holds all committed entries.
  6. (Analyze) Why must a Raft node persist votedFor before replying to a RequestVote?
  7. (Create) Design a job scheduler in which at most one worker processes each job at a time, using etcd. Explain where the fencing token comes from and who checks it.

Reflection

Where in your systems does "only one" matter: one leader, one active instance, one owner of a resource? How is that "only one" enforced today, and what would happen during a 30-second process pause?

Looking ahead

Consensus lets a group of replicas agree. But many operations span different groups: a transfer between two accounts stored on two shards, an order that touches inventory and payments. Chapter 8 tackles transactions across machines.

Further reading

  • Ongaro and Ousterhout, In Search of an Understandable Consensus Algorithm (extended version, 2014). Figure 2 is the most useful single page in this field.
  • Lamport, Paxos Made Simple (2001).
  • The Raft website (raft.github.io), with an interactive visualisation of elections and replication.
  • Kleppmann, How to do distributed locking (2016).
  • Howard and Mortier, Paxos vs Raft (PaPoC 2020).
Chapter 08

Transactions Across Machines

Isolation, atomic commit, and when to use sagas instead

Two doctors are on call at a hospital, and a rule says at least one doctor must be on call at all times. Both feel unwell on the same evening. At almost the same moment, each opens the scheduling app and requests to go off call. Each app runs a transaction:

  1. Count the doctors currently on call. (Result: 2.)
  2. Since 2 ≥ 2, it is safe for me to leave. Mark me as off call.
  3. Commit.

Both transactions succeed. Now nobody is on call.

The database was not broken. It ran at snapshot isolation, a widely used isolation level (PostgreSQL offers it as REPEATABLE READ, and Oracle under the name SERIALIZABLE), and each transaction saw a consistent snapshot in which two doctors were on call. No row was written by both transactions, so no conflict was detected. This anomaly, called write skew, is described in Kleppmann's Designing Data-Intensive Applications and has bitten many real systems. It happens on a single machine. Spread the data across several machines and things get harder still.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Explain the ACID properties and distinguish C in ACID from C in CAP (Understand)
  • Identify dirty reads, lost updates, read skew, write skew and phantoms in concrete histories (Analyze)
  • Compare two-phase locking, optimistic concurrency control and serializable snapshot isolation (Analyze)
  • Describe two-phase commit and explain why it blocks when the coordinator fails (Understand)
  • Design a saga with compensating actions and an outbox for a multi-service workflow (Create)
Essential question

When an operation spans several machines, is it better to make it atomic at great cost, or to accept that it is not atomic and design for that?

Before you start

Chapter 6 discussed consistency for single objects. Transactions extend the question to groups of operations on many objects. Chapter 7 gave us consensus, which we will need to make distributed transactions survive a failed coordinator.

The chapter in brief: ACID; isolation anomalies; snapshot isolation; serializability and how to achieve it; optimistic concurrency; two-phase commit; consensus-backed commit; sagas; the outbox pattern.

ACID, briefly and precisely

At a glance
  • Four properties, each with a precise meaning
  • Which ones become hard when data is distributed

The acronym ACID was coined by Theo Härder and Andreas Reuter in 1983.

Property Meaning Distributed difficulty
Atomicity All of a transaction's writes take effect, or none do. (It is about abort, not concurrency.) Needs atomic commit across nodes: all participants must reach the same decision
Consistency The transaction moves the database from one state satisfying the application's invariants to another Mostly the application's responsibility
Isolation Concurrent transactions do not interfere; ideally they behave as if run one at a time Needs coordination of concurrent access across nodes
Durability Committed data is not lost Needs replication (chapter 5)
Common mistake · Two different C's

The C in ACID ("the application's invariants hold") and the C in CAP ("linearizability") are unrelated concepts that share a letter. Treat them as different words.

Isolation anomalies

At a glance
  • Five anomalies that weaker isolation levels allow
  • Why "read committed" is not enough

Full isolation is expensive, so databases offer weaker isolation levels that permit some anomalies. To choose a level, you need to recognise the anomalies.

Anomaly What happens Example
Dirty read A transaction reads data written by another transaction that has not yet committed (and may abort) A report includes an order that is later rolled back
Lost update Two transactions read a value, modify it and write it back; one overwrites the other's change Two increments of a counter produce +1 instead of +2
Read skew (non-repeatable read) A transaction reads related data at different points in time and sees an inconsistent combination A backup sees account A before a transfer and account B after it, so money appears to vanish
Write skew Two transactions read overlapping data, then write different objects based on what they read, together violating an invariant The two doctors
Phantom A transaction's search condition matches a different set of rows when re-evaluated, because another transaction inserted or deleted rows Two bookings for the same meeting room and time slot both succeed

Read committed, the default in PostgreSQL, Oracle and SQL Server, prevents dirty reads and dirty writes but allows lost updates, read skew and write skew.

Snapshot isolation

At a glance
  • Each transaction reads a consistent snapshot
  • First-committer-wins prevents lost updates on the same row
  • Write skew remains possible
Definition · Snapshot isolation (SI)

Each transaction reads from a consistent snapshot of the database taken at its start, so it never sees partial results of other transactions. When two concurrent transactions write the same object, only one may commit; the other aborts (the "first-committer-wins" or "first-updater-wins" rule).

Snapshot isolation was characterised by Berenson and colleagues in A Critique of ANSI SQL Isolation Levels (1995). It is typically implemented with multi-version concurrency control (MVCC): the database keeps several versions of each row, tagged with transaction IDs, and each transaction sees only versions committed before it started.

SI prevents dirty reads, read skew and lost updates on the same row. It is popular because readers never block writers, and writers never block readers. PostgreSQL's REPEATABLE READ level and Oracle's SERIALIZABLE level are both, in fact, snapshot isolation.

But SI is not serializable. The doctors' transactions wrote different rows (each doctor's own record), so there was no write-write conflict, and both committed. The invariant depended on rows that each transaction read but did not write.

Test yourself

Under snapshot isolation, how could the on-call application prevent write skew without changing the isolation level?

Show answer

Make the conflict explicit. Options: lock the rows being read with SELECT … FOR UPDATE, so the second transaction waits for the first; or materialise the conflict, for example by updating a shared "shift" row in both transactions, so they now write the same object and SI detects the conflict; or enforce the invariant with a database constraint or trigger. The cleanest option, where available, is to use SERIALIZABLE isolation.

Serializability and how to get it

At a glance
  • The gold standard of isolation
  • Three implementation strategies and their trade-offs
Definition · Serializability

The outcome of executing transactions concurrently is the same as if they had executed one at a time, in some order. Strict serializability additionally requires that order to respect real time, combining serializability with linearizability.

There are three main ways to implement it:

1. Actual serial execution

Run transactions one at a time on a single thread. This is feasible when transactions are short and the active data fits in memory. VoltDB and Redis's Lua scripts work this way. It scales by partitioning, as long as most transactions touch only one partition.

2. Two-phase locking (2PL)

A transaction acquires shared locks for reads and exclusive locks for writes, and holds all of them until it commits or aborts. To prevent phantoms, it also locks ranges or predicates (for example "all bookings for room 4 on Friday"). 2PL is correct but can perform badly: readers block writers, writers block readers, and deadlocks must be detected and broken by aborting a transaction.

3. Optimistic concurrency control (OCC) and serializable snapshot isolation (SSI)

Optimistic approaches let transactions run without blocking, and check for conflicts at commit time. If a conflict is detected, the transaction aborts and retries.

Serializable snapshot isolation, introduced by Cahill, Röhm and Fekete in 2008, extends SI by tracking which transactions read data that others later wrote. If a pattern of read-write dependencies could produce a non-serializable result, one transaction is aborted. PostgreSQL has implemented SSI as its SERIALIZABLE level since version 9.1 (2011). In the doctors' example, SSI would abort the second commit.

Approach Blocking Best when Cost
Serial execution Total, but transactions are tiny Short in-memory transactions, single partition Limited to one core per partition
2PL Readers and writers block each other High contention, long transactions where aborts are costly Deadlocks, latency spikes
OCC / SSI None during execution Low contention Wasted work on abort under contention

Optimistic concurrency at the application level

The same idea appears in APIs as conditional writes: read a record with its version number, and write it back only if the version is unchanged (for instance UPDATE … SET balance = 70, version = 8 WHERE id = 42 AND version = 7, or HTTP's If-Match header with an ETag). If zero rows were updated, someone else got there first; re-read and retry. This is the easiest way to prevent lost updates in a distributed application, and it is safe to retry.

Atomic commit and two-phase commit

At a glance
  • Getting several nodes to commit or abort together
  • How 2PC works
  • Why it blocks, and why that matters

When a transaction writes to several nodes (shards of one database, or different databases), all of them must commit or all must abort. This is the atomic commit problem. The classic solution is two-phase commit (2PC).

How 2PC works

A coordinator (often the node or library that started the transaction) drives the protocol with all participants.

  1. Prepare phase. The coordinator sends Prepare to every participant. Each participant ensures it can commit: it writes all the transaction's data and a "prepared" record durably to disk, and checks constraints. If so it votes Yes, which is a promise: from now on it will commit if told to, even after a crash. Otherwise it votes No.
  2. Commit phase. If all voted Yes, the coordinator writes Commit to its own durable log; this is the commit point. It then sends Commit to all participants. If any voted No or did not respond, it decides Abort and sends that.

The blocking problem

Consider a participant that has voted Yes. It must not commit on its own (another participant might have voted No) and must not abort on its own (the coordinator might already have decided Commit and told others). It is in doubt. If the coordinator crashes at this moment, the participant must wait, holding all its locks, until the coordinator recovers and reads its log. Other transactions that need those rows are blocked too.

This is why 2PC is called a blocking protocol, and why the coordinator's availability matters so much. A coordinator that is down for an hour can leave rows locked for an hour.

Common mistake · Treating XA as free

Distributed transactions across heterogeneous systems, such as the X/Open XA standard used by Java application servers to span a database and a message broker, use 2PC. They are useful but costly: the coordinator is often a library inside an application server, so a crashed application can leave "in-doubt" transactions holding locks in your database until an operator resolves them manually. Use them knowingly, with monitoring for in-doubt transactions.

Making the coordinator fault-tolerant

The way out is to remove the single coordinator as a point of failure. Replicate the coordinator's decision with consensus, so that if the coordinator crashes, another node can read the decision from the replicated log and complete the protocol. Gray and Lamport's Consensus on Transaction Commit (2006) describes Paxos Commit. In practice, Google Spanner runs 2PC between participants, each of which is a Paxos-replicated group, so neither the coordinator nor any participant is a single machine. CockroachDB and similar databases use comparable designs on top of Raft.

Three-phase commit (3PC), proposed by Dale Skeen in 1981, adds a pre-commit phase to avoid blocking, but it is only safe in a synchronous network model with bounded delays and no partitions, so it is rarely used in practice.

Test yourself

In 2PC, why must the coordinator write its decision to durable storage before sending Commit to any participant?

Show answer

If it sent Commit to some participants and then crashed without having recorded the decision, it might decide Abort after restarting (for example because it never heard from one participant). Then some participants would commit and others abort, violating atomicity. The durable log entry is the single point at which the decision becomes irrevocable.

Sagas: giving up on atomicity, deliberately

At a glance
  • Long-running workflows across services
  • Compensating actions instead of rollback
  • Orchestration and choreography

Two-phase commit is a poor fit when a workflow spans independent services owned by different teams, takes seconds or days, or involves external systems (a payment provider, an email). Holding locks across all of them is unacceptable, and many systems do not support a prepare phase at all.

The alternative is a saga, a term introduced by Hector Garcia-Molina and Kenneth Salem in 1987.

Definition · Saga

A sequence of local transactions T1, T2, …, Tn, each committed independently in its own service. For each step Ti there is a compensating transaction Ci that semantically undoes it. If step Tk fails, the saga runs Ck−1, …, C1 in reverse order.

Example · Booking a journey

Saga: T1 reserve the train seat → T2 reserve the hotel room → T3 charge the card → T4 send the confirmation.

Failure: the card is declined at T3.

Compensation: C2 cancel the hotel reservation → C1 release the train seat. The customer receives a "payment failed" message.

Key insight: Compensation is semantic, not a physical rollback. The seat was briefly reserved and other customers may have seen it as unavailable. Some actions cannot be compensated at all (an email that was sent), so they are placed last, after every step that might fail.

Sagas give atomicity eventually but no isolation: other transactions can observe intermediate states. Designers handle this with techniques such as marking records as "pending", using commutative updates, or re-reading values before acting on them.

Orchestration or choreography?

  • Orchestration: a central saga orchestrator tells each service what to do next and tracks state. Easier to understand, monitor and change; the orchestrator must itself be durable (workflow engines such as Temporal or AWS Step Functions provide this).
  • Choreography: each service listens for events and reacts ("SeatReserved" triggers the hotel service). No central component, but the overall flow is spread across many services and harder to follow.

The outbox pattern: reliable events without 2PC

At a glance
  • The dual-write problem
  • Writing the event into the same database transaction

Many workflows need to update a database and publish an event ("order created"). Doing both separately is a dual write: if the service crashes between the two, either the database has the order but no event was published, or the event was published for an order that was never saved.

The transactional outbox pattern avoids 2PC:

  1. In the same local database transaction that creates the order, insert a row into an outbox table describing the event.
  2. A separate relay process reads new outbox rows (by polling, or by tailing the database's change log with change data capture tools such as Debezium) and publishes them to the message broker.
  3. Once publishing succeeds, it marks the rows as sent.

The relay may publish an event more than once (it can crash after publishing but before marking the row), so consumers must be idempotent, typically by recording processed event IDs, the effectively-once processing of chapter 2.

Lab · Gossip Glomers challenge 6: totally-available transactions
  1. Complete challenge 6a (Single-Node, Totally-Available Transactions): process transactions consisting of reads and writes on a key/value store.
  2. Continue with 6b (Read Uncommitted) and 6c (Read Committed) across multiple nodes, with network partitions. Maelstrom's checker (Elle) verifies the isolation level.
  3. Notebook: Which anomalies does your implementation in 6c still allow? Construct a history showing one of them. Why is "totally available" incompatible with serializability?

In Appendix A: A.9 Totally available transactions (Gossip Glomers 6a–6c) (hints)

Deep dive Deterministic databases and the Calvin approach

2PC exists because participants may disagree about whether a transaction can commit, for instance due to a lock conflict or a crash. Deterministic database systems such as Calvin (Thomson et al., SIGMOD 2012) take a different route: first agree on a global order of transactions using a replicated log (consensus), then have every partition execute the transactions in exactly that order deterministically. Because every replica reaches the same outcome independently, no commit protocol is required for non-deterministic failures. The trade-off is that transactions must declare their read and write sets in advance, or discover them in a reconnaissance step. FaunaDB was inspired by Calvin. The idea illustrates a recurring theme of this book: total order plus determinism replaces coordination at commit time.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Back to the doctors. Their problem was not missing locks or a broken database. It was an invariant spanning rows that each transaction read but did not write. Snapshot isolation could not see it; serializability (via SSI or 2PL) or an explicitly materialised conflict could. Across machines, the same choice reappears at a larger scale: you can enforce invariants with atomic commit (2PC, preferably on top of consensus) and pay with latency and blocking, or you can relax atomicity into a saga and pay with visible intermediate states and compensation logic.

So the answer to the essential question is: atomicity where invariants demand it, sagas where the business process tolerates intermediate states. A bank balance and a meeting-room booking need the former; a travel booking across independent partners is usually best served by the latter.

Key terms

ACID, atomicity, isolation level, dirty read, lost update, read skew, write skew, phantom, read committed, snapshot isolation, MVCC, serializability, strict serializability, two-phase locking, optimistic concurrency control, serializable snapshot isolation (SSI), conditional write, atomic commit, two-phase commit, in-doubt transaction, XA, three-phase commit, saga, compensating transaction, orchestration / choreography, dual write, transactional outbox.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the five isolation anomalies in this chapter and give a one-line example of each.
  2. (Understand) Explain why snapshot isolation prevents lost updates on a single row but not write skew.
  3. (Understand) What is an in-doubt transaction, and why can it not resolve itself?
  4. (Apply) Rewrite an increment of a stock count as a conditional write that is safe under concurrency and retries.
  5. (Analyze) A meeting-room booking system checks "no overlapping booking exists" and then inserts a booking. Which anomaly can occur under snapshot isolation, and what are two ways to prevent it?
  6. (Evaluate) Compare 2PC and a saga for an order flow spanning inventory, payment and shipping services owned by different teams. Which would you choose and why?
  7. (Create) Design a saga for cancelling a train ticket that was paid by card and included a seat reservation and a meal order. Specify each step, its compensation, and which step must come last.

Reflection

Find a place in a system you know where a service writes to its database and then publishes a message or calls another service. What happens if it crashes between the two?

Looking ahead

Consensus and atomic commit both require coordination, and coordination costs latency and availability. Chapter 9 asks the opposite question: which problems can be solved without any coordination at all?

Further reading

  • Kleppmann and Riccomini, Designing Data-Intensive Applications (2nd ed.), the chapter on transactions. The doctors' example and much more.
  • Berenson et al., A Critique of ANSI SQL Isolation Levels (SIGMOD 1995).
  • Garcia-Molina and Salem, Sagas (SIGMOD 1987).
  • Gray and Lamport, Consensus on Transaction Commit (ACM TODS, 2006).
Chapter 09

Convergence Without Coordination

Gossip protocols, CRDTs and the CALM theorem

Here is a paradox. The last three chapters showed that agreement is expensive: it needs majorities, round trips and a stable leader, and it stops entirely on the minority side of a partition. Yet millions of people edit shared documents offline on trains and planes, and when they reconnect, everyone's changes merge into the same result without any server deciding the order.

How can replicas that never coordinate end up identical? The answer is that agreement on order is only necessary when order affects the result. If you design your data so that order doesn't matter, replicas can accept every update immediately, exchange updates in any way they like, and still converge. This chapter is about the techniques that make this possible: gossip protocols that spread information like rumours, and data types that merge like sets.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Explain how gossip protocols spread information and estimate their convergence time (Understand/Apply)
  • Describe how SWIM detects failures and maintains group membership (Understand)
  • Define strong eventual consistency and the algebraic properties a merge function needs (Understand)
  • Implement a G-counter and a PN-counter and trace their merges (Apply)
  • Evaluate whether a given application can avoid coordination, using the CALM principle (Evaluate)
Essential question

Which problems truly require replicas to agree on an order, and which only seem to?

Before you start

In chapter 6 you met strong eventual consistency: replicas that have received the same updates are in the same state. In chapter 5 you saw multi-leader and leaderless replication struggle with conflicts. This chapter shows how to make conflicts impossible by construction.

The chapter in brief: gossip and epidemics; anti-entropy; SWIM membership; CRDTs and their properties; a catalogue of CRDTs; collaborative text; the CALM theorem.

Gossip: spreading information like a rumour

At a glance
  • Each node periodically talks to a few random peers
  • Information reaches everyone in about log n rounds
  • Robust, simple and decentralised

Imagine you tell a rumour to one random colleague each minute, and everyone who has heard it does the same. After one minute two people know, after two minutes about four, then eight. In an office of 1,000 people, the rumour reaches almost everyone in about ten minutes, because 2¹⁰ ≈ 1,000, even though nobody coordinated anything and some conversations happened twice.

Gossip protocols (also called epidemic protocols, after Demers et al.'s 1987 paper Epidemic Algorithms for Replicated Database Maintenance at Xerox PARC) work the same way.

Definition · Gossip protocol

In each round, every node selects one or a few peers at random and exchanges information with them. Three variants exist:

  • Push: send what you know to the peer.
  • Pull: ask the peer what it knows.
  • Push-pull: both, in one exchange. Converges fastest.

Gossip's properties make it popular for large clusters:

  • Fast: information reaches all n nodes in O(log n) rounds with high probability.
  • Robust: there is no single point of failure; lost messages and crashed nodes only slow propagation slightly.
  • Scalable: each node sends a constant number of messages per round, regardless of cluster size.
  • Eventually consistent: at any moment, some nodes may not yet know the latest information.

Systems that use gossip include Apache Cassandra (cluster membership and schema), HashiCorp Consul and Serf (membership), Amazon's Dynamo (membership and failure detection), and Redis Cluster (node state).

Test yourself

A cluster has 4,096 nodes and runs push-pull gossip with one peer per round, every 200 ms. Roughly how long until a new piece of information reaches nearly all nodes?

Show answer

log₂(4,096) = 12, so on the order of 12–20 rounds, considering randomness and the final "stragglers". At 200 ms per round that is roughly 2.5–4 seconds. The logarithm is what makes gossip scale: doubling the cluster adds only one round.

Anti-entropy: repairing differences

At a glance
  • Periodic background comparison of replicas
  • Merkle trees make comparison cheap

Gossip spreads new information. Anti-entropy repairs differences that remain, for example after a node was down. Two replicas periodically compare their data and exchange whatever the other is missing.

Comparing every key would be prohibitively expensive, so systems compare Merkle trees: hash trees in which each leaf is the hash of a range of keys and each internal node is the hash of its children. Two replicas compare root hashes; if they match, the data is identical. If not, they descend only into subtrees whose hashes differ. A single differing key out of millions is located in a number of steps proportional to the depth of the tree. Dynamo, Cassandra and Riak all use this technique.

SWIM: failure detection and membership at scale

At a glance
  • Why all-to-all heartbeats do not scale
  • Indirect probing reduces false suspicions
  • Piggybacking membership updates on probe messages

In chapter 3 we met heartbeat-based failure detectors. If every node sends heartbeats to every other node, a cluster of n nodes sends n² messages per period, which does not scale to thousands of nodes. SWIM (Scalable Weakly-consistent Infection-style process group Membership, Das, Gupta and Motivala, 2002) solves this:

  1. Probe. Every period, each node pings one randomly chosen member.
  2. Indirect probe. If there is no acknowledgement, the node asks k other members to ping the target on its behalf. This distinguishes "the target is down" from "the link between me and the target is bad".
  3. Suspect, then confirm. If indirect probes also fail, the target is marked suspect rather than dead, giving it time to refute the suspicion. Only after a timeout is it declared failed.
  4. Disseminate by gossip. Membership changes (joins, suspicions, failures) are piggybacked on the ping and acknowledgement messages, so they spread epidemically without extra traffic.

HashiCorp's memberlist library, which underlies Consul and Serf, implements SWIM with extensions to reduce false positives further. Note that the "weakly consistent" in SWIM's name is honest: at any moment, different nodes may hold slightly different membership lists.

CRDTs: data types that merge themselves

At a glance
  • The problem: concurrent updates without coordination
  • Strong eventual consistency
  • The algebraic recipe: commutative, associative, idempotent

Gossip and anti-entropy deliver updates to every replica eventually, in arbitrary order, perhaps more than once. For replicas to end in the same state, applying updates must give the same result regardless of order and duplication. Data types designed with this property are called CRDTs, conflict-free replicated data types, formalised by Marc Shapiro and colleagues in 2011.

Definition · Strong eventual consistency (SEC)

Any two replicas that have received the same set of updates are in the same state, regardless of the order in which they received them, and without any coordination at update time.

There are two styles of CRDT:

  • State-based CRDTs (CvRDTs): replicas periodically send their whole state to each other, and the receiver merges it into its own. The merge function must be:

    • commutative: merge(a, b) = merge(b, a), so order doesn't matter;
    • associative: merge(merge(a, b), c) = merge(a, merge(b, c)), so grouping doesn't matter;
    • idempotent: merge(a, a) = a, so duplicates don't matter.

    Together, these make the set of states a mathematical structure called a join-semilattice, and merge computes the least upper bound. In plain words: states only ever "grow", and merging takes the smallest state that includes both.

  • Operation-based CRDTs (CmRDTs): replicas send operations instead of states. Concurrent operations must commute, and the delivery layer must ensure each operation is delivered exactly once and (usually) in causal order.

State-based CRDTs work over the messiest networks, including gossip with duplicates, because merge is idempotent. Operation-based CRDTs send less data but need more from the network.

A catalogue of CRDTs

At a glance
  • Counters, sets and registers
  • How each one achieves convergence

G-counter (grow-only counter)

You cannot replicate a counter as a single integer: if two replicas each increment 5 to 6 and then merge, the result should be 7, but "max" gives 6 and "sum" double-counts on every repeated merge.

The G-counter keeps one entry per replica. Each replica only ever increments its own entry. The value is the sum of all entries. Merge takes the element-wise maximum, which is commutative, associative and idempotent.

Example · G-counter merge, step by step

Three replicas A, B and C start with [0, 0, 0] (entries for A, B, C).

  1. A increments twice: A = [2, 0, 0].
  2. B increments once: B = [0, 1, 0].
  3. A and B gossip: both become max([2,0,0], [0,1,0]) = [2, 1, 0]. Value 3.
  4. C increments three times: C = [0, 0, 3].
  5. B and C gossip: both become [2, 1, 3]. Value 6.
  6. A receives B's state twice (a duplicate): max([2,1,0], [2,1,3]) = [2, 1, 3], and again = [2, 1, 3]. Value 6.

Key insight: The duplicate in step 6 changed nothing. The G-counter is the vector clock of chapter 4 used as a data type.

PN-counter (positive-negative counter)

To support decrements, combine two G-counters: P for increments and N for decrements. Value = sum(P) − sum(N). Merge each G-counter separately. Used for things like "likes" and inventory reservations that can be cancelled.

Sets

CRDT Operations Behaviour on concurrent add and remove
G-set (grow-only) add Removal not supported. Merge = union.
2P-set (two-phase) add, remove An element, once removed, can never be added again (it is kept in a "removed" set forever)
OR-set (observed-remove) add, remove Add wins: a remove only deletes the specific additions it has observed; a concurrent add survives

The OR-set is the most practical. Each add is tagged with a unique ID; a remove deletes only the tags it has seen. If Alice removes "milk" from a shared shopping list while Bob, offline, adds "milk" again, Bob's addition has a new tag that Alice's remove never saw, so milk stays on the list. That matches what most users expect.

Registers

A register holds a single value. A LWW-register (last-writer-wins) keeps the value with the highest timestamp, which converges but discards concurrent writes, as chapter 4 warned. A multi-value register (MV-register) keeps all concurrent values, tracked with vector clocks, and lets the application or user choose, which is what Dynamo did for shopping carts.

Test yourself

A shared to-do list is modelled as an OR-set. Replica 1 removes "buy tickets"; at the same time, replica 2 renames the item by removing "buy tickets" and adding "buy tickets for Friday". After merging, what does the list contain?

Show answer

"buy tickets for Friday". Both replicas removed the original tag of "buy tickets", so it is gone. The new item has a fresh tag that replica 1's remove never observed, so it survives. Note that an OR-set has no concept of "rename": modelling a rename as remove-plus-add is a design choice with consequences, such as a concurrent edit of the old item being lost.

Collaborative text editing

At a glance
  • Sequence CRDTs for text
  • Real-world libraries
  • An alternative: operational transformation

Text is the hardest common case: users insert and delete characters at positions, and positions shift as others edit. Two families of algorithms solve it.

  • Operational transformation (OT) transforms each incoming operation against concurrent ones, adjusting positions. It was used by Google Docs and earlier collaborative editors. Most practical OT systems rely on a central server to order operations.
  • Sequence CRDTs (RGA, Logoot, YATA and others) give every character a unique, immutable identifier that encodes its position relative to its neighbours. Inserts and deletes refer to identifiers, not indices, so concurrent edits commute. No central server is needed. Libraries such as Automerge (co-developed by Martin Kleppmann) and Yjs implement sequence CRDTs and are used in production collaborative and local-first applications.

The movement known as local-first software (Kleppmann, Wiggins, van Hardenberg and McGranaghan, 2019) builds on CRDTs to make applications that work fully offline, sync peer-to-peer or through a server, and keep users' data on their own devices.

The CALM theorem: when coordination is needed

At a glance
  • Monotonic programs need no coordination
  • Non-monotonic decisions do
  • A practical test for your own designs

When exactly can we avoid coordination? Joseph Hellerstein and Peter Alvaro gave a crisp answer in Keeping CALM: When Distributed Consistency Is Easy (CACM, 2020), based on earlier work by Hellerstein and by Ameloot and colleagues.

Definition · CALM (Consistency As Logical Monotonicity)

A problem has a consistent, coordination-free distributed implementation if and only if it is monotonic: new information can only add to the conclusions, never retract them.

  • Monotonic (no coordination needed): "Is this item in the set?" when answered yes (more information never turns yes into no); counting likes; collecting log events; the union of sets; reachability in a graph.
  • Non-monotonic (coordination needed): "Is this item not in the set?"; "Is this the last seat?"; "Has everyone voted?"; garbage collection; enforcing "at most one" or "at least one" invariants.

The test is simple: could a future message change my answer from yes to no? If so, you must wait until you know no such message will arrive, which means coordination. If not, you can answer now.

Example · Applying CALM to train seat booking

Monotonic parts: recording booking requests; collecting payments; aggregating sales statistics; listing which seats have been booked. These can use CRDTs and gossip, and stay available during partitions.

Non-monotonic part: deciding that a seat is still free and giving it to this passenger. Another replica could concurrently give away the same seat, and that would change the answer. This requires coordination: a consensus-backed seat owner, a partition per train with a single leader, or an escrow scheme that pre-allocates disjoint seat ranges to each replica.

Key insight: You rarely need coordination for the whole system. CALM lets you find the narrow non-monotonic core and coordinate only there.

Lab · Gossip Glomers challenge 4: grow-only counter
  1. Complete challenge 4 (Grow-Only Counter). Nodes receive add requests and must answer read with the total. The challenge provides Maelstrom's sequentially consistent key/value service (seq-kv).
  2. Then implement a version without the key/value service: give each node a G-counter, gossip states periodically, and merge with element-wise maximum. Run it with --nemesis partition.
  3. Notebook: Why does your gossiped G-counter remain correct when messages are duplicated or reordered? Which of the three algebraic properties protects you from which network behaviour?

In Appendix A: A.10 Grow-only counter, two ways (hints)

Deep dive Delta-state CRDTs and the metadata problem

State-based CRDTs are robust, but shipping the entire state on every sync becomes expensive as state grows, and many CRDTs accumulate metadata: an OR-set keeps tombstones or causal context for removed elements, and sequence CRDTs keep identifiers for deleted characters. Two research threads address this. Delta-state CRDTs (Almeida, Shoker and Baquero, 2015) send only the change (a "delta" that is itself a valid state) since the last sync, while retaining idempotent merge. Metadata garbage collection removes tombstones once all replicas are known to have seen a removal, but knowing that "all replicas have seen it" is non-monotonic, so CALM predicts that this step needs coordination, and it does. A large part of building practical CRDT systems is managing this tension.

Synthesis

Synthesis

The paradox from the opening dissolves once you separate delivering updates from ordering them. Gossip delivers everything to everyone, eventually, with no coordination. CRDTs ensure that the order of delivery doesn't matter, because their merge is commutative, associative and idempotent. Together they give strong eventual consistency with full availability during partitions.

CALM tells us the price of admission: the application's questions must be monotonic. The design skill is to push as much of a system as possible into monotonic form and coordinate only where a "no" or a "last one" must be decided. Most real systems are therefore hybrids: consensus for a small, critical core, and coordination-free convergence everywhere else.

Key terms

Gossip / epidemic protocol, push / pull / push-pull, anti-entropy, Merkle tree, SWIM, indirect probe, strong eventual consistency, CRDT, state-based (CvRDT) / operation-based (CmRDT), commutative / associative / idempotent, join-semilattice, G-counter, PN-counter, G-set, 2P-set, OR-set, LWW-register, MV-register, operational transformation, sequence CRDT, local-first software, CALM, monotonic / non-monotonic.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the three algebraic properties a state-based CRDT's merge function needs.
  2. (Understand) Why does gossip converge in O(log n) rounds?
  3. (Understand) Explain how SWIM's indirect probing reduces false failure detections.
  4. (Apply) Two replicas of a PN-counter hold P = [3, 1], N = [0, 2] and P = [2, 4], N = [1, 0]. What is the merged state and its value?
  5. (Analyze) For each of the following, decide whether it is monotonic: (a) "has user U ever logged in?", (b) "is user U currently logged in?", (c) "how many messages has the system received?", (d) "has every node acknowledged configuration version 7?"
  6. (Evaluate) A team wants to build an inventory system on CRDTs to stay available during partitions. Evaluate where this works and where it would allow overselling. Propose a design.

Reflection

Think of a feature in a product you know that requires a central server today. Could it be redesigned so that most of it is monotonic? What would remain that truly needs coordination?

Looking ahead

Part II gave us the theory of data and agreement. Part III turns to building and running real systems. Chapter 10 starts with the architecture patterns used to scale services: load balancers, queues, fan-out, caches and actors.

Further reading

  • Shapiro et al., Conflict-free Replicated Data Types (SSS 2011) and the longer technical report A comprehensive study of Convergent and Commutative Replicated Data Types (INRIA, 2011).
  • Das, Gupta and Motivala, SWIM (DSN 2002).
