Part III · Building and running systems
Part III · Introduction

Surviving Production

Why scaling, resilience and observability decide what users actually experience

A design that is correct on a whiteboard still has to survive a Monday morning: a traffic spike when a storm cancels half the trains, a dependency that becomes slow instead of failing, a database failover at the worst possible moment, and a cache that empties just as everyone arrives. Users do not experience your consistency model or your consensus algorithm. They experience latency, errors and outages, and so do the colleagues who are on call when those happen.

Part III is about the gap between a correct system and one that keeps working under real load, real failures and real operators. Its subject is the behaviour that only appears in production: queues that grow without bound, retries that multiply, small slowdowns that become long outages, and dashboards that stay green while customers cannot buy a ticket.

  • Chapter 10, Scaling and architecture patterns, covers load balancing, queues, fan-out and tail latency, caching, and the difference between services that rebuild their state on every request and services that keep it resident.
  • Chapter 11, Resilience under failure and overload, covers timeouts, retries and their dangers, circuit breakers, bulkheads, rate limiting, backpressure, load shedding and metastable failures: how to make a system degrade gradually instead of collapsing.
  • Chapter 12, Observability, testing and operations, covers how to see what a distributed system is actually doing, how to define "working" in terms of users, and how to find failures by causing them on purpose before your customers find them for you.
Essential question

When your system is overloaded or partly broken, who gets hurt, how badly, and how quickly will you know?

If Part II is about being correct, Part III is about staying useful: serving as many people as possible, as well as possible, while parts of the system fail. Part IV then turns from machines to people, and to the changes they make.

Chapter 10

Scaling and Architecture Patterns

Load balancers, queues, fan-out, caches and actors

Here is a short quiz. A search service sends each query to 100 backend servers in parallel and waits for all of them. Each backend is fast: 99 % of its responses arrive within 10 ms, and only 1 in 100 takes a full second. What fraction of user queries take at least one second?

Most people guess about 1 %. The answer is 63 %. The probability that all 100 backends are fast is 0.99¹⁰⁰ ≈ 0.366, so in nearly two-thirds of queries at least one backend is slow, and the user waits for it.

Jeffrey Dean and Luiz André Barroso described this effect in The Tail at Scale (CACM, 2013), using numbers from Google's own systems. It is one of many places where an architecture that looks straightforward on a whiteboard behaves surprisingly under real load. This chapter is a guided tour of the patterns used to scale distributed services, each with its benefits and its catch.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Describe how load balancers distribute traffic and compare common balancing algorithms (Understand/Analyze)
  • Apply queue-based load levelling and competing consumers, and explain what queues do to latency (Apply)
  • Calculate the tail-latency effect of fan-out and apply hedged requests to reduce it (Apply)
  • Distinguish truly stateless, state-reconstructing and state-resident services, and evaluate what each costs in latency, load and consistency (Analyze/Evaluate)
  • Explain the actor model and virtual actors as state-resident designs, and place SEDA and the blackboard pattern historically (Understand)
  • Select an appropriate pattern for a given scaling problem and justify the choice (Evaluate)
Essential question

When a system must handle ten times more load, what should change: the number of machines, or the shape of the architecture?

Before you start

Chapter 5 showed how to partition and replicate data. This chapter does the same for work: how to spread requests across machines and how to keep them flowing smoothly. Little's law and queueing behaviour, introduced here, will be the foundation for the resilience chapter that follows.

The chapter in brief: "stateless" services and load balancing; queues and Little's law; scatter-gather and tail latency; caching; why most stateless services are really state-reconstructing; actors and virtual actors as state-resident services; staged and historical architectures; a pattern selection guide.

"Stateless" services and load balancing

At a glance
  • Why statelessness makes scaling easy
  • Layer 4 and layer 7 load balancers
  • Balancing algorithms, including the power of two choices

The simplest way to scale is to run many identical, stateless instances of a service behind a load balancer. "Stateless" means an instance keeps no data between requests that another instance would need: sessions, carts and uploads live in a shared database, cache or object store. Then any instance can serve any request, and you scale by adding instances.

Note · Hold on to the quotation marks

Notice what this definition actually says: the state has not gone away, it has moved to the database, cache or object store. Each instance must fetch that state again whenever it needs it. Later in this chapter we give this a more honest name, the state-reconstructing service, and see why the difference matters so much. For now, keep in mind that "stateless" describes the instances, not the service.

Layer 4 or layer 7?

  • A layer 4 (transport) load balancer forwards TCP connections or UDP flows without looking inside them. It is fast and protocol-agnostic, but it balances connections, not requests: one long-lived connection carrying thousands of requests goes to one backend.
  • A layer 7 (application) load balancer understands HTTP or gRPC. It can route by path or header, terminate TLS, retry failed requests and balance individual requests. Envoy, NGINX, HAProxy and Kubernetes ingress controllers typically operate at layer 7.
Common mistake · Long-lived connections and layer-4 balancing

gRPC uses long-lived HTTP/2 connections that multiplex many requests. Behind a layer-4 balancer, a client may send all its traffic to one backend for hours, even after new backends are added. Use a layer-7 proxy, client-side balancing, or periodic connection recycling.

Balancing algorithms

Algorithm How it chooses Strength Weakness
Round robin Next backend in turn Simple, even for uniform requests Ignores load and request cost
Least connections / least outstanding requests Backend with fewest in-flight requests Adapts to slow backends Needs global state; herding if many balancers act on stale data
Random Uniformly at random No state Uneven in the short term
Power of two choices Pick two at random, send to the less loaded Nearly as good as least-loaded, with little state Needs a load signal per backend
Consistent hashing Hash of a key (user, session) Keeps a key on one backend, good for caches Uneven if keys are skewed

The power of two choices deserves special mention. Analysed by Michael Mitzenmacher and others in the 1990s, it shows that choosing the less loaded of just two random candidates reduces the maximum load dramatically compared to choosing one at random, the difference between a logarithmic and a doubly logarithmic maximum load. It is used by Envoy's least-request balancer and many others because it avoids the "herd" effect where every balancer sends traffic to the same apparently idle backend.

Queues and Little's law

At a glance
  • Queues decouple producers from consumers
  • Little's law relates queue length, throughput and waiting time
  • Why latency explodes near full utilisation

Placing a queue between a producer and a consumer lets each run at its own pace. Two patterns are common:

  • Queue-based load levelling: a burst of requests is absorbed by the queue and processed at the consumers' steady rate, instead of overwhelming them.
  • Competing consumers: several consumer instances read from the same queue, each message going to one of them. To scale, add consumers.

Queues are used for background jobs, order processing, image conversion, notifications and countless other tasks. Their behaviour is governed by two pieces of queueing theory that every engineer should know.

Little's law

Definition · Little's law (John Little, 1961)

In a stable system, the average number of items in the system L equals the average arrival rate λ times the average time an item spends in the system W:

L = λ × W

If a service receives 200 requests per second and each takes 50 ms, on average 200 × 0.05 = 10 requests are in flight. If a queue holds 6,000 messages and consumers process 100 per second, a new message waits about 60 seconds. Little's law holds for almost any system regardless of arrival distribution, which makes it a powerful sanity check for capacity planning.

Utilisation and waiting time

As a server approaches 100 % utilisation, waiting time does not grow linearly: it explodes. In the simplest queueing model (M/M/1: random arrivals, random service times, one server), the average time in the system is W = 1 / (μ − λ), where μ is the service rate. At 50 % utilisation, requests spend twice their service time in the system; at 90 %, ten times; at 99 %, a hundred times.

Utilisation Time in system relative to service time (M/M/1)
50 % 2×
80 % 5×
90 % 10×
95 % 20×
99 % 100×

This is why services are typically run well below full utilisation, and why the queue in front of an overloaded service is not a cure but a warning sign. Chapter 11 turns this into a resilience principle: bounded queues and load shedding.

Test yourself

A worker pool processes 40 jobs per second. Jobs arrive at 38 per second on average. Using the M/M/1 formula as an approximation, how long does a job spend in the system on average, compared with the 25 ms it takes to process?

Show answer

W = 1 / (μ − λ) = 1 / (40 − 38) = 0.5 seconds, that is 20 times the 25 ms processing time, at 95 % utilisation. A modest increase in arrivals to 39.5 per second would push it to 2 seconds. Running close to capacity makes latency fragile.

Scatter-gather, fan-out and tail latency

At a glance
  • Splitting a request across many backends
  • Why the slowest backend dominates
  • Hedged and tied requests

In scatter-gather (also called fan-out/fan-in), a request is sent to many backends in parallel and their results are combined: a search across index shards, a page assembled from a dozen microservices, a price comparison across suppliers.

The benefit is parallelism. The catch is the opening quiz: the user-visible latency is the maximum of the parallel latencies, so rare slow responses become common slow requests as fan-out grows. With fan-out n and a per-backend probability p of a slow response, the probability that a request is slow is 1 − (1 − p)ⁿ.

Dean and Barroso described several techniques to tame the tail:

  • Hedged requests: send the request to one replica; if it has not answered within, say, the 95th-percentile latency, send a second copy to another replica and use whichever answers first. This adds only a few percent of extra load but cuts the tail sharply.
  • Tied requests: send to two replicas at once, each told about the other, so that whichever starts processing first cancels the other.
  • Partial results: return an answer from the shards that responded in time, marked as possibly incomplete. Search engines do this routinely.
  • Micro-partitioning and selective replication: split data into many small partitions so slow or hot ones can be moved or replicated quickly.
Common mistake · Hedging without a budget

Hedged requests add load. If a backend is slow because it is overloaded, hedging every request doubles the load and makes things worse. Hedge only after a delay (for example the 95th percentile), cap the fraction of hedged requests, and only hedge idempotent requests.

Caching

At a glance
  • The most effective scaling technique, and the most dangerous
  • Cache-aside, and the problems of staleness and stampedes

A cache keeps copies of frequently used data in a faster store. It can reduce database load by orders of magnitude, which also means that when a cache fails, the database may suddenly receive orders of magnitude more load than it is sized for.

The most common pattern is cache-aside: the application looks in the cache; on a miss, it reads from the database and stores the result in the cache with an expiry time (TTL). Writes go to the database and either delete or update the cache entry.

Three well-known problems:

  1. Staleness. A cache is a replica with no replication protocol. Readers see old data until the entry expires or is invalidated, and invalidation races (a delete arriving before a concurrent stale fill) can leave stale data indefinitely.
  2. Cache stampede (thundering herd). A popular entry expires and thousands of requests miss at once, all querying the database. Fixes include request coalescing (only one request refills the entry while others wait), adding random jitter to TTLs, and refreshing popular entries before they expire.
  3. Cold cache. After a restart or a deploy that changes cache keys, the hit rate is zero, and the database receives the full load. Warm caches gradually, or limit the rate of misses.
Example · A cache that became a dependency

Scenario: A timetable API has a 98 % cache hit rate. Its database is sized for twice the normal miss traffic, which is 4 % of total load.

Event: The cache cluster is restarted for maintenance. Its hit rate drops to 0 %. The database now receives 100 % of the load, 25 times what it is sized for, and stops responding. The API fails entirely.

Key insight: At a 98 % hit rate, the cache is not an optimisation; it is a critical dependency. Plan capacity for cache failure, or degrade gracefully (serve stale data, shed load) when it happens. Chapter 11 discusses how.

There are almost no stateless services

At a glance
  • Almost every "stateless" service rebuilds its state on every request
  • A more honest name: the state-reconstructing service
  • What reconstruction costs, and why caching only moves the problem
  • What statelessness genuinely buys you

Follow one request through a typical "stateless" booking service. A passenger asks to add a bicycle space to an existing train booking. The instance that receives the request has never seen this passenger before, or if it has, it has deliberately forgotten. So, before it can do anything useful, it must:

  1. validate the access token (perhaps fetching signing keys or a session record);
  2. load the passenger's profile;
  3. load the booking;
  4. load the current bicycle-space inventory for that train;
  5. load the pricing rules that apply.

Only then does it perform the actual work, a few microseconds of logic: check that a space is free, compute the price, update the booking. It writes the result back and then throws away everything it just assembled. The next request about the same booking, a second later, perhaps on a different instance, does it all again.

The instance was stateless. The service was not. It depended on exactly the same state on every call; it simply chose not to keep it.

Definition · Stateless, state-reconstructing and state-resident services
  • A truly stateless service computes its output from its input alone, like a pure function: resizing an image, converting a date format, validating a document against a fixed schema. Any instance can answer, and there is nothing to rebuild.
  • A state-reconstructing service keeps no state in memory between requests and therefore rebuilds, on each invocation, the state it needs from external stores. This describes most services that call themselves stateless.
  • A state-resident service keeps the state it serves in memory, close to the computation, across requests, and routes each request to the instance that holds the relevant state. Actors and virtual actors, described in the next section, are the best-known example.

The difference is not just a matter of words. Once you call the pattern by its honest name, its costs become visible.

What reconstruction costs

Cost What happens Where it hurts
Latency Several network round trips and deserialisations before any useful work Every request pays it, and fan-out to several stores adds tail latency (the opening quiz of this chapter)
Load on the stores The database or cache serves the whole working set again for every request The store, not the service, becomes the real scaling limit and the real stateful system
CPU Marshalling and unmarshalling the same objects over and over Often a larger share of CPU than the business logic itself
Consistency Each reconstruction is a read at some point in time, from some replica Stale reads, missing your own writes and time going backwards (chapters 5 and 6); read-modify-write races and lost updates (chapter 8)

The last row is the subtle one. Two instances that rebuild the same booking at almost the same moment, each modify it and write it back, have performed the lost-update anomaly of chapter 8. A "stateless" service is only as safe as its read-modify-write cycle, which is why conditional writes and version numbers are so common in such services.

Caching: memoised reconstruction

The natural response to the cost of reconstruction is to cache. A cache is reconstruction remembered: the result of loading the booking is kept for a while so the next request can skip the trip to the database. That helps latency and load, and immediately creates a new problem. The cache is a copy of the state, and every copy must be kept up to date or accepted as stale. As the previous section showed, a cache is a replica without a replication protocol.

There is a well-known joke in software engineering, usually attributed to Phil Karlton when he worked at Netscape: "There are only two hard things in Computer Science: cache invalidation and naming things." A later variant adds a third: "…and off-by-one errors." The joke lands because every engineer who has scaled a state-reconstructing service has met the first problem.

Atul Adya and colleagues at Google made a sharper version of this argument in the paper Fast key-value stores: An idea whose time has come and gone (HotOS 2019). They argued that remote in-memory caches such as Memcached and Redis, with their generic GET/PUT interfaces, push complexity back into the application and cost extra marshalling and network hops. They proposed stateful application servers, made practical by automatic sharding, as the better design for many workloads.

Example · The chain of consequences
  1. We make instances stateless, so that any instance can serve any request.
  2. Every request must now reconstruct its state from the database.
  3. The database becomes the bottleneck, so we add a cache.
  4. The cache serves stale data, so we add invalidation.
  5. Invalidation races with concurrent writes, so we add version checks and short TTLs.
  6. A cache restart sends the full load back to the database (the earlier example in this chapter), so we add warming and request coalescing.

Key insight: Each step is reasonable. Together they rebuild, piece by piece and outside the service, a replicated state store with weak consistency. The state never left; we only moved it somewhere harder to reason about.

What statelessness genuinely buys you

None of this means stateless instances are a mistake. They remain the right default for many services, because they offer real benefits:

  • Any instance can serve any request. Load balancing, autoscaling and rolling deployments become trivial.
  • Failure of an instance loses nothing. A crashed instance is replaced, and no state needs to be recovered or moved.
  • The hard problems are delegated. Replication, durability, consensus and failover become the job of a database that was built and tested by specialists, as described in Part II.

That last point is the real trade. A state-reconstructing service is a decision to outsource state to a system that is better at keeping it, and to pay for that on every request in latency, load and consistency. When the per-request cost is small (few requests per entity, small state, a database that is fast and close), it is a good bargain. When requests about the same entity arrive often, the state is large, or the entity needs strict ordering of updates, the bargain gets worse, and keeping the state resident starts to look attractive.

Test yourself

A service handling live positions for 4,000 trains receives a position update for each train every second, and each update must be checked against that train's timetable and its previous 60 positions. Estimate how much state a state-reconstructing design must load per second. What would a state-resident design load?

Show answer

A state-reconstructing design must, for every one of 4,000 updates per second, load the train's timetable and its last 60 positions: 4,000 reconstructions per second, plus a write each time, and the same data is reloaded about once per second per train. A state-resident design, with one in-memory owner per train, loads each train's state once (at activation), then only appends the new position in memory and persists it. The per-second reads drop from 4,000 reconstructions to roughly zero. The cost moves to routing every update for a train to the right owner, and to recovering that owner's state if its server fails.

State-resident services: actors and virtual actors

At a glance
  • Actors: private state, mailboxes, one message at a time
  • Orleans' virtual actors: state that lives where the computation is
  • What this changes, and the new problems it brings

If reconstructing state on every request is expensive, the alternative is to stop reconstructing it: keep the state in memory, next to the code that uses it, and send the requests to the state rather than the state to the requests. Database researchers have long called these two styles data shipping (move the data to the computation) and function shipping (move the computation to the data). State-reconstructing services ship data. Actors ship functions.

The actor model

The actor model, introduced by Carl Hewitt, Peter Bishop and Richard Steiger in 1973, structures a program as many small, independent actors. Each actor:

  • has private state that no other actor can access directly;
  • communicates only by sending asynchronous messages;
  • processes messages from its mailbox one at a time, so it needs no locks for its own state;
  • can create new actors and decide how to handle the next message.

Erlang (and its OTP framework), Akka (on the JVM) and Microsoft Orleans (.NET) are well-known actor systems. Erlang's "let it crash" philosophy, in which supervisors restart failed actors, comes from building telephone switches that had to run for years.

Virtual actors

Microsoft Research's Orleans introduced virtual actors, which it calls grains (Bernstein, Bykov and others, 2014). A grain:

  • has a stable identity (for example Train/IC-512 or User/42) and conceptually always exists;
  • is activated in memory on some server automatically when it receives a message, and deactivated when idle;
  • has mutable in-memory state, optionally persisted to storage;
  • processes requests one at a time by default (turn-based concurrency), so its own state needs no locks.

The runtime decides placement, moves grains when servers fail and routes messages to wherever a grain is active. Developers write code as if every object were always in memory.

Microsoft used Orleans for the cloud services behind the game Halo 4. Caitie McCaffrey, who worked on those services, made the general case in her talk Building Scalable Stateful Services (Strange Loop 2015): for workloads in which a client talks to the same piece of state many times in a session, as in games, shopping carts or live vehicle tracking, keeping that state in memory and routing requests to it avoids the constant database round trips of the stateless approach.

What changes when the state stays resident

Compare the bicycle-space request from the previous section, now sent to a grain Booking/8812:

Keeping the state resident is not only faster. It changes which design decisions are available:

  • One owner per entity. At any time, one activation of Booking/8812 serves all requests about that booking, one at a time. Concurrent requests are queued in its mailbox instead of racing each other, so the lost-update problem of chapter 8 disappears within the entity, without locks or version checks on the hot path.
  • No cache to invalidate. The in-memory state is the authoritative working copy, and all writes go through its owner. Because nobody else holds a copy that could become stale, the hardest part of caching goes away for that entity.
  • Per-entity consistency for free. Requests about one entity are serialised by its owner, which gives strong, linearizable-like behaviour per entity while different entities run fully in parallel.
  • The entity can act on its own. An actor can hold timers, aggregate events as they arrive, and push updates to subscribers instead of being polled, because there is a living thing in memory to do the work.
  • Persistence becomes a choice. The actor can write through on every change, batch writes, or record events in a log (event sourcing), depending on how much durability each entity needs.

What it costs instead

State-resident design does not make the problems of Part II disappear. It moves them to new places:

Concern State-reconstructing service State-resident service (actors)
Where the state lives In external stores, reloaded per request In memory at one owner, persisted to storage
Per-request cost Network round trips and deserialisation Routing to the owner; then local memory access
Concurrency control Version checks, transactions, locks in the store Turn-based execution inside each entity
Hard problem Cache consistency and store load Placement, routing and single ownership
Instance failure Nothing lost; any instance takes over Owner must be re-activated elsewhere and its state reloaded
Scaling unit Instances (until the store saturates) Entities, spread across servers; hot entities stay on one server

The central new problem is single ownership: making sure that each entity has exactly one active owner, and that everyone agrees which server it is on. This is the membership and directory problem of chapters 5, 7 and 9 in a new form. Orleans originally used an eventually consistent grain directory, which meant that after some failures two activations of the same grain could briefly coexist; in version 9.0 it moved to a strongly consistent directory. As a second line of defence, Orleans' grain storage uses ETags (optimistic concurrency, chapter 8), so that a stale activation cannot silently overwrite newer state. That is the fencing-token idea of chapter 7, applied to actors.

Other costs to plan for:

  • Activation latency. The first request for an entity pays the reconstruction cost once, while its state is loaded. Popular entities stay warm; rare ones are cold.
  • Hot entities. One entity receives all its requests on one server. An actor representing a whole train is fine; an actor representing "all departures today" is a bottleneck.
  • Memory bounds. Resident state must be bounded and evicted, or a long-running server slowly fills up.
  • Cross-entity operations. Moving a passenger from one train to another touches two actors; that is a distributed transaction or a saga (chapter 8).
Common mistake · Actors are not immutable

Some descriptions call actors "immutable objects". That is incorrect. An actor's identity is fixed, and its state is isolated (only the actor itself can change it), but the state is mutable. What the model removes is shared mutable state, not mutability itself.

The same idea in other systems

Virtual actors are one member of a family of state-resident designs that route work to a single owner of each piece of state:

  • Akka Cluster Sharding (JVM) distributes entity actors across a cluster by key and relocates them when nodes leave.
  • Dapr actors and Azure Service Fabric Reliable Actors offer the virtual-actor model on Kubernetes and Service Fabric respectively.
  • Cloudflare Durable Objects give each object a unique ID, single-threaded execution and its own storage colocated with the code, and route all requests for that ID to the one place the object lives.
  • Sharded stateful services without an actor framework, such as Uber's Ringpop-based services, use consistent hashing (chapter 5) and gossip-based membership (chapter 9) to send each key to the server that owns it.
Test yourself

A team says: "Our services are stateless, so they are simple. Actors are stateful, so they are complicated." Using this section, give a more precise description of where the complexity lives in each design.

Show answer

Both designs manage the same state; they put the complexity in different places. The "stateless" design is really state-reconstructing: its complexity sits in the database and cache layers (replication, invalidation, stale reads, lost updates via read-modify-write, cache stampedes and store load). The actor design keeps state resident: its complexity sits in the runtime (membership, placement, single ownership, re-activation after failure, hot entities). Neither is free. The useful question is which set of problems your workload makes cheaper.

In short, actors and other state-resident designs fit systems with many independent entities that each receive a steady stream of requests or events: game sessions, IoT devices, user sessions, shopping carts, trains and vehicles. They do not remove the problems of earlier chapters: messages can still be lost, an actor's persisted state is still a replication problem, and a transaction spanning two actors still needs the techniques of chapter 8. What they remove is the constant reconstruction of state, and with it most of the caching and consistency machinery that reconstruction requires.

Deep dive Data on the inside, data on the outside

Pat Helland's paper Data on the Outside versus Data on the Inside (CIDR 2005) offers a useful lens on this section. Data inside a service is private, current and changed under the service's own control. Data outside, sent between services in messages, is an immutable snapshot from some point in the past. A state-reconstructing service treats its own working state as if it were outside data: something to be fetched, deserialised and trusted only as of the moment it was read. A state-resident service treats it as inside data, owned and current. Seen this way, the choice between the two designs is a choice about where the boundary of ownership is drawn, and much of the difficulty in distributed systems comes from data that crosses that boundary more often than it needs to.

Historical and specialised architectures

At a glance
  • SEDA: stages connected by queues
  • The blackboard pattern
  • Where their ideas live on today

SEDA

The Staged Event-Driven Architecture was proposed by Matt Welsh, David Culler and Eric Brewer at SOSP 2001. A server is decomposed into stages (for example "parse request", "check cache", "read file", "send response"), each with its own event queue and thread pool, connected by queues. Each stage has controllers that adjust its thread pool size and can reject or degrade work when its queue grows.

The authors built a prototype platform called Sandstorm and a web server called Haboob on it. SEDA's lasting contributions are its ideas: explicit queues between stages make overload visible, and admission control at each stage keeps the system "well-conditioned" (throughput stays flat instead of collapsing under overload). Its specific thread-pool-per-stage design proved less influential; Welsh himself later wrote that he would design it differently. A 2003 paper, Capriccio: Scalable Threads for Internet Services (von Behren et al.), argued that a well-implemented thread package could match event-based servers, while acknowledging that its "blocking graph" for resource-aware scheduling was directly inspired by SEDA's stages and explicit queues. You can see SEDA's legacy in reactive streams, staged data pipelines and the bounded queues of chapter 11.

Blackboard

The blackboard pattern comes from artificial intelligence research in the 1970s, notably the Hearsay-II speech recognition system. Independent knowledge sources read from and write partial solutions to a shared blackboard, and a control component decides which knowledge source acts next. It suits problems with no deterministic solution path, where many specialists contribute pieces. In modern distributed systems, it is rare as such, but its ideas appear in shared state stores watched by many agents, and in some multi-agent AI architectures.

Choosing a pattern

At a glance
  • A selection guide by problem
Problem Pattern Watch out for
Pure computation with no state Truly stateless instances + load balancer Nothing much; this is the easy case
More request traffic than one instance can serve, with few requests per entity State-reconstructing instances + load balancer Reconstruction cost; store becomes the bottleneck; read-modify-write races
Bursty background work Queue + competing consumers Unbounded queues; duplicates (at-least-once)
Query over data spread across shards Scatter-gather Tail latency; partial failure
Repeated reads of the same data Cache-aside Stampedes; stale data; cache as hidden dependency
Many entities, each receiving frequent requests or events State-resident: actors / virtual actors / sharded stateful services Single ownership; placement; hot entities; cross-entity transactions
Data larger than one machine Partitioning (chapter 5) Hot keys; rebalancing
Expensive work you cannot afford to lose Durable workflow / saga (chapter 8) Compensation logic

This table is about scaling work. A different and even more consequential question is how to divide a system into services in the first place, so that teams can work independently. That is the subject of chapter 13, which builds on this chapter's distinction between state-reconstructing and state-resident services.

Lab · Measure the tail
  1. Write a small HTTP service that answers in 5 ms normally, but in 500 ms with probability 1 %.
  2. Write an aggregator that fans out to n instances and waits for all. Measure the median, 99th and 99.9th percentile latency for n = 1, 10, 50 and 100. Compare with 1 − 0.99ⁿ.
  3. Add hedging: after 20 ms, send a duplicate to another instance. Measure again, and count the extra requests sent.
  4. Notebook: What happened to the tail? What would happen if the slowness came from overload instead of randomness?

In Appendix A: A.11 Measure the tail (hints)

Lab · Reconstruct or reside?
  1. Build a tiny "train position" service twice. Version A (state-reconstructing): for each position update, load the train's last 60 positions from a database (PostgreSQL or Redis in Docker), append, check that the new position is plausible, write back. Version B (state-resident): route each train's updates, by consistent hashing of the train ID, to one worker that keeps the last 60 positions in memory and writes each update through to the database.
  2. Drive both with 1,000 simulated trains, one update per train per second. Measure latency percentiles, database queries per second and CPU.
  3. Run two instances of Version A and send updates for the same train to both at once. Do you lose positions? Add a version check and measure again.
  4. Kill the worker in Version B. How long until its trains are served again, and what did it have to reload?
  5. Notebook: Where did the complexity go in each version? Which version would you choose for 50 updates per train per day instead of one per second?

In Appendix A: A.12 Reconstruct or reside (hints)

Deep dive Universal scalability and why adding machines can make things slower

Amdahl's law says that if a fraction s of the work is serial, the speedup from N processors is at most 1 / (s + (1 − s)/N), which levels off. Neil Gunther's Universal Scalability Law adds a second term for coherency cost: the work of keeping nodes consistent with each other, which grows with the number of pairs of nodes, roughly N². With a coherency term, throughput doesn't just level off; beyond some point it decreases as you add nodes. Many systems show exactly this curve in load tests: a database cluster with heavy cross-node coordination, or a service whose instances share a lock. The lesson connects to chapter 9: the less coordination a design requires, the further it can scale.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Back to the quiz: 63 % of queries were slow because fan-out turns a 1 % event into a common one. That is the theme of this chapter. Scaling patterns change the statistics of your system, not just its capacity. Load balancers average out load, queues smooth bursts but trade them for waiting time, fan-out multiplies tail probabilities, and caches multiply the load that hits the database when they fail.

The same lens reveals the most misleading word in service architecture: stateless. Apart from pure computations, a service whose instances keep nothing in memory still needs its state on every request; it reconstructs it, every time, from somewhere else. Caching is then that reconstruction remembered, which is why the joke about cache invalidation never gets old. State-resident designs such as virtual actors turn this around and keep the state with its single owner, trading cache consistency for the problems of placement and ownership. The question is therefore never "stateful or stateless?" but "where does the state live, who owns it, and how often do we rebuild it?"

So the answer to the essential question is: both. Adding machines works only while the architecture's shape allows it. Little's law, the utilisation curve, the tail formula and the coherency cost tell you when the shape must change. The next chapter examines what happens when load exceeds capacity anyway, and how to keep a system from collapsing.

Key terms

Stateless (pure) service, state-reconstructing service, state-resident service, data shipping / function shipping, single ownership, load balancer, layer 4 / layer 7, round robin, least connections, power of two choices, queue-based load levelling, competing consumers, Little's law, utilisation, scatter-gather / fan-out, tail latency, hedged request, tied request, cache-aside, TTL, cache stampede, request coalescing, actor model, virtual actor / grain, SEDA, blackboard pattern, Universal Scalability Law.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) State Little's law and explain each term.
  2. (Understand) Why does a layer-4 load balancer distribute gRPC traffic poorly?
  3. (Understand) Explain why the power of two choices performs much better than random choice.
  4. (Apply) A page calls 20 microservices in parallel. Each has a 0.5 % chance of a slow response. What fraction of page loads include a slow call?
  5. (Apply) A queue holds 12,000 messages and grows by 50 per second; three consumers together process 150 per second. How long until a new message is processed? How many consumers do you need to drain the backlog within 10 minutes?
  6. (Analyze) Compare a queue with competing consumers and a pool of actors for processing events from 50,000 trains. What are the trade-offs?
  7. (Evaluate) A team proposes adding a cache in front of a database that is overloaded at peak. Evaluate the proposal and list what else they must plan for.
  8. (Understand) Explain why most "stateless" services are better described as state-reconstructing. Give one example of a service that is truly stateless.
  9. (Evaluate) For each of the following, argue whether a state-reconstructing or a state-resident design fits better: (a) a monthly invoice generator, (b) a live seat map for a train that many passengers view and change during boarding, (c) a password-reset endpoint, (d) a chat room.

Reflection

Look at a service you operate. What is its utilisation at peak? Where on the latency curve does that put it? What fan-out does a typical request have? And if it calls itself stateless: what state does it reconstruct on every request, and how many times per second is the same state rebuilt?

Looking ahead

Every pattern in this chapter has a failure mode that appears under stress: queues fill, retries multiply, caches empty. Chapter 11 is about surviving those moments with timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, bulkheads, backpressure and load shedding, and about a class of failure that keeps a system down even after its cause has gone.

Further reading

  • Brendan Burns, Designing Distributed Systems (2nd ed., O'Reilly, 2024). Container-era patterns, including scatter-gather and sharding.
  • Bernstein et al., Orleans: Distributed Virtual Actors for Programmability and Scalability (Microsoft Research, 2014).
  • Caitie McCaffrey, Building Scalable Stateful Services (Strange Loop 2015, video and slides online). The practitioner's case for state-resident services, from the Halo 4 backend.
  • Adya, Grandl, Myers and Qin, Fast key-value stores: An idea whose time has come and gone (HotOS 2019). Why remote caches push complexity back into "stateless" applications.
  • Pat Helland, Data on the Outside versus Data on the Inside (CIDR 2005).
  • Welsh, Culler and Brewer, SEDA: An Architecture for Well-Conditioned, Scalable Internet Services (SOSP 2001).
Chapter 11

Resilience: Surviving Failure and Overload

Timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, bulkheads, backpressure and metastable failures

At 09:00:00 a database fails over to a replica. The switch takes eight seconds. During those eight seconds, every request to the database fails, and every client does what it was programmed to do: retry. Three attempts each, immediately. Then the services one level up, seeing errors, retry their own calls three times, and each of those triggers three more attempts below. At 09:00:08 the new database primary is ready, and it receives roughly nine times its normal load.

Worse, it is cold. It has just started serving, so its buffer pool holds none of the hot pages, its query plan cache is empty, the operating system's page cache is empty, and every connection must be established from scratch. Queries that took 2 ms on the old, warm primary now take 20 or 200 ms, because almost every one goes to disk. At the very moment it receives nine times the load, it can serve perhaps a tenth or a hundredth of its usual capacity. It slows down, requests time out, clients retry, and the extra retries push the load higher still, so the caches never get the quiet moment they need to warm up…

At 09:30 the original cause, the failover, is long gone. The system is still down.

This is a metastable failure: a state in which the system stays broken because of how it reacts to being broken. A 2022 study of incidents across many large organisations (Huang et al., OSDI) concluded that at least four of fifteen major AWS outages over a decade involved metastable failures. This chapter shows how to build systems that do not trap themselves like this, using a set of patterns that each address one way in which failure spreads.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Explain how timeouts, retries and backoff interact, and calculate retry amplification across layers (Understand/Apply)
  • Implement capped exponential backoff with jitter and a retry budget (Apply)
  • Explain why routinely retrying timeouts is one of the most dangerous practices in distributed systems, and design a safe timeout policy (Understand/Create)
  • Describe circuit breakers and bulkheads and identify where each belongs in an architecture (Understand/Analyze)
  • Design configurable, per-customer rate limits that operators can adjust during an incident (Create)
  • Apply end-to-end backpressure, load shedding and graceful degradation to an overloaded service, and explain why pushing the wait back to the source beats building queues in the middle (Apply/Evaluate)
  • Analyze an incident for the trigger and sustaining effect of a metastable failure, and design mitigations (Analyze/Create)
Essential question

Why do so many large outages last much longer than the event that caused them?

Before you start

Chapter 2 introduced timeouts and idempotent retries. Chapter 10 showed that latency explodes near full utilisation and that caches can hide huge dependencies. Put these together and you get the mechanics of cascading failure, the subject of this chapter.

The chapter in brief: timeouts and deadlines; retries, backoff, jitter and budgets; why retrying timeouts is so dangerous; circuit breakers; bulkheads and cells; rate limiting, backpressure and load shedding; graceful degradation and static stability; metastable failures.

Timeouts and deadlines

At a glance
  • Every remote call needs a timeout
  • Timeouts must shrink as a request travels deeper
  • Deadline propagation

Chapter 2 established that a call without a timeout can wait forever, tying up a thread, a connection and memory. In a busy service, a dependency that hangs without timeouts will exhaust these resources within seconds, and the service fails with it. Every remote call needs a timeout. That includes database queries, DNS lookups, connection establishment and calls to "internal, always fast" services.

Timeouts must also be coordinated across layers. If a user-facing request has a 2-second budget, but a service three levels down waits 5 seconds for its database, that inner work continues long after the user has given up. It consumes capacity for a result nobody will see.

Definition · Deadline propagation

Pass the remaining time budget (or an absolute deadline) along with each request, and have each service set its outgoing timeouts from what remains, minus a margin. gRPC supports this directly: a client's deadline is propagated to servers, which can check it and stop working on requests that have already expired.

Tip

When a request arrives whose deadline has already passed, or whose remaining budget is too short to finish the work, reject it immediately. Doing work for an expired request is pure waste, and under overload, waste is what turns slowness into collapse.

Retries, backoff, jitter and budgets

At a glance
  • Retries are selfish: they help the caller and burden the callee
  • Retry amplification across layers
  • Capped exponential backoff with jitter
  • Retry budgets and token buckets

Retries are essential, because many failures are transient: a packet lost, a node restarting, a leader election in progress. But Marc Brooker of AWS put it well in the Amazon Builders' Library: retries are "selfish". A retrying client gets a better chance of success for itself by spending more of the server's capacity. When the server is failing because it is overloaded, retries make the overload worse.

Retry amplification

When retries happen at several layers, they multiply. If each of L layers makes up to r attempts (one try plus retries), the bottom layer can receive up to r^L times the original load.

Example · Counting the amplification

Scenario: A request passes through five layers: browser → API gateway → order service → inventory service → database. Each layer tries each call up to 3 times.

Calculation: 3⁵ = 243. If the database is failing, a single user action can produce up to 243 database calls.

Key insight: Retry at one layer, usually the one closest to the user or the one that knows whether the operation is idempotent. Other layers should fail fast and pass the error up.

Backoff and jitter

Immediate retries hammer a struggling service at the worst moment. Exponential backoff waits longer after each failed attempt: for example 100 ms, 200 ms, 400 ms, 800 ms, up to a cap. But if many clients fail at the same moment, as they do when a shared dependency fails, they all back off by the same amounts and retry in synchronised waves. Backoff alone just moves the spike a little later.

Jitter breaks the synchronisation by randomising the wait. In his widely cited 2015 post on the AWS Architecture Blog, Exponential Backoff And Jitter, Marc Brooker simulated many clients competing to update the same database row with optimistic concurrency, and compared four strategies:

Strategy Wait before attempt n What it does
No jitter min(cap, base × 2ⁿ) Everyone waits the same time; retries arrive in waves
Full jitter random(0, min(cap, base × 2ⁿ)) Pick uniformly from the whole window
Equal jitter half the window, plus random(0, half the window) Keeps a minimum wait, randomises the rest
Decorrelated jitter min(cap, random(base, previous_sleep × 3)) Each wait grows from the previous one, randomly

His conclusion was unambiguous: exponential backoff without jitter was "the clear loser", taking both more work and more time, while the jittered variants cut the total number of calls by more than half in his simulation with 100 clients. Full jitter and decorrelated jitter performed best, and he recommended jittered backoff as the standard approach for remote clients.

The key idea is worth spelling out, because it is easy to misremember. The exponential part does not decide when to retry. It decides how wide the window is, and the random part picks a moment inside that window. Each failure doubles the window, so the retries of many clients spread over a larger and larger stretch of time, and their combined rate falls.

Jitter is not only for retries. Any periodic activity across many machines, such as cron jobs, cache expiry, health checks and metric scrapes, should be jittered so that thousands of machines do not act in the same second.

Retry budgets

Backoff limits how fast one request retries. It does not limit the total retry load. A retry budget does: retries are allowed only while they stay below a fraction of normal traffic. Google's SRE book describes a per-client budget where retries may make up at most 10 % of requests. AWS describes limiting retries locally with a token bucket: each successful call adds a fraction of a token, each retry spends one, and when the bucket is empty, retries stop. When a dependency is healthy, the budget is never noticed. When it is failing, retries are throttled automatically, and load drops instead of multiplying.

Test yourself

A service gets 1,000 requests per second, 2 % fail transiently, and each failure is retried once. What is the extra load? What happens if the dependency starts failing 100 % of requests, with and without a 10 % retry budget?

Show answer

Normally the extra load is 20 requests per second (2 %), which is negligible. At 100 % failure without a budget, every request is retried: 2,000 requests per second, twice the normal load, sent to a dependency that is already failing (and more with additional attempts). With a 10 % budget, retries are capped at about 100 per second, so the dependency sees roughly 1,100 requests per second.

Retrying timeouts: the most dangerous retry

At a glance
  • A timeout is both an unknown outcome and a sign of overload
  • Why a retried timeout means doing the same work twice, for nobody
  • How one client can take down a shared service
  • Backoff defers load; only budgets and shedding remove it
  • A safe policy for timeouts

Of all the retries a system makes, the most common and the most harmful is the routine retry of a timed-out request. It looks harmless in code (catch (TimeoutException) { retry(); }), it makes dashboards look better on normal days, and it is behind a remarkable number of large outages. To see why, combine two ideas you already know.

Two things a timeout tells you

First, the outcome is unknown. Chapter 2 showed that a timeout is not a failure: it is the caller giving up. The request may never have arrived, may have failed, may still be in a queue, or may already have succeeded. The call you thought was synchronous has become asynchronous: the work carries on without you, and you no longer hear about its result. If you retry, you may now have two copies of the same work in flight.

Second, the cause is usually overload. Chapter 10 showed that as a server approaches full utilisation, queueing delay grows without bound. Most timeouts in a healthy, well-sized system therefore happen at exactly the moment when the server has no spare capacity: its queue is long, and requests wait in it until the client gives up. Errors such as "connection refused" or "503 Service Unavailable" arrive quickly and cost the server almost nothing. Timeouts arrive slowly, and each one represents work the server has accepted and will probably still do.

Put the two together, and a timeout retry is a request for more work, sent to a server that has no capacity left, while the original work is still waiting in its queue.

Goodput collapse

Marc Brooker describes the end state in his post Metastability and Distributed Systems (2021). Queues grow, latency rises, clients time out and stop listening for results. The system keeps working at full speed, completing requests, but the answers go to clients that have already left. Throughput stays high while goodput, the rate of useful completed work, falls towards zero. From the outside, the servers look busy and healthy; from the user's point of view, nothing works.

Example · How retrying timeouts turns a blip into an outage

Setup: A service can process 1,000 requests per second. Normal load is 800 per second (80 % utilisation). Clients use a 500 ms timeout and retry timeouts up to twice, immediately.

Trigger: A 20-second slowdown (a garbage-collection storm, a slow disk, a deploy) cuts capacity to 600 requests per second.

What follows:

  1. The queue grows by about 200 requests per second. Within seconds, waiting time exceeds 500 ms, and requests start to time out.
  2. Each timed-out request is retried, so offered load rises from 800 towards 2,400 requests per second (each request plus two retries), while the abandoned originals are still in the queue.
  3. The server spends much of its capacity on requests whose clients have gone. Goodput falls, so even more requests time out, and even more retries arrive.
  4. After 20 seconds, capacity is back to 1,000 per second. But offered load is now far above 1,000, and the queue is full of abandoned work. The trigger is gone, and the outage continues.

Key insight: This is the metastable failure of the next section, created by one line of retry code. Without timeout retries, the same slowdown would have caused 20 seconds of elevated latency and then recovered on its own.

One client can take down a shared service

The amplification does not need many clients. A single misbehaving consumer is often enough, and practitioners see this pattern again and again:

  • A batch job calls a shared service with a short timeout and an aggressive retry loop. On a busy day, some of its calls time out, so it retries, adding load, which slows the service for everyone, which causes more of the batch job's calls to time out.
  • The shared service's other clients, which were behaving well, now start timing out too. If any of them also retry timeouts, the feedback loop spreads.
  • The team that owns the shared service sees a traffic spike from "nowhere" and a CPU graph at 100 %. The team that owns the batch job sees "flaky timeouts" and increases its retry count.

The defence must therefore be on both sides. Clients must not retry timeouts freely. Servers must not depend on all clients behaving well: they need per-client limits (rate limits and concurrency limits per caller, a bulkhead in the sense of the next sections) so that one client's retry storm only exhausts that client's share.

Open and closed systems: why backoff is not enough

It is tempting to think that exponential backoff solves the problem. Brooker's post What is Backoff For? (2022) explains why it often does not, using a distinction from queueing theory:

  • In a closed system, a fixed set of clients each send a request and wait for the result before sending the next, like a pool of workers polling a queue. When these clients back off, they really do send less work, so backoff reduces load.
  • In an open system, new requests arrive independently of how earlier ones fared, like millions of visitors to a website. When some clients back off, new clients keep arriving and know nothing about it. Backoff only delays work; it does not remove it.

His conclusion: backoff helps with short-term spikes, but "the only way to deal with long-term overload is to reduce load, deferring load does not work." That means retry budgets (token buckets), load shedding and admission control. In a companion post, Fixing retries with token buckets and circuit breakers (2022), he simulated different retry strategies and found that a token-bucket retry budget gave a good balance between availability and extra load, while also showing that per-client strategies become less effective when there are very many small clients, because each one has too little information on its own. That is another reason the server side must protect itself.

In Open and Closed, Omission and Collapse (2023), Brooker connected the two ideas: in an open system, timeout-driven retries create exactly the feedback loop that makes queues grow without bound. Stable production systems avoid this by limiting concurrency (how many requests can be in progress at once) rather than only reacting to arrival rates, which makes them behave more like closed systems.

A safe policy for timeouts

Tip · Rules for retrying after a timeout
  1. Treat a timeout as two signals at once: "outcome unknown" and "the server may be overloaded". Never retry it immediately. The same caution applies to other ambiguous errors, such as connection resets after the request was sent and 502, 504 or 500 responses: the work may already have been done (chapter 2).
  2. Retry only idempotent operations, or operations protected by an idempotency key (chapter 2). Otherwise the retry may perform the action twice.
  3. Retry at one layer only, usually the outermost one that knows the user's intent.
  4. Make timeout retries spend from a retry budget (a token bucket). When the budget is empty, fail the request instead.
  5. Keep the total within the original deadline. One retry, with jittered backoff, inside the remaining time budget, is usually plenty.
  6. Prefer fast, explicit rejection from the server. A server that answers "429 Too Many Requests" or "503" with a Retry-After header in a millisecond gives the client a clear overload signal. Clients must honour it.
  7. On the server, stop doing work nobody is waiting for. Propagate deadlines and cancellations (gRPC does both), drop queued requests whose deadline has passed, and bound the queue.
  8. Measure it. Track timeout rates and retry rates per client, and alert when retries make up a growing share of traffic. A rising retry share is an early warning of a feedback loop.
Test yourself

A colleague argues: "Our client retries timeouts three times with exponential backoff and full jitter, so it is safe." Using this section, explain what is still missing.

Show answer

Backoff and jitter spread retries over time, but they do not reduce total work. In an open system with many clients, or during sustained overload, the deferred retries still arrive and keep the server above capacity. The policy is missing a retry budget (so that retries stop when many requests are failing), a check that the operation is idempotent, a limit to the original deadline, and protection on the server side (per-client limits, load shedding, dropping expired requests). Three retries per timeout can also multiply load by up to four during exactly the moments when the server is overloaded.

Circuit breakers

At a glance
  • Stop calling a dependency that is clearly failing
  • Closed, open and half-open states
  • Why AWS is cautious about them

Michael Nygard popularised the circuit breaker in Release It! (2007). Like an electrical breaker, it trips to protect the system.

Definition · Circuit breaker

A wrapper around calls to a dependency that tracks failures and has three states:

  • Closed: calls pass through normally; failures are counted.
  • Open: after failures exceed a threshold, calls fail immediately without contacting the dependency, for a cool-down period.
  • Half-open: after the cool-down, a few trial calls are allowed. If they succeed, the breaker closes; if they fail, it opens again.

A circuit breaker helps in two ways: callers fail fast instead of tying up resources waiting for timeouts, and the failing dependency gets breathing room to recover.

Circuit breakers also have critics. AWS engineers have noted that circuit breakers introduce modal behaviour, a sudden switch between "everything goes through" and "nothing goes through", that is hard to test and can turn a partial failure into a total one. If a dependency fails for 10 % of requests (say, one bad shard), a breaker that opens for all requests converts a 10 % problem into a 100 % one. Retry budgets and adaptive load shedding degrade more smoothly. A good compromise is to scope breakers narrowly (per endpoint or per shard, not per whole dependency) and to test them deliberately in fault-injection exercises (chapter 12).

Bulkheads and cells

At a glance
  • Isolate resources so one failure cannot consume everything
  • Thread pools, connection pools, and whole deployments as bulkheads

A ship's hull is divided into watertight compartments, bulkheads, so that a breach floods one compartment rather than sinking the ship. The software equivalent isolates resources:

  • Per-dependency pools. Give each dependency its own connection pool or concurrency limit. If the recommendations service hangs, it can exhaust only its own 20 connections, not the 200 the whole service needs.
  • Per-tenant or per-priority limits. One customer's traffic spike cannot use all capacity; interactive requests get capacity reserved from batch jobs.
  • Separate deployments. Critical and non-critical features run on different instances, so a memory leak in the reporting feature does not take down ticket sales.

At the largest scale, cell-based architecture applies bulkheads to entire stacks. The service is deployed as several independent cells, complete copies of the stack, each serving a subset of customers. A bad deployment or a poison request affects one cell's customers rather than everyone. AWS describes this approach for many of its own services, together with shuffle sharding, which assigns each customer to a random combination of resources so that two customers rarely share all of their resources.

Rate limiting, backpressure and load shedding

At a glance
  • Unbounded queues hide overload until it is too late
  • Rate limiting: a configurable dial, per customer, that you can turn during an incident
  • Backpressure: pushing the wait back to the source instead of building queues in the middle
  • Load shedding: refusing work early and cheaply

Chapter 10 showed that waiting time explodes near full utilisation. An unbounded queue in front of an overloaded service makes this worse: the queue grows, every request waits longer, clients time out and retry, adding more requests, while the server spends its capacity on requests whose clients have already given up. The service is busy doing useless work.

Three complementary responses decide how much work is let in, how the "slow down" signal travels, and what is refused when there is still too much.

Rate limiting: a dial you can turn

Definition · Rate limit

A cap on how many requests (or how much work) a given key may send per unit of time. The key may be the whole service, a customer or tenant, an API key, a client application, an endpoint or a priority class. Requests above the limit are rejected immediately, typically with HTTP 429 Too Many Requests and a Retry-After header.

The most common algorithm is the token bucket. A bucket holds up to b tokens and is refilled at a rate of r tokens per second; each request takes one token, and a request that finds the bucket empty is rejected. The rate r sets the long-term average, and the bucket size b allows short bursts. (The same structure was used for retry budgets earlier in this chapter.) Alternatives include the leaky bucket, which smooths output to a constant rate, and fixed or sliding window counters, which count requests per time window.

Three properties make rate limiting far more useful in practice:

  1. Per-customer (per-tenant) limits. A single global limit protects the server but not its users: one customer's runaway batch job or retry loop uses up the shared limit and everyone else is rejected. Limits keyed per customer give each one a fair share, and contain a misbehaving client, such as the timeout-retrying client from the previous section, to its own allowance. This is a bulkhead (described above) applied to request admission.
  2. Configurable at runtime. Limits should live in configuration that operators can change during an incident without a deploy: lower one customer's limit to protect everyone else, lower the global limit while a database recovers, or exempt a critical internal caller. A limit that can only be changed through a release pipeline cannot help you at 03:00.
  3. Safe defaults when configuration is unavailable. If the configuration service itself is down, the rate limiter should keep its last known values rather than fail open or fail closed. This is the static stability principle later in this chapter applied to the limiter itself.
Example · Using rate limits to bring a cold database back

Scenario: The database from this chapter's opening has just failed over. Its new primary is cold and can handle perhaps a tenth of normal load until its caches warm.

With configurable limits: The on-call engineer lowers the global limit at the API gateway to 15 % of normal, keeps a reserved allowance for ticket purchases, and sets bulk partner exports to zero. Rejected requests get 429 with Retry-After: 30. Over the next ten minutes, as cache hit rates rise on the dashboards, the limit is raised step by step: 15 %, 30 %, 60 %, 100 %.

Key insight: The rate limit turned an uncontrolled stampede into a controlled ramp. AWS used the same idea, fine-grained throttles that released a backlog gradually, to restore its EBS control plane in 2011 (chapter 16). The ramp can also be automated (admission that rises as latency stays low), but even a manual dial is far better than none.

Local or global? A local limiter runs inside each instance or proxy and needs no coordination, but each instance only sees its own share of traffic. A global limiter shares counters across instances (for example in Redis, or a dedicated rate-limit service such as the one Envoy can call), so it can enforce "customer X gets 1,000 requests per second in total", but it is itself a dependency that must be fast and must degrade safely. Many systems combine the two: approximate global limits per customer, with a local limit per instance as a safety net.

Backpressure: push the wait back to the source

Backpressure propagates "slow down" from consumer to producer. TCP's flow control is backpressure at the transport level: a receiver that cannot keep up advertises a smaller window, and the sender slows down. The question is how far back through a distributed system you can make that signal travel.

Consider a common shape. A job reads rows from a "to do" table as fast as it can and pushes them into a pipeline: an ingestion service, a queue, a processing service, a downstream API. If the downstream API slows down, the work has to wait somewhere. There are two very different places it can wait:

In the first design, the reader keeps going at full speed and a queue somewhere in the middle absorbs the difference. That queue has to be large, and it brings a long list of problems:

  • Memory and storage. Internal queues grow until they hit memory limits, disk limits or broker quotas, and then they fail in ways that are rarely tested.
  • Latency. By Little's law, every item now waits behind the whole backlog. Items enqueued during the slowdown are processed minutes or hours later, long after anyone cared.
  • Burstiness. When the downstream recovers, the backlog is released as a burst, which can overload the next component in line, or the downstream itself, which is often still fragile.
  • Ordering and races. Work that was meant to happen in sequence is now spread across a backlog, retries and the live flow. When the queue drains, old and new items interleave in ways nobody planned, and latent race conditions appear: an update applied after a later update, a delete processed before the create it refers to.
  • Hard to reason about. During and after an incident, nobody knows exactly which work is waiting where, which has been processed twice, and which is lost.

In the second design, each stage takes on only as much work as it can finish: queues between stages are small and bounded, and when one is full, the stage before it waits. The slowdown propagates step by step back to the reader, which simply reads its table a little more slowly. The to-do table itself becomes the queue. It is durable, it is already there, and it is visible to everyone.

The result is a system that is slower during a disruption but much calmer: no huge internal backlog, no memory blow-ups, steady and predictable latency for each item that is in flight, no thundering release when things recover, and far fewer surprising interleavings.

Tip · How to build end-to-end backpressure
  • Bound every queue and buffer, and decide what happens when it is full: the producer blocks, or receives an explicit "busy" signal.
  • Prefer pull over push. Consumers that fetch work when they have capacity (Kafka consumers, workers polling a table or queue) naturally apply backpressure; producers that push as fast as they can do not.
  • Use credit-based flow control where a framework supports it: in Reactive Streams and gRPC streaming, the receiver signals how many items it is ready for.
  • Limit concurrency per stage rather than accepting unlimited in-flight work. A stage with 50 workers and a queue of 100 has a known, bounded footprint.
  • Return explicit "slow down" signals (429, RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED, Retry-After) and make callers propagate them upward instead of absorbing them into a local queue.
  • Let the durable source be the buffer. If the work already lives in a table, log or file, read it at the pace the system can sustain rather than copying it into an in-memory queue.

Backpressure works best when the source can slow down, as a batch reader, a stream consumer or an internal service can. Some sources cannot: millions of users on a website do not stop arriving because a database is slow (the open systems of the previous section). At the edge of an open system, the "slow down" signal must turn into rate limiting and load shedding, so that the excess is rejected quickly at the front door rather than queued in the middle.

Common mistake · A queue is not a capacity plan

Adding a queue between a fast producer and a slow consumer does not add capacity; it adds waiting. If the producer is faster than the consumer on average, the queue grows without bound, however large you make it. Queues are excellent for absorbing short bursts and for decoupling availability (chapter 2), but they should be bounded, monitored for age (the oldest item's wait) as well as length, and backed by a plan for what happens when they are full.

Load shedding

When a service cannot keep up, it should reject excess work early and cheaply, before spending resources on it, with a clear "overloaded, try later" error (HTTP 503 or 429, gRPC RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED). It is far better to serve 90 % of requests successfully than to time out on 100 % of them.

Good load shedding:

  • Sheds before work is done. Reject at the front door, based on queue length, in-flight count or CPU.
  • Prioritises. Shed batch traffic, retries and non-critical features first.
  • Drops stale work. Facebook's 2015 paper Fail at Scale (Ben Maurer, ACM Queue) described two techniques: controlled delay (based on CoDel: when a queue has not been empty for a while, use short timeouts for queued requests) and adaptive LIFO (under congestion, serve the newest requests first, because the oldest have probably already been abandoned by their clients).
Example · Choosing what to shed

Scenario: A journey-planner API receives three times its capacity during a large disruption. Traffic includes interactive searches from passengers, background refreshes from apps, and bulk exports for partner companies.

Design:

  1. Reject bulk exports immediately with 503 and a Retry-After header.
  2. Serve background refreshes from a cached response, even if it is minutes old.
  3. Admit interactive searches up to capacity, using a concurrency limit; reject the excess immediately with a friendly message.
  4. Drop any request that has been queued longer than its client's timeout.

Key insight: Under overload, someone will be disappointed. Shedding lets you choose who, and keeps the system useful to the most important users.

Graceful degradation and static stability

At a glance
  • Doing less instead of failing
  • Designing systems that keep working when their control plane does not

Graceful degradation means turning off non-essential features to protect essential ones: hide recommendations, serve stale data, disable search suggestions, make the site read-only. Plan degradation modes in advance and make them switchable (feature flags) so that operators can use them under stress.

Static stability is a related AWS design principle, described in the Builders' Library article Static stability using Availability Zones. A statically stable system keeps working without needing to make changes when a dependency fails. For example, a service that runs with enough spare capacity in each Availability Zone to absorb the loss of one zone does not need to launch new instances during a zone failure, which matters because the APIs that launch instances may be impaired by that same failure. The general principle: in a crisis, prefer designs that do not depend on the control plane (deployments, scaling, configuration changes) to recover.

Metastable failures

At a glance
  • Failures that sustain themselves after the trigger is gone
  • Trigger, vulnerable state and sustaining effect
  • How to prevent and escape them

Nathan Bronson, Abutalib Aghayev, Aleksey Charapko and Timothy Zhu named this class of failure in Metastable Failures in Distributed Systems (HotOS 2021).

Definition · Metastable failure

A failure in which a system is pushed out of its normal state by a trigger (a load spike, a failover, a cache flush), and is then kept in a degraded state by a sustaining effect, a positive feedback loop such as retries or cache misses, even after the trigger has gone. The system is only vulnerable to this when it is running in a vulnerable state, typically at high utilisation where the feedback loop can outpace recovery.

Common sustaining effects include:

  • Retry storms: errors cause retries, which cause more load, which causes more errors.
  • Cache-dependent load: a cache is flushed; the database cannot serve the miss load; slow responses prevent the cache from refilling.
  • Expensive recovery paths: a timed-out request is more expensive to handle than a successful one (error logging, failover logic, connection re-establishment).
  • Garbage collection spirals: a slow service accumulates more in-flight requests in memory, which causes more GC pauses, which slows it further. Brooker's post Garbage Collection and Metastability (2024) describes this loop in open systems without concurrency limits.
  • Cold starts: a freshly started or newly promoted database, cache or service has empty buffer pools, query plan caches, operating system page caches and connection pools. Until they warm up, each request costs many times more than usual, so the component's real capacity is lowest at exactly the moment it receives a burst of pent-up and retried traffic.

Brooker's post Caches, Modes, and Unstable Systems (2021) generalises the last point: a system that relies on a cache has two stable modes, one where the cache is full and everything is fast, and one where the cache is empty, load on the backend is high, and the cache cannot fill because the backend is too slow. Load tests rarely visit the second mode, which is why it tends to be discovered in production.

Huang and colleagues' follow-up paper, Metastable Failures in the Wild (OSDI 2022), studied 22 such incidents from 11 organisations and identified retry policies as a frequent amplification mechanism. In the incidents they examined, recovery typically came from direct intervention to reduce load, such as throttling clients, shedding traffic or adding capacity, rather than from the systems escaping on their own.

Prevention and escape

  1. Know your vulnerable state. Load-test beyond capacity and look for throughput that collapses rather than levelling off.
  2. Break the loops in advance. Retry budgets, jittered backoff, load shedding, bounded queues and deadline propagation each cut a sustaining effect.
  3. Make recovery cheap. Rejecting a request should cost much less than serving it.
  4. Keep an emergency brake. Have tested ways to drop load quickly: rate limits that can be lowered, feature flags, traffic draining.
  5. Test the trigger. Fault-injection exercises (chapter 12) that simulate a failover under peak load reveal sustaining effects before real incidents do.
Test yourself

After a 5-minute outage of a login service, all mobile apps reconnect at the same moment. The login service cannot handle the burst, times out, and the apps retry every 2 seconds. Identify the trigger, the sustaining effect and two mitigations.

Show answer

The trigger is the original 5-minute outage, which created a synchronised reconnection burst. The sustaining effect is the apps' fixed-interval retries without jitter, which keep load above capacity. Mitigations: capped exponential backoff with full jitter in the app; a retry budget; server-side load shedding with Retry-After; admitting reconnections gradually (a token bucket at the login front door). Ideally, also make sessions survive login-service outages so that apps do not need to reconnect at all (static stability).

Lab · A retry storm in miniature
  1. Run two services A → B locally. Put a fault-injecting proxy between them, for example Shopify's Toxiproxy, or Linux tc netem for latency and packet loss.
  2. Give B a fixed capacity (a concurrency limit of, say, 20). Drive A with a steady load of about 70 % of B's capacity.
  3. Inject a 10-second outage of B. Observe A's success rate with: (a) immediate retries ×3; (b) exponential backoff without jitter; (c) full jitter; (d) full jitter plus a 10 % retry budget; (e) (d) plus load shedding in B.
  4. Plot success rate over time. In which configurations does the system recover as soon as the fault ends? In which does it stay degraded?
  5. Notebook: Describe the sustaining effect you observed in your own words.

Appendix A describes this lab in more detail, with hints, and adds a second lab for this chapter (A.14) that compares a large internal queue with end-to-end backpressure.

In Appendix A: A.13 A retry storm in miniature (hints) · A.14 Pushback instead of queues (hints)

Deep dive Admission control as a control problem
