Static thresholds ("reject if more than 100 requests are in flight") are hard to tune and wrong after every deploy. An active line of work treats admission control as a feedback control problem: measure latency or queueing delay and adjust the concurrency limit continuously, much as TCP adjusts its congestion window. Netflix's open-source concurrency-limits library applies TCP-style algorithms such as AIMD (additive increase, multiplicative decrease) and gradient-based variants to service concurrency. These adaptive limiters find a service's real capacity automatically and shed load as soon as latency starts rising, before queues build up. They are a practical way to keep a system out of its vulnerable state.

Synthesis

Synthesis

Why do large outages last longer than their causes? Because the system's own reactions (retries, reconnections, cache refills, failovers) are designed for individual failures, and when everything fails at once, those reactions combine into feedback loops that sustain the outage. The patterns in this chapter each cut one loop: deadlines stop work nobody is waiting for, jittered backoff and budgets stop retries from multiplying, circuit breakers stop calls to hopeless dependencies, bulkheads stop one failure from consuming shared resources, load shedding keeps the system useful above capacity, and static stability removes the need to change the system during a crisis.

Look again at the opening story with the whole chapter in mind. The failover was a trigger; the cold caches cut capacity just as load spiked; and the timeout retries turned an eight-second event into a feedback loop that the system could not leave on its own. Of all the habits this chapter warns against, retrying timeouts by routine deserves the most suspicion, because a timeout is both a sign that the server is out of capacity and a sign that the original work is probably still in progress.

The common principle is that a resilient system degrades gradually: when load or failures rise, useful throughput falls a little, not off a cliff. You cannot assume this property. You must design for it and then test for it, which is the subject of the next chapter.

Key terms

Rate limit, per-tenant limit, local / global rate limiting, end-to-end backpressure, pull-based consumption, timeout, deadline propagation, retry amplification, timeout retry, goodput, open / closed system, cold start, exponential backoff, jitter (full jitter), retry budget, token bucket, circuit breaker (closed / open / half-open), modal behaviour, bulkhead, cell-based architecture, shuffle sharding, backpressure, bounded queue, load shedding, adaptive LIFO, controlled delay, graceful degradation, static stability, metastable failure, trigger / sustaining effect / vulnerable state, adaptive concurrency limit.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the three states of a circuit breaker and what triggers each transition.
  2. (Understand) Explain why jitter is needed in addition to exponential backoff.
  3. (Understand) Why are retries described as "selfish"?
  4. (Apply) A request crosses four layers, each with 2 retries (3 attempts). What is the worst-case amplification at the bottom layer? Propose a change that limits it.
  5. (Apply) Write the backoff delays (as ranges) for attempts 0–5 with base 50 ms, cap 1 s and full jitter.
  6. (Analyze) Read the opening scenario again. Identify the vulnerable state, the trigger and the sustaining effect.
  7. (Evaluate) A team wants to add circuit breakers around every call in their system. Evaluate the proposal, including at least one way it could make an incident worse.
  8. (Create) Design an overload protection strategy for a public train-departure API that receives ten times its normal traffic during major disruptions.
  9. (Understand) Explain the two different things a timeout tells a client, and why together they make an immediate retry harmful.
  10. (Analyze) Explain why exponential backoff with jitter reduces load in a closed system but only defers it in an open system. What must be added in the open case?
  11. (Evaluate) A pipeline reads 10 million rows from a table and pushes them through three services into a partner API that sometimes slows to a tenth of its normal speed. Compare putting a large queue before the partner API with designing end-to-end backpressure. Consider memory, latency, ordering and recovery.
  12. (Create) Design the rate-limiting scheme for a public API used by 300 partner companies. Specify the keys, the algorithm, where limits are stored and changed, what callers receive when limited, and how operators would protect the API from one partner's runaway client at night.

Reflection

What is the retry policy, at every layer, of the most critical request path in a system you work on? Calculate its worst-case amplification. Does anyone own that number?

Looking ahead

None of these patterns can be trusted until they have been seen working. Chapter 12 is about observing distributed systems (metrics, logs, traces and profiles), defining what "working" means with SLOs, and testing systems by deliberately breaking them.

Further reading

  • Michael Nygard, Release It! (2nd ed., Pragmatic Bookshelf, 2018). The classic catalogue of stability patterns and antipatterns.
  • Marc Brooker, Timeouts, retries, and backoff with jitter (Amazon Builders' Library).
  • Marc Brooker, Exponential Backoff And Jitter (AWS Architecture Blog, 2015). The simulations behind full and decorrelated jitter, with charts.
  • Marc Brooker's blog (brooker.co.za/blog), especially Metastability and Distributed Systems (2021), Caches, Modes, and Unstable Systems (2021), Fixing retries with token buckets and circuit breakers (2022), What is Backoff For? (2022), Open and Closed, Omission and Collapse (2023) and Garbage Collection and Metastability (2024).
  • Google, Site Reliability Engineering (2016), chapters "Handling Overload" and "Addressing Cascading Failures". Free online.
  • Bronson et al., Metastable Failures in Distributed Systems (HotOS 2021), and Huang et al., Metastable Failures in the Wild (OSDI 2022).
  • Ben Maurer, Fail at Scale (ACM Queue, 2015).
Chapter 12

Observability, Testing and Operations

Seeing inside a distributed system, and breaking it on purpose

The incident review begins with a screenshot of the main dashboard taken during the outage. Every panel is green. CPU is normal, memory is normal, all instances pass their health checks, and error rates on the load balancer are near zero. Meanwhile, for 47 minutes, a third of customers could not complete a purchase, because a dependency returned successful responses containing empty results.

This story, a composite of many real incident reviews, shows the gap between monitoring (checking whether the things you thought of are healthy) and observability (being able to ask new questions about what the system is doing, including questions you did not anticipate). In a distributed system, the failures that hurt most are often the ones you did not anticipate. This chapter is about closing that gap, and about the testing practices that find failures before customers do.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Describe the four main telemetry signals (metrics, logs, traces, profiles) and explain what each is good for (Understand)
  • Explain distributed tracing and context propagation with W3C Trace Context and OpenTelemetry (Understand)
  • Apply the RED, USE and golden-signals methods to choose metrics for a service (Apply)
  • Define SLIs, SLOs and error budgets and calculate burn-rate alert thresholds (Apply)
  • Compare testing techniques for distributed systems, from property tests to chaos engineering, and design a fault-injection experiment (Analyze/Create)
Essential question

How can you know what a system made of hundreds of independent parts is actually doing, and whether it is doing it well enough?

Before you start

Chapters 1 and 3 introduced gray failure and fail-slow components: parts that look healthy to their own checks while users suffer. Chapter 11 ended by saying that resilience patterns must be tested. This chapter gives you the tools for both.

The chapter in brief: monitoring and observability; the four signals; tracing and context propagation; what to measure; SLOs and error budgets; alerting; testing distributed systems; chaos engineering; incidents and postmortems.

Monitoring and observability

At a glance
  • Known unknowns and unknown unknowns
  • Observability as a property of the system, not a tool

Monitoring answers questions you decided on in advance: is CPU above 90 %? Is the error rate above 1 %? It catches known failure modes.

Observability is a property of a system: how well its internal state can be understood from its external outputs. The term comes from control theory. In software, it means being able to answer questions you did not anticipate, such as "why are checkout requests from Android users in one region slow since 14:05?", without shipping new code.

Both matter. Good monitoring catches the predictable failures quickly; good observability shortens the investigation of the unpredictable ones. A distributed system needs both, because its failures are so often new combinations of conditions.

The four signals

At a glance
  • Metrics, logs, traces and profiles
  • What each is good and bad at
  • Correlating them
Signal What it is Good for Weak at
Metrics Numeric measurements aggregated over time: counters, gauges, histograms Dashboards, alerting, trends; cheap to store Explaining why; per-request detail
Logs Timestamped records of discrete events, ideally structured (key-value or JSON) Detail about specific events and errors Cost at volume; following a request across services
Traces The path of one request through the system, as a tree of timed spans Latency breakdown; dependencies; where an error originated Aggregate questions; cost unless sampled
Profiles Where CPU time and memory are spent, by function, sampled continuously Explaining resource usage and slow code paths Request-level context on their own

Metrics in practice

Metrics are cheap because they are aggregated: a counter of requests per status code costs the same whether you serve ten requests or ten million. That cheapness depends on cardinality, the number of distinct label combinations. A label like status_code has a handful of values; a label like user_id may have millions, and each value creates a separate time series. High-cardinality labels are the most common way to overload a metrics system.

For latency, record histograms, not averages. An average of 50 ms can hide a 99th percentile of 3 seconds, and chapter 10 showed that the tail is what users feel. Histograms let you compute percentiles across instances afterwards; pre-computed percentiles from each instance cannot be meaningfully averaged.

Correlating signals

The power of the four signals comes from connecting them. A latency spike on a metrics dashboard links, via an exemplar (a sample trace ID attached to a histogram bucket), to a slow trace; the trace's spans link to the logs written during that request, which carry the same trace ID; a slow span links to a profile of what the process was doing at that time. Open-source stacks such as Grafana's LGTM+ family (Loki for logs, Grafana for visualisation, Tempo for traces, Mimir for metrics, Pyroscope for profiles), as well as commercial platforms, are built around this kind of correlation.

Distributed tracing and context propagation

At a glance
  • Traces, spans and the context that connects them
  • W3C Trace Context and OpenTelemetry
  • Sampling

A trace represents one request's journey. It consists of spans: each span records one unit of work (an HTTP handler, a database query, a queue publish) with a start time, a duration, attributes and a parent span. Together, the spans form a tree that shows where the time went.

For this to work across services, each request must carry its trace context to the next service. The W3C Trace Context standard defines an HTTP header for this, traceparent:

traceparent: 00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-00f067aa0ba902b7-01
             │  │                                │                └ flags (01 = sampled)
             │  │                                └ parent span ID (16 hex digits)
             │  └ trace ID (32 hex digits)
             └ version

Each service reads the incoming header, creates its own spans as children of the parent span, and sends a new traceparent with its own span ID to the services it calls. Messages on queues carry the same context in message headers.

OpenTelemetry (OTel), a project of the Cloud Native Computing Foundation formed in 2019 from the merger of OpenTracing and OpenCensus, provides vendor-neutral APIs, SDKs, automatic instrumentation for common libraries, and the OpenTelemetry Collector (a pipeline that receives, processes and exports telemetry). Instrumenting with OTel means your telemetry can be sent to any compatible backend.

Sampling

Recording every span of every request is expensive at scale. Systems sample:

  • Head sampling: decide at the start of a trace (for example, keep 1 %) and propagate the decision in the traceparent flags. Cheap, but you may discard the rare slow or failing trace you needed.
  • Tail sampling: buffer all spans of a trace and decide after it completes: keep all traces with errors or high latency, plus a small fraction of normal ones. More useful, but it requires collecting all spans of a trace in one place before deciding, which costs memory and coordination.
Test yourself

A user reports that a request failed at 10:14. Logs from five services contain thousands of errors around that time. What one piece of information, if it were in every log line, would let you find exactly that request's logs across all services?

Show answer

The trace ID (or another correlation ID propagated through all services). With the trace ID in every structured log line, you can filter all logs for that request and open its trace to see which service failed first. This is why logging libraries should automatically add the current trace and span IDs to each log entry.

What to measure

At a glance
  • Three complementary methods
  • Measure what users experience

Three well-known methods help decide what to measure:

Method Origin Measure Best for
Four golden signals Google's SRE book (2016) Latency, traffic, errors, saturation Any user-facing service
RED Tom Wilkie Rate, errors, duration of requests Request-driven microservices
USE Brendan Gregg Utilisation, saturation, errors of each resource Resources: CPU, disks, network, pools, queues

RED tells you how a service is treating its users; USE tells you why, by examining the resources underneath. Saturation (how "full" a resource is, for example queue length or thread pool usage) deserves special attention, because chapter 10 showed that latency explodes as saturation rises.

Tip · Measure goodput, timeouts and retries

Two numbers are easy to forget and invaluable in an incident. First, goodput: the rate of requests completed successfully for a client that was still waiting, as opposed to raw throughput. A server that is busy completing abandoned requests shows high throughput and near-zero goodput (chapter 11). Second, timeouts and retries as their own metrics, broken down per calling client: the share of traffic that is retries, and which callers produce it. A rising retry share is often the first visible sign of a feedback loop, and the per-client breakdown tells you who is driving it.

Above all, measure what users experience, ideally as close to them as possible: at the load balancer, in the client, or with synthetic probes that perform real user actions (search for a journey, buy a ticket) every minute. The empty-results incident at the start of this chapter would have been caught by a probe that checked whether search results were non-empty.

SLOs, error budgets and alerting

At a glance
  • Defining "good enough" precisely
  • Error budgets as a decision tool
  • Burn-rate alerts
Definition · SLI, SLO and error budget
  • A service level indicator (SLI) is a measured ratio of good events to total events, for example "the proportion of journey searches answered successfully within 500 ms".
  • A service level objective (SLO) is a target for an SLI over a time window, for example "99.5 % over 30 days".
  • The error budget is the allowed failure: 100 % − SLO. With a 99.5 % SLO over 30 days, 0.5 % of requests may fail, about 3.6 hours' worth of total downtime.

SLOs change conversations. Instead of "is the system up?", teams ask "how much of our error budget have we used?". If the budget is healthy, the team can ship faster and take more risk; if it is nearly spent, reliability work takes priority. This framing comes from Google's SRE practice and is described in its books.

A service level agreement (SLA) is a contractual promise to customers, usually with penalties, and should be looser than the internal SLO.

Burn-rate alerting

Alerting directly on "error rate > 1 %" is noisy for short spikes and slow for long, low-level problems. The Site Reliability Workbook (2018) recommends alerting on burn rate: how fast the error budget is being consumed, relative to the rate that would exactly use it up over the SLO window.

Example · Calculating a burn-rate threshold

Setup: SLO 99.9 % over 30 days (720 hours), so the budget is 0.1 % of requests.

A burn rate of 1 uses the whole budget in exactly 30 days. A burn rate of 14.4 uses it in 720 / 14.4 = 50 hours, and consumes 2 % of the budget in one hour (14.4 × 1 h / 720 h = 0.02).

Alert rule: page someone if the burn rate over the last hour exceeds 14.4 and the burn rate over the last 5 minutes also exceeds 14.4 (so the alert clears quickly when the problem stops). Add a slower rule, such as a burn rate above 6 over 6 hours, for problems that are smaller but sustained.

Key insight: Burn-rate alerts are tied to user impact and to the budget the team has agreed on, so a page means "users are being hurt fast enough that we will miss our objective".

Alert on symptoms, not causes. Page a human when users are affected (the SLO is burning), not when CPU is high. High CPU with happy users is not an emergency; causes belong on dashboards to support diagnosis.

Testing distributed systems

At a glance
  • Why unit tests are not enough
  • A ladder of techniques, from property tests to formal methods
  • Jepsen and deterministic simulation

Unit tests run code in one process with a perfect "network" of function calls. They cannot find bugs that need a particular interleaving of messages, a crash at the wrong moment, or a clock jump. Distributed systems need additional techniques, roughly in order of increasing rigour:

Technique What it does Example tools
Property-based testing Generates many random inputs and checks that invariants hold QuickCheck, Hypothesis, proptest
Integration tests with fault injection Runs real components with injected delays, drops and crashes Toxiproxy, tc netem, testcontainers
Consistency checking (Jepsen) Runs concurrent clients under faults, records histories, checks them against a consistency model Jepsen with Knossos and Elle checkers; Maelstrom
Deterministic simulation testing Runs the entire system in a single-threaded simulator where network, disk and time are simulated; any failing run can be replayed exactly from its random seed FoundationDB's simulator; TigerBeetle's VOPR; libraries for Rust and Go
Model checking and formal specification Specifies the design in a formal language and exhaustively explores its states TLA+ (and its PlusCal language), P, Alloy

Two of these deserve a closer look.

Deterministic simulation. The FoundationDB team built their database from the start so that it could run inside a simulator, with all nondeterminism (network, disk, clocks, thread scheduling) controlled by a seeded random number generator. They then ran enormous numbers of simulated hours with injected faults. When a test failed, the seed reproduced the failure exactly. Will Wilson's 2014 talk Testing Distributed Systems w/ Deterministic Simulation describes the approach, and several newer databases have adopted it.

Formal methods. Newcombe and colleagues described in How Amazon Web Services Uses Formal Methods (CACM, 2015) how AWS engineers used TLA+ to specify designs for services including S3 and DynamoDB, finding subtle bugs that would have been extremely hard to find by testing, some requiring dozens of steps to trigger. TLA+ was created by Leslie Lamport. Raft itself has a TLA+ specification.

Chaos engineering

At a glance
  • Experiments, not random breakage
  • The principles of chaos engineering
  • Game days

Netflix built Chaos Monkey in 2010, early in its move to AWS, to randomly terminate production instances, forcing engineers to build services that survive instance loss. The practice grew into chaos engineering, described on principlesofchaos.org as "the discipline of experimenting on a system in order to build confidence in the system's capability to withstand turbulent conditions in production".

The emphasis is on experiment. A chaos experiment follows the scientific method:

  1. Define steady state in terms of a measurable output that reflects user experience, such as successful searches per minute.
  2. Form a hypothesis that steady state will continue in both the control group and the experimental group.
  3. Introduce real-world events: kill instances, add latency, partition a zone, fill a disk, expire certificates, skew clocks.
  4. Try to disprove the hypothesis by comparing the experimental and control groups.
  5. Minimise the blast radius: start small, in a test environment or with a tiny fraction of traffic, with a way to stop immediately.

The principles also encourage running experiments in production (because that is where the real system lives) and automating them to run continuously, once confidence has been built in safer environments.

Common mistake · Chaos without observability

Injecting faults into a system you cannot observe teaches you nothing and may cause an outage. Before running chaos experiments, make sure you can measure steady state and see the experiment's effect in your telemetry. A chaos experiment is also an observability test: if you cannot see what happened, that is the first finding.

A game day is a planned exercise in which a team injects a significant failure (losing a zone, failing over a database) while engineers respond as in a real incident. Game days test the system, the runbooks, the alerts and the people.

Incidents and blameless postmortems

At a glance
  • Responding to incidents
  • Learning from them without blame

When an incident happens, the priority is mitigation: restore service first, understand later. Rolling back a deployment, failing over, or shedding load are often faster than diagnosing a root cause. Clear roles help: an incident commander coordinates, others investigate, and someone communicates with stakeholders.

Afterwards, a postmortem (or post-incident review) records what happened, its impact, how it was detected and resolved, and what will change. Blameless postmortems, a culture promoted by organisations such as Etsy and Google, focus on how the system made the failure possible rather than who made a mistake. The reasoning is practical: if people fear blame, they hide information, and the organisation stops learning. A person typing the wrong command is never the root cause; the system that allowed a single command to cause an outage is.

Good postmortems also tend to avoid a single "root cause". Complex systems fail through combinations of contributing factors, as Richard Cook's short essay How Complex Systems Fail (1998) argues. The case studies in chapter 16 show this repeatedly.

Lab · Instrument and observe
  1. Take your Gossip Glomers broadcast node (chapter 6) or your Raft implementation (chapter 7). Add OpenTelemetry tracing: one span per message handled, with attributes for message type, source and destination node.
  2. Propagate the trace context inside messages (for example as a traceparent field in the message body).
  3. Export traces to a local backend (the OpenTelemetry Collector with Grafana Tempo or Jaeger, for example in Docker).
  4. Trigger a leader election (Raft) or a partition (broadcast) and find it in your traces. How long did it take, and which messages were involved?
  5. Define one SLI for your system (for example, the proportion of broadcast messages known to all nodes within 1 second) and measure it.
  6. Take out the one-page system model you wrote in chapter 3. Which of its assumptions could your telemetry confirm or refute, and which failures in it would your current signals not show at all?

In Appendix A: A.15 Instrument and observe (hints)

Deep dive Observability and the cost of telemetry

Telemetry is not free. At scale, the cost of storing logs and traces can rival the cost of the systems they describe, and high-cardinality metrics can overwhelm time-series databases. Practical observability therefore involves engineering trade-offs: tail sampling of traces, log levels and dynamic verbosity, dropping or aggregating labels in collectors, and retention tiers. A useful principle is to keep detailed data for a short time and aggregated data for a long time, and to make sure that what you keep can answer the questions your SLOs and incident reviews actually ask. The OpenTelemetry Collector's processors (filtering, sampling, attribute transformation) are a common place to enforce these choices before data reaches storage.

Synthesis

Synthesis

The essential question asked how you can know what a system of hundreds of independent parts is doing. The answer has two halves. Observability gives you the means to ask: metrics for the big picture, traces for the path of a request, logs for the details, profiles for resource use, all connected by shared identifiers. SLOs give you the standard against which to judge what you see, expressed in terms of user experience rather than machine health. That is what would have turned the green dashboard of the opening into a red one: a probe and an SLI measuring what users actually received.

Testing then closes the loop. Chaos engineering and fault injection deliberately create the conditions of chapters 2 to 11 (lost messages, partitions, crashes, overload), and observability lets you see whether the system behaved as designed. A system you have never seen fail is a system whose failure behaviour you do not know.

Key terms

Monitoring, observability, metric, cardinality, histogram, log (structured), trace, span, context propagation, W3C Trace Context / traceparent, OpenTelemetry, head / tail sampling, exemplar, profile (continuous profiling), golden signals, RED, USE, synthetic probe, SLI / SLO / SLA, error budget, burn rate, property-based testing, deterministic simulation testing, TLA+, chaos engineering, steady state, blast radius, game day, blameless postmortem.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the four golden signals and the three letters of RED and USE.
  2. (Understand) Why should latency be recorded as a histogram rather than an average?
  3. (Understand) Explain how a traceparent header connects spans across services.
  4. (Apply) A service has an SLO of 99.95 % over 30 days and serves 50 million requests in that period. How many requests may fail? What burn rate consumes the whole budget in 3 days?
  5. (Analyze) Compare head and tail sampling for a system where only 0.01 % of requests fail. Which would you choose and why?
  6. (Evaluate) A team proposes to page on-call engineers whenever any instance's CPU exceeds 80 %. Evaluate the proposal and suggest an alternative.
  7. (Create) Design a chaos experiment that tests whether a service survives the loss of one Availability Zone. Specify steady state, hypothesis, injection, blast-radius controls and abort conditions.

Reflection

Think of the last incident you were involved in or read about. How was it detected: by an alert, by a customer, or by chance? What signal would have detected it sooner?

Looking ahead

You now have the technical toolkit, from system models to observability. Part IV turns to the decisions that shape real systems over years: how to divide a system between teams (chapter 13, microservices), how to change interfaces without breaking the people who depend on them (chapter 14, versioning and backward compatibility), and how to put changes into production safely (chapter 15, deployment). Chapter 16 then applies everything to real outages.

Further reading

  • Google, Site Reliability Engineering (2016) and The Site Reliability Workbook (2018), chapters on monitoring, SLOs and alerting. Free online.
  • Majors, Fong-Jones and Miranda, Observability Engineering (O'Reilly, 2022).
  • Newcombe et al., How Amazon Web Services Uses Formal Methods (CACM, 2015).
  • principlesofchaos.org, the principles of chaos engineering.
  • Richard Cook, How Complex Systems Fail (1998). Two pages.
Part IV · Architecture and change
Part IV · Introduction

Being a Good Neighbour

Why teams, interfaces and deployments belong in a book about distributed systems

So far, this book has treated a distributed system as a collection of machines: nodes that crash and recover, networks that drop and delay messages, clocks that drift, replicas that disagree. Parts I to III were about making those machines cooperate despite partial failure.

But look at any real distributed system and you will find something else that is just as distributed: people. A booking platform is not built by one programmer. It is built and changed by many teams, each owning a few services, each deploying on its own schedule, each depending on services other teams own, and each with consumers of its own: other teams, partner companies, mobile apps installed on phones you will never see, and customers who simply expect the train departure board to work. Every network boundary in the system is, very often, also a boundary between teams. And every boundary between teams tends to become a network boundary, as Conway's law predicts (chapter 13).

That is why Part IV exists. Its subject is still partial failure, in a form the earlier parts only touched on: partial change.

Partial change is a kind of partial failure

A single program changes all at once. You stop it, replace it and start it again. A distributed system never does. At any moment, some services run the new version and some the old; some clients have upgraded and some have not; messages written last month wait in queues next to messages written a second ago; a rollback sends part of the system back in time while the rest stays where it was. Chapter 15 calls this the distribution of versions that every real system runs.

This is not a rare situation. Google's Site Reliability Engineering book reports that roughly 70 % of outages are due to changes in a live system. Several of the incidents in chapter 16 began with a change: a network configuration, a capacity command, an automation run, a permission update. The techniques of Parts I to III (timeouts, idempotency, consensus, resilience patterns) protect a system against machines that fail. Part IV is about protecting a system, and everyone who depends on it, against the changes its own owners make.

A system is somebody else's dependency

The most useful shift in perspective for this part of the book is to see your own service the way its consumers see it. To you, it is code you understand and can change. To them, it is a dependency: something they cannot see inside, cannot control, and must trust to keep behaving as it did yesterday. Hyrum's Law (chapter 14) says they will depend on everything they can observe, whether or not you promised it.

You are, in other words, somebody's neighbour. And distributed systems reward good neighbours:

  1. Own your boundary. Draw service boundaries where teams need to work independently, own what is inside completely, and do not reach into other teams' data (chapter 13).
  2. Never surprise your consumers. Add, do not change; keep old and new versions compatible in both directions; treat behaviour, not only schemas, as a promise (chapter 14).
  3. Change in small, reversible steps. Deploy progressively, observe, and be able to roll back at any point; treat configuration and data changes as deployments too (chapter 15).
  4. Fail politely. When you are overloaded, say so quickly and clearly, with rate limits, 429 or 503 and a Retry-After, instead of timing out and letting your problem become everyone's (chapter 11).
  5. Be a considerate client. Bound your retries, back off with jitter, respect deadlines and cache what you can, so that your recovery never becomes your neighbour's outage (chapters 2 and 11).

The first three are the subject of the next three chapters. The last two were covered in chapter 11, seen from the other side of the fence.

How the chapters fit together

  • Chapter 13, Microservices, properly understood, asks where boundaries should go. It argues that microservices were invented to let teams deploy independently, and that splitting further than that adds network hops, failure points and cost without adding independence.
  • Chapter 14, Versioning and backward compatibility, asks how a team can change its service without coordinating with everyone who depends on it, and shows why the answer has as much to do with time and behaviour as with schemas.
  • Chapter 15, Deployment, rollback and roll forward, asks how to put a change into a running system so that a mistake reaches as few people as possible, for as short a time as possible.
  • Chapter 16, Case studies, reads five real incidents with everything in the book, and shows how often machine failures and human changes combine.
Essential question

How do you keep changing a system that many teams and customers depend on, without ever forcing them to change at the same moment as you?

Keep this question in mind as you read. Every chapter of Part IV is a partial answer to it, and the answers turn out to be the same ideas you have met throughout the book, applied to people and versions instead of machines: independence instead of coordination, compatibility instead of lockstep, and small reversible steps instead of big, irreversible ones.

Chapter 13

Microservices, Properly Understood

A team-sized answer to an organisational problem, and what happens when it is forgotten

Start with a quiz. How big should a microservice be?

  • (a) A few hundred lines of code, doing one small thing.
  • (b) As small as possible; a team might own fifteen of them.
  • (c) As large as one team can own, build, deploy and run on its own.

Many conference talks, blog posts and cloud reference architectures suggest (a) or (b). The people who named the style were closer to (c). In their 2014 article, James Lewis and Martin Fowler reported that "the largest sizes reported follow Amazon's notion of the Two Pizza Team", no more than about a dozen people, and they organised their whole description around teams, business capabilities and independent deployment, not around lines of code.

The difference matters, and it costs money. In 2023, a team at Amazon Prime Video described how they rebuilt one internal monitoring service, which had been built from distributed serverless components, as a single process. The system had been unable to handle more than about 5 % of its expected load, and the move cut its infrastructure cost by about 90 %. It was one tool, not all of Prime Video, but the story spread because so many engineers recognised the pattern. This chapter is about where microservices came from, what problem they solve, and how to tell a good split from an expensive one.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Explain the organisational problem that microservices were invented to solve, and define a monolith in terms of teams and deployment units (Understand)
  • Describe the history of the style, from service-oriented architecture to the 2014 definition and the later backlash (Remember/Understand)
  • Calculate the latency and availability cost of network hops using typical latency numbers (Apply)
  • Distinguish a well-sized service from a distributed monolith and from nano-services, and justify when to split further (Analyze/Evaluate)
  • Design a modular monolith with enforced boundaries that can later be split along those boundaries (Create)
  • Design communication between team-owned services that limits coupling, preferring asynchronous collaboration across team boundaries (Create)
Essential question

Is the size of a service a technical decision, an organisational one, or both?

Before you start

The introduction to Part IV argued that a distributed system is also distributed between teams, and that your service is somebody else's dependency. This chapter starts with the most basic question that raises: where should the boundaries between teams' services go? You have the technical tools: the cost of remote calls (chapter 2), partial failure and availability arithmetic (chapter 1), state-reconstructing versus state-resident services (chapter 10), resilience patterns (chapter 11) and tracing (chapter 12). This chapter applies all of them to one of the most consequential and most misunderstood architectural decisions in the industry.

The chapter in brief: the problem microservices solve; a short history; the right size; the cost of a network hop; communication across team boundaries; the distributed monolith anti-pattern; the modular monolith; incentives, fashion and evidence; how to get there.

The problem microservices solve

At a glance
  • A monolith defined by teams, not by size
  • Symptoms of a shared deployment unit
  • Release trains as a symptom, not a cure
  • Conway's law

Consider a ticketing system built by one team of seven people. It is 150,000 lines of code, deployed as one unit. Is it a monolith that should be broken up? For most purposes, no. The team knows the code, deploys it when it wants, and rolls it back when it must. Nobody needs to coordinate with anybody.

Now let five teams work on the same code base and the same deployable artifact. Every release contains changes from five teams. A bug in one team's feature blocks everyone's release. A rollback undoes everyone's work. Test suites grow until they take hours, merge conflicts multiply, and the release date becomes a negotiation. Organisations respond by batching changes into large, infrequent releases with coordinated planning, and the problems get worse, because larger batches carry more risk.

Definition · Monolith (in the microservices sense)

A deployment unit that is changed by several teams. The defining problem is not size in lines of code, but the need for teams to coordinate to build, test, release and roll back a shared artifact.

Seen this way, the classic symptoms are all symptoms of shared ownership:

  • frequent rollbacks that affect unrelated features;
  • long stabilisation periods and code freezes before releases;
  • release managers, release calendars and change advisory boards whose main job is coordination;
  • release trains: in the Scaled Agile Framework (SAFe), an Agile Release Train is a long-lived "team of agile teams", typically 50–125 people, that plans, commits, develops and delivers together on a fixed cadence of several weeks.

A release train is a sensible way to coordinate many teams that share one release. But the need for it is itself a signal: the teams are coupled through a shared deployment unit.

Note · Conway's law

In 1968, Melvin Conway observed that "any organization that designs a system will produce a design whose structure is a copy of the organization's communication structure." Lewis and Fowler quoted this in their microservices article. The useful reading for this chapter: if you want teams to work independently, the system must have boundaries that let them. Microservices are one way of drawing those boundaries in the deployed system, so that each team owns a unit it can release on its own.

The original solution, then, is simple to state: split the shared deployment unit into pieces that each team can own completely, and let them communicate over well-defined interfaces. Each team can then build, test, deploy and roll back its own service on its own schedule. Deploying becomes easier, not harder, because each deployment contains one team's changes, which that team understands.

Test yourself

A 600,000-line application is owned and deployed by one team of nine engineers, who release it several times a week without problems. Their manager asks whether they should "move to microservices". Using this section, what would you ask first?

Show answer

Ask what problem the change would solve. The defining problem microservices address, several teams coordinating to release one artifact, does not exist here. Valid reasons might still exist (for example a component with very different scaling needs, or a security boundary), but "the code base is large" is not one of them on its own. If none applies, the team would pay the costs of distribution (network hops, partial failure, versioning, more infrastructure) for no organisational benefit.

A short history

At a glance
  • From service-oriented architecture to microservices
  • Amazon, Netflix and the naming of the style
  • Monolith first, and the backlash against nano-services

Service-oriented architecture (SOA) in the early 2000s already split systems into services, but it was often implemented with heavyweight middleware: enterprise service buses that held business logic, centrally governed schemas, and shared databases. Many organisations found that it centralised control instead of distributing it.

Amazon took a different path in the same period, moving to services owned end to end by small teams (the "two-pizza teams"). In a 2006 interview with ACM Queue, Amazon's CTO Werner Vogels summarised the operating model as "You build it, you run it": the team that writes a service also operates it in production.

Netflix, moving from its own data centres to AWS between 2008 and 2016, built hundreds of fine-grained services and published much of its tooling (circuit breakers, service discovery, chaos tools) as open source. Its architects, notably Adrian Cockcroft, described the approach as "fine-grained SOA".

The name arrived in 2011–2012, when a group of software architects at a workshop near Venice discussed a common style and settled on "microservices". In March 2014, James Lewis and Martin Fowler published Microservices, which defined the style as "an approach to developing a single application as a suite of small services, each running in its own process and communicating with lightweight mechanisms", and listed nine characteristics:

Characteristic What it means
Componentisation via services Services are the unit of independent deployment and replacement
Organised around business capabilities Teams own a business area end to end, not a technical layer
Products, not projects Teams own a service for its lifetime, not until a project ends
Smart endpoints and dumb pipes Logic lives in the services; the transport (HTTP, lightweight messaging) is simple
Decentralised governance Teams choose their own tools within reason
Decentralised data management Each service owns its data; no shared database
Infrastructure automation Automated testing and deployment make many small deployments affordable
Design for failure Every remote call can fail, and services must cope gracefully
Evolutionary design Services can be replaced and re-cut as understanding improves

Almost immediately, the authors added warnings. In 2015, Fowler wrote Microservice Premium (microservices carry a cost that only pays off for sufficiently complex systems) and Monolith First, observing that "almost all the successful microservice stories have started with a monolith that got too big and was broken up".

The warnings were not always heeded. In the following years, many organisations adopted very fine-grained services, sometimes called nano-services, and several later wrote publicly about the costs:

  • In 2018, Segment described consolidating more than 140 destination services back into one, after a small team found that maintaining them had become most of its work.
  • In 2020, Uber, with roughly 2,200 critical microservices, introduced Domain-Oriented Microservice Architecture (DOMA), grouping services into domains behind gateways. Its engineers described debugging that required working "through around 50 services across 12 different teams", and services "that appear to be independent" but "all have to be deployed together".
  • In 2023, the Prime Video monitoring team described the 90 % cost reduction from the opening.

The right size for a service

At a glance
  • One team, one service (or a few): the default
  • Good reasons to split further
  • Bad reasons to split
  • Cognitive load as the real size limit

If the original problem is coordination between teams, the natural default follows: each team owns one service, or a small number of services, that it can build, deploy and run on its own. Such a service may well be 50,000 or 150,000 lines of code. What matters is that one team can hold it in their heads and change it without asking anyone.

The book Team Topologies (Matthew Skelton and Manuel Pais, 2019) gives a useful way to think about this: the limit is the team's cognitive load, the amount of domain and technical complexity a team can handle while still delivering well. A service boundary should keep each team's load manageable. Splitting a team's work into fifteen services usually increases its cognitive load: now it must also understand fifteen deployments, fifteen sets of dashboards, and the network between them.

There are good reasons to split a team's service further, but they are specific:

Good reasons to split further Bad reasons to split
A part needs to scale very differently (for example a CPU-heavy image pipeline beside a light API) "Microservices should be small"
A part needs failure isolation: if it crashes, the rest must not One service per database table or entity ("entity services")
A security or compliance boundary (payment data, personal data) A reference architecture or vendor diagram suggests it
A part needs a different runtime or hardware (GPUs, a different language for a library) To use more cloud services or a new technology
A part changes at a very different pace or with different risk Because each function "could be" a separate deployment
A part has very different availability needs To give each developer their own service
Common mistake · Entity services

A common anti-pattern is to create one service per noun: a Customer service, an Order service, a Product service, each a thin wrapper around a table. Almost every business operation then needs several of them, synchronously, in sequence. Michael Nygard has called this "the entity service anti-pattern". Services should follow business capabilities (for example "ticket sales" or "traffic disruption information"), which can complete most operations with their own data.

The cost of a network hop

At a glance
  • What chapter 2's latency numbers mean for service boundaries
  • What a chain of services costs in latency and availability
  • The hidden infrastructure cost of each service

Every service boundary turns some function calls into network calls. Chapter 2's table of latency numbers showed what that means: a call to another service in the same data centre costs around a million times more than a function call in the same process, before the other service does any work at all, and a call between regions costs tens to hundreds of millions of times more. In a monolith, that difference is paid at a handful of boundaries (the database, a few external services). In a microservice architecture, it is paid at every boundary you draw.

Example · Twenty calls, two ways

Scenario: Handling a booking request involves twenty steps: validating the passenger, checking the train, pricing, seat allocation, loyalty points, and so on.

In one service: twenty function calls. Their overhead is microseconds in total; the request is dominated by its few database queries, say 5 ms.

As twenty nano-services called in sequence, each hop costing about 2 ms with serialisation and proxies:

  • Latency: at least 20 × 2 ms = 40 ms of pure communication, and the tail is far worse, because each hop adds its own slow responses (chapter 10).
  • Availability: if each service is 99.9 % available, the chain is at most 0.999²⁰ ≈ 98.0 % available, about 7 days of failed requests per year (chapter 1).
  • Consistency: a booking now spans twenty data stores, so it needs a saga or distributed transaction (chapter 8) where a local transaction would have done.

Key insight: The architecture did not make any step faster. It added a network, twenty failure points and a consistency problem to work that fit in one process.

There is also a cost per service that has nothing to do with latency. Each service typically brings its own replicas (often at least two or three for availability, even when idle), a sidecar proxy, a build and deployment pipeline, dashboards and alerts, a log stream, secrets and certificates, and on-call knowledge. Multiplied by hundreds of services, this becomes a large share of an organisation's infrastructure bill and of its engineers' time. These costs are worth paying when they buy team independence. They are pure waste when they do not.

Test yourself

In one sentence: why does chapter 1's fallacy "latency is zero" matter more in a microservice architecture than in a monolith?

Show answer

Because every service boundary converts in-process calls costing nanoseconds into network calls costing milliseconds, a difference of about a million times, so the number and arrangement of boundaries directly determines latency, tail latency and availability.

Communication across team boundaries

At a glance
  • The danger of long synchronous chains across teams
  • Asynchronous collaboration and local copies of data
  • When synchronous calls are fine

When services owned by different teams call each other synchronously in long chains, a single user request becomes a distributed call stack across many teams. Its latency is the sum of all of their latencies; its availability is the product of all of theirs; and to debug it, someone must follow a trace through code owned by people they may never have met. Each team is now coupled to every other team in the chain, in time and in failure, which is the coordination problem microservices were meant to remove, reintroduced at runtime.

That is why much of the early guidance on microservices, and related styles such as Self-Contained Systems (scs-architecture.org), recommends that communication between team-owned systems be asynchronous wherever possible. The Self-Contained Systems principles say it directly: each system "is owned by one team", and should remain usable "even if other SCSs are temporarily offline".

Asynchronous collaboration in practice

  • Publish events, not commands. When something changes in your domain ("ticket purchased", "train cancelled"), publish an event. Other teams' services subscribe and react in their own time.
  • Keep local copies of the data you need. Instead of calling the timetable service on every request, a service can keep its own copy of the timetable data it needs, updated from events. Martin Fowler calls this event-carried state transfer. It is the state-resident idea of chapter 10 applied across teams: data is consulted locally and kept up to date by subscription, not reconstructed by a remote call per request.
  • Use the tools of Part II. Events arrive late, twice or out of order (chapter 2), so consumers must be idempotent, use version numbers, and accept eventual consistency (chapters 6 and 9). Publishing reliably needs the outbox pattern (chapter 8).

When synchronous calls are fine

Asynchronous collaboration is a default, not a dogma. Synchronous calls are reasonable when the caller genuinely needs an authoritative answer right now (for example authorising a payment), when the call stays inside one team's boundary, or when the chain is short. In those cases, apply everything from chapter 11: deadlines, a single retry layer with budgets, circuit breakers or load shedding, and a degraded mode when the dependency is down.

The distributed monolith: an anti-pattern

At a glance
  • Services that must be deployed together
  • Synchronous coupling as the defining mistake
  • The signs, and the way out

The worst outcome is to get the costs of both styles and the benefits of neither: a distributed monolith. Uber's DOMA article described exactly this: services "that appear to be independent all have to be deployed together".

Definition · Distributed monolith (anti-pattern)

A set of "microservices" that cannot be changed, deployed or kept running independently, usually because they are wired together with synchronous calls, shared data or shared release schedules. It has all the costs of a distributed system (network hops, partial failure, versioning across process boundaries, more infrastructure) and all the coupling of a monolith.

The most common way to build one is to take a monolith's internal function calls and turn them, one for one, into synchronous HTTP or gRPC calls between services. Lewis and Fowler warned about this in 2014: "a naive conversion from in-memory method calls to RPC leads to chatty communications which don't perform well." The call graph stays the same; only now every edge is a network hop with its own latency, its own failure mode and its own version. If service A cannot answer without B, and B cannot answer without C, then A, B and C are one system that happens to be deployed in three pieces: when C is slow, A is slow; when C is down, A is down; and when C changes its API, A and B must change in step.

Chatty systems break when latency changes

Chatty communication has a property that is easy to miss until it is too late: the cost of a request is multiplied by the number of sequential round trips it makes. A design that is comfortably fast today can become unusable after a change that looks harmless, because latency does not stay constant:

  • a service or database is moved to another data centre or Availability Zone (for example, going from one site to two for resilience);
  • a failover sends traffic to a region further away;
  • a service mesh, proxy or TLS layer is added to every hop;
  • the network degrades without failing: a little packet loss or congestion raises round-trip times and adds retransmission delays (chapter 3).
Example · Same code, new data centre

Scenario: Rendering a booking overview makes 150 sequential calls: the booking service asks the seat service about each seat, which asks the timetable service about each stop. Today everything runs in one data centre, with about 0.3 ms per round trip.

Today: 150 × 0.3 ms ≈ 45 ms of network time. Nobody notices.

After moving the seat and timetable services to a second data centre, roughly 2 ms away: 150 × 2 ms ≈ 300 ms. Users notice, and the 99th percentile is much worse.

After a failover to a region about 25 ms away (roughly Stockholm to Frankfurt): 150 × 25 ms ≈ 3.75 seconds. The calling service's 1-second timeout now fires on almost every request, clients retry, and the retries add load to services that are already slow (chapter 11). The system is not slow; it is down.

Key insight: Nothing in the code changed. The design was only ever correct for one specific latency. A chunky design (one call that fetches all seats with their stops, or a local copy kept up to date by events) makes, say, 3 round trips instead of 150, and survives the same move with a few extra milliseconds.

This is not hypothetical. In GitHub's October 2018 incident (chapter 16), databases were failed over from the US East Coast to the West Coast. GitHub's postmortem explains that applications running on the East Coast were "unable to cope with the additional latency introduced by a cross-country round trip for the majority of their database calls". Simply running against the West Coast was therefore not an option, which is part of why GitHub chose the slower path of restoring and failing back. When you design how components talk to each other, ask: how many sequential round trips does one user request make, and what happens to it if every round trip becomes ten times slower?

Signs that you have one:

  • a single user request makes long chains of synchronous calls between services, especially across teams;
  • a feature requires coordinated changes and deployments across several services;
  • services share a database, or share libraries containing business logic;
  • a breaking change in one service's API must be released in lockstep with its callers (chapter 14);
  • releases still need a release train or a change board across teams;
  • an outage of one "small" service takes down many others;
  • nobody can draw the dependency graph from memory.

The cure is rarely "more services". Usually it is fewer, larger services aligned with teams and business capabilities, strict API compatibility (chapter 14), and asynchronous collaboration across team boundaries. Where the services are owned by one team anyway, the cure is often to bring them back together into a modular monolith.

The modular monolith: the recommended starting point

At a glance
  • One deployment unit, many well-defined modules
  • How to enforce the boundaries
  • Extracting a module when you really need to

For a system built by one team or a few, the modular monolith is usually the best architecture available. It is cheap to build and run, simple to deploy, fast (its internal calls cost nanoseconds, not milliseconds), and, if it is built with discipline, easy to split later along boundaries that already exist.

Definition · Modular monolith

A single deployment unit whose code is divided into modules organised around business capabilities, with enforced boundaries: each module exposes a small published interface, owns its own data, and may only be used through that interface.

What makes it modular rather than just a monolith is that the boundaries are enforced by tools, not by good intentions:

  • Module interfaces. Each module publishes a small API (a set of classes, functions or a facade); everything else is internal. Language features help: Java's module system, internal visibility in .NET and Kotlin, package-private code, separate projects in a solution.
  • Dependency checks in the build. Architecture tests fail the build when a module reaches into another's internals or a forbidden dependency appears. Examples include ArchUnit for Java, Spring Modulith (which also verifies module structure in Spring applications), and Shopify's open-source Packwerk for Ruby.
  • Data ownership. Each module owns its tables or schema; no module reads another module's tables directly. This is the rule most often broken, and the one that matters most for later extraction.
  • Communication inside the process. Modules call each other's interfaces directly, or publish in-process events for loose coupling. The events can later travel over a message broker without changing the modules' logic.
Example · Shopify's modular monolith

Shopify is one of the best-known examples. In 2019, Kirsten Westeinde described why the company chose to restructure its very large Ruby on Rails monolith into components organised by business domain (such as billing and orders) instead of breaking it into microservices: "We realised all the things we liked about our monolith, were a result of the code living in and being deployed to one place." The team built tooling to measure and enforce boundaries between components, and later open-sourced Packwerk for this purpose.

Key insight: The goal was modularity and team autonomy within one deployment unit, keeping the simplicity of one place to build, test and deploy.

Extracting a module when you need to

Because a well-built modular monolith already has the boundaries, extracting a module into its own service becomes a contained engineering task rather than a rewrite. Extract when there is a specific reason from the table earlier in this chapter: a new team will own the module, it needs to scale very differently, it needs failure or security isolation, or it needs different hardware. Then:

  1. Make sure the module's interface is the only way in, and that it owns its data (fix this first, inside the monolith).
  2. Put the interface behind a network boundary, prefer asynchronous events for collaboration with the rest, and apply the compatibility rules of chapter 14.
  3. Move the module's data to its own store, using the expand-and-contract steps of chapter 14.
  4. Route traffic gradually to the new service (strangler fig, below), with the deployment safety of chapter 15.
Test yourself

A team of eight runs eleven services that call each other synchronously and are always deployed together. What would you recommend, and what would you check before you recommend it?

Show answer

This is a distributed monolith owned by one team, so the coordination benefit of separate services does not exist, while the costs do. A strong candidate is to merge the services into a modular monolith with enforced module boundaries, keeping each former service as a module. Before recommending it, check whether any of the services has a genuine reason to stay separate: very different scaling needs, a security or compliance boundary, a need for failure isolation, or different hardware. Those can stay separate (and should then be decoupled, ideally asynchronously); the rest can be merged.

Incentives, fashion and evidence

At a glance
  • Why fine-grained microservices became fashionable
  • A critical perspective, and its limits

Why did the industry drift so far from "one team, one service"? Several forces pushed in the same direction, and it is worth naming them so you can recognise them:

  • Success stories from very large organisations (Netflix, Amazon, Uber) were copied by organisations a hundred times smaller, without their problems or their platform teams.
  • Tooling and platforms (containers, Kubernetes, service meshes, serverless functions) made creating a new service very cheap at the moment of creation, while the cost of running it for years stayed hidden.
  • Commercial incentives. Fine-grained architectures consume more infrastructure: more instances, more managed services, more network traffic, more observability data. Cloud providers and tool vendors benefit from that consumption, and their reference architectures and training material often promote fine-grained, service-heavy designs. Many practitioners argue that this has shaped the industry's idea of "best practice". This is a critical perspective, not a proven claim about intent: vendors also publish warnings, and the Prime Video case was published on Amazon's own technology blog. But it is a good reason to ask, of any reference architecture: who pays for this, and who benefits?
Note · Weighing the evidence

Well supported: the organisational motivation for microservices (Conway's law, independent deployment, team autonomy) is central to the original definitions; network calls are orders of magnitude more expensive than in-process calls; very fine-grained designs have led several well-known organisations to consolidate. Contested: where exactly the right size lies. Some organisations operate thousands of services successfully, with strong platform teams and tooling. Opinion: that the industry trend towards nano-services was driven mainly by vendor incentives. It is plausible and widely argued, but motives are hard to prove.

How to get there

At a glance
  • Split along team and capability boundaries
  • The strangler fig pattern
  • A checklist before creating a new service

When a shared deployment unit really does need splitting, do it incrementally. Martin Fowler's strangler fig pattern (named after vines that grow around a tree until they replace it) routes traffic for one capability at a time to a new service, while the rest stays in the old system, until the old system can be retired. Start with a capability that has clear boundaries, its own data, and one team ready to own it.

Tip · Before you create a new service, answer these questions
  1. Which team will own it, build it, deploy it and carry its pager for years?
  2. What problem does the boundary solve: team independence, scaling, isolation, security? If the answer is "none", keep it in-process.
  3. Can it be deployed alone, without coordinating with any other service?
  4. Does it own its data, or will it share a database?
  5. How will it communicate with other teams' services: events and local copies, or synchronous calls? If synchronous, what is the deadline and the fallback?
  6. What does it cost to run when idle, including replicas, pipelines, dashboards and on-call?
Lab · Measure the hop, and map your deployment units
  1. Measure the cost of the same small operation (for example, looking up a price in a map) as: an in-process function call; an HTTP call over localhost; an HTTP call to another container on the same machine; and, if available, a call to another machine in the same data centre and in another Availability Zone. Use a constant-rate load generator and record the median and 99th percentile.
  2. Take a system you know and draw its deployment units. For each, write which teams change it. Mark every unit changed by more than one team (a monolith in this chapter's sense) and every team that owns more than three units.
  3. For one typical user request, count the synchronous network hops and estimate their latency and combined availability using your measurements.
  4. Notebook: if you could move one boundary, which would it be, and why?

Appendix A describes this lab in more detail, with hints.

In Appendix A: A.16 Measure the hop, and map your deployment units (hints)

Synthesis

Synthesis

Return to the quiz. Answer (c), "as large as one team can own, build, deploy and run on its own", is the answer that follows from the problem microservices were invented to solve. A monolith, in this sense, is not a large program; it is a deployment unit shared by several teams, and release trains are what organisations build to cope with one. Splitting that unit along team and business-capability boundaries removes the coordination and makes deployment easier. Splitting further, without a specific reason, buys the costs of chapters 1–11 (network hops a million times slower than function calls, multiplied failure points, distributed transactions) and returns little.

So the size of a service is both an organisational and a technical decision, in that order. Draw boundaries where teams need independence; split further only where a genuine technical need (scaling, isolation, security) justifies paying for another network hop; and let teams collaborate across boundaries asynchronously, so that each one keeps working when the others do not. Where one team or a few own the system, start with a modular monolith: cheap, fast and easy to split along its enforced boundaries when a real need appears. And avoid the anti-pattern at the other extreme: "microservices" wired together with chains of synchronous calls are a distributed monolith, with the costs of distribution and none of its freedom. The next chapter tackles the one discipline that makes independent deployment possible at all: changing an interface without breaking the people who depend on it.

Key terms

Monolith (shared deployment unit), microservice, Conway's law, Agile Release Train, two-pizza team, you build it, you run it, business capability, cognitive load, nano-service, entity service, distributed monolith (anti-pattern), modular monolith, module boundary enforcement, event-carried state transfer, Self-Contained Systems, strangler fig.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) State Lewis and Fowler's definition of microservices and name four of their nine characteristics.
  2. (Understand) Explain why, in this chapter's sense, a 150,000-line application owned by one team is not a monolith, but a 40,000-line application changed by four teams is.
  3. (Understand) Why is the need for a release train a symptom of coupling?
  4. (Apply) A request passes synchronously through 12 services, each 99.95 % available and adding about 3 ms. Estimate the minimum added latency and the maximum availability of the chain.
  5. (Analyze) List four signs of a distributed monolith and explain how each one reintroduces coordination between teams.
  6. (Apply) A page makes 80 sequential calls at 0.5 ms each. Estimate its network time if the callees move to a site 4 ms away, and if a failover moves them 30 ms away. At what point does a 2-second timeout start to fire?
  7. (Evaluate) A team of six proposes to split its service into 18 services "to follow microservices best practice". Evaluate the proposal using the good and bad reasons in this chapter.
  8. (Create) Redesign a synchronous chain in which a booking service calls a timetable service, a pricing service and a customer service on every request, so that the booking service keeps working if any of them is down. What consistency trade-offs does your design accept?
  9. (Create) Sketch a modular monolith for a ticketing system with four business capabilities. Name the modules, their published interfaces, which data each owns, how you would enforce the boundaries in the build, and which module you would extract first, and why.

Reflection

How many deployment units does your team own? How many teams change each of them? If you drew your organisation chart next to your service map, where would Conway's law show?

Looking ahead

Independent deployment has one hard prerequisite: when a team changes its service, every consumer must keep working, during the deployment, after it, and if it is rolled back. Chapter 14 is about versioning and backward compatibility, the discipline that makes this possible.

Further reading

  • James Lewis and Martin Fowler, Microservices (martinfowler.com, 2014). The definition and the nine characteristics.
  • Martin Fowler, Monolith First and Microservice Premium (2015).
  • Sam Newman, Building Microservices (2nd ed., O'Reilly, 2021) and Monolith to Microservices (O'Reilly, 2019). The most balanced book-length treatment, with independent deployability at its centre.
  • Matthew Skelton and Manuel Pais, Team Topologies (2019). Team boundaries, cognitive load and Conway's law in practice.
  • Adam Gluck, Introducing Domain-Oriented Microservice Architecture (Uber Engineering, 2020).
  • Prime Video Tech, Scaling up the Prime Video audio/video monitoring service and reducing costs by 90% (2023).
  • Self-Contained Systems (scs-architecture.org).
  • Kirsten Westeinde, Deconstructing the Monolith: Designing Software that Maximizes Developer Productivity (Shopify Engineering, 2019).
Chapter 14

Versioning and Backward Compatibility

Changing a system without breaking the people who depend on it

A team improves the error message returned when a passenger enters an invalid travel date. The old message read Invalid date; the new one reads The travel date must be within the next 90 days. The change passes code review, the schema is unchanged, the status code is unchanged, and the version number gets a patch bump. Within an hour, a partner's booking app starts showing a generic crash screen. Their code had been checking whether the error message equalled Invalid date, to show a friendly date picker.

Whose fault was it? The partner relied on something nobody had promised. But the team also broke a customer, and in production, the customer's experience is all that counts. Hyrum Wright, then an engineer at Google, put this into words that are now known as Hyrum's Law:

"With a sufficient number of users of an API, it does not matter what you promise in the contract: all observable behaviors of your system will be depended on by somebody."

This chapter is about living with that law. Independent deployment, the whole point of chapter 13, depends on it: you can only release on your own schedule if your release does not break anyone.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Define backward and forward compatibility, and distinguish schema (syntactic) compatibility from behavioural (semantic) compatibility (Understand)
  • Classify API changes as safe or breaking, including the non-obvious cases in responses (Analyze)
  • Reason about mixed versions in time: requests that move between old and new servers, rollbacks, and data written by one version and read by another (Analyze)
  • Plan a schema or database change as a sequence of backward-compatible steps that preserves rollback at every point (Create)
  • Evaluate strategies for versioning, deprecation and contract testing (Evaluate)
Essential question

If every observable behaviour will be depended on by somebody, how can a system ever change?

Before you start

Chapter 2 showed that a retried request may reach a different server; chapter 5 that replicas lag; chapter 10 that a request may land on any instance behind a load balancer. Combine these with a deployment, and you have a system in which different versions of the same code answer requests at the same time. This chapter is about making that safe.

The chapter in brief: two directions of compatibility; schema and behaviour; semantic versioning and why major versions are a cost to customers; which changes are safe; thinking in time during deployments; database and schema changes without breaking rollback; deprecation and contract testing.

Two directions of compatibility

At a glance
  • Backward compatibility: new code works with old clients and old data
  • Forward compatibility: old code works with new clients and new data
  • Why a rolling deployment needs both
Definition · Backward and forward compatibility
  • A change is backward compatible if old clients, old messages and old stored data keep working with the new version.
  • A system is forward compatible if the old version keeps working when it meets new clients, new messages or data written by the new version.

Most teams think about backward compatibility: "will existing clients still work after we deploy?" Forward compatibility is the one that bites during deployments and rollbacks. During a rolling deployment, some instances run the new version and some the old; a client may talk to both. If the new version writes data that the old version cannot read, then rolling back, the one action you take when things go wrong, breaks the system further.

The working rule for any system deployed while running: version N must be able to coexist with version N − 1 in both directions, at the same time, against the same data.

Schema compatibility and behavioural compatibility

At a glance
  • The shape of the data versus what the system does
  • Behavioural compatibility is the harder and more important kind

Compatibility has two layers. Google's API design guidance (AIP-180) distinguishes source, wire and semantic compatibility; for this chapter, two layers are enough:

Layer Question Examples of breaking it
Schema (syntactic) compatibility Can old clients still parse requests and responses? Removing or renaming a field; changing a field's type; making an optional field required
Behavioural (semantic) compatibility Do old clients still get what they reasonably expect? Changing a default; tightening validation; returning results in a different order; new error cases; slower responses; different rounding

Schema compatibility can be checked by tools. Behavioural compatibility cannot, and it is where most real breakage happens. AIP-180 phrases the requirement well: code written against a previous version "must continue to receive what most reasonable developers would expect". Hyrum's Law warns that, at scale, even unreasonable expectations will exist.

Common mistake · "The schema didn't change, so it's compatible"

A schema check proves that clients can still parse your responses. It says nothing about whether they still work. Changing what a field means, when an error is returned, the order of a list, or how long a call takes can break clients without touching the schema. Treat every observable change in behaviour as a potential breaking change, and ask who could depend on it.

Semantic versioning, and why major versions are expensive

At a glance
  • MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH and what each promises
  • A major version moves the cost of change to your customers
  • Why the best APIs almost never break

Semantic Versioning (SemVer, semver.org) is the most common convention for version numbers: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH. You increase

  • PATCH for backward-compatible bug fixes,
  • MINOR for backward-compatible new functionality,
  • MAJOR for incompatible changes.

SemVer is valuable because it is honest: a major version bump tells consumers that their code may break. But notice what it does not do. It does not make the break any cheaper. It only labels it. Every consumer must now find out what changed, change their code, test it, and deploy it, and their consumers may have to do the same. For an internal API used by twenty teams, a major version is twenty unplanned projects. For a public API, it is hundreds.

Note · A strong stance: treat the major version as almost frozen

Many experienced API designers go further than SemVer requires: design so that you never need to increase the major version. Add new capabilities beside the old ones; never change or remove what exists. If a truly incompatible design is needed, treat it as a new product that runs alongside the old one for as long as anyone uses it, not as a version that replaces it. The reason is organisational as much as technical. A breaking change forces your release into lockstep with every consumer, and their consumers, which is exactly the cross-team coordination that independent services were meant to remove (chapter 13). Stripe is a well-known example of taking compatibility seriously: each account stays pinned to the API version it started with, and Stripe's code transforms responses back into the shape older versions expect, so that existing integrations keep working while the API evolves.

Which changes are safe?

At a glance
  • Additive changes are usually safe, but not always
  • The asymmetry between requests and responses
  • Hidden breaking changes in responses

The general rule is add, never change, never remove. But "add" is not always safe, and the asymmetry between requests and responses is worth learning by heart.

Change Usually safe? Why
Add a new endpoint or operation Yes Nobody uses it yet
Add an optional field to a request, with a default that preserves old behaviour Yes Old clients don't send it and get the old behaviour
Add a required field to a request No Old clients don't send it and now fail
Add a field to a response Usually Safe for tolerant readers that ignore unknown fields; breaks strict parsers
Add a new enum value to a response Often not Clients with exhaustive switch statements or strict deserialisers fail on the unknown value
Add a new error code or exception case No Clients have no handling for it; code that treated "not success" as one thing may now misbehave
Change a default value No Behaviour changes for every client that relied on the default
Tighten validation (reject what was accepted) No Requests that worked yesterday fail today
