Loosen validation (accept what was rejected) Usually But downstream systems may receive values they never saw before
Change the order of results, rounding, time zone or format of a value No Behaviourally breaking, even with an identical schema
Make a call much slower, or much faster Sometimes not Clients tuned their timeouts and retries to the old latency (Hyrum's Law)
Example · A new field that round-trips

Scenario: A seat-search API gains a new response field, continuationToken, so that clients can fetch the next page faster. New clients send the token back in their next request. During the rolling deployment, half the servers run the new version.

What happens: A client gets a token from a new server and sends it with its next request, which the load balancer routes to an old server. The old server does not know the field. Depending on its implementation, it ignores it (and silently returns the first page again, a behavioural bug) or rejects the unknown field (an error).

Key insight: Adding a field to a response is only safe if every value you hand out can be sent back to every version that might receive it. Anything a client may echo back (tokens, IDs in a new format, cursors, new enum values) must be understood by the old version before the new version starts producing it.

Test yourself

Which of these changes to a ticket API are backward compatible: (a) adding an optional seatPreference request field, (b) adding "WAITLISTED" to the status enum in responses, (c) returning 409 Conflict where you previously returned 400 for duplicate bookings, (d) adding a co2Grams field to responses?

Show answer

(a) is compatible, provided the default when it is absent preserves the old behaviour. (b) is risky: clients that handle only the statuses they know may crash or mishandle the new value, so it needs to be introduced carefully (documented, and ideally only returned to clients that opt in). (c) is a breaking change: clients that handle 400 specifically now see an unknown error. (d) is compatible for tolerant clients, but check for clients with strict deserialisation, and remember that once added, it can never be removed.

Thinking in time: mixed versions during deployment

At a glance
  • Requests that move between versions
  • Rollback as a time machine
  • Data that outlives the code that wrote it

The hardest part of compatibility is not a single change but time. During a deployment, and for as long as a rollback might happen, you must be able to move backwards and forwards in time in your head, because you never know in which order things will happen. For every change, ask what happens in each of these sequences:

  1. New, then old. A client calls a new server, gets a response, and its next call lands on an old server (the round-trip example above).
  2. Old, then new. A client calls an old server, then a new one. Does the new server accept everything the old one produced?
  3. New, old, new. A long-running workflow alternates between versions while a deployment is in progress.
  4. Rollback. The new version has been running for an hour, writing data and handing out tokens. Now all servers go back to the old version. Can the old version read everything the new one wrote?
  5. Replay. A message produced by the old version sits in a queue for a day, or a client stored a response and sends it later. The new version must still accept it.
  6. Different services at different versions. Service A has been upgraded and service B has not, or B was rolled back and A was not.

This sounds overwhelming at first. In practice, it becomes second nature quickly once you make it a habit: for each feature, ask "how does this behave during the rollout, and how does it behave when we roll it back?" The two techniques below make most changes straightforward.

Technique 1: read before you write

To introduce a new data format, field, token or enum value, split the change into two releases:

  1. Release 1 teaches every version to read and accept the new form, but nothing produces it yet. Deploy it everywhere and let it settle. Now every running version, and the version you would roll back to, understands the new form.
  2. Release 2 starts producing the new form. If you roll back release 2, you return to release 1, which can read everything release 2 wrote.

A feature flag (chapter 15) often replaces the second release: release 1 contains both the reading and the writing code, with writing switched off, and the flag is turned on only once release 1 is fully deployed.

Technique 2: expand and contract

For changes to existing structures (renaming a field, changing a column's type, splitting a table), use expand and contract, also called parallel change: first expand (add the new structure beside the old, and support both), then migrate (move readers and writers to the new one, and copy existing data), then, only when nothing uses the old structure any more, contract (remove it).

Database and schema changes

At a glance
  • Never couple a schema change to a code release
  • A step-by-step plan that preserves rollback
  • Messages and event schemas

Databases are where compatibility mistakes are hardest to undo, because data outlives code. A common and dangerous practice is to run the database migration as part of the application release. If the migration renames a column and the new code uses the new name, then the old code (still running on other instances during the rollout, or restored by a rollback) breaks immediately.

The safe rule: deploy schema changes separately from code changes, and make every schema change work with both the old and the new code. It may take several releases to reach the desired end state, and that is fine. At every step, you must be able to roll back.

Example · Renaming dep_time to departure_time without breaking anything
Step Change Old code New code Rollback safe?
1 Add a nullable column departure_time (schema only) Ignores it Not deployed yet Yes
2 Deploy code that writes both columns and still reads dep_time Works Works Yes
3 Backfill departure_time for existing rows, in small batches Works Works Yes
4 Deploy code that reads departure_time (still writes both) Works Works Yes
5 Deploy code that stops writing dep_time Works only if it reads departure_time, so roll back only to step 4 Works To step 4
6 After a safe period, drop dep_time (schema only) Not running Works One-way: do last

Key insight: Six steps for a rename looks slow. But each step is small, independently deployable and reversible, and nothing ever needs a coordinated "big bang". The only irreversible step comes last, when nothing depends on the old column.

The same principles apply to messages and events. Serialisation formats help if you follow their rules:

  • In Protocol Buffers, never reuse or change the number of a field; mark removed fields as reserved; prefer adding new optional fields. Current implementations preserve unknown fields when they re-serialise a message, which helps messages pass through older services intact.
  • In Avro with a schema registry, configure the compatibility mode (backward, forward or full) and let the registry reject schema changes that violate it.
  • In JSON, write tolerant readers that ignore unknown fields, and never treat an unknown enum value as fatal. Martin Fowler's Tolerant Reader pattern describes this.
Common mistake · Be liberal in what you accept, within limits

The robustness principle ("be conservative in what you send, liberal in what you accept", from Jon Postel's specifications for TCP and IP) helps compatibility, but it has a cost. A server that silently accepts malformed input creates clients that depend on that leniency, which is Hyrum's Law again, and can hide bugs and security problems. RFC 9413 (2023) discusses these trade-offs. Accept unknown additions gracefully; reject invalid data clearly and consistently from the start.

Deprecation and contract testing

At a glance
  • How to retire something without breaking anyone
  • Contract tests that catch breaking changes before release

Sometimes an old capability really must go. Do it as a slow, observable process, not an event:

  1. Measure who uses it. You cannot retire what you cannot see; log usage per client.
  2. Announce the deprecation, with a date and a migration path. For HTTP APIs, the Sunset response header (RFC 8594) can tell clients when an endpoint will be removed.
  3. Help the remaining users migrate, one by one if necessary.
  4. Remove only when usage is zero, or the remaining users have explicitly agreed.

To catch breaking changes before they reach production, use contract tests. In consumer-driven contract testing (for example with the Pact tool), each consumer publishes the requests it makes and the parts of responses it relies on; the provider's build runs those contracts and fails if a change would break any of them. Contract tests make Hyrum's Law visible: they record what consumers actually depend on, not only what the documentation promises.

Lab · Break it on purpose: a mixed-version test
  1. Take a small HTTP or gRPC service you own (or write one with two endpoints). Create version 2 with three changes: a new optional request field, a new response field that clients send back in later requests, and a new enum value in a response.
  2. Run version 1 and version 2 side by side behind a load balancer that alternates between them.
  3. Write a client test that performs the sequences from this chapter: new then old, old then new, new-old-new, and a rollback (stop version 2 after it has written data).
  4. Record which sequences fail and why. Then fix each failure using the read-before-write technique or a feature flag, and run the sequences again.

Appendix A describes this lab in more detail, with hints.

In Appendix A: A.17 Break it on purpose: a mixed-version test (hints)

Synthesis

Synthesis

The essential question was how a system can change at all if every observable behaviour will be depended on. The answer is: by adding rather than changing, by separating reading from writing in time, and by keeping every step reversible. Schema compatibility is the easy, tool-checkable part. Behavioural compatibility (defaults, errors, ordering, timing) is where clients break, and it requires judgement and contract tests. Time is the hidden dimension: during every deployment, old and new versions coexist, and a rollback sends data written by the new version back to the old one.

A major version bump does not make a break cheaper; it passes the cost to every consumer and forces releases into lockstep. That is why mature APIs almost never break, and why compatibility is the precondition for the independent deployment that microservices promise. The next chapter turns to the deployment itself: how to roll a change out, observe it, and roll it back or forward safely.

Key terms

Hyrum's Law, backward compatibility, forward compatibility, N / N − 1 compatibility, schema (syntactic) compatibility, behavioural (semantic) compatibility, Semantic Versioning, breaking change, tolerant reader, read before write, expand and contract (parallel change), schema migration, deprecation, Sunset header, consumer-driven contract test.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) State Hyrum's Law.
  2. (Understand) Explain the difference between backward and forward compatibility, and why a rollback requires forward compatibility.
  3. (Understand) Why is adding an optional field to a request usually safe, while adding an error code to a response is not?
  4. (Apply) Plan the steps to change a price column from integer öre to a decimal amount in kronor, keeping rollback possible at every step.
  5. (Analyze) A new server version starts issuing booking IDs in a new format. List the sequences in which an old server could encounter one, and how each could fail.
  6. (Evaluate) A team proposes to release version 2.0.0 of an internal API with a cleaner design and ask all 14 consuming teams to migrate within a month. Evaluate the proposal and suggest an alternative.
  7. (Create) Write a one-page compatibility checklist for code review in your team, covering requests, responses, errors, data and rollback.

Reflection

Think of the last change that broke a consumer of your system. Which kind was it: schema, behaviour, or time? What would have caught it?

Looking ahead

Compatibility makes it possible to deploy without coordination. Chapter 15 is about the deployment itself: rolling, blue/green and canary releases, feature flags, automatic rollback, and choosing between rolling back and rolling forward.

Further reading

  • Hyrum Wright, Hyrum's Law (hyrumslaw.com), and Titus Winters, Tom Manshreck and Hyrum Wright, Software Engineering at Google (O'Reilly, 2020).
  • Google, AIP-180: Backwards compatibility (google.aip.dev/180).
  • Tom Preston-Werner, Semantic Versioning 2.0.0 (semver.org).
  • Brandur Leach, APIs as infrastructure: future-proofing Stripe with versioning (Stripe blog, 2017).
  • Martin Fowler, Tolerant Reader and Danilo Sato, Parallel Change (martinfowler.com).
  • Martin Kleppmann and Chris Riccomini, Designing Data-Intensive Applications (2nd ed.), the chapter on encoding and evolution.
Chapter 15

Deployment, Rollback and Roll Forward

Putting changes into a running distributed system, safely and often

On 1 August 2012, Knight Capital, one of the largest trading firms in the United States, began the trading day with new software for a new stock exchange programme. The code had been deployed to its eight order-routing servers over the previous days, by hand. On one of the eight, a technician had not copied the new code.

The new code was switched on by a flag that had once been used for a different, long-retired feature called "Power Peg". On seven servers, the flag activated the new code. On the eighth, it activated the old Power Peg code, which was still present and had not been used for years. That server began sending orders into the market at enormous volume. According to the US Securities and Exchange Commission's 2013 order, in about 45 minutes Knight's systems produced more than 4 million executions in 154 stocks, and Knight realised a loss of about 460 million dollars. The firm did not survive as an independent company.

Every element of that story is a topic of this chapter: a manual deployment that left one machine on an old version, a mixed-version fleet, a reused feature flag, dead code that could still run, and no fast way to stop. None of it required exotic technology to prevent.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Distinguish deploying from releasing, and explain how feature flags separate them (Understand)
  • Compare rolling, blue/green, canary and shadow deployments, and select one for a given risk (Analyze/Evaluate)
  • Design a progressive deployment pipeline with bake times and automatic rollback on alarms (Create)
  • Decide between rolling back and rolling forward, and explain why rollback must always remain possible (Evaluate)
  • Explain why configuration and data changes need the same care as code deployments (Understand)
Essential question

Is deploying more often a risk to reliability, or the way to improve it?

Before you start

Chapter 14 showed how to make a change compatible with the versions around it. This chapter puts that compatibility to work: a deployment is the period during which old and new versions run side by side, and a rollback is a deliberate trip back in time. Chapter 12's observability provides the signals that tell you whether to continue.

The chapter in brief: deploy is not release; deployment strategies; progressive delivery and automatic rollback; feature flags; rolling back and rolling forward; configuration and data are deployments too; batch size, release trains and continuous delivery.

Deploying is not releasing

At a glance
  • Two separate decisions: when code reaches production, and when users see it
  • Dark launches

Deploying puts a new version of the code on production machines. Releasing makes a change visible to users. Traditionally these happened at the same moment, so every deployment was also a risky product event. Separating them changes the risk completely:

  • A deployment that contains new code switched off can be rolled out, observed and rolled back without any user noticing.
  • The feature can then be released gradually, by turning on a flag for internal users, then 1 % of users, then everyone, and turned off in seconds if something goes wrong.

A dark launch goes one step further: the new code runs in production on real traffic, but its results are not shown to users. For example, a new pricing engine computes prices in parallel with the old one, and the two are compared, before the new one is used.

Deployment strategies

At a glance
  • Recreate, rolling, blue/green, canary and shadow
  • What each one costs and what it protects against
Strategy How it works Rollback Main risk or cost
Recreate Stop all old instances, start all new ones Redeploy the old version Downtime; no mixed versions, but no safety either
Rolling Replace instances a few at a time (in Kubernetes, controlled by maxSurge and maxUnavailable) Roll back the same way Mixed versions during the rollout (chapter 14); a bad version can reach everything before it is noticed
Blue/green Run the new version (green) beside the old (blue), then switch all traffic at once Switch traffic back Double capacity during the switch; shared databases must work with both versions
Canary Send a small fraction of traffic (or one host, one zone) to the new version, compare its metrics with the old, then expand step by step Stop sending traffic to the canary Needs good metrics and enough traffic to detect problems; mixed versions throughout
Shadow (mirroring) Copy production requests to the new version but discard its responses Nothing to roll back Only for safe, read-only work; side effects (emails, payments) must be disabled

These are not mutually exclusive. A common pattern is canary, then rolling: deploy to one host and watch it, then roll out progressively across the fleet.

Progressive delivery and automatic rollback

At a glance
  • Small blast radius first, then wider
  • Bake time and alarms
  • Rolling back before a human has even looked

Amazon describes its approach in the Builders' Library article Automating safe, hands-off deployments (Clare Liguori, 2020). A change passes through pre-production stages and then reaches production in waves of increasing size. The first production step is often a single box: one instance receives the new version while the rest of the fleet runs the old one for some time, for example 30 minutes or an hour. Each wave is followed by a bake time, during which the pipeline watches the service's alarms on customer-facing metrics such as error rates and latency. If an alarm fires, the pipeline rolls back automatically. The article notes that the rollback is often already in progress by the time the on-call engineer has been paged. The pipeline also refuses to start deployments while alarms are already active, and avoids times when engineers would be slow to respond.

The same ideas are available in open-source tools. In Kubernetes environments managed with Argo CD, for example, Argo Rollouts can run canary and blue/green deployments with automated analysis: it queries metrics (for example from Prometheus) at each step and aborts and reverts the rollout when they cross a threshold.

Tip · What makes a canary meaningful
  • Compare against a baseline, not against a fixed number: the canary's error rate versus the old version's, in the same period.
  • Watch what users experience: the SLIs of chapter 12, not only CPU.
  • Give it enough traffic and time to see rare errors and slow paths. A canary that receives ten requests in five minutes proves almost nothing.
  • Make the canary realistic: same zone mix, same kinds of clients, real dependencies.
  • Make rollback automatic. A human watching graphs is slower and less consistent than an alarm.

Feature flags

At a glance
  • Kinds of flags and their lifetimes
  • Flags as kill switches
  • Flag debt, and the Knight Capital lesson

A feature flag (or feature toggle) is a configuration value that decides at runtime whether a code path is used. Pete Hodgson's article Feature Toggles (aka Feature Flags) on martinfowler.com distinguishes several kinds, which differ mainly in how long they live:

Kind Purpose Typical lifetime
Release toggle Ship incomplete or unreleased features switched off; release them gradually Days to weeks, then remove
Experiment toggle A/B tests Until the experiment ends
Ops toggle (kill switch) Turn off an expensive or risky feature during an incident; degrade gracefully (chapter 11) Long-lived, deliberately
Permission toggle Enable features for specific customers or groups Long-lived

Flags are powerful, and they have costs. Every flag doubles the number of possible code paths; old flags become flag debt. Two rules from the Knight Capital story are worth adopting:

  1. Never reuse a flag for a new purpose. A flag's name and meaning should be unique for its whole life.
  2. Delete dead code and dead flags. Code that "can't run" can run, if some machine, configuration or flag value makes it reachable.

Flags are also configuration, and configuration changes are deployments, as discussed below. A flag flip that reaches every server in seconds has the blast radius of a global deployment.

Rolling back and rolling forward

At a glance
  • Two ways out of a bad deployment
  • Why rollback must always be possible
  • One-way doors

When a deployment goes wrong, there are two ways out:

  • Roll back: return to the previous version, which is known to have worked.
  • Roll forward: deploy a new version that fixes the problem.
Roll back Roll forward
Speed Usually fastest: the old version is already built and tested Requires writing, reviewing, building and deploying a fix under pressure
Certainty High, if the old version is compatible with everything the new one wrote (chapter 14) Depends on the fix; a rushed fix can make things worse
When it is the right choice Almost always, as the first move When rollback is impossible (an irreversible change), or the fix is trivial and well understood, or the old version has its own serious problem

The strong default is: mitigate first, by rolling back; diagnose and fix afterwards. This only works if every deployment is designed to be rollback-safe, which is exactly what chapter 14's techniques provide: read-before-write releases, expand-and-contract schema changes, and no database migration coupled to a code release.

Some changes are genuinely one-way doors: deleting data, dropping a column, sending messages to customers, migrating to a format the old version cannot read. Identify them explicitly, separate them from ordinary releases, schedule them last, and make them as small as possible. Everything else should be a two-way door that you can walk back through at any time.

Example · Choosing between rollback and roll forward

Scenario: 20 minutes after a deployment, error rates on the journey planner rise from 0.1 % to 3 %. The team believes it knows the bug: a missing null check, a one-line fix.

Bad choice: write the one-line fix, rush it through the pipeline with the bake times skipped "because it's urgent", and deploy it everywhere. If the diagnosis is wrong, or the fix has its own bug, users suffer for another hour.

Good choice: roll back immediately (the deployment was rollback-safe), confirm that error rates return to normal, and then fix the bug calmly and deploy it through the normal pipeline, with its bake times.

Key insight: The time pressure is on restoring service, not on fixing the bug. Rollback restores service; the fix can wait an hour.

Test yourself

A team uses blue/green deployment. The green version adds a nullable column to a shared table and starts writing a new status value, PARTIALLY_REFUNDED, into an existing column. Twenty minutes after the switch, errors rise, and the team switches all traffic back to blue. What can still go wrong, and how should the change have been prepared?

Show answer

Switching traffic back does not switch the data back. Blue now reads rows that green wrote, including a status value it has never seen. If blue fails or misbehaves on unknown values, the rollback becomes a second incident. (The added column is harmless, as long as blue ignores columns it does not know.) The change should have been split as in chapter 14: first deploy a version that can read PARTIALLY_REFUNDED safely without producing it, so that it becomes the version you would roll back to; only then deploy the version that writes it, ideally behind a flag. Blue/green makes the traffic switch fast. It does nothing to make the database compatible.

Configuration and data are deployments too

At a glance
  • Configuration changes cause many large outages
  • Treat them with the same pipeline as code

Many of the largest incidents in this book were not code deployments at all. Cloudflare's 2025 outage (chapter 16) came from an automatically generated configuration file pushed to every machine within minutes. The AWS DynamoDB DNS incident of 2025 came from automation applying a DNS "plan". The Amazon S3 outage of 2017 came from a command that changed capacity. Each was a change to production that bypassed the protections code changes usually get.

Apply the same discipline to every kind of change:

  • Validate configuration and generated data before it is used, as strictly as user input; reject files that are too large, malformed, or suspiciously different from the last good version.
  • Roll out progressively: one host, one zone, one region, with bake times and automatic rollback, even for configuration.
  • Keep the last known good version and fall back to it automatically when a new one fails validation or causes errors.
  • Provide kill switches that can stop a propagation globally and quickly.
  • Version configuration like code, so that "what changed?" always has an answer.

Batch size, release trains and continuous delivery

At a glance
  • Why smaller and more frequent deployments are safer
  • Release trains versus independent, continuous delivery
  • Measuring delivery performance

It seems intuitive that deploying less often is safer. The evidence points the other way. Large batches contain more changes, so each release carries more risk, is harder to test, harder to diagnose when it fails, and harder to roll back without losing unrelated work. Small batches make each change easy to understand, observe and reverse.

The research programme behind the book Accelerate (Nicole Forsgren, Jez Humble and Gene Kim, 2018) and the DORA reports measured software delivery with four key metrics: deployment frequency, lead time for changes, change failure rate and time to restore service. Their central finding was that speed and stability are not a trade-off: the best-performing organisations deployed more often and had lower change failure rates and faster recovery.

This connects back to chapter 13. Release trains exist where many teams share one release artifact, and they push towards large, infrequent, coordinated batches. Splitting the system into team-owned, independently deployable services, kept compatible as in chapter 14, is what allows each team to move to continuous delivery: small changes, deployed when ready, through an automated pipeline that can stop and roll back on its own.

Tip · A deployment safety checklist
  1. Is this change compatible with the version before it, in both directions (chapter 14)?
  2. Can it be rolled back at every step? If not, which step is the one-way door, and is it isolated?
  3. Is new behaviour behind a flag, so that deploy and release are separate?
  4. Does the pipeline deploy progressively, with bake times and automatic rollback on customer-facing alarms?
  5. Are configuration and data changes going through the same process?
  6. Is every server actually running the version you think? (Verify it; do not assume it.)
  7. Is there a kill switch for anything that could misbehave at scale?
Lab · A canary that rolls itself back
  1. Run a local Kubernetes cluster (for example kind or k3d) with Prometheus, and install Argo Rollouts.
  2. Deploy version 1 of a small HTTP service that exposes request counts and error counts as metrics.
  3. Define a Rollout with canary steps (for example 10 %, 30 %, 60 %, 100 %, with a pause between steps) and an analysis that queries the canary's error rate from Prometheus.
  4. Deploy version 2, which returns errors for 5 % of requests. Drive steady traffic and watch the rollout abort and revert automatically.
  5. Deploy version 3, which is healthy, and watch it complete.
  6. Notebook: how much traffic, and how much time, did the analysis need to detect version 2 reliably? What happens if you lower the error rate to 0.5 %?

Appendix A describes this lab in more detail, with hints.

In Appendix A: A.18 A canary that rolls itself back (hints)

Deep dive Why a fleet never runs "one version"

At any moment, a large system runs a distribution of versions: some hosts mid-deployment, some in a canary, a region that was rolled back, a batch job started yesterday on the old code, clients that have not updated, messages written months ago. Knight Capital's eighth server is an extreme case of something that is always true. This is why chapter 14's compatibility rules are not an optional refinement: they are the only way to reason about a system whose version is never a single number. Mature organisations make this visible, with dashboards that show which version every instance is running, and alarms when the distribution looks wrong (for example a host that has not updated when all its neighbours have).

Synthesis

Synthesis

Knight Capital's 45 minutes combined almost every risk in this chapter: a manual deployment that silently left one server behind, a flag whose meaning changed, dead code that could still run, and no automated way to detect and stop the problem. The remedies are not exotic: automated, verified deployments; progressive rollout with bake times and automatic rollback; flags that are never reused; and changes designed to be reversible.

Is deploying more often a risk or a cure? With these practices, it is a cure. Small, frequent, reversible changes are easier to understand, observe and undo, and the evidence from Accelerate shows that the fastest teams are also the most stable. The precondition is everything Part IV has built: services owned by single teams (chapter 13), changes kept compatible in time (chapter 14), and deployment treated as a controlled experiment that can always be walked back.

Key terms

Deploy versus release, dark launch, recreate / rolling / blue-green / canary / shadow deployment, progressive delivery, one-box deployment, bake time, automatic rollback, feature flag (release, experiment, ops, permission), kill switch, flag debt, roll back / roll forward, one-way door, last known good configuration, batch size, continuous delivery, DORA metrics.

Review questions

  1. (Remember) Name the four DORA metrics.
  2. (Understand) Explain the difference between deploying and releasing, and why separating them reduces risk.
  3. (Understand) Why must a canary be compared with a baseline rather than a fixed threshold?
  4. (Apply) Design a progressive deployment for a service running in three regions with three zones each. Specify the stages, bake times and rollback triggers.
  5. (Analyze) Identify every contributing factor in the Knight Capital incident and map each one to a practice in this chapter.
  6. (Evaluate) A team argues that because its last three incidents followed deployments, it should move from daily deployments to a monthly release train. Evaluate the argument using this chapter and chapter 13.
  7. (Create) Write the rollout plan for a change that adds a new required behaviour to a payment API, including the compatibility steps from chapter 14, the flags, the canary and the rollback plan.

Reflection

How long does it take your team to roll back a bad deployment, from the moment someone decides to? Has anyone timed it? What would make it ten times faster?

Looking ahead

You now have the full picture, from partial failure to safe change. Chapter 16 applies it to five real incidents, several of which began with a change: a network configuration, a capacity command, an automation run, a permission update.

Further reading

  • Clare Liguori, Automating safe, hands-off deployments (Amazon Builders' Library, 2020).
  • Pete Hodgson, Feature Toggles (aka Feature Flags) (martinfowler.com, 2017).
  • Martin Fowler, BlueGreenDeployment (martinfowler.com, 2010).
  • Jez Humble and David Farley, Continuous Delivery (Addison-Wesley, 2010).
  • Nicole Forsgren, Jez Humble and Gene Kim, Accelerate (IT Revolution, 2018).
  • US Securities and Exchange Commission, In the Matter of Knight Capital Americas LLC (Release No. 70694, 2013).
  • Argo Rollouts documentation (argoproj.github.io/rollouts).
Chapter 16

Case Studies: Learning from Real Outages

Five incidents, read with everything you have learned

In the five incidents in this chapter, nothing truly exotic happened. A network link went down for 43 seconds. A routine network change sent traffic to the wrong network. A command was mistyped. Two copies of an automation process raced each other. A database permission was changed. Each is the sort of event that happens somewhere every day without anyone noticing.

Yet these events led to a day of degraded service at GitHub, more than three days of disruption to Amazon's block storage, hours of outage across Amazon's largest region, twice, and a global failure of a large part of Cloudflare's network. The interesting question is never "what was the trigger?" It is "why could this trigger have this effect?" Answering that question requires every chapter of this book.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

  • Analyze a published postmortem by mapping its events onto the concepts of this book (Analyze)
  • Identify contributing factors (system model violations, consistency trade-offs, sustaining effects, observability gaps) rather than a single root cause (Analyze)
  • Evaluate the remediations chosen by each organisation, and propose alternatives (Evaluate)
  • Write a blameless postmortem for an incident of your own (Create)
Essential question

If incidents usually come from ordinary events, what makes a system able to absorb them?

Before you start

Chapter 12 described blameless postmortems and Richard Cook's observation that complex systems fail through combinations of factors. Chapters 14 and 15 showed how many incidents begin with a change: a new version, a configuration push, a migration. Here you will practise reading incidents that way.

The chapter in brief: a method for reading postmortems; GitHub, October 2018; Amazon S3, February 2017; AWS us-east-1, October 2025; Cloudflare, November 2025; Amazon EBS, April 2011; patterns across incidents; writing your own postmortem.

Common mistake · About the facts in this chapter

The summaries below are based on the companies' own published post-incident reports, which are linked in the further reading. They simplify complex events. Before you quote a figure or a timeline in your own work, read the original report.

How to read a postmortem

At a glance
  • Six questions that connect an incident to the book's concepts

For each incident, work through these questions. Each points back to a part of the book.

Question Concepts Chapters
1. What was the trigger, and which assumption of the system model did it violate? Failure models, partitions, pauses 1, 3
2. How did the failure spread? What was in series, and what was correlated? Dependencies, failure domains, fan-out 1, 10
3. Which consistency or coordination trade-off was involved? Replication, failover, consensus, CAP 5–8
4. Was there a sustaining effect that kept the system degraded? Retries, overload, metastable failure 11
5. How was the incident detected and diagnosed? What did observability show, and hide? Signals, SLOs, gray failure 12
6. What did the organisation change, and does it address the contributing factors? Remediation, static stability 11, 12

Case 1: GitHub, 21 October 2018

Case study · 43 seconds of network loss, 24 hours of degraded service

What happened. At 22:52 UTC on 21 October 2018, routine maintenance to replace failing optical equipment caused a loss of connectivity between GitHub's US East Coast network hub and its primary US East Coast data centre. Connectivity was restored after 43 seconds.

During that brief partition, GitHub's database failover tool, Orchestrator, which itself uses Raft to coordinate between its nodes, worked as designed. Orchestrator nodes on the other side of the partition (in the US West Coast data centre and a public cloud region) formed a quorum, concluded that the East Coast primaries were unreachable, and promoted replicas in the West Coast data centre to be the new MySQL primaries.

When connectivity returned, the application tier began sending writes to the new West Coast primaries. But the East Coast databases held a short window of writes that had not been replicated to the West Coast before the partition. The two sides had diverged. GitHub could not simply fail back without losing the writes made on the West Coast in the meantime, and could not stay as they were without losing the East Coast writes. In addition, applications running on the East Coast now had to reach their databases across the continent, and many could not tolerate the added round-trip latency.

GitHub's engineers chose to prioritise data integrity over availability. They paused webhook delivery and GitHub Pages builds, restored databases from backups and replayed writes, and reconciled the unreplicated East Coast writes. The site remained degraded for 24 hours and 11 minutes.

What GitHub changed. Among other measures, GitHub adjusted Orchestrator's configuration to prevent database primaries from being promoted across regional boundaries, and worked towards an architecture able to serve from multiple data centres.

Analysis

  1. Trigger and model. A short network partition, exactly the event chapter 3 told us to expect. The failover system's failure detector was complete (it noticed the unreachable primaries) but, from the point of view of the business, not accurate: the primaries were alive and the partition healed quickly.
  2. Consistency trade-off. Asynchronous replication (chapter 5) meant that some acknowledged writes existed only on the old primaries. Failover therefore created a split-brain-like divergence: two sets of writes that could not be ordered automatically.
  3. Consensus does not cover everything. Orchestrator's own Raft cluster correctly agreed on a decision. But consensus among the orchestrator nodes did not make the database replication synchronous. Agreement in the control plane did not imply consistency in the data plane.
  4. PACELC in practice. The West Coast primaries were "available" but remote: the latency cost of cross-continent writes was something applications had never been designed for. This is the fragility of chatty communication described in chapter 13: an application that makes many sequential database round trips per request is only correct for the latency it was built with, and a failover to a distant site multiplies every one of them.
  5. The sustaining factor was not a feedback loop but data reconciliation: restoring and replaying terabytes of data takes hours.
Test yourself

Would synchronous replication between the East and West Coast data centres have prevented this incident? What would it have cost?

Show answer

It would have prevented the divergence: no write would have been acknowledged without being on both coasts, so failover would not have lost data. But every write would then have paid a cross-continent round trip (PACELC's "else, latency"), and a partition between the coasts would have blocked writes (CAP). GitHub's fix, keeping failover within a region, chose a different point on the same trade-off: fewer cross-region failovers, accepting that a whole-region outage needs a different, slower procedure.

Case 2: Amazon S3, 28 February 2017

Case study · One mistyped command, four hours of outage

What happened. On the morning of 28 February 2017 (Pacific time), the Amazon S3 team was debugging a problem that was making the S3 billing system slow. Following an established playbook, an authorised team member ran a command intended to remove a small number of servers from one of the S3 subsystems used by the billing process. One of the command's inputs was entered incorrectly, and a much larger set of servers was removed than intended.

Those servers supported two other critical subsystems in the US-EAST-1 region. The index subsystem manages the metadata and location information of all S3 objects in the region; it is needed to serve every GET, LIST, PUT and DELETE request. The placement subsystem allocates storage for new objects and depends on the index subsystem. With too little capacity left, both subsystems required a full restart.

S3 had been designed to tolerate the loss of significant capacity, but these subsystems had not been completely restarted in the larger regions for many years, and S3 had grown enormously in the meantime. The restart, including the safety checks to validate the integrity of the metadata, took longer than expected. The index subsystem was fully recovered after a little under four hours, and the placement subsystem about half an hour later. Many other AWS services that depend on S3 in the region were affected too, and for part of the incident AWS could not update the individual service status on its Service Health Dashboard, because the dashboard's administration console itself depended on S3.

What AWS changed. The tool was modified to remove capacity more slowly and to refuse to take any subsystem below its minimum required capacity. AWS also prioritised partitioning the index subsystem into smaller cells to reduce the blast radius and recovery time, and moved the Service Health Dashboard so that it could run across multiple regions.

Analysis

  1. Trigger. Human input, but the postmortem explicitly focused on the tool that allowed a single input to remove too much capacity, a blameless framing (chapter 12).
  2. Missing guard rails. A capacity-removal tool without a minimum-capacity check let one operation do unlimited damage. It is the bulkhead idea of chapter 11 applied to operations instead of resources: limit how much any single action can affect.
  3. Recovery paths are code paths too. A full restart of the index subsystem was an operation nobody had performed at current scale. Recovery procedures that are never exercised are a form of untested code, the argument for game days in chapter 12.
  4. Observability had a shared dependency. The status dashboard depended on the service it was meant to report on: a correlated failure of the monitoring and the monitored (chapter 1).
  5. Cells. The remediation, splitting the index into cells, is the cell-based architecture of chapter 11 applied after the fact.

Case 3: AWS us-east-1, 19–20 October 2025

Case study · A race in DNS automation and a slow return to normal

What happened. Amazon DynamoDB manages the DNS records for its regional endpoints with an automated system. A component called the DNS Planner periodically produces new DNS "plans" based on the health and capacity of the load balancers behind the endpoint. Independent DNS Enactors, running redundantly in three Availability Zones, apply those plans to Amazon Route 53.

On 19 October 2025 (late evening, Pacific time), one Enactor was experiencing unusually high delays while applying a plan. Meanwhile, the Planner continued producing newer plans, and a second Enactor applied one of them and then started a clean-up process that deletes plans significantly older than the one just applied. At about this moment, the delayed first Enactor finally applied its older plan, overwriting the newer one. The clean-up then deleted that older plan because it was so old, which removed all IP addresses for the regional DynamoDB endpoint dynamodb.us-east-1.amazonaws.com. The record was left empty, and the system was left in a state that prevented the automation from applying further plans. Engineers had to repair it manually.

From 23:48 PDT on 19 October to 02:40 PDT on 20 October, customers and AWS services in the region could not resolve the DynamoDB endpoint. Many AWS services depend on DynamoDB, so the effects spread. One example: the EC2 subsystem that manages the physical servers hosting instances, called the DropletWorkflow Manager (DWFM), maintains a lease for each server, and its lease checks depend on DynamoDB. During the DNS outage, leases timed out. When DynamoDB came back, DWFM attempted to re-establish leases across the whole fleet at once; the volume of work caused requests to time out and be queued again, and DWFM entered what AWS called congestive collapse, unable to make progress. Engineers recovered it by throttling incoming work and restarting DWFM hosts. Further backlogs in network configuration propagation and health-check problems with Network Load Balancers extended the impact on EC2 and dependent services well into the afternoon of 20 October.

What AWS changed. AWS disabled the DynamoDB DNS Planner and Enactor automation worldwide while fixing the race condition and adding protections against applying incorrect DNS plans, and announced further measures, including changes to throttling and to how DWFM recovers.

Analysis

  1. A concurrency bug in a control plane. Two Enactors applying plans without a check of plan versions at write time is the lost-update problem of chapter 8 in automation code. A conditional write or a fencing token (chapter 7) on the plan generation ("apply only if this plan is newer than the one currently live") would have rejected the stale plan.
  2. Redundancy that wasn't independent. The Enactors were redundant across three zones for availability. But redundancy without coordination created the race. Adding replicas of a writer adds concurrency.
  3. A hidden dependency in series. Many services, including parts of EC2, depended on DynamoDB in the region. Multi-AZ deployments did not help, because the shared dependency was regional (chapter 1's independence trap).
  4. A metastable failure in recovery. DWFM's "congestive collapse" fits chapter 11's definition exactly: the trigger (DynamoDB unavailable) was over, but the recovery workload sustained the degraded state. It was broken only by throttling and restarts, direct intervention to reduce load.
  5. Leases. The lease mechanism of chapter 7 is safe, but its expiry created a burst of renewal work proportional to the size of the fleet: a reminder to design lease renewal so that it can recover gradually.
Test yourself

Propose two changes to DWFM's design, drawn from chapter 11, that could have shortened its recovery.

Show answer

Examples: (1) admission control and rate limiting on lease re-establishment, so the fleet is re-leased in bounded batches instead of all at once; (2) jittered, budgeted retries for timed-out lease work, so timeouts do not immediately re-enqueue work; (3) static stability: allow servers whose leases expired because of a dependency outage (rather than a server failure) to keep operating, so that recovery does not require re-establishing everything. (4) Prioritise work that makes progress (for example, newest-first, adaptive LIFO) over work that is likely to time out again.

Case 4: Cloudflare, 18 November 2025

Case study · A configuration file that doubled in size

What happened. Cloudflare's Bot Management system uses a machine learning model whose input is a feature file. The file is generated every few minutes by a query against a ClickHouse database cluster and pushed to every machine in Cloudflare's network, so that the model can react quickly to new bot behaviour.

On 18 November 2025, a change to database permissions in the ClickHouse cluster, made as part of improving access control, had an unintended effect: the metadata query that generated the feature file began returning duplicate rows, because it now also saw columns from an underlying database it had not seen before. The feature file more than doubled in size.

Cloudflare's core proxy software preallocates memory for the model's features and enforces a limit on the number of features (200, according to Cloudflare's report, well above the roughly 60 in normal use). When the oversized file arrived, the proxy's code hit that limit and panicked, and the proxy began returning HTTP 5xx errors for traffic that depended on it. Starting around 11:20 UTC, large parts of the network failed.

Two factors made diagnosis harder. First, the ClickHouse permission change was being rolled out gradually across the cluster, so each generation of the file was good or bad depending on which node ran the query. The network therefore recovered and failed again every few minutes, which looked more like an attack than a bug. Second, Cloudflare's status page, which is hosted outside Cloudflare's own infrastructure, happened to go down at the same time, reinforcing the suspicion of an attack.

Engineers eventually identified the feature file, stopped its generation and propagation, and inserted a known-good file. Core traffic was largely flowing again by 14:30 UTC, and all systems were back to normal at 17:06 UTC.

What Cloudflare changed. Cloudflare announced measures including treating its internally generated configuration files with the same validation as user-generated input, adding more global kill switches for features, preventing error reports and core dumps from overwhelming system resources, and reviewing failure modes for error conditions across its core proxy modules.

Analysis

  1. Configuration is code that skips the pipeline. A file regenerated every few minutes and deployed globally bypasses the gradual rollouts and canaries used for code. Fast propagation is a feature (quick response to new bots) and a fan-out of failure (chapter 10) at the same time. Chapter 15's rule applies directly: configuration and generated data are deployments, and need validation, progressive rollout and a last known good version.
  2. A hard limit without graceful degradation. Exceeding the feature limit crashed the proxy instead of, for example, rejecting the new file and continuing with the last good one. Chapter 11's principle applies: degrade, don't die, and keep the last known-good configuration.
  3. Gray, flapping behaviour. The alternating good and bad files produced an intermittent failure that confused the responders. Intermittent failures are hard to diagnose because they appear to recover on their own.
  4. Observability can mislead. A coincidental outage of the status page created a plausible but wrong hypothesis (an attack). Chapter 12's correlation of signals (which change preceded the errors?) is the antidote, as is a culture of testing hypotheses rather than locking onto the first plausible one.
  5. A distributed systems lesson in a database permission change. A change to the metadata visible to a query, in a system far from the proxy, altered the output of a pipeline that crossed several teams' boundaries. This is Lamport's joke from chapter 1 in modern form.

Case 5: Amazon EBS, 21–24 April 2011

Case study · A network change, a re-mirroring storm and three and a half days of recovery

This is the oldest case in the chapter, and in many ways the most instructive, because it shows almost every mechanism of chapters 3 and 11 in a single incident.

Background. Amazon Elastic Block Store (EBS) provides network-attached disk volumes for EC2 instances. Each volume is replicated across EBS nodes within an Availability Zone. If a node loses contact with the node holding its replica, it assumes the replica has failed and starts re-mirroring: it searches the cluster for free capacity and copies its data there, so that the volume is protected again. Normally this is exactly the right reaction to a failed node.

The trigger. At 00:47 PDT on 21 April 2011, during routine work to upgrade network capacity in one Availability Zone of the US East region, a configuration change meant to shift traffic onto another router in the primary network instead sent it onto EBS's lower-capacity secondary network, a backup network built for communication between EBS nodes, not to carry all of the primary network's traffic. That network could not carry the load. Many EBS nodes were effectively cut off from their replicas, and nothing had crashed.

The re-mirroring storm. When the network change was rolled back, a large number of nodes, having lost their replicas, all began re-mirroring at once. The free capacity in the affected cluster ran out quickly. Nodes that could not find space kept searching, retrying over and over; AWS later said the retry logic had not backed off aggressively enough. About 13 % of the volumes in the Availability Zone became "stuck", unable to serve reads or writes while they looked for somewhere to put a replica. Under this unusual load, a previously latent race condition in the EBS nodes was triggered: concurrent closing of many connections could crash a node, and every crashed node left more volumes needing to re-mirror.

Spread to the region. The EBS control plane, which handles API requests such as creating volumes, was a regional service shared by all Availability Zones. Requests to the degraded cluster were slow and had long timeouts, so they held threads; the control plane ran out of threads, and API calls began to fail in other Availability Zones too. Engineers disabled communication between the degraded cluster and the control plane to protect the rest of the region.

Recovery. It took most of the first day to stop the storm, by changing the nodes so they stopped the futile searches. Then new capacity had to be physically brought in. Finally, the control plane had a large backlog of pending state changes from the stuck cluster. Releasing it all at once would have overwhelmed the control plane again, so AWS built fine-grained throttles and processed the backlog gradually. Full API access to the affected zone returned on the afternoon of 23 April, and the last automated volume recovery finished on 24 April. AWS reported that about 0.07 % of the volumes in the affected zone could not be restored to a consistent state. Some RDS database instances were also affected, including a small number of multi-AZ instances that did not fail over automatically because of a previously unseen bug.

What AWS changed. Its summary listed more spare capacity for large recovery events; changes to the EBS retry logic so that it would "back off more aggressively" and avoid re-mirroring storms; a fix for the race condition; better timeouts and load shedding in the control plane; and moving more of the control plane into per-cluster services so that one zone's problems would not exhaust shared regional resources.

Analysis

  1. Slowness, not a crash. The trigger was a network that still worked but could not carry the load: the "slow is a failure mode too" lesson of chapter 3. To the EBS nodes, a congested link looked exactly like a dead replica.
  2. A sensible local reaction became a collective disaster. Re-mirroring is the right response when one node fails. When thousands react at once, the recovery traffic itself becomes the overload. This is a metastable failure (chapter 11): the trigger was quickly reversed, but the storm sustained itself.
  3. Retries without backoff or budget. Nodes kept searching for capacity that did not exist. AWS's own remediation names more aggressive backoff, the theme of chapter 11's sections on retries and timeouts.
  4. A latent race, uncovered by unusual timing. The race condition had always existed. It took a storm of simultaneous connection closures to trigger it, exactly the kind of race that chapter 3 predicts slowness will reveal.
  5. Long timeouts and shared threads. The control plane's threads were held by slow requests to one zone and starved requests for all the others, the absence of a bulkhead between zones.
  6. A backlog that had to be released slowly. The pent-up work in the control plane is the "queue in the middle" of chapter 11's backpressure section. AWS's fine-grained throttles are rate limiting used as a recovery tool, the same controlled ramp described there.
Test yourself

Imagine you are designing the re-mirroring logic for a storage system like EBS. Using chapters 3 and 11, propose three mechanisms that would have prevented a storm, without making the system slower to protect data when a single node genuinely fails.

Show answer

Examples: (1) A cluster-wide re-replication budget (a token bucket): a single failure re-mirrors immediately, but when many nodes ask at once, they queue for tokens instead of all searching at once. (2) Jittered exponential backoff on failed capacity searches, with a cap, so that nodes stop hammering the cluster when no space is available. (3) Correlated-failure detection: if many nodes lose their replicas at the same instant, suspect the network rather than the replicas, and wait a little longer before re-mirroring. (4) Reserved spare capacity for recovery, so that a burst of re-mirroring does not exhaust space. (5) Per-zone control-plane resources so that one cluster's slowness cannot starve the region.

Note · A shorter companion case: DynamoDB, 20 September 2015

Four years later, a similar pattern appeared in a different system. A brief network disruption caused a number of DynamoDB storage servers to ask DynamoDB's internal metadata service, all at about the same time, to confirm which table partitions they were responsible for. Rapid customer adoption of global secondary indexes had made those membership responses much larger than before, so the simultaneous requests took longer than the allowed time limit. They timed out, the servers retried, and the retries overloaded the metadata service further. Error rates for customer requests in the region rose to around 55 %. Recovery required pausing the requests to the metadata service so that capacity could be added, then letting traffic back in. AWS's remediations included more metadata capacity, monitoring of membership size, longer processing time allowances, and splitting the metadata service into separate instances.

The same three ingredients are present as in EBS 2011: a short network event as the trigger, many nodes reacting at the same moment, and timeouts followed by retries sustaining the overload. It is also a reminder that a system's safety margin can erode silently as its data grows: nothing about the retry logic had changed, but the work behind each request had.

Patterns across the incidents

At a glance
  • What the five incidents have in common
Pattern GitHub 2018 S3 2017 AWS 2025 Cloudflare 2025 EBS 2011
Ordinary trigger 43 s partition Typo in a command Delayed automation process Permission change Network change onto a low-capacity network
Control plane or automation involved Failover tool Capacity tool DNS automation Config generation pipeline Automatic re-mirroring of volumes
Missing guard against stale or wrong input Cross-region promotion allowed No minimum-capacity check No plan-version check at apply time No validation of generated file Retries without enough backoff; no limit on recovery traffic
Correlated or hidden dependency Apps not designed for cross-country latency Status dashboard on S3 Many services on regional DynamoDB Global propagation to every machine Regional control plane shared by all zones
Long recovery Data reconciliation Cold restart at new scale Congestive collapse of recovery work Flapping, misleading signals Re-mirroring storm, then a throttled backlog

Four lessons stand out:

  1. Automation is a distributed system too. Failover tools, capacity tools, DNS automation and configuration pipelines all make decisions based on information that may be stale, and all need the same protections as data paths: version checks, fencing, validation and limits on blast radius.
  2. Guard rails belong at the point of action. Each incident lacked a check exactly where a harmful action was taken: "is this plan newer?", "is this too much capacity to remove?", "is this file valid?", "should we promote across regions?"
  3. Recovery is a mode of operation that needs design and testing. Cold restarts, re-leasing a fleet and reconciling diverged data are all rare, large and slow, which is exactly why they fail.
  4. Speed of propagation is a double-edged sword. Fast, global, automatic propagation is what makes modern infrastructure responsive, and what turns small mistakes into large outages. Staged rollouts, cells and kill switches trade a little speed for a lot of safety.

Writing your own postmortem

At a glance
  • A template for blameless postmortems
Lab · Capstone: write a postmortem

Choose an incident, either one you experienced (anonymised if necessary) or one of the published postmortems in the further reading list below. Write a blameless postmortem of 2–4 pages with the following sections:

  1. Summary: two or three sentences on what happened and the user impact.
  2. Impact: who was affected, how, and for how long; error budget consumed.
  3. Timeline: detection, key decisions, mitigation, recovery (in UTC).
  4. Contributing factors: at least three, each linked to a concept from this book (a system model assumption, a consistency trade-off, a sustaining effect, an observability gap).
  5. What went well: detection, tooling or decisions that limited the damage.
  6. Action items: each with an owner and a priority, and each addressing a contributing factor, not a person.
  7. Lessons: what this incident teaches about the system's design.

Then swap postmortems with a colleague and review each other's: is any contributing factor phrased as blame? Does each action item address a factor?

In Appendix A: A.19 Capstone: write a postmortem (hints)

Synthesis: the book in one page

Synthesis

The essential question of this chapter asked what makes a system able to absorb ordinary events. The case studies point to an answer that draws on every part of the book:

  • Assume partial failure (Part I). Write down your system model, and expect partitions, pauses, clock errors and lost messages.
  • Choose consistency deliberately (Part II). Know which writes may be lost on failover, which reads may be stale, and which invariants need coordination. Use consensus for the small core that needs it, and coordination-free designs elsewhere.
  • Design for degradation and recovery (Part III). Bound queues, budget retries, shed load, keep last known-good configurations, guard actions at the point where they happen, and practise recovery before you need it.
  • Make the system observable and test it by breaking it. Measure what users experience, and run the failures you fear in controlled experiments.

Lamport's joke in chapter 1 described a computer you did not know existed bringing down your own. The work of distributed systems engineering is to know as many of those computers as you can, and to design so that the ones you do not know cannot bring everything down with them.

Key terms

Postmortem / post-incident review, contributing factor, control plane / data plane, guard rail, congestive collapse, last known-good configuration, kill switch, staged rollout.

Review questions

  1. (Understand) For each case, name the trigger and explain why it alone does not explain the impact.
  2. (Apply) Map the AWS 2025 incident onto chapter 11's model of metastable failure: vulnerable state, trigger, sustaining effect.
  3. (Analyze) Two of the five incidents involved status or communication tools that failed or misled during the incident. What design principle would prevent this?
  4. (Analyze) In the GitHub incident, which side of CAP did GitHub's recovery strategy choose? Which side did the original failover choose?
  5. (Evaluate) Rank the remediations in the five cases by how much they reduce the likelihood of a different future incident (not just a repeat). Justify your ranking.
  6. (Create) For a system you know, identify one automated process that makes changes to production. Design three guard rails for it, using concepts from chapters 7, 8 and 11.

Reflection

Which of the five incidents could happen in your own environment tomorrow? What is the cheapest change that would make it less likely, or less severe?

Where to go from here

You have reached the end of the main text. The Recommended reading guide that follows explains which books, courses and papers to read next, why, and in which order, depending on whether you want to go deeper into theory, into building systems, or into operating them.

Further reading

  • GitHub, October 21 post-incident analysis (GitHub Blog, 2018).
  • Amazon Web Services, Summary of the Amazon S3 Service Disruption in the Northern Virginia (US-EAST-1) Region (2017).
  • Amazon Web Services, Summary of the Amazon DynamoDB Service Disruption in the Northern Virginia (US-EAST-1) Region (aws.amazon.com/message/101925, 2025).
  • Cloudflare, Cloudflare outage on November 18, 2025 (Cloudflare Blog, 2025).
  • Amazon Web Services, Summary of the Amazon EC2 and Amazon RDS Service Disruption in the US East Region (aws.amazon.com/message/65648, 2011). The EBS re-mirroring storm.
  • Amazon Web Services, Summary of the Amazon DynamoDB Service Disruption and Related Impacts in the US-East Region (aws.amazon.com/message/5467D2, 2015).
  • Dan Luu's curated list of public postmortems on GitHub (danluu/post-mortems), a good source of further cases.
Back matter · Appendix A

Appendix A: Lab Guide

Detailed instructions for every lab, followed by hidden hints and solution approaches

The labs are where the ideas in this book stop being words. Every lab in the chapters is described here in more detail: what you need, what to build, how to know you are done, what usually goes wrong, and how to extend it. Part 2 of this appendix contains hidden hints and solution approaches. They describe the algorithms and design decisions, never the finished code, because writing the code yourself is where the learning happens.

Tip · How to use the hints

Try each lab for at least an hour before opening anything in Part 2. Then open Hint 1 only. If you are still stuck after another serious attempt, open Hint 2. Open the Solution approach only when you have a working attempt, or have given up for the day; compare it with your own design rather than copying it. Being stuck is not a sign you are doing it wrong. Distributed systems are hard precisely because the obvious design is often subtly broken, and finding out why is the point of the lab.

Overview of the labs

Lab A.14 is an extra lab for chapter 11's section on rate limiting and backpressure. The chapter points to it but describes it only here.

Before you start

Environment

  • Go (a recent stable release). Both Gossip Glomers and MIT 6.5840 assume it, although Maelstrom works with any language that can read and write lines of JSON.
  • A JDK, Graphviz and gnuplot for Maelstrom.
  • Docker, for the database, tracing backend and Toxiproxy used in labs A.12–A.15.
  • Linux (or a Linux VM) for tc netem, which needs root on the machine where it runs.

How Maelstrom works

You will spend a lot of time with Maelstrom, so it pays to understand it before you start:

  • Maelstrom starts your binary once per node and talks to it over standard input and output, one JSON message per line. Anything you print on standard error goes to the logs, which makes it the right place for debugging output.
  • Every message has the shape {"src": "c1", "dest": "n1", "body": {...}}. The body has a type, and request/response pairs are matched with msg_id and in_reply_to. The Go library handles this for you.
  • The first message each node receives is init, which tells it its own ID and the IDs of all nodes.
  • Nodes can send messages to each other as well as to clients. Maelstrom simulates the network between them: it can add latency (--latency), and its nemesis (--nemesis partition) cuts the network into pieces during the test.
  • Maelstrom also provides services your nodes can call like other nodes: seq-kv (a sequentially consistent key/value store), lin-kv (linearizable) and lww-kv (last-writer-wins).
  • At the end, a checker examines the history of client operations and reports :valid? true or explains what went wrong. Results, logs and message diagrams are written to store/; run ./maelstrom serve to browse them.

A lab notebook template

For every lab, write one page with these headings. It takes ten minutes and doubles what you learn.

  1. Prediction: what I expect to happen, and why.
  2. Design: my approach in five sentences or fewer, and one diagram.
  3. What happened: results, numbers and the first failure I saw.
  4. Explanation: why it failed or succeeded, using the book's vocabulary.
  5. What I would do differently.

Part 1: The labs

A.1 Maelstrom setup and Echo

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 0. Goal: a working environment and a first node that passes Maelstrom's checker.

Steps

  1. Install the tools listed above and download the latest Maelstrom release from the jepsen-io/maelstrom repository.
  2. Create a Go module with a main package and import the Maelstrom Go library (github.com/jepsen-io/maelstrom/demo/go).
  3. Implement a handler for messages of type echo that replies with type echo_ok and the same echo field.
  4. Build and install the binary, then run: ./maelstrom test -w echo --bin ~/go/bin/maelstrom-echo --node-count 1 --time-limit 10
  5. Run ./maelstrom serve and open the message diagram for your run. Find one client request and its reply.

Done when: the output ends with "Everything looks good!".

Common problems: printing debugging output to standard output (which corrupts the JSON protocol; use standard error); building to a different path than the one in --bin; a missing JDK.

Extension: run with --node-count 3 and look at how the client spreads requests across nodes in the message diagram.

A.2 Unique ID generation

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 2. Goal: generate globally unique IDs on several nodes, with no coordination, even during partitions.

Specification: a client sends {"type": "generate"}; the node replies {"type": "generate_ok", "id": ...}. The ID may be of any JSON type.

Steps

  1. Before writing code, answer in your notebook: why does "the current time in milliseconds" not work? Why does "a counter in a shared database" violate the requirements?
  2. Implement your scheme.
  3. Run: ./maelstrom test -w unique-ids --bin ~/go/bin/maelstrom-unique-ids --time-limit 30 --rate 1000 --node-count 3 --availability total --nemesis partition

Done when: the checker reports no duplicate IDs, with total availability under partitions.

Extension: make your IDs roughly sortable by creation time (as Twitter's Snowflake IDs were) and discuss what "sortable" can and cannot mean across machines, using chapter 4.

A.3 Write a system model

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 3. Goal: practise stating assumptions explicitly.

Steps

  1. Choose a real system you know, for example monitoring agents shipping metrics to a central store, or a set of services and a database.
  2. Write one page answering, for that system: which node failure model applies (crash-stop, crash-recovery, omission, Byzantine)? Which network model? Which timing model? What is the longest process pause you can imagine? Which failures are correlated?
  3. For each assumption, describe one real event that would violate it.
  4. Add a section "slow, not dead": which component, if it became ten times slower without failing, would hurt the most?

Done when: a colleague can read your page and find one assumption they disagree with. That disagreement is the most valuable output.

A.4 Clocks: compute, simulate, causal broadcast

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 4. Goal: make Lamport clocks and vector clocks second nature, then use them to build causal broadcast.

Part 1, on paper

  1. Draw a space-time diagram with three processes and at least six messages, including two pairs of concurrent events.
  2. Compute Lamport timestamps and vector clocks for every event.
  3. List all pairs of concurrent events, using only the vector clocks. Then check with the diagram.

Part 2, simulation

  1. Write a program with three threads or goroutines that exchange messages over channels with random delays (for example 0–50 ms), and perform random local events.
  2. Each process maintains a vector clock and logs every event as (process, event type, vector).
  3. Write a separate checker that reads the log and verifies: for every message, the send event's vector is strictly less than the receive event's vector; and for any two events on the same process, the vectors increase.

Part 3, causal broadcast

  1. Change the program so that every message is broadcast to all processes, and the network may reorder messages (deliberately delay some).
  2. Implement causal delivery: a process must not deliver a message until it has delivered every message that causally precedes it. Buffer messages that arrive too early.
  3. Add a checker that verifies that no process delivered a message before one of its causal predecessors.

Done when: your checkers pass over thousands of random runs, and you can explain why removing the buffering makes the causal checker fail.

A.5 Consistent hashing ring

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 5. Goal: measure load balance and data movement for different partitioning schemes.

Steps

  1. Implement a ring with a configurable number of virtual nodes per physical node, with lookups that find the owner of a key.
  2. Generate 100,000 random keys. For 5 physical nodes, record how many keys each node owns with 1, 10, 50 and 200 virtual nodes each. Compute the standard deviation and the ratio of the most loaded to the least loaded node.
  3. Add a sixth node. Count how many keys changed owner. Repeat with hash(key) mod N for N = 5 and N = 6.
  4. Give one node twice as many virtual nodes as the others and check that it receives about twice as many keys.

Deliverable: a table of the results and three sentences of interpretation.

Extension: implement replication: each key is stored on its owner and the next R − 1 distinct physical nodes clockwise. Make sure virtual nodes of the same machine are skipped. (Optional, MIT 6.5840 lab 5 implements sharding with reconfiguration on top of replicated groups; attempt it after A.7.)

A.6 Broadcast (Gossip Glomers 3a–3e)

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 6. Goal: spread every message to every node, correctly under partitions, then efficiently.

Specification: clients send broadcast (with an integer message), read (reply with all messages seen so far) and topology (Maelstrom's suggested neighbours for each node).

Steps

  1. 3a, single node: store messages and return them on read.
  2. 3b, multi-node: propagate messages so that all nodes eventually return all messages. Run with --node-count 5.
  3. 3c, fault tolerant: run with --nemesis partition. Messages sent during a partition must still arrive everywhere after it heals.
  4. 3d, efficient part I: with 25 nodes and 100 ms latency per message (--node-count 25 --time-limit 20 --rate 100 --latency 100), achieve fewer than 30 messages per operation, a median latency below 400 ms and a maximum below 600 ms. It must still pass with --nemesis partition.
  5. 3e, efficient part II: same command; now fewer than 20 messages per operation, with a median below 1 second and a maximum below 2 seconds.

Where to read the numbers: at the end of store/latest/results.edn, look for :msgs-per-op under :net → :servers and for the latency quantiles under :workload → :stable-latencies.

Common problems: forwarding a message you have already seen (infinite loops); replying to the client only after forwarding completes (slow); retrying with no bound on outstanding messages (memory); trusting Maelstrom's grid topology for 3d (you are allowed to ignore it).

Notebook question: which consistency model does your broadcast provide? Why does the checker accept incomplete reads during a partition?

A.7 Raft (MIT 6.5840 labs 3A–3D and 4)

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 7. Goal: implement a complete consensus algorithm that survives the lab's adversarial tests. This is the hardest lab in the book and the one that teaches the most.

Setup: follow the instructions on the current 6.5840 course website to get the lab skeleton and tests. Read the extended Raft paper, especially figure 2, and the course's lab guidance before starting. Many students also find Jon Gjengset's Students' Guide to Raft invaluable.

Parts

  1. 3A, leader election: elect a single leader, re-elect after a failure, and keep heartbeats flowing. The tests require a new leader within a few seconds.
  2. 3B, log replication: append client commands, replicate them, commit them, and deliver committed entries in order on the apply channel.
  3. 3C, persistence: persist the required state, survive crashes and restarts, and handle an unreliable network where messages are lost and reordered. This is where most subtle bugs appear.
  4. 3D, log compaction: let the service take snapshots, discard old log entries, and bring lagging followers up to date with InstallSnapshot.
  5. Lab 4 (optional): build a fault-tolerant key/value service on your Raft, including duplicate detection for client retries.

Done when: every test passes hundreds of times in a row, also with Go's race detector enabled. A Raft that passes once has not been tested.

Common problems: holding a lock while sending an RPC or waiting on a channel (deadlock); resetting the election timer at the wrong moments; truncating the log on every AppendEntries instead of only on conflict; acting on an RPC reply without checking whether your term changed while you waited; off-by-one errors in log indexes, especially after snapshots.

A.8 Kafka-style log (Gossip Glomers 5a–5c)

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 7. Goal: a replicated log with per-key offsets, built first on one node, then on several nodes using a linearizable store.

Specification: send (a key and a message; reply with the assigned offset), poll (offsets per key; reply with messages from those offsets onwards), commit_offsets and list_committed_offsets. The checker looks for lost writes (a client sees offset 10 but not offset 5) and requires monotonically increasing offsets per key.

Steps

  1. 5a, single node: ./maelstrom test -w kafka --bin ~/go/bin/maelstrom-kafka --node-count 1 --concurrency 2n --time-limit 20 --rate 1000
  2. 5b, multi-node: the same with --node-count 2, using Maelstrom's lin-kv service for anything that must be agreed.
  3. 5c, efficient: reduce messages per operation and compare-and-swap failures significantly, still with two nodes.

Common problems: two nodes assigning the same offset to different messages; offsets with gaps that look like lost writes; committed offsets moving backwards.

A.9 Totally available transactions (Gossip Glomers 6a–6c)

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 8. Goal: a key/value store with transactions that stays available during partitions, at weak but precisely defined isolation levels.
