Specification: a txn message carries a list of operations such as ["r", 1, null] and ["w", 1, 6]; the reply txn_ok returns the same list with read values filled in (null for keys that do not exist).

Steps

  1. 6a, single node: ./maelstrom test -w txn-rw-register --bin ~/go/bin/maelstrom-txn --node-count 1 --time-limit 20 --rate 1000 --concurrency 2n --consistency-models read-uncommitted --availability total
  2. 6b, read uncommitted, multi-node: the same with --node-count 2, then add --nemesis partition. Read uncommitted forbids only G0, cycles of write-write dependencies.
  3. 6c, read committed: change the consistency model to read-committed. Now aborted reads (G1a), intermediate reads (G1b) and circular information flow (G1c) are also forbidden.

Notebook question: construct a history that your 6c implementation allows but serializability would forbid. Why can no totally available system provide serializability?

A.10 Grow-only counter, two ways

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 9. Goal: compare a counter built on a shared store with one built as a CRDT.

Specification: add with a delta (reply add_ok) and read (reply with the current value). After writes stop, each node's final read must return the correct total.

Steps

  1. Version 1 (the official challenge): use Maelstrom's seq-kv service. Run: ./maelstrom test -w g-counter --bin ~/go/bin/maelstrom-counter --node-count 3 --rate 100 --time-limit 20 --nemesis partition
  2. Version 2 (CRDT): do not use seq-kv. Give each node a G-counter, gossip states, and merge.
  3. Run both versions with partitions. Compare messages per operation and how quickly the final reads converge.

Notebook question: which of the three properties of the merge function (commutative, associative, idempotent) protects you from which network behaviour?

A.11 Measure the tail

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 10. Goal: see the fan-out effect with your own numbers, and tame it with hedging.

Steps

  1. Write a backend that answers in about 5 ms, but with probability 1 % sleeps 500 ms first. Run several instances.
  2. Write an aggregator that, for each incoming request, calls n backends in parallel and answers when all have answered.
  3. Use a load generator (for example wrk2, vegeta or k6) with a constant arrival rate, not a fixed number of concurrent users, and measure the median, 99th and 99.9th percentile for n = 1, 10, 50 and 100.
  4. Compare the fraction of slow aggregate requests with 1 − 0.99ⁿ.
  5. Add hedging: if a backend has not answered after 20 ms, send the same request to another instance and use the first answer. Measure again and count the extra requests.

Common problem: measuring with a closed-loop tool that waits for each response before sending the next request. This hides the tail, an effect known as coordinated omission (a term coined by Gil Tene, and discussed by Marc Brooker in Open and Closed, Omission and Collapse). Use a constant-rate tool.

A.12 Reconstruct or reside

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 10. Goal: experience the difference between state-reconstructing and state-resident designs.

Steps

  1. Run PostgreSQL or Redis in Docker.
  2. Version A, state-reconstructing: an HTTP service that, for each position update (train, position), loads the train's last 60 positions from the database, checks the new position for plausibility (for example a maximum speed), appends it, and writes the result back. Run two instances behind a load balancer.
  3. Version B, state-resident: route each train's updates by consistent hashing of the train ID to one worker that keeps the last 60 positions in memory and writes each update through to the database. Run two workers.
  4. Simulate 1,000 trains sending one update per second. Measure latency percentiles, database queries per second and CPU use for both versions.
  5. In version A, send updates for the same train to both instances at the same time. Do you lose positions? Add a version check and measure again.
  6. In version B, kill one worker. Measure how long its trains go unserved, and what it must reload.

Notebook question: which version would you choose for 50 updates per train per day? Why?

A.13 A retry storm in miniature

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 11. Goal: create a metastable failure on your laptop, then remove it pattern by pattern.

Steps

  1. Build service B with a fixed capacity: a worker pool of 20 and a processing time of about 10 ms, so roughly 2,000 requests per second. Give it an unbounded queue in front of the workers at first.
  2. Build client A, which sends requests to B at a constant rate of about 70 % of B's capacity, with a 200 ms timeout.
  3. Put Toxiproxy (or tc netem) between A and B so you can inject a 10-second outage or a large added latency.
  4. For each configuration, run 60 s of steady load, inject a 10 s fault, and keep the load running for 2 more minutes:
    • (a) immediate retries, up to 3;
    • (b) exponential backoff without jitter;
    • (c) full jitter;
    • (d) full jitter and a retry budget of 10 %;
    • (e) (d) plus a bounded queue in B that rejects quickly when full, and B dropping requests whose deadline has passed.
  5. Plot goodput (responses that A received before its timeout) and B's throughput over time for each run.

Done when: you have at least one configuration that stays degraded after the fault is removed, and one that recovers as soon as the fault ends, and you can explain the difference.

A.14 Pushback instead of queues

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 11 (extra lab). Goal: compare a pipeline with a large internal queue with one that has end-to-end backpressure.

Steps

  1. Create a table with 1,000,000 "to do" rows. Include an entity ID (say 1,000 entities) and a per-entity sequence number.
  2. Build a pipeline of three stages: a reader that reads rows, a transformer, and a sink that calls a slow downstream. Give the downstream a switch that cuts its speed to 10 % for two minutes.
  3. Version Q: the reader reads as fast as it can into an unbounded in-memory queue between stages.
  4. Version P: every queue between stages is bounded (for example 100 items), stages block when the next queue is full, and the reader reads from the table only when its output queue has space.
  5. During and after the slowdown, measure: process memory, the age of the oldest queued item, end-to-end latency per item, the burst rate at the downstream after it recovers, and the number of ordering violations (an entity's sequence number processed before a lower one).
  6. Add retries with concurrency in the sink of both versions and measure ordering violations again.

Notebook question: where did the work wait in each version, and what would happen to each if the process crashed during the slowdown?

A.15 Instrument and observe

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 12. Goal: see a distributed algorithm from the inside with traces, and define an SLI.

Steps

  1. Choose your broadcast node (A.6) or your Raft implementation (A.7).
  2. Add the OpenTelemetry SDK for your language. Create a span for every message handled, with attributes for message type, source and destination.
  3. Propagate trace context inside your messages, for example as a traceparent field in the message body, so that spans on different nodes join the same trace.
  4. Export spans with OTLP to an OpenTelemetry Collector and a tracing backend (Grafana Tempo or Jaeger) running in Docker.
  5. Trigger a partition (broadcast) or a leader crash (Raft) and find the event in your traces.
  6. Define an SLI, for example "the proportion of broadcast messages known to all nodes within one second", compute it from your telemetry and compare runs with and without partitions.
  7. Return to the system model you wrote in lab A.3. For each assumption, note whether your telemetry could confirm or refute it, and which of the failures it describes your current signals would not show at all.

A.16 Measure the hop, and map your deployment units

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 13. Goal: replace intuition about network costs with your own numbers, and see your system through the lens of teams and deployment units.

Steps

  1. Implement one tiny operation, for example looking up a price in an in-memory map, and expose it four ways: as a function call; behind an HTTP endpoint called over localhost; in a second container on the same machine; and, if you have access, on another machine in the same data centre and in another Availability Zone.
  2. Drive each variant with a constant-rate load generator for at least a minute. Record the median, 99th and 99.9th percentile, and CPU use on both sides.
  3. Add the layers a production call usually has: TLS, a sidecar or reverse proxy, JSON serialisation of a realistic payload. Measure again.
  4. Draw the deployment units of a system you know. For each, list the teams that change it. Mark units changed by several teams, and teams that own many units.
  5. For one typical user request, count the synchronous hops and estimate end-to-end latency and availability with your measurements.

Deliverable: a table of your measurements next to the latency table in chapter 2, and the annotated map.

A.17 Break it on purpose: a mixed-version test

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 14. Goal: experience the time dimension of compatibility, and fix it with read-before-write.

Steps

  1. Write (or take) a small service with a search endpoint and a book endpoint, backed by a database.
  2. Create version 2 with three changes: (a) a new optional request field seatPreference; (b) a new response field continuationToken that clients send back to fetch the next page; (c) a new value WAITLISTED in the booking status enum, which version 2 can write to the database.
  3. Run version 1 and version 2 side by side behind a load balancer that alternates requests between them.
  4. Write a test client that runs these sequences, checking results at each step: new then old; old then new; new, old, new; a rollback (stop version 2 after it has written WAITLISTED bookings, and keep only version 1 running); and a replay of a stored version 1 response sent to version 2.
  5. Record which sequences fail and how. Then redesign the change as two releases (read first, write later) or with a feature flag, and run the sequences again until all pass.

A.18 A canary that rolls itself back

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 15. Goal: build a progressive deployment that detects a bad version and reverts it without a human.

Steps

  1. Create a local Kubernetes cluster with kind or k3d. Install Prometheus (for example with the kube-prometheus-stack Helm chart) and Argo Rollouts.
  2. Write a small HTTP service that exposes request and error counters in Prometheus format, labelled with its version. Build three images: v1 (healthy), v2 (5 % errors) and v3 (healthy).
  3. Replace the Deployment with an Argo Rollouts Rollout using a canary strategy, for example steps of 10 %, 30 %, 60 % and 100 % with pauses between them.
  4. Define an AnalysisTemplate that queries the canary's error rate from Prometheus and fails if it exceeds, for example, 1 %. Attach it to the canary steps.
  5. Run a steady load generator. Deploy v2 and watch the rollout abort and revert. Deploy v3 and watch it complete.
  6. Lower v2's error rate to 0.5 % and then 0.1 %. At what point does your analysis stop catching it, and what would you need (more traffic, longer pauses, a baseline comparison) to catch it?

A.19 Capstone: write a postmortem

Hints and solution approaches ↓

Chapter: 16. Goal: apply the whole book to one real incident.

Steps

  1. Choose an incident: one you experienced (anonymised if needed) or a published postmortem from the further reading in chapter 16 or Dan Luu's list of public postmortems.
  2. Write a blameless postmortem of 2–4 pages using the template in chapter 16.
  3. For each contributing factor, cite the chapter and concept it relates to.
  4. Swap with a colleague and review each other's postmortem using the rubric in Part 2.

Part 2: Hints and solution approaches

Each lab has two hints and a solution approach. Open them in order.

A.1 Maelstrom setup and Echo

↑ Back to lab A.1

Hint 1

The Go library does the protocol work for you: create a node, register a handler for the message type echo, and call the node's run function. In the handler, take the incoming body, change its type field and reply with it.

Hint 2

If Maelstrom reports that your node never responded, check that nothing else writes to standard output. A single debugging print to standard output corrupts the JSON stream. Log to standard error instead.

Solution approach Echo

Decode the request body into a generic map, set type to echo_ok, and reply to the original message with that map. The library fills in in_reply_to from the request's msg_id. Nothing else is needed; the purpose of this lab is the toolchain.

A.2 Unique ID generation

↑ Back to lab A.2

Hint 1

What does each node know that no other node knows? It is given to you in the init message.

Hint 2

Two pieces of information together are unique: something unique to the node, and something unique within the node. The second one never needs to leave the node.

Solution approach Unique IDs

Combine the node ID (unique across the cluster) with a local counter that the node increments for every ID it issues (unique within the node), for example as the string n2-81734. Protect the counter with a mutex or an atomic increment, because handlers run concurrently. No communication is needed, so partitions do not matter: this is the coordination-free design of chapter 9 in its simplest form. A random 128-bit UUID also works, with a negligible probability of collision. Timestamps alone fail because two nodes, or two requests on one node, can read the same millisecond; and a shared counter would require coordination that a partition prevents.

For the extension, put a timestamp in the high-order bits, then the node ID, then a per-millisecond counter (the Snowflake layout). IDs are then roughly ordered by time, but only as well as the nodes' clocks agree, so "sortable" means "approximately in creation order", not "in happens-before order".

A.3 Write a system model

↑ Back to lab A.3

Hint 1

Look for the assumptions nobody has written down because they are "obvious": that clocks agree within a second, that the network inside a data centre does not partition, that a process that answers health checks is healthy.

Hint 2

For each component, ask three questions: can it lose its memory (crash-recovery)? can it say something wrong (Byzantine, or simply corrupted data)? can it be slow without being dead?

Solution approach Example: monitoring agents

A reasonable model for agents shipping metrics: crash-recovery nodes (agents restart and may lose their in-memory buffer); fair-loss links (data may be lost or duplicated, so the store must deduplicate or tolerate duplicates); partially synchronous timing (usually fast, sometimes delays of minutes when a network or the store is congested); process pauses of several seconds (garbage collection, an overloaded host). Violating events: a host clock jumping back after an NTP correction (breaks ordering by timestamp); an agent replaying a buffer after restart (duplicates); a store that accepts writes but is minutes behind (silent lag). Under "slow, not dead", the ingestion endpoint is usually the critical one: if it becomes ten times slower, agents' buffers fill, they drop data or retry, and the retries add load. Most teams discover from this exercise that their alerting assumes the opposite of one of these assumptions.

A.4 Clocks: compute, simulate, causal broadcast

↑ Back to lab A.4

Hint 1

For the checker, you do not need to reconstruct the whole execution. You only need to pair each receive event with its send event, which is easy if every message carries a unique ID that both events log.

Hint 2

For causal delivery, keep a vector that counts how many messages you have delivered from each sender, not one that counts all events. Compare an incoming message's vector with that.

Solution approach Causal broadcast

Use the classic algorithm associated with Birman, Schiper and Stephenson. Each process i keeps a vector D, where D[j] is the number of messages from process j it has delivered. When i broadcasts, it increments D[i] and attaches a copy of D as the message's vector V. When i receives a message from j with vector V, it may deliver it only if:

  • V[j] = D[j] + 1 (it is the next message from j), and
  • V[k] ≤ D[k] for every k ≠ j (everything j had delivered before sending has also been delivered here).

Otherwise the message goes into a buffer. After each delivery, set D[j] = V[j] and re-check the buffer, since the delivery may have unblocked other messages. The checker records, for each delivery, which messages the sender had delivered before sending, and verifies that each receiver had delivered all of them first. If you remove the buffer and deliver on arrival, the checker fails as soon as the network reorders two causally related messages.

A.5 Consistent hashing ring

↑ Back to lab A.5

Hint 1

Keep the ring as a sorted array of (position, node) pairs. Finding a key's owner is then a binary search for the first position greater than or equal to the key's hash, wrapping around to the start of the array.

Hint 2

To place virtual nodes, hash a string such as the node name followed by a separator and the virtual node index. Use a hash with good distribution (for example the first 8 bytes of SHA-256, or xxHash), not a language's default string hash.

Solution approach What to expect

With one virtual node per physical node, the arcs of the ring have very different lengths, so the most loaded node often holds several times as many keys as the least loaded, and a ratio of ten or more is not unusual with five nodes. The relative standard deviation of load falls roughly as one over the square root of the number of virtual nodes: with 200 virtual nodes it is typically around 5–10 per cent. When a sixth node joins, about one sixth of the keys move, all of them to the new node. With hash mod N, going from 5 to 6 moves about five sixths of the keys, because a key keeps its owner only if h mod 5 and h mod 6 happen to agree. For replication, walk clockwise from the key's position collecting owners, and skip positions belonging to physical nodes you already have, until you have R distinct machines.

A.6 Broadcast

↑ Back to lab A.6

Hint 1 (3b and 3c)

Keep a set of messages you have seen. When a broadcast arrives, reply to the sender immediately; if the message is new, add it and forward it. If it is not new, do nothing, which is what stops infinite loops. For partitions, ask yourself how a node would ever learn about a message it missed while it was cut off.

Hint 2 (3d and 3e)

The number of messages per operation depends on your topology and on how many messages you send per broadcast. The grid topology has long paths and many edges. For 3e, think about sending many messages in one network message.

Solution approach Broadcast, from correct to efficient

3b: on a new message, forward it to your neighbours, except the one you got it from. 3c: forwarding once is not enough because messages are dropped during partitions. Two approaches work. Either keep, per neighbour, a set of messages not yet acknowledged and resend them periodically until each is acknowledged; or run periodic anti-entropy: every few hundred milliseconds, send each neighbour the messages you think it lacks (or your whole set, which is simple but expensive), and merge whatever arrives. Both are idempotent, because adding a message to a set twice changes nothing.

3d: replace the grid with a shallow tree, for example a star where one hub node is connected to all others, or a two-level tree. With a star, a broadcast costs one message to the hub and one from the hub to each other node, about 25 messages for 25 nodes. The longest path is two hops, so 200 ms plus processing, well inside the latency limits. Keep the retry or anti-entropy mechanism from 3c so that it still survives partitions; the hub is a single point of failure for latency, not for correctness.

3e: batch. Instead of forwarding each message at once, buffer new messages and send all buffered messages to each peer in one network message every 150–300 ms. At 100 broadcasts per second, one batch carries many client messages, so the messages-per-operation figure falls far below 20, while the median latency stays under a second. This is the PACELC trade-off of chapter 6: fewer messages, in exchange for latency.

A.7 Raft

↑ Back to lab A.7

Hint 1 (3A)

Reset a follower's election timer only in three situations: when it receives an AppendEntries from the current leader (a term at least as large as its own), when it starts an election, and when it grants a vote. Resetting it on any other message lets a stale or failed leader prevent elections. Because the tester limits heartbeats to about ten per second, use election timeouts that are larger than the paper's 150–300 ms, for example randomly between a few hundred milliseconds and about a second.

Hint 2 (3B and 3C)

Follow figure 2 literally, including the rules that look optional. Two rules catch most people: a follower must truncate its log only when an existing entry conflicts with a new one (same index, different term), never just because an AppendEntries arrived; and a leader may count replicas to commit an entry only if that entry is from its current term. Whenever an RPC reply comes back, first check whether your term or role changed while you were waiting, and ignore the reply if so.

Solution approach Structure that tends to work
  • One mutex protects all Raft state. Never hold it while sending an RPC, sleeping, or sending on the apply channel.
  • Background goroutines: one ticker that checks the election timeout and, if leader, sends heartbeats; one applier that waits on a condition variable and sends committed entries, in order, from lastApplied + 1 up to commitIndex.
  • Persistence: save currentTerm, votedFor and the log every time any of them changes, before replying to the RPC that changed it.
  • Elections: on timeout, increment the term, vote for yourself, persist, and send RequestVote in parallel. Count votes as they arrive; become leader on a majority, and immediately send heartbeats. Grant a vote only if you have not voted for someone else in this term and the candidate's log is at least as up to date as yours (compare last log term, then last log index).
  • Replication: the leader keeps nextIndex and matchIndex per follower. On rejection, move nextIndex back. The lab's 3C tests require a fast backup: have the follower return the term of the conflicting entry and the first index it holds for that term (or its log length if the entry is missing), so that the leader can skip a whole term at a time.
  • Commit: after updating matchIndex, find the highest index N replicated on a majority with log[N].term == currentTerm, and set commitIndex = N.
  • 3D: store lastIncludedIndex and lastIncludedTerm with the snapshot, and translate between log positions and Raft indexes everywhere through one helper function. Send InstallSnapshot when a follower's nextIndex is at or below lastIncludedIndex.
  • Lab 4: clients attach a client ID and a sequence number to each request; the state machine remembers the last sequence number applied per client and ignores duplicates. Reads go through the log too, or use ReadIndex.
  • Testing: run each test hundreds of times with the race detector, in parallel, and save the logs of failing runs. Logs that print the term, role and log length at every state change make most bugs findable.

A.8 Kafka-style log

↑ Back to lab A.8

Hint 1 (5b)

With two nodes, two send requests for the same key may arrive at different nodes at the same moment. Something must decide which gets which offset. Which Maelstrom service can provide an atomic "take the next number" operation?

Hint 2 (5c)

Compare-and-swap conflicts happen because both nodes compete for the same keys in lin-kv. What if each log key always had the same node responsible for it?

Solution approach From shared store to owners

5a: keep, per key, an in-memory list of messages; the offset is the position in the list. poll returns the messages from the requested offset onwards (a limited number is fine); committed offsets are a map from key to offset, which should never move backwards.

5b: use lin-kv for anything that must be agreed. Allocate offsets with a compare-and-swap loop on a per-key counter ("read the latest offset n, CAS it from n to n + 1, and if the CAS fails, read again and retry"), then store the message under a key such as the log key plus the offset. poll reads messages from lin-kv. Store committed offsets in lin-kv too, using CAS so that they only increase.

5c: give each log key an owner, chosen by hashing the key over the node list, which every node computes identically. A send that arrives at the wrong node is forwarded to the owner, which assigns offsets locally without any CAS, because it is the single writer for that key. This is the single-ownership idea of chapter 10's state-resident services. Polls are either forwarded to the owner or served from data the owner replicates. Keep committed offsets in lin-kv. Compare messages per operation and the number of CAS retries with 5b.

A.9 Totally available transactions

↑ Back to lab A.9

Hint 1 (6b)

Total availability means you cannot wait for the other node before answering. Apply the transaction locally, reply, and tell the other node afterwards. The question is what the other node does with what you tell it.

Hint 2 (6b and 6c)

G0 is a cycle of write-write dependencies: node 1 thinks T1's write to x came before T2's, while node 2 thinks the opposite for key y. You avoid it if every node agrees on one order of versions for every key, without needing to communicate at write time. Chapter 4 has a tool for exactly that.

Solution approach Read uncommitted and read committed

6a: execute each transaction's operations in order against an in-memory map, holding one lock for the whole transaction. That makes each transaction atomic and isolated on a single node.

6b: each node executes transactions locally as in 6a and replies immediately. It then asynchronously replicates the transaction's writes to the other nodes, retrying until acknowledged so that writes survive partitions. To prevent G0, give every write a version that all nodes order the same way, for example a (Lamport timestamp, node ID) pair, and apply a replicated write to a key only if its version is newer than the version stored for that key (last-writer-wins by logical timestamp). All nodes then converge on the same order of writes per key, so no write-write cycle can form.

6c: read committed also forbids reading values that were only intermediate (a transaction writes x twice, and another reads the first value) and reading partial transactions. Replicate whole transactions, carrying only the final value each transaction wrote to each key, and apply each replicated transaction atomically under the lock, using the version rule from 6b per key. Local readers then only ever see complete, committed transactions. The isolation is still far weaker than serializability: two transactions on different nodes can read the same old value and both write, and with total availability nothing can prevent that, because preventing it would require the nodes to communicate before answering.

A.10 Grow-only counter

↑ Back to lab A.10

Hint 1 (seq-kv)

If all nodes increment the same key, they will overwrite each other's increments. Either make updates conditional, or give each node something of its own to write.

Hint 2 (seq-kv)

seq-kv is only sequentially consistent: a node's read can return a value older than what other nodes have written. The final reads happen after the writes have stopped, so you need convergence, not instant freshness. Think about what a node can do to make sure its view of the store has moved forward before it answers a final read.

Solution approach Two counters

Version 1, seq-kv: the cleanest design gives each node its own key (for example the node ID). On add, the node updates its own key (only it ever writes there, so there is no conflict, although a CAS loop is still a good safety net). On read, it reads every node's key and returns the sum. An alternative is a single shared key updated with a compare-and-swap loop: read the value, CAS it to value plus delta, and retry on a precondition failure. For stale reads, many solvers write a unique dummy value to a scratch key before reading, which pushes the node's view of the sequentially consistent store forward, and re-read periodically until the values stop changing.

Version 2, CRDT: each node keeps a map from node ID to count. add increments only its own entry. Every few hundred milliseconds, each node sends its whole map to the others (or to random peers), and a receiver merges by taking the maximum of each entry. read returns the sum of the local map. Duplicated and reordered gossip messages are harmless because the merge is idempotent and commutative, and lost messages are repaired by the next round. Compared with version 1, it sends more messages in total, but never waits for a store and never conflicts.

A.11 Measure the tail

↑ Back to lab A.11

Hint 1

Expected fractions of slow aggregate requests from 1 − 0.99ⁿ: about 1 % for n = 1, 9.6 % for n = 10, 39.5 % for n = 50 and 63.4 % for n = 100. If your measurements are far below these, check your load generator.

Hint 2

For hedging, start a timer when you send the first request. If it fires before the response arrives, send the duplicate. Cancel whichever request loses, so that it does not keep consuming a backend.

Solution approach What you should see

Without hedging, the 99th percentile of the aggregator jumps to about 500 ms as soon as n is around 10 or more, and the median follows once n approaches 100. With a 20 ms hedge, a slow backend response is almost always overtaken by the duplicate, so the aggregate's tail falls to roughly 20 ms plus the normal response time, at the cost of only a few per cent more backend requests (only the slow ones are duplicated). Then repeat the experiment with slowness caused by overload (make the backend slower as its concurrency rises): hedging now adds load to the backends that are already slow, and the tail may get worse. That is why hedging needs a budget.

A.12 Reconstruct or reside

↑ Back to lab A.12

Hint 1

For version B, use the consistent hashing ring from lab A.5 in the load balancer or in a small routing layer, so every update for a train reaches the same worker.

Hint 2

For step 5 in version A, use a version column on the train's row and a conditional update: write only if the version is still the one you read. Count how many updates fail the condition and need a retry.

Solution approach What you should see

Version A performs at least one read and one write per update, so database queries per second are at least twice the update rate, and latency includes both round trips plus deserialising 60 positions. Without a version check, concurrent updates for the same train lose positions (the lost update of chapter 8); with one, they are safe but some must retry. Version B reads from the database only when a worker activates a train, so database reads fall to almost nothing; writes remain, but no conditional logic is needed because each train has a single owner that processes its updates one at a time. Killing a worker in B leaves its trains unserved until the router notices and another worker reloads their recent positions: that is the price of residence. At 50 updates per train per day, the reconstruction cost is negligible, and version A's simplicity wins.

A.13 A retry storm in miniature

↑ Back to lab A.13

Hint 1

To get a metastable failure, B's queue must be able to hold work longer than A's timeout. Then B keeps processing requests whose clients have already given up, which is what keeps goodput low after the fault ends.

Hint 2

Measure goodput at the client: count only responses received before the timeout. B's throughput graph will look healthy during the collapse, and that contrast is the lesson.

Solution approach What you should see, and why

(a) Immediate retries: during the fault, A sends up to four times its normal rate. When the fault ends, B's queue is full of requests whose clients have timed out, and the retries keep it full. Goodput stays near zero even though B runs at 100 %. (b) Backoff without jitter: load arrives in synchronised waves, and recovery is slow and spiky. (c) Full jitter: smoother, but in this open system (constant arrival rate), backoff only defers the extra load, so recovery can still stall. (d) Retry budget: retries are capped at about 10 % of traffic, so offered load stays near 77 % of capacity (70 % plus 10 % of it), the queue drains, and the system recovers within seconds. (e) Bounded queue, fast rejection and dropping expired requests: B never works on requests nobody is waiting for, so the system recovers almost immediately after the fault. The essential fixes are the ones that reduce load (budget, rejection, deadline dropping), not the ones that only move it in time.

A.14 Pushback instead of queues

↑ Back to lab A.14

Hint 1

In Go, a channel with a small buffer is a bounded queue: sending to a full channel blocks. Most languages have an equivalent blocking bounded queue.

Hint 2

For ordering, record per entity the highest sequence number processed so far, and count an ordering violation whenever a lower one arrives later. Concurrent retries in the sink are the main source of reordering.

Solution approach What you should see

In version Q, the reader finishes reading the table quickly while the downstream is slow, so millions of items sit in memory; memory grows with the backlog, the oldest queued item ages by minutes, and when the downstream recovers, it receives a burst at full speed. In version P, memory stays flat, because at most a few hundred items are ever in flight; the reader simply slows down, so the table itself acts as the durable queue, and the downstream sees a steady rate before, during and after the slowdown. End-to-end latency per item stays roughly constant in P and grows without bound in Q. With concurrent retries in the sink, both versions can reorder items, but Q reorders many more because far more items are in flight at once; ordering per entity can be restored in P by routing each entity to one sink worker. If the process crashes during the slowdown, Q loses (or must replay) its whole in-memory backlog, while P loses only the few items in flight, and simply resumes reading the table from its last checkpoint.

A.15 Instrument and observe

↑ Back to lab A.15

Hint 1

OpenTelemetry's propagators work with any "carrier" that maps strings to strings. A map inside your message body works as well as HTTP headers.

Hint 2

When a node receives a message, extract the context from the message and start the handling span as its child. When it sends a message, inject the current context into the outgoing message.

Solution approach Structure that works

Initialise a tracer provider at node start-up with an OTLP exporter pointing at the collector, and a resource attribute naming the node (for example service.name = broadcast and service.instance.id = n3). For each incoming message, extract the trace context from the body and start a server span named after the message type; for each outgoing message, start a client span, inject its context into the body, and end it when the reply arrives. A client's broadcast then appears as one trace spreading across nodes. For the SLI, record the time each node first sees each message (as span events or logs with the message ID) and compute, per message, the time until the last node saw it; the SLI is the share of messages where that time is under one second. Expect it to drop sharply during partitions and recover after them.

A.16 Measure the hop

↑ Back to lab A.16

Hint 1

Make sure the server for each variant is warmed up before you measure, and that the load generator runs on a separate core or machine. Otherwise you measure the generator, not the hop.

Hint 2

For the map, a deployment unit is "a thing that is built and released as one". Count teams by who merges changes into it in a typical quarter, not by who is formally listed as the owner.

Solution approach What you should see

A function call costs nanoseconds; an HTTP call over localhost typically costs tens to hundreds of microseconds, mostly in the network stack and serialisation; a call to another container adds a little more; a call across machines in one data centre adds network round-trip time, and TLS, proxies and larger payloads push typical calls towards a millisecond or more. The 99.9th percentile grows much faster than the median as you add layers. On the map, most teams find at least one unit changed by several teams (a candidate for splitting along team lines) and at least one team that owns many tiny units (a candidate for merging). The typical request often turns out to have more synchronous hops than anyone expected; multiplying their availabilities is usually a sobering moment.

A.17 Mixed-version test

↑ Back to lab A.17

Hint 1

The sequences that fail are the ones in which a value produced by version 2 reaches version 1: a token sent back to an old server, and a WAITLISTED row read by version 1 after the rollback.

Hint 2

Ask, for each new value: which versions produce it, and which versions might receive it? A change is safe only when every possible receiver was deployed before any producer started.

Solution approach Fixing the change

Release 1 adds the ability to understand everything new: version 1.1 accepts and correctly processes a continuationToken (or at least rejects it with a clear, retryable error rather than silently returning page one), and maps an unknown status such as WAITLISTED to a safe behaviour in all code paths that read bookings. It also accepts seatPreference but may ignore it. Nothing yet produces a token or writes WAITLISTED. Deploy 1.1 everywhere. Release 2 (or a flag in 1.1) starts producing tokens and writing WAITLISTED. Now every sequence passes: any server a client reaches understands the token; a rollback goes back to 1.1, which can read WAITLISTED rows; replays of old responses still work because nothing was removed. The seatPreference field needs one more thought: during the rollout, an old server may silently ignore it, so a client must not depend on it being honoured until the rollout is complete, which is a good reason to enable it with a flag.

A.18 Canary with automatic rollback

↑ Back to lab A.18

Hint 1

Write the Prometheus query as a ratio of rates over a short window, filtered to the canary's version or pod labels: errors per second divided by requests per second. Test it in the Prometheus UI before putting it in the analysis.

Hint 2

If the analysis passes v2 too often, look at how many requests the canary actually receives during each analysis interval. At 10 % of a small load, a 5 % error rate may only be a handful of errors.

Solution approach What you should see

With a 5 % error rate and enough traffic, the analysis fails at the first or second canary step, the Rollout aborts, and traffic returns to the stable version automatically. At 0.5 % and 0.1 %, a fixed threshold of 1 % does not catch the bad version at all: the more traffic the canary receives, the more reliably its measured error rate settles below the threshold, and the rollout completes. (With very little traffic, a short window may occasionally cross 1 % by chance, which gives a random abort rather than real detection.) Lowering the threshold helps only if the stable version's own error rate is lower still. Three improvements help: compare the canary with the stable version's error rate in the same window rather than with a fixed threshold; give each step enough traffic and time (rare failures need many requests to show up); and include latency percentiles as well as errors. This is chapter 15's point in practice: a canary is only as good as the statistics behind it.

A.19 Capstone: write a postmortem

↑ Back to lab A.19

Hint 1

Write the timeline first and the analysis last. Many weak postmortems are weak because the timeline was reconstructed from memory instead of from logs and graphs.

Hint 2

If a contributing factor names a person or a team ("the engineer ran the wrong command"), rewrite it to name the system property that made the action dangerous ("the tool allowed removing capacity below the safe minimum in one step").

Solution approach Review rubric

A strong postmortem: states user impact in the first paragraph, with numbers; has a timeline in one time zone with sources; names at least three contributing factors, each linked to a concept from this book (a system model assumption, a consistency trade-off, a sustaining effect, an observability gap); explains why the trigger had the effect it had, not only what the trigger was; lists what went well; has action items that each address a contributing factor, with an owner and a priority; and contains no blame. Score each of these from 0 to 2; a total of 12 or more out of 14 is excellent.

Back matter

Recommended Reading

What to read next, why, and in which order

There is no shortage of material on distributed systems. The difficulty is choosing. A beginner who starts with Nancy Lynch's Distributed Algorithms will likely give up after fifty pages of formal automata; an experienced engineer who rereads introductory blog posts will learn little. This guide recommends a small number of resources, explains why each one earned its place, and suggests reading paths for different goals.

The recommendations are based on what leading university courses use, what practitioners repeatedly recommend, and how well each resource fits the sequence of this book. Editions and dates were checked in autumn 2026; check for newer editions before buying.

If you only read three things

Why this one · 1. Kleppmann and Riccomini, Designing Data-Intensive Applications (2nd ed.)

The single most useful book for engineers in this field. It explains replication, partitioning, transactions, consistency and consensus by grounding every concept in how real databases behave, and it is honest about the gaps between theory and marketing. Read it after this book's Part II.

Why this one · 2. Martin Kleppmann's Cambridge lecture course Distributed Systems (free)

Eight lectures (about seven hours of video) with 87 pages of lecture notes and exercises. It covers the theoretical core of this book (system models, time, broadcast, replication, consensus, CRDTs) with precision and excellent diagrams. Watch it alongside Parts I and II.

Why this one · 3. Fly.io's Gossip Glomers challenges (free)

Reading cannot replace the experience of watching your own code fail under a simulated partition. These challenges, built on Jepsen's Maelstrom, are short, language-independent and automatically checked for consistency. They are the labs of this book for a reason.

Reading paths

Choose the path that matches your goal. Each step assumes the previous ones.

Path A: the developer who builds and operates services (recommended default)

  1. Takada, Distributed systems for fun and profit (one evening). A gentle overview to warm up.
  2. Vitillo, Understanding Distributed Systems (all of it). Practical, short, and covers operations as well as theory.
  3. Kleppmann's Cambridge lectures with this book's Part II.
  4. DDIA, 2nd edition, especially the chapters on replication, sharding, transactions, the trouble with distributed systems, consistency and consensus.
  5. Nygard, Release It!, and the Google SRE book's chapters on overload and cascading failure, with this book's Part III.
  6. Newman, Building Microservices, Skelton and Pais, Team Topologies, and Forsgren, Humble and Kim, Accelerate, with this book's Part IV.
  7. A handful of Jepsen analyses of databases you use, and the papers marked "essential" below.
  8. Labs throughout: Gossip Glomers, then optionally MIT 6.5840's Raft.

Path B: the developer who wants the theory in depth (or is heading towards research)

  1. Kleppmann's Cambridge lectures and notes.
  2. van Steen and Tanenbaum, Distributed Systems (4th ed.), chapters on coordination, consistency and replication, and fault tolerance.
  3. Cachin, Guerraoui and Rodrigues, Introduction to Reliable and Secure Distributed Programming.
  4. Selected chapters of Lynch, Distributed Algorithms, or Attiya and Welch, Distributed Computing.
  5. The MIT 6.5840 reading list, working through the papers in order.

Path C: learning by building

Read Vitillo while doing Gossip Glomers challenges 1–4. Then read DDIA in parallel with MIT 6.5840 labs 2–4 (key/value server, Raft, key/value service on Raft). Finish with Petrov's Database Internals to see how real storage and replication engines are structured.

Books, with reasons

Each entry explains why the book is recommended, when to read it and what to be aware of.

Kleppmann and Riccomini, Designing Data-Intensive Applications, 2nd edition (O'Reilly, 2026)

Level: intermediate to advanced. Focus: theory grounded in practice.

Why: It is the bridge between academic distributed systems and the databases engineers actually use. It does not just define consistency models or isolation levels; it shows which databases provide which ones, where the documentation is misleading, and what anomalies you will meet. The chapter on "the trouble with distributed systems" is the best single treatment of partial failure, clocks and process pauses in print, and many of this book's examples (the doctors' write skew, fencing tokens, the 2012 GitHub failover) are discussed there in more depth. The first edition (2017) is still useful, but the second edition, co-authored with Chris Riccomini, brings the material up to date.

When: after Part II of this book, or alongside it.

Be aware: it is long (about 670 pages). Read selectively: the chapters on replication, sharding, transactions, distributed systems problems, and consistency and consensus matter most for this book's readers.

Roberto Vitillo, Understanding Distributed Systems, 2nd edition (self-published, 2022)

Level: beginner to intermediate. Focus: practice.

Why: It covers the whole territory (communication, coordination, scalability, resilience and maintainability, including monitoring and observability) in about 340 pages, written by an engineer for engineers. It is the best "first real book" because it explains the why without drowning the reader in formalism, and its resilience and operations chapters match Part III of this book closely.

When: early, ideally right after Part I of this book.

Be aware: breadth comes at the cost of depth. Treat it as a map, then go deeper with DDIA.

Maarten van Steen and Andrew S. Tanenbaum, Distributed Systems, 4th edition (2023)

Level: intermediate. Focus: broad textbook.

Why: The classic university textbook, covering architectures, processes, communication, naming, coordination, consistency and replication, fault tolerance and security. A personal digital copy is free from distributed-systems.net, along with complete slide sets, which makes it ideal as a reference and for teachers. It has been the course book for CMU 15-440 and many other courses. (Note that the authors are van Steen and Tanenbaum; earlier editions credited Tanenbaum first.)

When: as a reference while reading this book, and for topics this book only touches, such as naming and security.

Be aware: its structure follows topics rather than a narrative; it is better for looking things up than for reading cover to cover.

George Coulouris, Jean Dollimore, Tim Kindberg and Gordon Blair, Distributed Systems: Concepts and Design, 5th edition (Addison-Wesley, 2011)

Level: intermediate. Focus: broad textbook.

Why: A respected alternative to van Steen and Tanenbaum, with good coverage of fundamentals.

When: only if you already own it or your course uses it.

Be aware: the 2011 edition predates much of the modern landscape (Raft, CRDTs in practice, cloud-native operations).

Christian Cachin, Rachid Guerraoui and Luís Rodrigues, Introduction to Reliable and Secure Distributed Programming, 2nd edition (Springer, 2011)

Level: advanced. Focus: algorithms.

Why: It builds distributed algorithms as a tower of abstractions (failure detectors, reliable and causal broadcast, shared memory registers, consensus, total order broadcast, Byzantine variants), each specified precisely and implemented in modular pseudocode on top of the layers below. Working through it trains you to think in terms of specifications and system models, the skill introduced in chapter 3. Advanced courses such as KTH's are built in this style.

When: after this book and Kleppmann's lectures, if you want rigour.

Be aware: it is a book to study with pen and paper, not to skim.

Nancy Lynch, Distributed Algorithms (Morgan Kaufmann, 1996)

Level: research. Focus: formal theory.

Why: The definitive reference for proofs and impossibility results, written by one of the authors of FLP and of the formal CAP proof. When you need to know exactly what can and cannot be done in a given model, this is where to look.

When: for specific topics, as a reference.

Be aware: it is not an introduction. Read selected chapters.

Hagit Attiya and Jennifer Welch, Distributed Computing: Fundamentals, Simulations, and Advanced Topics, 2nd edition (Wiley, 2004)

Level: advanced. Focus: theory.

Why: A more approachable theoretical text than Lynch, covering models, simulations between models, and lower bounds. It is listed as course literature for KTH's advanced course ID2203.

When: if you follow Path B.

Alex Petrov, Database Internals (O'Reilly, 2019)

Level: intermediate to advanced. Focus: implementation.

Why: Part I explains storage engines (B-trees, log-structured merge trees); Part II explains the distributed side (failure detection, leader election, replication, anti-entropy, consensus) from the perspective of someone implementing a database. It turns the concepts of this book's Part II into data structures and code paths.

When: after DDIA, when you want to know how things work inside.

Michael Nygard, Release It! Design and Deploy Production-Ready Software, 2nd edition (Pragmatic Bookshelf, 2018)

Level: intermediate. Focus: operations and stability.

Why: The origin of the stability patterns in chapter 11 (circuit breakers, bulkheads, timeouts, steady state) and of the corresponding antipatterns (cascading failures, blocked threads, unbounded result sets). Every pattern is motivated by a real production failure, told as a story, which makes the lessons memorable. Particularly valuable for SRE and platform teams.

When: with Part III of this book.

Google, Site Reliability Engineering (2016), The Site Reliability Workbook (2018) and Building Secure and Reliable Systems (2020)

Level: intermediate. Focus: operating systems at scale.

Why: All three are free to read at sre.google. The first introduces SLOs, error budgets, monitoring and incident response, and its chapters "Handling Overload" and "Addressing Cascading Failures" complement chapter 11 directly. The workbook makes the ideas practical, including the burn-rate alerting described in chapter 12.

When: with Part III, and whenever you design alerting or on-call.

Be aware: they describe Google's environment. Adapt rather than copy.

Charity Majors, Liz Fong-Jones and George Miranda, Observability Engineering (O'Reilly, 2022)

Level: intermediate. Focus: observability practice.

Why: A clear argument for observability as the ability to ask new questions, with practical guidance on structured events, tracing, SLO-based alerting and observability-driven development. It complements chapter 12.

Brendan Burns, Designing Distributed Systems, 2nd edition (O'Reilly, 2024)

Level: beginner to intermediate. Focus: patterns in the container era.

Why: A compact catalogue of patterns (sidecar, ambassador, adapter, sharded services, scatter-gather, batch workflows), written by a co-founder of Kubernetes. The second edition adds material on AI inference and training workloads. Useful alongside chapter 10.

Unmesh Joshi, Patterns of Distributed Systems (Addison-Wesley, 2023)

Level: intermediate. Focus: implementation patterns.

Why: It breaks systems such as Kafka, ZooKeeper and Cassandra into recurring building blocks (write-ahead log, high-water mark, leader and followers, generation clock, lease, gossip dissemination), each explained with code. It is a good companion when you implement the labs or read the source code of real systems.

Sam Newman, Building Microservices, 2nd edition (O'Reilly, 2021), and Monolith to Microservices (O'Reilly, 2019)

Level: intermediate. Focus: architecture and organisation.

Why: The most balanced book-length treatment of microservices. Newman puts independent deployability at the centre of the definition, discusses the costs as seriously as the benefits, and is explicit that microservices are not a sensible default for every organisation. Monolith to Microservices is about the migration itself: how to split a system incrementally, including the strangler fig pattern and the hard part, splitting the database. Read both with chapter 13.

Matthew Skelton and Manuel Pais, Team Topologies (IT Revolution, 2019)

Level: beginner to intermediate. Focus: team design.

Why: It makes Conway's law practical. The book's ideas of team cognitive load, stream-aligned teams and platform teams give you a principled way to decide service boundaries from the team structure, which is the starting point of chapter 13. Short and very readable.

Titus Winters, Tom Manshreck and Hyrum Wright, Software Engineering at Google (O'Reilly, 2020)

Level: intermediate. Focus: engineering practice at scale.

Why: Where Hyrum's Law comes from, with chapters on dependency management, large-scale changes, testing and deprecation that deepen chapter 14. Free to read online.

Jez Humble and David Farley, Continuous Delivery (Addison-Wesley, 2010), and Nicole Forsgren, Jez Humble and Gene Kim, Accelerate (IT Revolution, 2018)

Level: intermediate. Focus: deployment and delivery.

Why: Continuous Delivery is the foundational book on deployment pipelines, automated testing and low-risk releases, including blue/green and canary releases. Accelerate presents the research behind the DORA metrics and its central finding that speed and stability go together. Read them with chapter 15. For the operational detail of safe, automated rollouts, add Clare Liguori's Builders' Library article Automating safe, hands-off deployments.

Mikito Takada, Distributed systems for fun and profit (free, 2013)

Level: beginner. Focus: short conceptual overview.

Why: A free, short online book covering abstractions, time and order, replication and CRDTs. It is the gentlest possible introduction and an ideal warm-up before Vitillo or this book's Part II.

Be aware: it dates from 2013 and does not cover Raft.

Courses and online resources

Resource Why it is recommended
Martin Kleppmann, Cambridge Distributed Systems (YouTube and lecture notes, CC BY-SA) The best free introduction to the theory, in the same order as this book's Parts I and II. Lecture notes include exercises. Its licence allows adaptation with attribution.
MIT 6.5840 Distributed Systems Engineering (pdos.csail.mit.edu/6.824) A paper-driven course with demanding Go labs: MapReduce, a key/value server, Raft, a key/value service on Raft, and a sharded store. The reading list is an excellent tour of influential systems. Lecture videos from 2020 are online; the schedule changes from year to year.
CMU 15-440 Distributed Systems An undergraduate systems course with Go projects, historically using van Steen and Tanenbaum. Good if you want a course structured around a textbook.
KTH ID2203 Distributed Systems, Advanced Course A Swedish advanced course covering failure detectors, broadcast, shared memory, consensus (Paxos and Raft), reconfiguration and distributed transactions. Relevant for readers in Sweden; check the current course memo for literature.
Fly.io Gossip Glomers Six series of hands-on challenges on Maelstrom, checked by Jepsen's checkers. Language-independent and free.
Jepsen analyses and consistency map (jepsen.io) Real-world reports of databases violating their claimed guarantees, and the clearest reference for consistency models. Reading the analysis of a database you use is eye-opening.
Amazon Builders' Library Articles by AWS engineers on timeouts and retries, idempotent APIs, static stability, shuffle sharding and more. Practical, concise and based on operating at scale.
Kyle Kingsbury's distsys-class (GitHub: aphyr/distsys-class) A compact outline of an intensive distributed systems class, useful as a checklist of topics.
Caitie McCaffrey, Building Scalable Stateful Services (Strange Loop 2015, video and slides) The clearest practitioner's argument for keeping state resident with its computation, drawn from building the Halo 4 backend on Orleans. Watch it together with chapter 10's sections on state-reconstructing and state-resident services.
Raft website (raft.github.io) The paper, an interactive visualisation, and a list of implementations.
Marc Brooker's blog (brooker.co.za/blog) and his AWS Architecture Blog post Exponential Backoff And Jitter (2015) Short, rigorous posts by a senior AWS engineer, often backed by simulations and charts. Start with Exponential Backoff And Jitter, then read Metastability and Distributed Systems, Caches, Modes, and Unstable Systems, What is Backoff For?, Fixing retries with token buckets and circuit breakers and Open and Closed, Omission and Collapse. Together they are the best short course on why retries and timeouts behave so differently under overload, and they underpin chapter 11.

Papers, in the order to read them

Papers are where the ideas in this book were first described. These fifteen are listed in a sequence that follows the book. Those marked essential are worth reading in full even on Path A.

# Paper Why read it Chapter
1 Saltzer, Reed and Clark, End-to-End Arguments in System Design (1984) A principle you will apply forever, in about a dozen pages. Essential. 0
2 Lamport, Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System (CACM, 1978) Introduces happens-before, logical clocks and state machine replication in one short paper. Essential. 4
3 Fischer, Lynch and Paterson, Impossibility of Distributed Consensus with One Faulty Process (JACM, 1985) The FLP result. Read it to see how an impossibility proof works. 3
4 Lamport, Shostak and Pease, The Byzantine Generals Problem (1982) Where Byzantine faults and the 3f + 1 bound come from. 3
5 Lamport, Paxos Made Simple (2001) A short paper describing the algorithm that underlies most production consensus. 7
6 Ongaro and Ousterhout, In Search of an Understandable Consensus Algorithm (extended version, 2014) Raft. Figure 2 is a complete specification on one page. Essential. 7
7 Gilbert and Lynch, Brewer's Conjecture and the Feasibility of Consistent, Available, Partition-Tolerant Web Services (2002) The formal CAP proof. Short, and it fixes what C and A mean. 6
8 Brewer, CAP Twelve Years Later: How the "Rules" Have Changed (IEEE Computer, 2012) Brewer's own correction of the "2 of 3" myth. Essential. 6
9 Abadi, Consistency Tradeoffs in Modern Distributed Database System Design (IEEE Computer, 2012) PACELC: the latency side of the trade-off. 6
10 DeCandia et al., Dynamo: Amazon's Highly Available Key-value Store (SOSP 2007) Quorums, sloppy quorums, vector clocks and anti-entropy, all in a production system. Essential. 5
11 Burrows, The Chubby Lock Service for Loosely-Coupled Distributed Systems (OSDI 2006), and Hunt et al., ZooKeeper (USENIX ATC 2010) Why coordination services exist and how they are used. 7
12 Corbett et al., Spanner: Google's Globally-Distributed Database (OSDI 2012) TrueTime, commit wait and 2PC over Paxos groups. 4, 8
13 Shapiro et al., Conflict-free Replicated Data Types (2011) The formal foundation for chapter 9. 9
14 Dean and Barroso, The Tail at Scale (CACM, 2013) Why fan-out makes rare slowness common, and what to do. Essential. 10
15 Bronson et al., Metastable Failures in Distributed Systems (HotOS 2021), with Huang et al., Metastable Failures in the Wild (OSDI 2022) A name and a model for the outages that outlast their causes. Essential for anyone on call. 11
Tip · How to read a paper

Read a systems paper in three passes, following S. Keshav's How to Read a Paper: first the abstract, introduction, headings and conclusion (five minutes) to decide whether it is relevant; then the whole paper, skipping proofs, while noting questions; finally, for important papers, try to reconstruct the design yourself and compare. Write a one-paragraph summary in your notebook after each paper: what problem, what idea, what evidence, what limitation.

Postmortem collections

Real incidents are the best test of the concepts in this book. Good sources include the AWS Post-Event Summaries page, the Cloudflare blog, the GitHub blog's availability reports, Google Cloud's incident reports, and Dan Luu's curated list of public postmortems on GitHub. Read one a week, and map it with the six questions from chapter 16.

A note on what this guide leaves out

This guide deliberately omits security-focused texts, stream-processing books and vendor-specific documentation. These are valuable, but they are better read once the foundations here are solid. It also omits much excellent material simply to keep the list short enough to be useful. When in doubt, choose the resource that makes you do something: implement, test, break and observe.

Back matter

Glossary

Key terms with the chapter where each is introduced

Term Definition Ch.
Agile Release Train In SAFe, a long-lived team of agile teams (typically 50–125 people) that plans and delivers together on a fixed cadence; a sign that teams share a release 13
Anti-entropy Background process in which replicas compare their data (often via Merkle trees) and copy missing updates to each other 5, 9
At-least-once / at-most-once Delivery semantics: retry until acknowledged (possible duplicates) or send once (possible loss) 2
Atomic commit The problem of ensuring all participants in a distributed transaction either commit or abort 8
Availability Fraction of time a service works correctly; in CAP, the stricter requirement that every non-failing node responds 1, 6
Availability Zone A group of data centres within a cloud region with independent power, cooling and networking 1
Backpressure Signalling from consumers to producers to slow down, for example via bounded queues 11
Backward compatibility New versions keep working with old clients, old messages and old stored data 14
Bake time A period after each deployment stage during which alarms are watched before the rollout continues 15
Blue/green deployment Running the new version beside the old and switching all traffic at once, with the option to switch back 15
Bulkhead Isolating resources (pools, instances, cells) so that one failure cannot exhaust everything 11
Burn rate How fast an error budget is being consumed, relative to the rate that would exactly use it up over the SLO window 12
Byzantine fault A fault in which a node may behave arbitrarily, including lying; tolerating f such faults needs 3f + 1 nodes 3
CALM Consistency As Logical Monotonicity: a problem has a coordination-free consistent implementation if and only if it is monotonic 9
Canary deployment Sending a small share of traffic to a new version and comparing its metrics with the old before expanding 15
CAP theorem In an asynchronous network that may partition, a system cannot guarantee both linearizability and (CAP) availability 6
Causal consistency Causally related writes are seen in the same order by all processes; concurrent writes may be seen in different orders 6
Cell-based architecture Deploying several independent copies of a whole stack, each serving a subset of customers, to limit blast radius 11
Circuit breaker A wrapper that stops calling a failing dependency for a while (closed, open, half-open states) 11
Consensus Getting processes to decide on one value with agreement, validity and termination 7
Consistent hashing Placing nodes and keys on a hash ring so that adding or removing a node moves only about 1/N of keys 5
Conway's law Systems tend to mirror the communication structure of the organisations that build them 13
Crash-recovery A failure model in which nodes may crash and later restart with their durably stored state 3
CRDT Conflict-free replicated data type: a data type whose replicas converge without coordination 9
Data shipping / function shipping Moving data to the computation (as state-reconstructing services do) versus moving the computation to the data (as actors do) 10
Deadline propagation Passing a request's remaining time budget to downstream calls 11
Deploy versus release Putting code on production machines versus making a change visible to users; separated with feature flags 15
Deterministic simulation testing Running a whole system inside a simulator with all nondeterminism controlled by a seed, so failures are reproducible 12
Distributed monolith Services that cannot be changed or deployed independently, combining the costs of distribution with the coupling of a monolith 13
Effectively-once processing At-least-once delivery plus idempotent or deduplicated processing, so that each operation's effect occurs once 2
End-to-end argument Reliability functions can be completely implemented only at the application endpoints 0
Error budget The amount of failure an SLO allows (100 % minus the SLO) 12
Eventual consistency If updates stop, all replicas eventually return the same value 6
Expand and contract (parallel change) Changing a structure by adding the new form beside the old, migrating, and removing the old form last 14
Failover Promoting a replica to replace a failed leader 5
Failure detector A component that outputs which nodes are suspected to have failed, typically based on heartbeats and timeouts 3
Feature flag A runtime switch that decides whether a code path is used; kinds include release, experiment, ops (kill switch) and permission flags 15
Fencing token A monotonically increasing number issued with a lock or lease, checked by the protected resource to reject stale holders 7
FLP impossibility No deterministic algorithm can guarantee consensus in an asynchronous system if one process may crash 3
Forward compatibility Old versions keep working with new clients, messages and data; required for safe rollback 14
Goodput The rate of useful work: requests completed successfully for clients that were still waiting, as opposed to raw throughput 11
Gossip protocol Spreading information by having each node periodically exchange it with random peers 9
Gray failure Partial, subtle failure that the system's own health checks may not detect 1
Happens-before (→) Lamport's causal ordering of events: same process order, send before receive, and transitivity 4
Hedged request Sending a duplicate request to another replica after a delay, and using whichever answer comes first 10
Hinted handoff Temporarily storing a write on a non-home node and passing it on when the home node returns 5
Hybrid logical clock (HLC) A timestamp combining physical time and a logical counter, respecting causality while staying close to wall-clock time 4
Hyrum's Law With enough users, every observable behaviour of a system will be depended on by somebody, whatever the contract says 14
Idempotency Property of an operation whose repeated application has the same effect as a single application 2
Idempotency key A client-generated unique ID for a logical operation, used by the server to detect retries 2
Lamport clock A logical counter such that if a → b then L(a) < L(b) 4
Lease A lock with an expiry time 7
Limpware / fail-slow Hardware or networks that keep working but far below their normal performance; often more damaging than outright failure 3
Linearizability Every operation appears to take effect atomically at one instant between its start and end; the system behaves like a single copy 6
Little's law L = λW: items in system equals arrival rate times time in system 10
Liveness A property stating that something good eventually happens 3
Load shedding Rejecting excess work early and cheaply under overload 11
Merkle tree A hash tree allowing two replicas to find differing data by comparing few hashes 5, 9
Metastable failure A failure sustained by a feedback loop (such as retries) after its trigger has gone 11
Microservice A service owned, built, deployed and operated by one team, organised around a business capability and deployable on its own 13
Modular monolith One deployment unit with strictly enforced internal module boundaries 13
Monolith (shared deployment unit) A deployment unit changed by several teams, which must coordinate to release and roll back 13
Monotonic clock A clock that measures elapsed time and never jumps backwards; for durations on one machine 4
Monotonic reads / writes Session guarantees: a client never sees older data after newer; a client's writes are applied in order 6
Multi-leader replication Several nodes accept writes and replicate to each other, requiring conflict resolution 5
Network partition A period in which some running nodes cannot communicate with others 3
Open / closed system Queueing models in which new requests arrive independently of earlier ones (open) or only after earlier ones complete (closed); backoff reduces load only in closed systems 11
OpenTelemetry A vendor-neutral standard and toolkit for metrics, logs and traces 12
OR-set Observed-remove set CRDT, in which concurrent adds win over removes 9
Outbox pattern Writing an event to an outbox table in the same transaction as the state change, then publishing it asynchronously 8
PACELC If Partition, choose Availability or Consistency; Else, choose Latency or Consistency 6
Partial failure Some components fail while others continue, and the survivors may not know which failed 1
Partially synchronous model A timing model in which the system is eventually well-behaved for long enough periods, but not always 3
Partitioning (sharding) Splitting data so that different nodes store different subsets 5
Paxos Lamport's consensus algorithm using prepare/promise and accept/accepted phases with numbered proposals 7
PRAM (FIFO) consistency All processes see each single process's writes in the order issued; equivalent to RYW + MR + MW 6
Quorum A set of nodes whose agreement is required; with R + W > N, read and write quorums overlap 5, 7
Raft A consensus algorithm designed for understandability, with terms, leader election and log replication 7
Rate limit A cap on requests per unit of time for a key (service, customer, client, endpoint), ideally configurable at runtime and per customer 11
Read repair Writing the newest value back to stale replicas discovered during a read 5
Read your writes (RYW) Session guarantee: a client always sees its own previous writes 6
Re-mirroring storm Many storage nodes re-replicating data at once after a network event, overloading the cluster with recovery traffic (Amazon EBS, 2011) 16
Replication lag The delay between a write on the leader and its application on a follower 5
Retry budget A limit on retries as a fraction of normal traffic 11
Roll back / roll forward Returning to the previous version versus deploying a fix; rollback is the default first move 15
Safety A property stating that something bad never happens 3
Saga A sequence of local transactions with compensating actions instead of a distributed atomic commit 8
Semantic Versioning MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH version numbers, where a major increase signals an incompatible change 14
Sequential consistency All processes see one global order of operations that respects each process's program order 6
Serializability Concurrent transactions have the same effect as some serial order 8
SLI / SLO / SLA Service level indicator (a measured ratio), objective (a target), agreement (a contract) 12
Sloppy quorum Accepting writes on any reachable nodes, not only the key's home replicas 5
Snapshot isolation Transactions read a consistent snapshot; concurrent writes to the same object conflict; permits write skew 8
Span / trace A timed unit of work / the tree of spans for one request 12
Split brain Two nodes simultaneously acting as leader 5, 7
State machine replication Replicating a deterministic service by applying the same log of commands on every replica 7
Stateless service (truly) A service whose output depends only on its input, like a pure function, so nothing needs to be stored or rebuilt 10
State-reconstructing service A service whose instances keep no state between requests and therefore rebuild the state they need from external stores on every invocation; what most "stateless" services actually are 10
State-resident service A service that keeps the state it serves in memory with a single owner and routes requests to that owner, as actors and virtual actors do 10
Static stability A design that keeps working during a dependency failure without needing to make changes 11
Strangler fig Migrating from an old system by routing one capability at a time to a new service until the old system can be retired 13
Strong eventual consistency Replicas that have received the same updates are in the same state 6, 9
Tail latency High-percentile latency (p99, p99.9), which dominates when requests fan out 10
Term (Raft) A numbered period with at most one leader; acts as a logical clock exposing stale leaders 7
Timeout A local decision to stop waiting; it means the outcome is unknown, not failed 2
Tolerant reader A consumer that ignores unknown fields and values instead of failing on them 14
Total order broadcast All nodes deliver all messages in the same order; equivalent to consensus 4, 7
TrueTime Spanner's clock API that returns an interval guaranteed to contain the true time 4
Two Generals Problem Agreement over a lossy channel is impossible with a finite number of messages 2
Two-phase commit (2PC) Atomic commit protocol with a prepare phase and a commit phase; blocks if the coordinator fails 8
Vector clock A vector of per-process counters that captures causality exactly and detects concurrency 4
Virtual actor (grain) An actor with a stable identity that is activated on demand by the runtime (Orleans) 10
Write skew An anomaly where two transactions read overlapping data and write different objects, jointly violating an invariant 8
